# A Ship of Compassion on the Sea of Awakening: Ferrying Beings Across in Hong Kong — Records of the Life of the Venerable Master Hsuan Hua

## The Hong Kong Years

*Compiled by the Editorial Committee for the Records of the Life of the Venerable Master Hsuan Hua*

## Prologue

In the Venerable Master's own words:

This too came about through a kind of causal connection. The karmic winds [Tr.: the force of one's karma, which drives beings from place to place like a wind] blew me from Manchuria to Tianjin, and then from Tianjin to Shanghai. I came to Hubei, and not long after I arrived in Hubei, they blew me to Mount Putuo. Then, passing by way of Lingyan Mountain in Suzhou, I went to Guangdong to draw near to Elder Master Hsu Yun. I stayed in Guangdong for a while, and then these karmic winds blew me to Hong Kong. Once in Hong Kong, I stayed for more than ten years, so I had this affinity with Hong Kong, neither large nor small; probably it was all meant to be. In 1960 I went to Australia; in 1961 I returned to Hong Kong, and not long after, in 1962, I came to the United States.

---

# Part One: Coming to Hong Kong and Awaiting Conditions

*Ages 32 to 34*

*1949 to 1951 CE*

## 01. Going to Hong Kong in Accord with Conditions

In the summer of 1949, the Venerable Master visited Hong Kong for the first time. After he returned to the mainland, however, Shaoguan changed hands, so he could not go back to Yunmen; he therefore took a boat from Guangzhou to Hong Kong once again. From then on, the Venerable Master stayed in Hong Kong, according with conditions, and began his journey of propagating the Dharma and teaching overseas...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In the thirty-eighth year of the Republic (1949), I went to Hong Kong for the first time and stayed as a visiting monk [Tr.: the monastic custom of lodging temporarily at a monastery while traveling] at Tung Po Tor Monastery. In the seventh month, I went back from Hong Kong to Guangzhou, planning to return to Dajue Monastery at Yunmen after the Mid-Autumn Festival. But in the eighth month Shaoguan was liberated (Editor's note: according to the *Chronicle of Major Events in the Local History of Shaoguan*, Shaoguan was liberated on October 7, 1949, which was the sixteenth day of the eighth lunar month), and I could not get back to Yunmen. At that time Guangzhou was in imminent danger. I wanted to go to Hong Kong again, but I didn't have even a single dollar in my hand, and on top of that I had a disciple with me, Guo Yi (Heng Ding). On the eighteenth day of the eighth month, the two of us, master and disciple, went to the ship terminal to see whether there were conditions for us to get to Hong Kong, and there we ran into Upasika Chen Kuan Man. I remember that when I was at Nanhua Monastery, the lay Buddhist Xie Kuan Hui, who had come from Guangzhou, said to more than ten laypeople, "Many of the Dharma Masters at Nanhua Monastery don't have enough clothes to wear!" So these lay Buddhists resolved to buy cloth as an offering to the Sangha, and Upasika Chen Kuan Man was one of the donors. Someone introduced me to her at that time, and later we met again at Liurong Monastery in Guangzhou. When Chen Kuan Man saw us at the ship terminal, she said to me, "I'm going to Hong Kong today. Would you like to go?" I said, "I'd like to go. Will you buy me a ticket?" So she bought two tickets for me, and that night we left Guangzhou by boat, arriving in Hong Kong early the next morning. Those were the conditions that brought me to Hong Kong.

I was born the kind of person who fears neither heaven nor earth, neither gods nor ghosts, and I'm not even afraid of Americans speaking Chinese. The newspapers said that if I hadn't been afraid of the Communists, I wouldn't have drifted overseas. I didn't run overseas because I was afraid of the Communists; it was brought about by the conditions of the times. All along the way, no sooner had I taken a step forward than the ground behind me was occupied by the Communists. Transportation was cut off, and even if I had wanted to go back, I couldn't. That is how I came to Hong Kong. I lived in Hong Kong for more than ten years, until I went to America in 1962.

**[Addendum 1]** Tung Po Tor Monastery was originally a Way-place for only a few dozen monastics, but in the late 1940s it took on the responsibilities of a public monastery of the ten directions [Tr.: open to all monastics, as opposed to a private hereditary temple]. The reason was the war on the Chinese mainland, after which many monastics fled from the mainland to Hong Kong as refugees: "By day they wandered the streets; by night they had nowhere to rest."

At that time Dharma Master Mao Feng, the abbot of Tung Po Tor, brought forth great compassion and vowed to take in the refugee monks. He insisted on "opening the monastery gates wide and receiving all comers with lodging as vast as the sea," and took the attitude of accepting monastics from the mainland unconditionally. He said, "Every blade of grass has its drop of dew; every Arhat has his share of the meal." In a single day, as many as a thousand monastics came to lodge there. Tung Po Tor turned no one away, and when faithful devotees everywhere heard of it, they donated rice and grain one after another, so that the monastery never suffered from running out of food.

Dharma Master Mao Feng also led the monks in "carrying the timber on their own shoulders and cutting the bamboo poles themselves" to build a "Cloud and Water Hall" to receive the male monastics, and they also built a "palm-leaf shed" to house the female monastics.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi has known the Venerable Master since she was a child and has been his refuge disciple [Tr.: one who has formally become a Buddhist disciple by taking refuge in the Buddha, Dharma, and Sangha] for more than 50 years now. Upasika Tan recounted:

When the Master first came to Hong Kong, before he found Guanyin Cave, he stayed as a visiting monk at Tung Po Tor, where Dharma Master Mao Feng was the abbot. Once the Master took me to see Dharma Master Mao Feng. The Master respected him very much. Regardless of how other Dharma Masters behaved, whenever the Master saw a monastic of an older generation, he would always bow to them first.

**[Addendum 3]** Dharma Master Heng Ding, whose Dharma name was Guo Yi, was the fourth of the Venerable Master's monastic disciples to receive full ordination. The four were Dharma Master Guo Neng (Heng Ji), Dharma Master Guo Shun (Heng Yu), Dharma Master Guo Wei, and Dharma Master Heng Ding. Dharma Master Heng Ding was the only disciple who followed the Venerable Master to Hong Kong; he passed away in Hong Kong in 2003. Bhikshu Heng Ding's oral account:

I met the Master in Shenyang when I was still a student, but it was not until I went to Nanhua Monastery that I followed the Master and left the home-life. In the thirty-eighth year of the Republic (1949), having just finished receiving the precepts, I followed the Master to Hong Kong. When the Master came from Manchuria to Nanhua Monastery, he didn't bring anything with him except a string of recitation beads; when he got to Hong Kong, he still had nothing, just that same string of recitation beads.

**[Addendum 4]** Upasika Chen Kuan Man was a refuge disciple of Elder Master Hsu Yun and supported Buddhism for decades. In 1980, when she was in her eighties, she went to Gold Mountain Monastery in America to pay her respects to the Venerable Master.

## 02. Journey to Thailand

In early 1950, the Venerable Master joined a delegation of study-abroad monks organized by the Chinese Buddhist Vinaya Academy and went to Thailand to observe Theravada Buddhism. He stayed at Wat Mangkon Kamalawat (Longlian Monastery) in Thailand for about four months, studying firsthand the differences between Mahayana and Small Vehicle Buddhism and taking the strengths of each, and he resolved to reform the bad customs of Chinese Buddhism...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I went on alms rounds in Hong Kong, but in Thailand I didn't. Why not? Because I was staying at a Chinese monastery; they cooked their own food there, unlike the Thai monasteries. Thailand was terribly hot; I couldn't stand it. I had to take a shower every day, because the heat was just too fierce.

In Hong Kong there was a Dharma Master Hui De. When he heard I was planning to go to Thailand to observe Theravada Buddhism, he didn't want me to go. He wanted me to go live in the mountains with him so that we could practice together as "fellow investigators." I said to him, "You investigate your Hui De, and I investigate my An Tse. How can we investigate together?" [Tr.: "fellow investigators" is the usual Chinese term for fellow Chan practitioners; the Master plays on its literal sense.] At last he understood a little, and he no longer tried to stop me from going to Siam (Thailand).

He sat in Chan meditation every day, but because he didn't know the method of applying effort, he sat until he coughed up blood. So in cultivation, it isn't that just because "I'm applying effort" you're sure to succeed; applying effort comes with demonic obstacles just the same. As soon as he applied effort he coughed up blood, and even coughing up blood he kept on applying effort. He wouldn't accept offerings from people, and he wouldn't take money. Wherever he went to stay, if you didn't bother him, didn't pester him, and didn't talk to him, he would stay there peacefully. But if some layperson talked to him, or someone offered him a little money or a box of crackers, he considered it a nuisance; without even looking at it, he would run off and not stay there anymore.

He stayed at Po Lin Monastery on Lantau Island for several years, and he also stayed at Man Cheung Po for a year or half a year. Later some layperson or other caused him trouble, and he ran off—not even I knew about it. Because this man truly cultivated, some laypeople wanted to make offerings to such a cultivator and tried every way they could to find him so they could make offerings to him; but as soon as they found him, he would run off. This is the true character of cultivating the Way! He wasn't someone who chattered with laypeople from morning to night, squeezing out a few words to curry favor even when there was nothing to say. He was not like that!

Someone like Dharma Master Hui De is in the state of an Arhat, the state of a Solitary Enlightened One. He lived in a little thatched hut, and his clothes were all in tatters. His voice was very faint, as though he were about to run out of breath; he had to lean right up to your ear before you could hear what he was saying. When he stayed at the Tung Lin Buddha Recitation Hall, he ate other people's leftovers. Food that others didn't eat, he took and ate. If they didn't give him this food, he would leave. After he finished eating, he would shut his door and not talk to anyone, not a single word. For him the path of words was truly cut off; he really wanted to be a mute, and it wasn't that he was pretending not to talk.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshu Elder Zhi Ding recounted in his article *In Memory of Elder To Lun*:

On the last day of 1949, I arrived in Hong Kong. At the Hong Kong Buddhist Association I met Dharma Master To Lun again. We truly had affinities; we kept running into each other everywhere. At that time Elder To Lun, with Dharma Master Cheng Yuan as the leader, was going through the formalities for going to Thailand as a study-abroad monk.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Zheng met the Venerable Master when she was 16 and has been his refuge disciple for more than 50 years now. Upasika Tan Guo Zheng recounted:

The Master went to Thailand with a delegation of monks to study Small Vehicle Buddhism, and Dharma Master Heng Ding went along too. The Master stayed in Thailand for several months and closely observed the traditional system of Small Vehicle Buddhism, and he resolved that in the future he would reform the bad customs of Chinese Buddhism. That is why later, at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas in America, he established for the Sangha such family traditions as "eating one meal a day, at midday" [Tr.: the Buddha's own practice of taking a single meal before noon] and "never lying down to sleep" [Tr.: sitting up in meditation all night instead of lying down].

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Ding's oral account:

We once went to Bangkok, Thailand, for four months. We had originally gone to learn the system of Theravada Buddhism, but because our paperwork hadn't been completed, we had no choice but to come back. In Thailand we still wore Chinese monastic robes, not Theravada robes, and we didn't go out on alms rounds [Tr.: going out with the bowl to receive food from donors, as monks did in the Buddha's time] either. We stayed at Wat Mangkon Kamalawat (Longlian Monastery), where the monastics were all Chinese and spoke Mandarin. Originally Thailand did not allow Chinese people to build monasteries, but the Cantonese Dharma Master who founded it was very capable, and his recitation of the Great Compassion Mantra brought strong responses, so the Thai government let him establish Longlian Monastery. This was no easy thing; without the consent of the Thai government, it would have been impossible.

**[Addendum 4]** Longlian Monastery, built in 1871 and located in Bangkok, the capital of Thailand, is the earliest Chinese Mahayana monastery established in Thailand. Dharma Master Xu Xing of Guangdong Province, China, went to Thailand to propagate the Buddhadharma in the first year of the Tongzhi reign of the Qing dynasty (1862). He was received with courtesy by the King of Thailand, who granted land by royal decree for building a Chinese monastery. The site had originally been a place where merchants sold "dragon lotus seeds" (that is, double-hearted lotus seeds, a Chinese medicinal herb that can also be made into a cooling drink), and so the monastery was named "Longlian (Dragon Lotus) Chan Monastery." Apart from its buildings differing from Thai-style temples, the monastery has also always preserved Chinese-language Buddhist scriptures and the practice of vegetarianism.

## 03. Settling in a Stone Cave

In early summer of 1950, the 33-year-old Venerable Master returned to Hong Kong from Thailand with nothing to his name but the patched robe [Tr.: a robe pieced together from patched rags, worn by monks as a mark of frugality] he wore, covered all over with patches. A layperson who knew the Venerable Master was looking for a place to cultivate introduced him to Guanyin Cave on Fu Yung Shan in Tsuen Wan...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In my first year in Hong Kong I had no money, and I didn't go seeking alms from people either. I often held seven-day sessions [Tr.: an intensive seven-day period of meditation or recitation], with nothing to eat. After I came back from Thailand, a layperson named Guo Kuan Pu introduced me to Fu Yung Shan to live in Guanyin Cave.

When I first lived in Guanyin Cave, never mind teacups, there wasn't even a table. No stools, no bed, nothing at all; everything was empty. During the day I would do walking meditation all over the place. On the wall there was a stone ledge. How big was it? Just big enough for one person to sit on with his back straight. At night I sat in meditation on it. Every day I just sat like that.

Guanyin Cave was damp by nature. At first I didn't notice it, but after sitting there the first day, what do you think happened on the second day? My whole body had turned to stone! I couldn't move. It was even as though I had merged into one with the stone wall; I wanted to move but couldn't. After I had been sitting for a little more than half a month, one day I wanted to get up, and what do you think my legs did? They just wouldn't cooperate! My legs had gone so numb that there was no feeling in them at all. Only after moving them about for half an hour could I slowly stand up. I sat like this for about a month.

One day I actually couldn't get down from the stone ledge, and I started having idle thoughts: "This place is so damp, it's very bad for my health. If I keep living here all the time, how can I stand it?" So I wanted to leave. But if I left Guanyin Cave, where would I go? There was no suitable place for me to live, either.

But when I moved in, it was through Guo Kuan Pu's introduction. At the start he had said, "There's nothing at all in the cave. If you can live there, I'll introduce you; if you can't, don't force yourself." At the time I didn't know it would be this bad. Sitting there every day, my body seemed to have congealed into one lump, and my blood wasn't circulating. Then I thought again: "If I stop living here now, it would be like starting something and not finishing it. Someone had to call in a big favor to get this place. If I don't stay, how can I face them? How would I explain it?" I really couldn't bring myself to say it.

So I made up my mind and said to myself, "Even if I die in this cave, I won't come out! Damp is damp; let it be!" Because "If you can't give up death, you can't gain life; if you can't give up the false, you can't accomplish the true," and "Undergoing suffering ends suffering; enjoying blessings uses up blessings." If this suffering is something I'm supposed to undergo, I'll just go ahead and undergo it! Once I thought this way, that was that: if I die here, so be it; I won't care. After I had lived there for a while, my body stopped being partners with the stone—it closed down the joint-stock limited company—and I could move again. When I got up in the morning, I could get down off the stone ledge again.

In this way I lived there at Guanyin Cave for several years. In the end someone saw that I had no table, no stools, not even a bed, so a layperson resolved to give me a meditation box [Tr.: a wooden box-seat in which a monk sits in meditation day and night] and two stools. With that, I was much more comfortable than before. Every day at mealtime I would go down the mountain to beg for food, going on alms rounds to Chuk Lam Sim Yuen to get food and bring it back to eat.

One day when I came back, I found that a big snake had gotten into Guanyin Cave. This snake was so thick and so big! When it saw me come back, it ran toward the back door, and when it ran, I chased it. When it got to the doorway, it seemed to want to fight with me; it turned its head back to look at me, and then it ran off out the back. After that it came often; I don't know what it wanted. Sometimes when I went out, it would come in; when I came in, it would go out.

I never told anyone that I had lived in Guanyin Cave. There was a man who lived on a mountain for three months, and when he came down he went around advertising, selling his cultivation, always telling people, "Living on a mountain is wonderful! Living on the mountain now is just like being in closed retreat! Up there I did such-and-such practice; it's really great!" I said to him, "You're really clever! You lived on a mountain for three months and you already know to advertise it everywhere. I've lived on a mountain for more than ten years and nobody knows. I'm too foolish, too stupid!"

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Ding's oral account:

Guanyin Cave was not a natural cave but a man-made one, so it was very damp. The Master was very capable. When he lived in Guanyin Cave, within just a few months, the cave's appearance changed.

**[Addendum 2]** Dharma Master Heng Ding, together with Dharma Masters Heng Yue and Heng Zhi, who had come from Sanyuan Monastery in Manchuria (these two Dharma Masters were the Venerable Master's Dharma nephews), stayed as visiting monks at Tung Po Tor Monastery and Chuk Lam Sim Yuen respectively. Dharma Master Rong Qiu, the founding Patriarch of Chuk Lam Sim Yuen, arrived in Hong Kong in 1928 and built Chuk Lam Sim Yuen on Fu Yung Shan in Tsuen Wan. During the Second World War, Dharma Master Rong Qiu led his disciples in clearing land and farming so that together they could get through the hard times. In 1949, when monks were coming south to escape the turmoil, the Dharma Master opened wide the door of convenience and provided them with food and lodging.

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Jin Ci, a native of Taishan, Guangdong, was one of the Venerable Master's early refuge disciples from his time at Guanyin Cave in Hong Kong; the Dharma name she received when she took refuge was Guo Sen.

In 1950, Guo Sen, then just past 40, was the young mistress of a wealthy family and had worshipped Guanyin since she was small. One day her friend Mrs. Huang (Luo Guo Ming) asked her, "Would you like to take refuge and bow to a teacher?" At that time she didn't even know what "bowing to a teacher" (taking refuge) was. Mrs. Huang told her about the benefits of taking refuge and about this Dharma Master's compassion and so on, so she met the Master at Wang Guo Hai's home and formally took refuge. When she first met the Master, he was only about 30 and very thin. Later Guo Sen's family business ran into trouble, and the family's fortunes declined. When the Venerable Master learned of her situation, he told her to bear adversity with patience. Guo Sen took tonsure in 1998 and received full ordination in 2000, with the Dharma name Qin Ren and the style name Jin Ci. She passed away peacefully in the eighth lunar month of 2008, at the age of 100.

**[Addendum 4]** Upasaka Huang Guo Jun took refuge with the Venerable Master at the age of 11, more than 50 years ago now. Upasaka Huang Guo Jun recounted:

On the Master's way from Nanhua Monastery to Hong Kong, he met an Upasaka Fang, a disciple of Elder Hsu, who therefore knew the Master. Upasaka Fang asked the Master whether he knew anyone in Hong Kong. The Master said, "No, I don't know a single person." Upasaka Fang said, "I'll introduce some friends to you." In 1950, Upasaka Fang introduced him to a Mrs. Wang (Wang Guo Hai). Mrs. Wang was a former neighbor of my mother (Luo Guo Ming); she and my mother, four or five people in all, were the first group of Hong Kong disciples to take refuge with the Master. A few days later, at Mrs. Wang's home, I took refuge with the Master together with Upasika Guo Sen (Bhikshuni Jin Ci).

I remember that right after I took refuge, the Master said I was "stubborn by nature and had a temper." At the time I only knew that having a temper was bad and that I should change it; as for being stubborn by nature, I even thought back then that it was a good quality! It was only later, when I read in the *Earth Store Sutra*, "The beings of Jambudvipa are stubborn and difficult to tame and subdue," that I finally understood what the Master meant. If my temper now can be said to be not too bad, the credit goes entirely to the Master's teaching.

## 04. The Bearing of the Greatly Virtuous

As the situation on the Chinese mainland was in violent upheaval, many monastics poured into Hong Kong. The Venerable Master, concerned for the safety of Elder Master Lai Guo, an eminent and virtuous elder of the Chan lineage, wrote him a letter urging the Elder Master to come to Hong Kong. From the letters they exchanged, one can sense the bearing of the greatly virtuous.

To Great Master Lai Guo, for your perusal in the Way:

Venerable One, with your great wisdom you carry on the Buddhadharma; you are a great vessel that bears the Dharma. As the situation is turbulent at present, I beg you, Venerable One, to take good care of your precious health and let not a single hair be harmed, just as you protect and uphold the Buddhadharma.

The Second Patriarch cut off his arm to seek the Dharma; the Sixth Patriarch, to protect the Dharma, hid himself among a band of hunters for 16 years. If you, Venerable One, could follow the example of the Patriarchs and come to Hong Kong to protect the Buddhadharma and rescue living beings, my mind would be at ease! This still awaits the full ripening of causes and conditions.

Peace in the Way,

With bows, An Tse

To Great Master An Tse, for your illustrious perusal:

I received your letter yesterday and have respectfully noted its contents. When the Great Dharma is upheld in its entirety, how can there be any division of territory? Even when the human lifespan has dwindled to ten years [Tr.: the lowest point of a kalpa in Buddhist cosmology], I intend to come here to prop up Bodhidharma's banner pole. As descendants of Shakyamuni, we simply practice true practice and shrink from nothing whatsoever. Please be at ease! Respectfully replying, I wish you

peace in the Way.

Personally written by Lai Guo

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Elder Master Lai Guo of Gaomin Monastery in Yangzhou once wrote me a letter. What I want to talk about now isn't that letter but the envelope. What about his envelope? He used one envelope twice. How did he use it twice? This envelope had been addressed to him by someone else and had already been used; he turned it inside out and used the blank inner side to write to me. So the greatly virtuous, eminent monks of old were all extremely frugal; they didn't waste things or use them carelessly. They would not write a few words on a sheet of paper and then throw it away.

The last time I wrote a verse [Tr.: a gatha, a Buddhist verse form] for Guo Qian, I had him cut the verse off and took the paper back to use again. Why did I do this? It's not that I begrudge even a sheet of paper; it's that we should cherish the material resources of this world. I hope none of you will waste things. If you keep wasting things, then imperceptibly you will have no merit and virtue [Tr.: the blessings and spiritual virtue gained through good deeds and practice], and it won't be easy to succeed in cultivation either. So people who cultivate the Way must be careful in every situation.

## 05. Sweet Dew in Front of the Cave

Guanyin Cave had a problem with water shortage. After the Venerable Master moved into Guanyin Cave, the pools gushed with sweet, clear water. When the Venerable Master later moved away, the water source in front of the cave dried up as well...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

My neighbor was a Dharma Master from Fujian. He was performing a thousand Flaming Mouth ceremonies [Tr.: a ritual for feeding hungry ghosts], one every day. A thousand Flaming Mouth ceremonies take a thousand days, which is three years. At about three o'clock in the afternoon he would perform the Flaming Mouth ceremony. First he would put on his robe and sash and go outside the door to look down the hill. When he saw eight or ten tourists coming up, he would go back in, pick up his bell, and, all by himself, beat the drum and strike the bell as he performed the Flaming Mouth ceremony, making a great racket. When the tourists passed by and leaned in at the door to look—"Oh, this Dharma Master is really diligent!"—this one would give money, and that one would give money.

Before I went there, he had made two pools in front of the cave to store the water that flowed down from the mountain. There wasn't much water in the pools; they were usually dry. After I got there, water came into the pools every day, and the water was so clear! Today I'm not afraid to tell you: why was there water once I got there? Because in Manchuria ten dragons took refuge with me. I'm not trying to scare you, and I'm not saying how wonderful I am. All these gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division [Tr.: the eight classes of beings who protect the Dharma] and all the ghosts and spirits listen to what I say—unlike you people, who don't know any better and sometimes are very disobedient.

When I was at Guanyin Cave with no food to eat and no clothes to wear, I came to know Yu Guo Man. She was from Shanghai and was my greatest Dharma protector in Hong Kong. Before she met me, what she feared most was monks. She didn't dare see monastics; when she saw a monk she was frightened out of her wits. If there had been a mouse cage around, she would even have tried to hide in it; that's how scared she was! I said, "In your past lives you must have been cheated by monastics far too often; that's why you're so afraid!"

The first time she came to Guanyin Cave, I was eating. She asked, "What are you eating?" I said, "Noodles!" I invited her to stay and eat noodles with me. She didn't dare to eat, yet she wanted to, and in the end she ate. She drank some water at my place and asked me, "Did you put sugar in this water?" I said, "No! This is plain boiled water." "Then why is it so sweet? It's sweeter than if you'd put sugar in it." I said, "Sweet? Then maybe Guanshiyin Bodhisattva added sweet dew for you, so drink a bit more!" She was so happy she drank four or five bowls of water. She also often bought rice and made short jackets and trousers to give me; she was very sincere. Seeing that the clothes I wore were all in tatters, she resolved to go home and make me two pairs of trousers out of very thick cotton cloth; they could be worn for about ten years without wearing out. That's because she was very honest, and honest people make honest things like that.

**[Addendum]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Jin Ci's oral account:

When the Master first came to Guanyin Cave, there wasn't a single drop of water in the pool. Later, water slowly began to flow out of the rock face, and the pool was always full. I drank from it, and it was very sweet. But after the Master left Guanyin Cave, the pool dried up again. In those days the Master went to Chuk Lam Sim Yuen every day to get food. Because the Master would sit for several days at a stretch, sometimes, when it happened that the monastery had no leftover food, he would have to go hungry for ten days or so all told. But he never told anyone, and no one knew about the hard life he led. All this was discovered gradually by his disciples later on. Once they knew, they would bring some food to offer to the Master whenever they went up the mountain.

## 06. Out of Food and Awaiting Death

Guanyin Cave was severely damp; it was no place to settle down in. The Venerable Master overcame all kinds of difficulties, moved away a large boulder outside the cave, and put up a small thatched hut—but unexpectedly, this brought on demonic obstacles...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I've seen quite a few cultivators who, when living where the assembly lives, mostly shirk work and take it easy, unwilling to do any more than they must; but once they live in their own thatched hut, they can do everything. Why? Because if they don't do their own work, nobody else will do it. For example, when a certain person lived at Chuk Lam Sim Yuen, he slept from morning to night. And once he lived in his own thatched hut? He was really vigorous: he kept everything around the hut clean and tidy, and the fence he made was very handsome. But in the monastery he wouldn't lift a finger. There was another person who, in the Way-place, put on the airs of an old cultivator and acted very dull, doing nothing at all. Once he lived in his own thatched hut, he could do this and he could do that. Although he was very clumsy, he could do it. Why? Because he considered the thatched hut his own, while the monastery wasn't his.

My temperament is just the opposite of theirs. In a monastery I like to work, but when I live in my own little thatched hut, I'm casual and easygoing about everything. Guanyin Cave had been built by others beforehand; while I lived there, I neither added anything nor took anything away. I sat in that spot every day and did nothing. It was only because the cave was too damp that I later built a small thatched hut, and I had to move a big boulder. The workers couldn't do it, so I moved it myself. That's my temperament.

I remember that the two workers I hired then were both big fellows, and both very strong. One was over six feet tall, and the other was five foot eight. I told them to move the boulder away; it weighed as much as two tons. The two of them pried at it with iron bars for ages, about four hours, but there was no way they could shift it; they couldn't move it even a fraction of an inch. There was nothing they could do. They came back and told me that the boulder would have to be blasted with explosives; otherwise there was no way to move it. I went over to have a look and said, "This boulder doesn't need to be blasted. You two can't move it, but I'll make a bet with you two now: if I move it, you two give up your wages for today; if I can't move it, I'll pay you double wages. What do you say?" Ah! They said, "Of course, that's fine!"

Why couldn't they pry it up? They were using iron bars, and when they pried, the force underneath it was against the dirt; prying against the dirt, it wouldn't move, because the dirt naturally wasn't as strong as that rock. I moved this big boulder all by myself. How was I able to move it? I put a stone at the edge of the gap underneath the boulder, and then, with an iron bar, gave a pry forward, and up it came. It took me about 20 minutes to move the boulder away. The big boulder went from up top to down below; it moved house all by itself.

I said to them, "How many meals do you two eat a day?" "Three." "How much do you earn a day?" They told me how much they earned, and I said, "If I went out to work, everyone would want to hire me and not you. Because I eat only one meal a day, and the work you can't do, I can get done." That left the two workers with nothing to say.

A year later, I built a tiny thatched hut outside the cave. I put it up with wood and then covered it with tar paper—a small space of about 15 feet by 15 feet. At last I had a roof over my head. As soon as the thatched hut went up, demonic obstacles came too. Because many people came and saw me cultivating so austerely, some would toss in a little money for incense and oil, or bring some food and drink, and my neighbor next door became jealous and couldn't let it go. Unable to let it go, he told the people who came, "I'm the manager here. Whatever else you bring, hand it over to me. The money you bring has to be handed over to me, and the things you bring have to be handed over to me." That's what this neighboring monk said. And so he would stand blocking the doorway and not let them come over to me. He even fabricated rumors, telling Chuk Lam Sim Yuen that I had a lot of money: "Don't make offerings to him anymore. He has money to build a thatched hut—how could he not have money for food?" The manager there believed it was true and stopped making offerings to me; they stopped giving me food. After they stopped giving me food, some 15 or 20-odd days went by; I don't remember clearly. At that time there really was no one making offerings, and with no one making offerings, I thought, "I'll eat the little bit I have, and then just sit there in meditation."

I didn't tell anyone that I had no food, and I didn't go out or see anyone. If I starved to death, that would be that. I slowly ate the dry provisions stored in the cave to stave off hunger, and barely held out for half a month. By then everything had been eaten up; I was truly out of food, so I just sat there waiting to starve to death, because my principle had always been: "Freezing, we do not scheme. Starving, we do not beg. Dying of poverty, we ask for nothing. We accord with conditions, yet do not change; we do not change, yet accord with conditions."

**[Addendum]** Lay Buddhist Tang Guo Shan's oral account:

I took refuge in 1950, but I didn't understand the Buddhadharma. Once I took some relatives and friends to Guanyin Cave to visit the Master. We saw rice and vegetables in the Master's bowl and, thinking that eating it would lengthen our lives and make us healthy, we went right ahead and ate it all up. Only later did we learn it was the Master's midday meal, which he had brought back from Chuk Lam Sim Yuen. We hurriedly bought some bread for the Master, and that day the Master got by on just that bit of bread. Looking back on it, what an offense!

## 07. Weituo Bodhisattva Appears in a Dream

Weituo Bodhisattva [Tr.: the Dharma-protector Bodhisattva, guardian of monasteries] made a vow: "If there is anyone in the world who truly cultivates, I will certainly go and protect his Dharma." Seeing the Venerable Master determined to sit there and wait to starve to death, Weituo Bodhisattva grew truly anxious!

In the Venerable Master's own words:

At that time I just sat there waiting to starve to death. I didn't say to anyone, "Have pity on me, I've got nothing to eat—make me an offering!" or "Help me out—just think of it as giving to a beggar!" I didn't say it, but Weituo Bodhisattva, watching, couldn't help getting anxious.

I often say that I, as a monastic, don't bow to Weituo Bodhisattva. You may think my way of thinking isn't normal: "Weituo Bodhisattva is a Dharma protector, so why don't you bow to him? If you don't bow to Weituo Bodhisattva, what if he doesn't protect your Dharma?" I'll tell you: I want him to protect other people's Dharma, not mine. Why? If I am a true cultivator, then even if I don't ask him to protect the Dharma, he'll still protect my Dharma; he has to fulfill his duty. If I don't cultivate and just kowtow to Weituo Bodhisattva a few times and he comes to protect the Dharma, then he wouldn't be qualified to be Weituo Bodhisattva. Why? He'd be corrupt, and he'd be selling the Dharma. All of you, think about it: if people kowtow to you a few times, you protect them; if they don't kowtow, you don't fulfill your duty—wouldn't that be corruption? So I don't kowtow to him.

At that time there was a woman of 50 or 60, Liu Kuan Sheng, a Cantonese, who would speak the Buddhadharma to everyone she met, so people called her the "Local Dharma Master." "Local" means from right here, not from somewhere else. She wasn't a Dharma Master at all; she was a layperson. A few months earlier, she had been bitten on the foot by a dog. What kind of dog bit her? Probably a mad dog. She had seen doctors of both Chinese and Western medicine, but none of them had cured it; two or three months went by and it still hadn't healed. One night she had a dream in which Weituo Bodhisattva said to her, "If you want your foot to get well, go and make offerings at Guanyin Cave behind Fu Yung Shan. There's a Dharma Master there named An Tse. Go and make offerings to him, and you'll be all right. Hurry! Dharma Master An has nothing to eat there. Take him some food as an offering, and your dog bite will heal." In the dream, Weituo Bodhisattva showed her my photograph, and once she saw it, she remembered it.

She had this dream three days running, so she believed it was real. She went around everywhere collecting donations and collected more than HK$70, and she bought 30 catties [Tr.: a catty is about 0.6 kg, so roughly 18 kg or 40 lb] of rice herself and carried it up the mountain on her own back to Guanyin Cave. She was short, and her foot had been bitten by a dog besides—now tell me, was she sincere or not!

When the Dharma Master next door saw a donor coming, he hurried out to greet her. Liu Kuan Sheng asked him, "There's a Dharma Master An Tse here. Is he in?" My neighboring monk blocked her at the doorway and said, "I'm the manager here. Whatever you've brought, just hand it to me." He asked her what she had brought; she said it was rice and a little money, and he said it all had to be handed over to him. But Liu Kuan Sheng said, "In my dream Weituo Bodhisattva said he was called Dharma Master An Tse, and in the photograph he didn't look like you. I can't give this to you!" This monk finally lost his temper and started quarreling with her: "What do you mean it doesn't look like me? You're talking nonsense! I'm the manager here, and he isn't the manager at all. All the offerings you bring should be handed over to me!" The laywoman said, "If it isn't you, it isn't you. Why should I give it to you?"

Hearing the two of them quarreling, I came out to the doorway and looked up. There the two of them were, arguing endlessly, so I asked what was going on. At that, Liu Kuan Sheng said, "Oh, it's him! It's him! It's this monk! Ah, and I even know what his name is!" The neighboring monk said furiously, "You're not allowed to come up this mountain again!" Liu Kuan Sheng paid him no mind and handed the things over to me. I said, "All right, he knows you've come too, so half for each of us!" She said, "I don't want to give him any." I said, "I'll give it to him. He knows you aren't giving him any, and he won't be happy." So I split off 15 catties of rice for him and gave him 35 dollars. Even so, he still wasn't satisfied and was very unhappy.

When Liu Kuan Sheng went back, she told everyone everywhere, "At Guanyin Cave there's a Dharma Master An Tse. I dreamed that Weituo Bodhisattva told me to make offerings to him. After I brought the offerings and came back, my foot healed. It's truly miraculous!" Everyone who heard it thought it inconceivable. And so, after that, more and more people came to know about me, and knew that I was going hungry there, so people often went to make offerings. This one brought offerings, and that one brought offerings. No matter who brought offerings, I always gave half to the Dharma Master next door, but he still wasn't satisfied. Every time someone came to see me, he would first have a quarrel with them, and he also used all sorts of methods to undermine me. Later I stopped living there.

While I lived there, the two pools in front had water every day, enough for ten-odd or even 20 people to use. As soon as I moved away, the pools had no water. Originally there was no water at Western Bliss Gardens either, but when I went to build Western Bliss Gardens, there was water. So the monks living on Fu Yung Shan all said that I had taken the water away with me, taken it to Western Bliss Gardens.

## 08. Strictly Upholding His Principles

The Venerable Master was the Dharma Master whom the elderly Upasika Liu Kuan Sheng had seen in her dream; at that time the Venerable Master had already been without food for half a month. The story of Weituo Bodhisattva appearing in a dream so that offerings would be made to the Venerable Master spread from one person to ten and from ten to a hundred, and everyone thought it inconceivable. All his life the Venerable Master carefully upheld "Freezing, we do not scheme. Starving, we do not beg..."

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Often not eating is a good method: it saves money, and you don't need to ask people for offerings. We monastics should worry about whether we have accomplished our work in the Way, not about whether we receive offerings. If you receive offerings but don't become enlightened and don't accomplish your work in the Way, you're still an ordinary person. If you accomplish your work in the Way, then even without offerings, you're a sage. Even though I was about to starve to death there, I didn't starve to death. Now, it's precisely because you're afraid of starving to death that nobody makes offerings to you. If you have cultivation and you aren't afraid of starving to death, naturally there will be a response. So don't worry about whether you receive offerings.

I have never told anyone what I needed. I never said, "I don't have such-and-such clothing; make me one." "Freezing, we do not scheme": I don't tell people I lack this or I lack that. On the mainland I wore a patched robe, and in Hong Kong I also wore very ragged clothes, with patch upon patch, which probably weren't washed even once in several decades; yet they didn't smell of sweat or dirt. Was it that I had no clothes to wear? Not necessarily; it's just that I liked wearing old clothes. Because of this, some people spread a rumor that when I went to take a bus, the driver saw how ragged my clothes were and how bad I smelled and wouldn't let me get on the bus. As for food, I don't tell anyone what I'd like to eat either—unless there's a special situation where I need to take some medicine; that can be mentioned. Apart from that, many people want to buy things for me and ask me, "What do you need? What would you like to eat?" I always say that I eat everything and I eat nothing. I've never said you must give me something—never.

## 09. Organizing the Refugee Monks

In the late 1940s, as the situation on the Chinese mainland was in turmoil and transition, monks from the north moved south and gathered in the tiny territory of Hong Kong. In 1951, the Venerable Master served as chairman of the Fellowship Association of Mainland Chinese Monastics in Hong Kong and was responsible for conducting a full count of the mainland monastics staying in Hong Kong...

Historical background:

In 1939, the Second World War began. In 1941, Japan made a surprise attack on Pearl Harbor and launched war in Southeast Asia; in the same year Hong Kong, after surrendering on December 25, was occupied by Japan. Hong Kong was under occupation for three years and eight months, and many Hong Kong monastics fled as refugees to the interior of China. In August 1945, the Second World War ended, and Hong Kong remained a British colony. In May 1948, the National Government was formally reorganized as the Government of the Republic of China. On November 22, the first phase of the Huaihai Campaign in the civil war between the Nationalists and the Communists ended in a great victory for the Communist army. In July 1949, the armed forces of the Republic of China began withdrawing from the mainland to Taiwan, and most of the mainland was occupied by the Communists. On October 1, the People's Republic of China was founded, and on December 7, the Government of the Republic of China moved its capital from Chengdu, Sichuan, to the city of Taipei, Taiwan.

In the 1950s, postwar Hong Kong had huge numbers of destitute people without work, and it lacked natural resources; on top of that, refugees were pouring into Hong Kong at a rate of 100,000 a month, and the population problem became extremely serious.

During this period, large numbers of northern monastics came south to Hong Kong; Hong Kong people called it the period of the "southward migration of monks and nuns." Among the waves of monastics who came south, some came from the White Mountains (the Changbai Mountains) and the Black Waters (the Heilong River) and from both sides of the Great Wall, and others from north and south of the Yangtze, from Guangdong and Guangxi, from Hunan and Hubei, and elsewhere. Faced with monastics arriving from all over, some newly built viharas, quiet huts, and thatched huts in the outskirts of Hong Kong, as well as wooden houses built by local Buddhists, became places where the refugee monastics could lodge. The place with the largest number of lodging monastics was, first and foremost, Tung Po Tor Monastery in Tsuen Wan in the New Territories.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

At that time many of the monastics in Hong Kong were afraid of me; almost all of them had been scolded by me, because they didn't look after the refugee monks who had fled to Hong Kong. In Hong Kong I knew a Dharma Master Qing Yan. At that time I was chairman of the "Sangha Fellowship Association," and he helped out by running around everywhere; he was a very young monastic. Later he "ran off" first, in Taiwan [Tr.: i.e., he passed away], and Taiwan had another flesh-body Bodhisattva [Tr.: a monastic whose body remains incorrupt after death and is enshrined] appear. So if you apply effort in cultivation, whether you're young or old, you can accomplish the Way.

In Hong Kong there was once a little Arhat. He was a young monk who was smiling and cheerful from morning to night, and very innocent. When people made offerings to him, he wouldn't take them; or even if he accepted them, he would immediately give them away to someone else. So people gave him the name "Little Arhat." A person with real skill in cultivation, no matter how many days after death, remains sitting upright and dignified; his head will not droop.

In Hong Kong I helped a certain Dharma Master a great deal, yet he still undermined me to my face. I don't blame him. Later this man died of cancer. Before he died, he was still very concerned with saving face. He said that after he died, he too wanted to be seated in a vat [Tr.: the body is sealed upright in a large urn; if found incorrupt when the urn is reopened, it is gilded and enshrined] like Dharma Master Ci Hang, and when the vat was opened three years later, his remains should be gilded and covered in gold leaf. Unexpectedly, the day after he died, his head drooped. His disciples knew it wouldn't hold up and never dared to open the vat.

**[Addendum 1]** Taiwan's Second Flesh-Body Bodhisattva: Master Qing Yan, Who Manifested the Appearance of Illness and Suffering

Master Qing Yan (1924–1970) left the home-life at 12, received full ordination at 18, and at 22 served as guest prefect at Gude Monastery in Hankou. During the civil war between the Nationalists and the Communists, to escape the fighting, he made his way by a roundabout route to Tung Po Tor in Hong Kong at the age of 25. During his five years in Hong Kong, he took part in the affairs of the Fellowship Association of Mainland Monastics in Hong Kong, safeguarding the rights and interests of the monastic community. In the forty-third year of the Republic (1954) he went from Hong Kong to Taiwan, finally taking up residence at Haizang Monastery in Xindian, and he manifested stillness at the age of 47. A month before entering perfect stillness, he instructed his disciples to keep him seated in a vat for six years. In the sixty-fifth year of the Republic (1976), the vat was opened in public: his flesh body was intact, his features majestic, and his kashaya [Tr.: the monastic precept sash worn over the robe] undamaged. It caused a sensation in society at the time. Afterward the body was gilded, and to this day it remains seated, presiding over Haizang Monastery. During his 15 years in Taiwan, Master Qing Yan constantly manifested the appearance of illness and suffering. In ordinary circumstances he honed his integrity; content in poverty and delighting in the Way, he remained the same for decades, as if it were a single day. He said, "Illness and suffering spur me on so that I dare not slacken." The more seriously ill he became, the more vigorous his practice grew; he was always reciting with his mouth, bowing with his body, and praying with his mind to Guanshiyin Bodhisattva. His famous saying was: "Don't fear having no temple; fear only not accomplishing the Way."

**[Addendum 2]** In 1951, Dharma Master Meng Sheng was the first chairman of the Fellowship Association of Mainland Chinese Monastics in Hong Kong. That year, in order to conduct a count of the mainland monastics staying in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master compiled the *Membership Roster of the Fellowship Association of Mainland Chinese Monastics in Hong Kong*; not long afterward, the Venerable Master took over the post of chairman.

## 10. A Ghost Who Knew "Four and Six"

The Venerable Master's virtuous conduct was revered by humans and gods alike; and such was his vow power that even ghosts and spirits begged to take refuge with him. Chen Ruichang was a well-known Hong Kong businessman. His niece had lost both her parents when she was little and had been raised by Mr. and Mrs. Chen Ruichang...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

If I told people to take refuge with me, someone might ask, "Are you qualified to be a teacher? Are you trying to take advantage of me, wanting to be my teacher so you can rank a generation above me?" Then what would I say? It's precisely because of this that I have never told anyone to take refuge with me. If I accept a refuge disciple but don't teach and transform him with the Proper Dharma and can't save him, I've let him down, and I would feel very ashamed. And if I do teach him with the Proper Dharma but he has no true sincerity and won't listen to the teaching, what can I do about it? So I don't tell people to take refuge with me.

Now so many of you have taken refuge with me; ask yourselves, which one of you did I tell to come and take refuge with me? Even with those who want to take refuge with me, I have to examine them a bit. Sometimes there are so many people that I can't examine them all, so I'm a bit careless and just shut my eyes: "All right, everyone's approved!" In Manchuria and in Hong Kong, when I accepted refuge disciples, I would first talk with each person individually: why do you want to take refuge with me? And it wasn't just so with people; it was the same even with ghosts. Truly, in Hong Kong I accepted a ghost as a refuge disciple.

Many people in Hong Kong said that Dharma Master To Lun had the ability to catch ghosts; this is one of the stories behind that. This ghost—when you talk about it, it really was inconceivable! The niece of Chen Ruichang of the Daguang Company came down with a demonic illness: a ghost possessed her, making her go mad and act demonic, so that the whole household was in an uproar, with no peace even for the chickens and dogs.

Chen Ruichang invited seven monks to recite sutras; these monastics usually made their living by reciting sutras and performing Buddhist services. When they got there, they put on their robes and sashes, set out the *Vajra Sutra* and the Great Compassion Mantra on the table, and began to recite: "Na mo he la da nuo duo la ye ye, na mo e li ye..." From morning to night it was "Na mo, na mo, na mo"; they kept on "na mo"-ing without stopping. They recited the *Vajra Sutra*: "Thus I have heard. At one time the Buddha dwelt at Shravasti, in the Jeta Grove, in the Garden of the Benefactor of Orphans and the Solitary, together with a gathering of great Bhikshus, twelve hundred fifty in all. At that time, the World Honored One, at mealtime, put on his robe, took up his bowl, and entered the great city of Shravasti to beg for food. Having begged in sequence within the city, he returned to his own place. When he had finished eating, he put away his robe and bowl, washed his feet, arranged his seat, and sat down."

Strangely enough, when the monks recited the sutras, the ghost recited along with them. The people recited people's sutras, and the ghost recited ghost sutras. When you said, "arranged his seat, and sat down," she also said, "arranged his seat, and sat down"; every day she dueled with the Dharma Masters in a contest of powers. It got to where these seven monks had recited sutras for seven days and still couldn't get away; she had them at their wits' end, with no way at all to deal with her.

Among these seven monks was my Dharma nephew Heng Yue (a grand-disciple of Dharma Master Chang Yi). He said to Chen Ruichang, "If you want her to get well, you'll have to invite my Dharma uncle So-and-so here. He'll have a way!" The man believed what he said and ran off to Guanyin Cave to invite me.

Ever since I had withdrawn into seclusion at Guanyin Cave, I had been as if deaf and mute, as if dull and witless, single-mindedly cultivating the Way and paying no attention to worldly affairs. So the first time they invited me, I didn't agree. They came a second time, and again I didn't agree. The third time he came to me, he knelt before me and wouldn't get up. Originally I didn't want to meddle in other people's business, but since he pleaded again and again, I said, "All right! You're so sincere. I'll go!"

When I got there, the patient was on the bed in the middle of a duel with the monks! When the monks recited sutras, she recited sutras; when the monks recited mantras, she recited mantras. She was swaying back and forth there, ever so full of herself! I sat down beside her bed and didn't recite any sutra; I just sat quietly beside the bed. After ten minutes, the patient—the person carrying the ghost, the ghost carrying the person—quietly got down off the bed, crawled over to me, and knelt in front of me. I asked her:

"Who are you?"

"I am a ghost."

"Why are you harassing this person?"

"Because I have an affinity with her from past lives."

I didn't say much at the time; I just took off the string of recitation beads I wore around my neck and slipped it over the patient's neck. Immediately she cried out:

"Aiya! Dharma Master! Spare me! I won't dare do it again."

"What's the matter with you?"

"I'm about to be burned to death by these recitation beads of yours."

"When I wear them, why don't they burn? Why do they burn when you wear them?"

"Dharma Master, please be compassionate and let me go! I really can't bear it!"

"What do you want?"

"I want to take refuge with you."

"You want to take refuge with me? These seven monks have recited sutras for you for seven or eight days; why didn't you take refuge with them? What do you want to take refuge with me for?"

"Them? Even if they took refuge with me, I wouldn't want them. I find them stinking!" You see, this ghost was that conceited. She also said she knew a certain elder Dharma Master. I said, "Since you know that elder Dharma Master, wouldn't it be good to take refuge with him? I don't even have a place of my own right now. What do you want to take refuge with me for?"

"I know him, but I can't take refuge with him. He isn't qualified to be my teacher either!"

"Then how am I qualified?"

"I've been searching for so many years, and it's you I've been looking for!"

"What are you looking for me for?"

"I just want to take refuge with you."

"What's the benefit of taking refuge with me?"

"Your vow power is great: you want every one of your refuge disciples to become a Buddha. Because you have this vow power, I've been looking for you for a long time. I must take refuge with you."

"All right then, if you take refuge with me, what about this patient's illness? Will she get well or not?"

"She'll get well right away!"

After this ghost took refuge with me, the patient got well too. The seven monks who were there all saw the whole thing with their own eyes. You see, even this ghost understood "four and six"! What is "four and six"? The "four" is the character for "father," which has four strokes; the "six" is the character for "mother," which has six strokes. So when we say someone doesn't understand "four and six," it means he doesn't even recognize his own father and mother. This ghost understood "four and six" and knew to take refuge.

Chen Ruichang had once vowed, "If my niece doesn't get well, I won't believe in Buddhism anymore!" Fortunately, his niece did get well afterward. It wasn't that I was afraid he would stop believing in Buddhism; rather, I saw how pitiful his niece was, so I made her well.

Today I'm not telling you this to show what abilities I have; I'm only relating a fact. After this ghost took refuge with me, the patient, once she was well, also asked to take refuge, and the patient's relatives followed suit and wanted to take refuge too. So this whole group of people took refuge; the whole family took refuge. So ghosts can introduce people to take refuge, and when people see a ghost take refuge, they get envious too: "I want to take refuge too!"

After Chen Ruichang's niece got well, the whole family believed in Buddhism; they even had their children take refuge with the Triple Jewel as soon as they were born, and hosted vegetarian meals. Every time a grandson or granddaughter was born, they would host a vegetarian meal for their Buddhist friends, starting a custom of not taking life for celebrations. From then on, I had a reputation for "catching ghosts," and many people in Hong Kong believed that this Dharma Master To Lun had the ability to catch ghosts. Some said I was a "Maoshan (old) Taoist" [Tr.: Maoshan Taoists are popularly known as exorcists and ghost-catchers]; in Malaysia, too, some people spread the word that I was an "Old Demon King," "one of the Five Great Oddities of Hong Kong." Whatever anyone calls me, I won't mind in the least; otherwise, how could I call myself a little ant, a little mosquito, a horse!

**[Addendum 1]** In 1971 the Venerable Master said: Chen Ruichang was one of my longtime refuge disciples. In August of this year he passed away and went to rebirth in the Land of Ultimate Bliss. In the hospital he recited the Buddha's name every day; reciting and reciting, he fell asleep, and once asleep he passed away, without any great suffering.

**[Addendum 2]** Chen Ruichang saw that within half an hour of the Venerable Master's entering the room, his niece had returned to her former healthy, normal self, and he knew deeply that the Venerable Master was a true cultivator whose awesome virtue could subdue deviant demons; so he brought his whole family to take refuge with the Venerable Master. After that, every New Year, they respectfully asked the Venerable Master to write a New Year's blessing. One year Chen Ruichang's son didn't want the Venerable Master's blessing, and that year business was dismal. From then on they earnestly asked the Venerable Master to write a New Year's blessing every year; after the Venerable Master came to America, they even sent registered letters to America imploring him to write it.

## 11. Lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra*

In November 1951, Upasaka Chen Ruichang and other lay Buddhists invited the Venerable Master to lecture on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Tung Sin Tan [Tr.: a Taoist hall of the Quanzhen school in Hong Kong] in Hong Kong. The Dharma banner was raised high and the door of expedient means was opened. He lectured for more than 40 days, and no fewer than a hundred faithful devotees came to listen every day.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In the winter of 1951, the lay Buddhists Luo Guo Ming, Chen Guo Fa, Yuan Guo Lin (Mrs. Chen Ruichang), Tang Guo Shan, Mai Guo Lian, and others acted as sponsors and invited me to lecture on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Tung Sin Tan. The following autumn I lectured on the *Vajra Sutra* there, and I also lectured on the *Amitabha Sutra* at Po Kok Monastery.

You all know that I am always lecturing on the sutras, because if I don't lecture on the sutras, I can't go on living. So as long as I have a breath left, I will lecture on the sutras; only when this breath is cut off will I stop. If you ask, "Can you lecture?" I can't. I can't lecture, but I practice lecturing; I'm learning to lecture. In Hong Kong there were people spreading the word: "Him, lecture on the sutras? He can't even read; how could he lecture on the sutras?" When I lectured on the sutras in Taiwan, there were also people who said this about me: "He can't even read; how can he lecture on the sutras? Don't go and listen!" When you tell people not to go and listen, they get all the more curious and all the more want to see what sutra this illiterate Dharma Master is lecturing on. I find it strange myself: "I can't even read, so why do I lecture on the sutras?"

My handwriting isn't good either. Although some people see that the Master's handwriting isn't good, they still want to keep it to sell as an antique in the future. I'll tell you very frankly and clearly: I don't like to write letters, because, first, I'm busy, and second, I think my handwriting isn't good, and I don't want my handwriting to become antiques in the future. During the more than ten years I lived in Hong Kong, many monastics said I couldn't read, and I admitted that I couldn't read; I can't read, but I'm willing to learn. Although this prajna of written words [Tr.: wisdom conveyed through language and texts, as distinct from the wisdom of direct realization] isn't the real thing, you still need to know a little of it.

Don't think that whatever I say is right; there may be mistakes in what I say. Why? Because before I lecture on a sutra, I haven't even read it through once. Now in America I'm lecturing on the *Avatamsaka Sutra* for you, and this is the first time I've held the *Avatamsaka Sutra* in my hands to read it. Although I held an "Avatamsaka session" [Tr.: a session devoted to reciting the *Avatamsaka Sutra*] at the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Hong Kong, where everyone recited the *Avatamsaka Sutra* for 21 days, at the time I was busy managing affairs, and in those 21 days I didn't read a single word. So I have never read the *Avatamsaka Sutra*, nor have I recited it. Generally speaking, before lecturing on a sutra, a Dharma Master reads the commentaries, and after reading the commentaries he reads the sutra text, studying it back and forth many times. I haven't read it once, haven't recited it once, and haven't studied the commentaries either, so the principles I explain may be wrong or mistaken in places. Why? Simply because I'm very foolish, and I don't necessarily understand all the principles the Buddha spoke.

All of you are wise people, especially the young wise ones, whose wisdom is who knows how many tens of millions of feet higher than mine. Even if what I say isn't right, I have at least cleared this wasteland for you, so that you can plant it and till it yourselves. Where what I say is right, put it into practice; where it isn't right, you can correct it with your own true wisdom.

**[Addendum]** Upasika Tan Guo Zheng recounted:

At that time my family didn't have any real religious faith. Although I attended a Catholic English-medium school, nothing the Bible said could make me believe enough to be baptized a Catholic. My father, Tan Pucai (Dharma name Guo Pu), didn't have any particular religious faith either, but he liked to go to Tung Sin Tan. At that time many businessmen took part in the Taoist group at Tung Sin Tan, and my father often went too. He was over 70 then, and I usually went with him to Tung Sin Tan, but I always stayed outside on the veranda, because they burned so much incense inside that it was very smoky, and I had no interest in what they did there.

In the winter of 1951, Upasaka Chen Ruichang invited the Master to explain the *Earth Store Sutra*. Upasaka Chen had a shop selling fabric; his family lived on the second floor, and on the third floor there was a small loft, where the Master stayed. I didn't attend the Master's lectures on the *Earth Store Sutra*, because I had always been indifferent to what went on at Tung Sin Tan. When the Master was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Tung Sin Tan, Cai Guo Su, who was 14 or 15, saw the Dharma seat emitting light that illuminated the entire hall, and she also saw many other states. The Master said she had come from the heavens, and that was why she had the heavenly eye.

## 12. Taoist Friends Take Refuge

Tung Sin Tan was founded in 1938 and is dedicated to Patriarch Lü Chunyang; belief in Taoism is the foremost condition for a disciple to join. Its aim is "to propagate Taoism and carry out charitable activities for society." At that time the Taoist members would invite spirits to descend to the altar...

In the winter of 1951, while the Venerable Master was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Tung Sin Tan, a planchette message from Patriarch Lü encouraged the faithful to take refuge with the Triple Jewel. The message read:

> I wish to speak the Dharma for the people of the world;  
> To discuss the sutras and recite the Buddha's name is right and fitting.
>
> <small>願與世人來說法  談經念佛理所宜</small>

> With body and mind, too, exhort and transform;  
> Cultivate merit within—it is good to take refuge.
>
> <small>亦要身心來勸化  內修功果好皈依</small>

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Lü Dongbin is generally called Patriarch Lü. In Taoism, Patriarch Lü is worshipped. Actually, who is this Patriarch Lü? He is Guanshiyin Bodhisattva. Guanshiyin Bodhisattva manifests as Patriarch Lü to save these old Taoists. Why did he then take refuge with the Triple Jewel? This was returning home and acknowledging his ancestors. You could also say that he went into Taoism and manifested as an old Taoist in order to "cast out a brick to draw forth jade"; in reality, he was also opening the way for Buddhism.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasaka Tan Guo Pu recounted in his article "Starting from How I Came to Study Buddhism":

It was the first month of winter in the year Xinmao (the tenth lunar month of 1951). Because I revered the Ancestral Teacher, Patriarch Lü Chunyang, I often visited the Tung Sin Taoist altar. It so happened that Upasaka Chen Ruichang had invited Dharma Master To Lun, the Venerable Master, a native of Jilin in Manchuria, to come to the altar to expound the *Sutra of the Past Vows of Earth Store Bodhisattva*. Having heard the essentials of the Dharma, I suddenly awakened to the compassion of the Buddha's Way; deeply moved in my heart, I thereupon took refuge with the Triple Jewel.

**[Addendum 2]** Tung Sin Tan originated from Qingyun Cave at Chashan in Nanhai, Guangdong. It belongs to the Quanzhen school of Taoism and venerates Patriarch Lü Chunyang as its principal deity. In 1938, several disciples of Qingyun Cave at Chashan in Nanhai came to Hong Kong to do business, and, for the convenience of cultivating their minds and natures, they enshrined the Ancestral Teacher, Patriarch Lü, in a private residence in Central, naming it "Qunxian Tan" (Altar of the Assembled Worthies). It was later renamed "Tung Sin Tan of Chashan Qingyun Cave in Hong Kong." Its original site was on Gage Street in Central; in the 1960s it moved to Wellington Street.

**[Addendum 3]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

At that time the Taoist members of Tung Sin Tan would invite spirits to descend to the altar and would practice divination and planchette writing [Tr.: spirit writing, in which characters traced by a medium's stylus are believed to come from a deity]. The medium supported with the fingers the underside of the top crossbar of a T-shaped implement, and it would write characters in a tray of sand by itself; a person standing by would read them aloud, and the spirit's predictions were written down. By the time my father saw this poem, it was already the last day of the sutra lectures. That day my father had me copy this poem from the register of planchette messages. Each line of this poem began with a number, from one to ten. The last line, "Cultivate merit within—it is good to take refuge," exhorted the disciples of Tung Sin Tan to take refuge with the Triple Jewel. After my father saw this poem, he decided to go and see the Master, and I reluctantly went along with him.

I saw that this monk, in his tattered, patched robe, compassionate yet stern, was clearly as different as heaven and earth from the monks I had seen before, and different too from all the Catholic priests and nuns I had met. The Master's bearing revealed an extraordinary dignity that inspired admiration. At that time the Master gave me a copy of the *Earth Store Sutra*, and from that day on I felt differently about Buddhism. I read the *Earth Store Sutra* with great energy; even at mealtimes I couldn't bear to put it down for a moment. I didn't understand myself why I had suddenly become interested in believing in Buddhism.

On the fifteenth day of the third lunar month of 1952, my mother and I took refuge together; my mother's Dharma name was Gan Guo Yan. Later my younger sister Tan Mujie (Tan Guo Shi), my younger brother Tan Qiaojian (Tan Guo Xing), and my youngest sister Tan Muyan (Tan Guo An) all took refuge with the Master. Except for my elder brother, who followed Esoteric Buddhism, our whole family took refuge with the Venerable Master. I didn't take refuge because my father had taken refuge; I took refuge because of the teachings of Buddhism.

At the Catholic school there were catechism classes and Bible classes. I had asked the nuns who taught us many questions, and none of them could answer. After meeting the Master, I found that the teachings of Buddhism were very different: everyone can become a Buddha.

The Master once hoped I would write an account of how my father came to take refuge, but I didn't want to write it and said to the Master, "Better to hide my clumsiness than to put my poor skills on display." The Master said, "Why are you attached to the mark of self? If everyone thought the way you do, wouldn't it mean no one would write articles? Lately few people submit articles to the Buddhist magazines; I hope those of you who can write will write more articles." Looking back, there are truly so many of the Master's teachings that I failed to follow!

**[Addendum 4]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

My Dharma name is Guo Shi, but the Master always called me Mujie. I was able to meet the Master because of my father. Tung Sin Tan invited some businessmen to join, and my father was one of its members. This group often donated money to help the poor, giving out food, medicine, and other daily necessities in relief. My father had read many classical books and works on Chinese medicine. When he heard the Master lecture on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Tung Sin Tan, he found that the Master was so young and yet so learned. The faithful of Tung Sin Tan all revered the Master, something that had never happened before. At the time the Master was only just over 30, and about 10 to 15 Taoist believers took refuge with the Master; my father, then in his seventies, was one of them.

My father, Tan Pucai, was a man of letters who delighted in doing good and giving generously. During the Second World War, he sold off family property and bought rice to give out as porridge, saving the lives of the whole village. He also built a school in the village and ran education there. Perhaps because the villagers respected my father, during the Cultural Revolution both the school and our family house remained completely intact. My father had a certain reputation in the business world; that he could take as his teacher someone younger than his own son, humbly accepting instruction, was very rare. After taking refuge, his Dharma name was Guo Pu, and his name can often be seen at the DRBA Way-places in Hong Kong. In the early records of the Master's life, he wrote a preface, expressing how fortunate he felt that, at nearly 80, he had been able to meet such a great and noble teacher.

I remember that I was in primary school then. He came back from Tung Sin Tan and told my mother in high spirits, "Do you know? I have a teacher now." My mother said, "You're so old, so your teacher must be even older." "No, my teacher is very young. He's even younger than our eldest son!" "Really?" "Yes, I think he's 30-something at most." "How could you take such a young person as your teacher? What could he know?" My father said, "You're wrong! If you had heard him lecture on the sutras, you would know that he's a monk of vast learning and dignified bearing. At my age I've never met anyone so young and so learned, so I took him as my teacher. In a couple of days I'll take you to pay your respects to him, and you'll see that what I've told you is right!"

## 13. A Life Extended by Twelve Years

One of the legendary anecdotes of the Venerable Master's time in Hong Kong is that he used the "Jeweled Seal Hand," one of Guanshiyin Bodhisattva's Forty-two Hands and Eyes [Tr.: 42 practices, each a hand gesture with its own mantra, in the Great Compassion Mantra tradition], to lengthen the life of the elderly Upasaka Tan Guo Pu...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I've done many very important things that nobody knows anything about, and if I told about them, nobody would believe me. Among the immortals of old there was a seal called the "Heaven-Overturning Seal"; Guangchengzi [Tr.: a legendary Taoist immortal] had one. In Taoism it's called the "Heaven-Overturning Seal"; with Guanshiyin Bodhisattva it's called the Jeweled Seal Hand. If you have this Jeweled Seal Hand, the gods in the heavens all obey your commands, and King Yama and the ghost kings in the hells also obey your commands; it has the wondrous function of bringing the dead back to life. If you can't perfect it, though, it's of no great use. Someone heard me say the Jeweled Seal Hand had such great benefits and thought, "I'll hurry and perfect this Jeweled Seal Hand. When someone is about to die, I'll stamp him with it once and not let him die!" You can do that. But when your own life comes to an end, there won't be anyone who can use this seal to save you.

I've used this seal twice, when I had no choice: once in Manchuria and once in Hong Kong. What does "had no choice" mean? It means this thing had to be done; I couldn't not do it. Who was it in Hong Kong? It was the father of Madelena (Tan Guo Shi). Her father was 79 that year, and the fortune-tellers who read his birth chart all said he was certain to die. He asked me, "Shifu, can you let me live ten more years? Because my children are still young. If I can live ten more years, I'll be completely content." I said, "All right, it can be done! Not just ten years: I'll let you live 12 more years. How's that?" "Of course that's good!" Afterward he did indeed live 12 more years; he died at 91.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

My father's request came after Western Bliss Gardens had been built, and I was there at the time. Because my father was old, climbing up to Western Bliss Gardens was very tiring for him. When he sat down on a small wooden stool to rest and talked with the Master, I was standing right beside them.

Actually, how my father came to ask the Master to lengthen his life began with his asking the Master whether he should return to America to live out the rest of his years. My father was a Chinese American with U.S. citizenship who had come back to Hong Kong to do business for my mother's sake. If he returned to America, he could take all of us to America with him, so he told the Master about this idea and asked for the Master's guidance. The Master asked my father, "Would your life in America be as comfortable as in Hong Kong?" "Of course not. Hiring help in America is very expensive!" "Then why go back? Children and grandchildren have their own blessings; why be a horse or an ox for them? If your children have the affinity, they will go there someday." "Actually, I'm already very content. Although I'm not greatly wealthy, I have a driver and servants, and business is doing all right. My only worry is that my daughters are still young. If I could live ten more years and see my children grow up, I would be completely content!"

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

After my father took refuge at 77, he said to the Master, "Shifu, I'm going to die soon!" Earlier, my father had told the Master that at around 30 he had already died once. His family had laid him out in the main hall for several days, dressed him in burial clothes, and were preparing to choose a day to bury him. Without his realizing it, my father's spirit encountered my great-grandfather, who asked him, "Why have you come here? Go back, go back! You should come only when you reach my age." As a result, my father actually came back to life. My father remembered that my great-grandfather had passed away at 89.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

At the end of 1993, my mother, then in her eighties, suddenly fell ill. Both her thighs were in unbearable pain; she couldn't move, let alone walk. Every night she had nightmares and cried out and shouted, constantly yelling that people had come to collect debts from her, yet she didn't recognize those people. We sought out all the famous doctors of Chinese and Western medicine and tried acupuncture as well, but none of the various methods worked. In the end we decided to phone the Master for help. That very day (in 1994), the Master and Dharma Master Heng Lai came to our home. The Master told us that these were karmic creditors come to collect their debts.
