Besides doing meritorious deeds on my mother's behalf, one of her descendants also had to resolve to become vegetarian, and we were to recite "Namo Greatly Compassionate Guanshiyin Bodhisattva" for her and recite Guanyin's ten great vows in the Great Compassion Mantra, "I vow to quickly know all dharmas..." If we did this, my mother would still have 11 years of life. We did everything as the Master instructed, and two or three months later my mother's legs suddenly no longer hurt very much, and she gradually became able to get up and walk. She told us about her nightmares: "I saw a monster with nine heads and many tails, and there were many chains on the hands and feet. Suddenly all of it disappeared!" From then on my mother's life was extended by 11 years, until she passed away in 2004.

---

# Part Two: Building Monasteries and Propagating the Dharma

*Ages 34 to 36*

*1951 to 1953 CE*

## 14. Clearing Dry Land

In 1951, during the time that Tung Sin Tan in Hong Kong had invited the Venerable Master to lecture on the *Earth Store Sutra*, no fewer than a hundred people came every day to hear the sutra. After the lectures concluded in the first ten days of the twelfth lunar month, several lay Buddhists proposed building a vihara for the Venerable Master...

The causes and conditions for choosing land and building the vihara came together very quickly. Before long, someone reported that on the ridge above Ma Shan Village at Sai Wan Ho in Shau Kei Wan, Hong Kong, there was a piece of wasteland (Editor's note: after the Japanese surrender, much government-owned public land was left unmanaged; at the time such land was called "overlord land," and private persons could borrow it temporarily) measuring some several dozen square feet. The drawback, however, was that there was no water source on the mountain. One had to rely on the public "street hydrant" beside the road at the foot of the mountain, that is, draw water from the public tap-water pipe on the street, and then carry the water on shoulder poles up several hundred stone steps to the top. The mountain path was steep and rugged, so one can imagine how hard it was!

In the Venerable Master's own words: Not long after, my neighbor at Guanyin Cave, the Dharma Master, didn't welcome my living in the thatched hut and persuaded the people of Fu Yung Shan to move me out, so I moved to Ma Shan Village in Shau Kei Wan and built a small Buddha hall on the mountain. Western Bliss Gardens used to be a piece of mountain land; to get up there you had to climb more than 300 stone steps, and there was no water on the mountain either. Although there was no water, I felt that a small Way-place could be built there.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

A reporter who had not originally believed in Buddhism told the Master that up on the mountain there was a piece of public land where a small temple could be built, except that it was in a poor district and had no water source. The Master said, "Let's go and take a look!" After seeing it, the Master paid the rent and obtained the right to use it. Not long after, my father took my mother and my elder sister to pay their respects to the Master. I didn't go, because at that time I was attending primary school at a Christian school, and I thought that believing in Buddhism was something only ignorant people did, and that monastics did nothing more than rush from one sutra recitation and repentance rite to the next. After meeting the Master, my mother and elder sister decided to take the Master as their teacher.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

In Hong Kong there was a reporter who specialized in reporting news of the religious world; monks and Taoists alike all had to stay on his good side. The Master said, "Even so, if it weren't for him, Buddhism in Hong Kong might not have flourished as it has, so his merit and virtue toward Buddhism is great. If his circumstances were ever bad, it would be only right for Buddhism to support his whole family." The Master's extraordinary breadth of mind filled me with heartfelt admiration.

## 15. Western Bliss Gardens Monastery

The construction of Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, from the initial planning and design through the supervision of the building work, was handled entirely by the Venerable Master himself. Throughout, the Venerable Master held firmly to his principles: he did not scheme for support, he refused to print notices appealing for funds, and still less did he go from door to door seeking donations to cover the costs...

In the last days of the twelfth lunar month of the xinmao year (late January 1952), the vihara was completed. The Venerable Master named it "Western Bliss Gardens Monastery," and the Three Tathagatas were enshrined there. In August 1984, the Hong Kong government requisitioned the land of Western Bliss Gardens, and the Venerable Master handed it over to the government unconditionally; the Buddha images and memorial tablets were moved to Cixing Monastery (known locally as Tsz Hing Monastery). High-rise buildings have now been built on the original site.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When Western Bliss Gardens was being built, I was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra*: I lectured on the *Earth Store Sutra* from seven to nine every evening. I was living in Central, and every day I went over twice to see how the construction was coming along. It was a half-hour ride from Central to Shau Kei Wan, so the round trip took an hour. Once there, I also lent a hand with the work, because I had watched so much construction that I could tell at a glance how a job should be done. So I became an engineer and graduated without ever having to go to school! It isn't only me who is willing to work for a Way-place like this. The great Good and Wise Advisors of the past did the same. Take Elder Hsu: when he built monasteries, he too went to the work site every day to see how the construction was going. He would put away even a single piece of timber and save it to be used later. No material of any kind was ever carelessly wasted.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi recounted on September 25, 2000:

Building Western Bliss Gardens back then was very hard. At the time there were just two people, Dharma Master Heng Ding and Dharma Master Heng Yue, plus one refuge disciple, and two workers were hired to help.

Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi's oral account, given on January 7, 2001:

It wasn't easy for the Master to build a monastery! Back when Western Bliss Gardens was being renovated, every day there was more work than could ever be finished. The nails in those boards were all hammered straight by the Master, one by one, and used again. Not the tiniest bit was wasted.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Mei Miaoling recounted:

When I was little, it was always my mother (Yuan Guo Zhen) who carried me up the mountain on her back. It was a long way, and every round trip took a whole day. At that time the Master had taken in two monastic disciples, Guo Liang and Guo Li.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Because my father was already quite old when I was born, I was frail and sickly from childhood, and every time I fell ill I would say strange, peculiar things. My health was always my parents' greatest worry. So my father gave me a piece of ancient dragon jade to wear to ward off evil, as protection. One night I came down with a high fever all over my body and kept saying that there were lots of people (ghosts) in the house: some were laughing, and some were doing weird things. My mother was terrified, and the whole family was kept from sleeping. When I woke up the next morning, that thick jade pendant had actually cracked. Although I was all right, my mother still couldn't wait to drag me off first thing in the morning to see the Master.

I remember that we climbed a great many stone steps to reach Western Bliss Gardens, passing many dilapidated thatched huts and little wooden shacks, and brushing shoulders along the way with ragged adults and children. I had never seen so many poor people. They all gave off the sour smell of people who had not bathed for a long time. That was my first trip to Western Bliss Gardens. When we finally made it up the mountain, I found that Western Bliss Gardens at that time was a very crude house with a roof of sheet iron. When the weather was hot, it was like an oven inside; when it rained you could hear the dripping, and it leaked too. Even ordinarily, inside the house you could still hear the sounds outside. The ground was covered everywhere with gravel. There was hardly anyone there; apart from the Master, there was only an old woman who did the cooking.

Before my father took the Master as his teacher, he often took us to Tung Lin Monastery in Tsuen Wan. I saw some monastics there, but none of them left any particular impression on me; the first monastic I truly met was the Master. The Master was sitting on a small wooden stool. At first I was a little afraid of him, because the Master's eyes were different from other people's. Being a chatterbox, I asked the Master, "Shifu, what's wrong with your eyes? It seems you can't look straight at people." The Master told me how he had read the *Dharma Flower Sutra* until his eyes bled, and that moved me deeply. My mother told the Master about my condition, saying, "This is my youngest daughter. She is always seeing unclean things. Shifu, can you help her? Has something attached itself to her?" The Master smiled and said to me, "It's nothing. Go home and recite the Buddha's name diligently; recite 'Namo Greatly Kind, Greatly Compassionate Guanshiyin Bodhisattva,' and you'll get better! Recite 'Namo Greatly Kind, Greatly Compassionate Guanshiyin Bodhisattva' every day; you must keep it up day after day." Still uneasy, my mother said, "That's all it takes? Will just doing that be enough?" From that day on, I have constantly recited the holy name of Namo Greatly Kind, Greatly Compassionate Guanshiyin Bodhisattva.

The Master asked whether my Chinese was very good, and I said yes. The Master said, "Then I'll give you some books to take home and read." Then the Master gave me three books: one was the *Wonderful Dharma Lotus Flower Sutra*, one was the *Sixth Patriarch's Dharma Jewel Platform Sutra*, and the other was *Records of the Life of Elder Master Hsu Yun*. The Master said, "I'm giving you these three books. You must read them properly; you mustn't take them home and toss them aside. I'm going to test you. How long will it take you to finish them? How about a month?" "That's enough! No problem!" I had always been competitive, and my memory was excellent: when the teacher had us memorize texts, I only had to read them or hear them once to remember them. I had no idea that what the Master had given me were such profound scriptures. That is how I first met the Master.

In the blink of an eye the month was up, and I took the three books and ran up to Western Bliss Gardens to report back to the Master. The Master asked me, "Are you ready?" I said, "Ready!" The Master asked which book I liked best. Of course it was *Records of the Life of Elder Master Hsu Yun*. It was in comic-strip form, so you could guess the content just by looking at the pictures; the edition I read was thread-bound. The *Dharma Flower Sutra* was the hardest; I did not understand that scripture as well as I understood the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra*. The Master said, "All right! Then let's have a test on the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra*." With him asking and me answering, I passed that test.

The Master then explained a good deal of the principles in the sutras, especially the meaning of the lines in the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra*, "Produce a thought that dwells nowhere," and "Not thinking of good, not thinking of evil, at just this moment, what is the Senior Monk Ming's original face?" At the time I only half understood what I heard. The Master also had me recite the Great Compassion Mantra and the Shurangama Mantra. Although I had not taken refuge then, the Master gave me a whole new understanding of Buddhism, so whenever I had free time I would go to see the Master, listen to his stories of his days in Manchuria, and study classical Chinese with him, and my health gradually got better.

In 1994, when the Master's health was very weak, I went to Los Angeles to see him. Many people had heard my name but had never met me, and the Master introduced me to them, saying, "She has been my disciple since she was 11. She grew up under my wing!"

## 16. A Clear Spring Wells Up from the Rock

Because there was no source of water near Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, water became an urgent problem. After the Venerable Master moved into Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, he prayed before the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, asking them to grant a spring of sweet water. Later, beneath the rock face behind the monastery, the Venerable Master found a crack...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Before Western Bliss Gardens was built, this place was originally a big pit 7 or 8 feet deep; only after it was filled in could the Way-place be built. At first there was no water on the mountain, but after the little Buddha hall was finished, a rock behind the house unexpectedly split open with a crack, and a trickle of clear spring water came flowing out of the crack. Up on that mountain, drinking water was like consuming oil; it was even more expensive than oil. Suddenly there was water, and the neighbors from all around came over to grab it. The buckets were lined up one after another, dozens of buckets set out there waiting: only when this one was full was it taken away, and then that one would be pushed forward. There were lots of them and only one of me. I couldn't beat them in a fight, and I couldn't out-argue them either: they all spoke Cantonese, and back then I didn't understand what they were saying. They filled and filled and filled... and then suddenly the water stopped coming. With no water, they stopped coming. Once I had fenced it in all around with wire mesh, the water came back, and they could no longer grab it. From then on, even at Dharma assemblies when there were lots of people, there was always enough water.

One year, Hong Kong went more than half a year without rain, and there was water rationing every day. "Water rationing" meant you could only use water for one hour a day, and very little water could be collected in that hour; after that, the water was cut off. There was no water anywhere, but on my mountain there was water. Some people said I had brought this water from Fu Yung Shan, because when I lived at Guanyin Cave, the two pools in front of the cave were full every day, and ever since I moved away, those two pools had dried up. Western Bliss Gardens originally had no water, yet now it had water. Could it be that I really did move the water here? Because I have disciples who are dragons, there was bound to be a response. All of this was a response from the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, a manifestation of the protection of the gods and dragons!

(Editor's note: The following is excerpted from the Venerable Master's *Simple Explanation of the "Worthy Leader Chapter" of the Avatamsaka Sutra*, dated August 1975, when Guo Tong asked the Venerable Master:)

Guo Tong: The mountain where Shifu built the monastery was all hard rock. Other people said it was a very good place, but there was no water, so what could be done? Would we have to go down the mountain to fetch water? Then Shifu stuck a piece of bamboo into a crack in the rock...

The Venerable Master: No, no! No take bamboo. (I didn't use any bamboo.)

Guo Tong: Did you use something else?

The Venerable Master: No, no other thing. (I didn't use anything.)

Guo Tong: Then you just went to crack in the rock? (So you just made a crack right in the rock?)

The Venerable Master: That's how he wrote it (referring to the *Records of the Venerable Master*), and it isn't right! There was no bamboo. So you people keep "putting a head on top of a head" [Tr.: a Chan expression for adding something superfluous]: there was no such thing, and you've made something up. No bamboo, don't have bamboo. It's these people don't understand this. Not true, no bamboo, no any bamboo, no any stick. (There was no bamboo; they don't know. That isn't true! There wasn't any bamboo, and there wasn't any stick either.)

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

When the water first came, there was no bamboo; only later was a piece of bamboo inserted to let the water flow into the pool.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasaka Huang Guo Jun recounted:

This pool was very strange. Normally it stayed 70 to 80 percent full. When there were lots of people, with several hundred people at a Dharma assembly using the water, it never ran out; and when there were few people, the water never overflowed either. At the time there was an elderly Upasika named Guo Ping who lived at Western Bliss Gardens to help out; she was in charge of cleaning and odd jobs. The Master said to Guo Ping, "Don't go cleaning the pool whenever you feel like it! Before you clean the pool, you must tell me first." One day Guo Ping saw that there was a lot of moss in the pool and couldn't resist cleaning it, and after that the pool dried up. The Master said, "Guo Ping, now there's no water, and you're responsible! Go pray to Guanshiyin Bodhisattva and ask for the water to come back." Guo Ping was very honest. She spent the whole night in the Buddha hall reciting the Great Compassion Mantra and reciting Guanyin Bodhisattva's name. The next day the pool had water again, and the Master said to everyone, "This water was brought back by Guo Ping's prayers!"

**[Addendum 3]** As recorded in *Looking Out at the World* (Fangyan Guan Shijie) on September 18, 1978:

After the meal, we all went together to visit Western Bliss Gardens, the first Way-place the Venerable Master built after coming out to Hong Kong. Western Bliss Gardens is located on the ridge above Ma Shan Village in Shau Kei Wan. The slope is steep and the path winding, and only after climbing more than 300 stone steps do you arrive. The Venerable Master insisted on climbing the mountain with us. Our party of more than ten, men and women, old and young, made several large loops around the mountainside, and the older ones were all panting for breath. Seeing the Venerable Master's resolute, unyielding spirit, undaunted by hardship and striding forward just like us young people, gave us a kind of wordless teaching and encouragement.

All along the way we saw scenes of withered poverty: dilapidated wooden-hut settlements all around, a desolate expanse. After walking for about 20 minutes, we rounded a bend and came into a hollow in a small mountain recess, and we had reached Western Bliss Gardens. As we stepped through the little mountain gate, a fresh breeze came to greet us, and at once the summer heat was completely gone. In the garden, tree shadows swayed, clumps of bamboo moved in the wind, and moss lay in mottled patches: truly the serene stillness of "A winding path leads to a secluded spot; the meditation hall lies deep amid flowers and trees." The temple itself measured only 30 feet by 15 feet, with grayish-green tiles and plain cement walls, simple and unadorned, a "humble dwelling" in every sense of the word. Closing my eyes and gathering my thoughts, I faintly pictured the Venerable Master living quietly in his thatched hut in those days, and I could not help sighing softly. Though its outward form was dilapidated, his backbone and integrity remained and his joy was undiminished; dwelling hidden in the mountains, beyond worldly things, with his nature kept whole, he was truly "poor in body but not poor in the Way"!

Having heard so many stories about Western Bliss Gardens, we all wanted to see that natural spring. Walking to the back of the temple, we found a wooden frame about 3 feet square enclosing a rock on all sides, and a pool of clear spring water welling up from the rock. The water was as clear as a mirror. I scooped up a few mouthfuls in my hands and found it pure, sweet, and delicious, and of course the dusty toil and stifling heat we had brought from the city vanished completely.

## 17. The Opening-the-Light Ceremony

On May 1, 1952 (the eighth day of the fourth lunar month), Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, the first Way-place the Venerable Master built in Hong Kong with his own toil-worn hands and feet, held its Opening-the-Light ceremony [Tr.: a consecration ceremony that formally installs Buddha images and invites the Buddhas' presence into them]...

Material goods at the new monastery were extremely scarce; food, bowls, plates, and other utensils were all lacking, so at the Opening-the-Light ceremony the assembly was served tea and peanuts.

In the Venerable Master's own words: Western Bliss Gardens cultivated the Pure Land Dharma-door, so the first line of the couplet was "Recite Amitabha Buddha's name, and from here one realizes the Three Classes and Nine Grades." In the Land of Ultimate Bliss there are three classes: the upper class, the middle class, and the lower class; and nine grades: upper-upper, upper-middle, and upper-lower; the middle grades: middle-upper, middle-middle, and middle-lower; and the lower grades: lower-upper, lower-middle, and lower-lower. Once you reach the Land of Ultimate Bliss, even the lower-lower grade counts as having ended birth and death, but you still have to wait 91 great kalpas before becoming a Buddha. That is why it says that by reciting Amitabha Buddha's name one can realize the Three Classes and Nine Grades. The second line was "Cultivate the paramita dharmas, and from this the Six Perfections and myriad practices are perfected." Paramita here means the six paramitas. This was the first couplet I wrote after coming to Hong Kong.

I had a refuge disciple in Hong Kong, Luo Guo Ming, whose arm often hurt terribly. She asked me what she should do to get better. I said, "Do some merit and virtue! Do a bit more merit and virtue and you'll get better." At that time the monastery was holding its Opening-the-Light ceremony, and the basins, the bowls, and the vegetables were all bought by her. After the Opening of the Light, she said, "This arm of mine still hurts so much I can't move it." I said, "Lift your arm forward." With just that one sentence, without doing anything else, her arm got better right away, and the ailment was gone. So sometimes things are truly inconceivable!

**[Addendum 1]** Upasaka Tan Guo Pu recounted in his essay "Starting from How I Came to Study Buddhism":

In the twelfth month of that year (the twelfth lunar month of the xinmao year, December 28, 1951 to January 26, 1952), the lay devotees, wishing to support and protect the Proper Dharma, pooled funds to build a Way-place. Choosing a site by divination on the summit of Ma Shan in Sai Wan Ho, they established Western Bliss Gardens and earnestly requested the Master to expound the holy teaching, to ferry across those lost at the ford, and to turn the Dharma wheel. The Master generously accepted the charge without declining. As for me, although I had not been studying Buddhism for long, I knew that Buddha images can cause those who see them to give rise to faith, to praise them and make offerings, and so to obtain immeasurable blessings. Therefore I asked craftsmen to sculpt with great care three golden bodies of the Tathagatas to be enshrined at Western Bliss Gardens, and from then on everything was complete. The fragrant light filled the hall; people bowed in repentance and cultivated purely, reciting the Buddha's name together.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

My father asked the Master, "The monastery is about to be finished. What do you think I should donate?" The Master said, "Most things have already been donated by others. You could donate three Buddha images." So my father donated them.

## 18. The Porcelain Image of Amitabha

The Venerable Master had an image of Amitabha Buddha. Because the Venerable Master bowed to it with utmost sincerity, the porcelain image gradually turned from snow-white to pale red, as lifelike as a real person. Between the Venerable Master and this image there was an inconceivable, wondrous affinity...

The Venerable Master was in closed retreat at Guanyin Cave for more than a year. Because he had nowhere to enshrine the Buddha image, he asked two Dharma Masters from a nearby monastery to keep it for him. When the Venerable Master decided to build Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, he had prayed to Amitabha Buddha to help him accomplish the construction of the monastery. When the work began, the Venerable Master first moved to the Western Bliss Gardens building site, and he wanted to bring this image of Amitabha Buddha back. But the two Dharma Masters were unwilling to comply, and yet they had no legitimate reason to refuse, so they came to call on the Venerable Master on the first day of the lunar New Year.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

There was once a monastic who had Way-places in both Hong Kong and Taiwan. He had a lot of money, and no small amount of offenses either. Although he was a monastic, he killed several people and drove people to suicide; the retribution for that is inconceivable. In Hong Kong, he and another monastic were driven out by the people at Tung Po Tor, and no one would have anything to do with them; it was I who took care of them. Later, I also "ding"-ed a floor for them on Ladder Street ("ding" is Hong Kong parlance meaning to rent), and I had an image of Amitabha Buddha that I lent them to worship. The image was made of porcelain.

To my surprise, on the evening of the first day of the first lunar month, the two of them knelt down together in front of me and, acting like a pair of scoundrels, said that if I didn't give them the Buddha image, they would kneel in front of me until they died. Back then I was also very foolish and went to pull them up. If it were me today: if you want to kneel till you die, then kneel till you die; I couldn't care less what you do. Both of them were fat monks. I'd pull this one up, and that one would kneel down again; I'd struggle to pull that one up, and this one would kneel down again. I dragged them outside: no sooner had I dragged one out and come back to drag the second one out than the first one would run back in and kneel there; I'd drag the second one out and come back to chase out the first, and the second one would run back in and kneel. They took turns wearing me down like that and kept it up for five hours.

By this time I was really unhappy. I lost my temper and smashed the teapot and teacups, but they paid no attention and just kept up their shameless act, until I had no way out. I said, "All right, I surrender to you! It's not as if I can't become a Buddha without this image; I'll give it to you." Only when they heard me say I was willing to give it to them did they get up. I saw them out the door and said to them, "From now on, no matter where I am, you are forever forbidden to come through my door again!" With that, I shut the door. For several years after that, we had nothing to do with each other, until a monastic said, "Let me mediate between you!" I said, "What's there to mediate? I'll go and see them." I went there, and we were on good terms again.

**[Addendum]** The very next day the Venerable Master fell ill, with a high fever that would not go down; he was gravely ill. Fortunately, Dharma Master Heng Yue, who was living with those two Dharma Masters, learned that the two had made an unreasonable scene on the first day of the New Year and that the Venerable Master had fallen ill because of it, and he immediately took that image of Amitabha Buddha back and returned it to the Venerable Master. Once the image came back, the Venerable Master gradually recovered, and the construction of the monastery went ahead smoothly.

## 19. Hearing of His Ways, Others Follow Suit

The Venerable Master's talent and learning were outstanding, and he had an extraordinary, uncommon wisdom. Whether as a shramanera, as a Bhikshu, as an abbot, or even as president of the Fellowship of Mainland Monks Residing in Hong Kong, in everything he had his own unique style of doing things, and for a time others followed his lead as grass bends before the wind...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In the thirty-fifth year of the Republic (1946), 14 of us monks traveled south together. When we took the ship from Tianjin to Shanghai, I was a shramanera. As a rule, whatever you are doing, the Bhikshus go in front and the shramaneras behind. But they knew nothing at all about travel procedures and had no common sense whatsoever; they didn't even know about going to the customs office to get vaccinations and smallpox inoculations. So they had no choice but to call me up to the front to lead them in dealing with the officials, and I took them to get all these things done.

Later, when I came to Hong Kong, most of the people in Buddhist circles were also following along behind me. At first, nobody knew how to use English-style correspondence. After I came to Hong Kong, I used English envelopes and letter paper, and only then did they follow suit. I was the first to apply to the government for tax exemption for a Buddha hall, and afterward they followed along behind me and got tax exemption too. I'm not saying how great I am; it's just that they didn't use their brains when they did things. For instance, there was one person in Hong Kong who did the most to undermine me. His mind for scheming for connections was also the biggest, and he was very attentive when it came to serving women: he would carry suitcases and handbags for wealthy female lay devotees. Why was he like this? Because he could barely read. If he didn't act like that, he really had no other way to get by. I hope you will not scheme for connections the way he did!

**[Addendum]** Dharma Master Heng Sure recounted:

One year we went to New Zealand and met Elder Master Xuan Zhi. He had known the Master back in Hong Kong and had once stayed at Cixing Monastery. Elder Dharma Master Xuan Zhi said that the Venerable Master's way of speaking the Dharma was unique and famous throughout Hong Kong. He said, "Your Master was in a class of his own, a model for monastics speaking the Dharma in those days. He could effortlessly recite the *Three Hundred Tang Poems*, and he could also quote offhand from *Laozi* and the Confucian classics as supporting evidence. Very few monastics had learning like his."

Elder Dharma Master Xuan Zhi also told a story that was widely known in Hong Kong Buddhist circles at the time. One day, when the Master was lecturing on the *Amitabha Sutra* at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, some monastics came into the hall and sat down to listen to the Dharma. From the Dharma seat, the Master suddenly said, "All of you, go sit at the back." Just as these monastics were about to get up and give up their seats, the Master said, "No need! Monastics should sit in front. I was talking to the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division, telling them to sit at the back."

(Dharma Master Heng Sure is an American disciple of the Venerable Master in America. From May 7, 1977 to November 4, 1979, to pray for world peace, he made a pilgrimage of "three steps, one bow" [Tr.: bowing to the ground once after every three steps] from Gold Wheel Monastery in Los Angeles, Southern California, to the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas in Northern California.)

## 20. Printing Sutras and Making Buddha Images

The sutras spoken by the Buddha are, without exception, Dharma treasures that expound the holy teaching and benefit living beings; their value and meaning are like a bright lamp in the dark of night. If the world had no sutras of the Buddhas, then the heavens and the human world would be like an endless long night. How then could the multitudes of living beings nurture their Bodhi wisdom-life?

Translating the Buddhist sutras and circulating the precious canon of the Tripitaka is the foremost work in saving people's wisdom-life, and it was also the Buddha's original intent in coming into the world. Therefore, during his years in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master never for a single moment slackened in his compassionate vow to propagate the Buddhadharma. One after another, he printed large quantities of the *Dharma Flower Sutra*, the *Universal Door Chapter*, the *Earth Store Sutra*, the *Shurangama Sutra*, and others, and sent them everywhere for distribution and circulation.

He also engaged painters to illustrate the life of Elder Master Hsu Yun and had it printed, and the Venerable Master composed verses for it. The result is a biography of a sage that no one should fail to read: the *Pictorial Biography of Elder Master Hsu Yun*.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Wherever I go, there are lots of sutras. In Manchuria I had no other property; all I had were sutras. At that time in Hong Kong it was extremely hard to find a copy of a sutra, so I did all I could to promote the printing of sutras. I spent more money on printing sutras than on anything else: something over HK$1 million. First I printed the *Dharma Flower Sutra* and the *Shurangama Sutra*. The copies we used in our early days in America for the *Shurangama Sutra* lectures were the ones printed when I lectured on the *Shurangama Sutra* in Hong Kong. Once I promoted it like this, other Buddhist organizations in Hong Kong later started printing sutras too, so now there are lots of sutras in Hong Kong.

Printing sutras is one way of propagating the Buddhadharma: on the one hand you print sutras, and on the other you open your own wisdom. Why haven't you opened your wisdom? It's because your merit and virtue are small. Without merit and virtue, you have little wisdom. Just before I came to America, I gave away all my sutras, worth something over 100,000, because, since I was coming to America, I had nowhere to keep them anyway; so I hurried to give the sutras away. What I like best is printing sutras. This is also part of propagating the Dharma, and I hope every one of you will put some heart into the work of printing sutras.

Western Bliss Gardens had a large collection of scriptures: there was the Tripitaka, and other scriptures besides. The Tripitaka I had obtained from Japan at great expense, and there were other very ancient scriptures that simply can't be bought anymore. There was a Bhikshuni who had originally worked as a servant for others and couldn't read a single character. Because I wasn't there, she got rid of all the scriptures and sent them off to a sutra distribution center. Today Upasika Tan told me, "I didn't dare tell you! If I told you, you'd be sure to lose your temper!" I said, "You guessed wrong! Never mind sending them to a sutra distribution center; even if she had burned the scriptures, I wouldn't care! The Shifu I am now is not the Shifu I used to be. In the past, in Hong Kong, I often lost my temper and often scolded people; now, no matter what you do, I don't scold anymore!" Although I don't scold, in my heart I'm still not happy to have a disciple like this. She is just too ignorant!

I hope that my American disciples, whether or not they can read Chinese characters, will never put Chinese sutras into the stove and burn them; even photocopies must be handled with great care. If you carelessly throw them into the fire and burn them, or carelessly destroy them, in the future you will become more foolish with each life. All scriptures, whether in Chinese or in English, must be specially protected. If a scripture is damaged, it should be repaired. Don't be like this disciple, always doing such dark and benighted things. All of you, pay special attention to this point! Because she couldn't read, she thought that sending them to the sutra distribution center was putting them where they belonged, not realizing that this was completely mistaken! People who study Buddhism must respect the scriptures; only by respecting the scriptures can you open your wisdom.

Why is this disciple so foolish? I'll tell you a little about the causes and conditions. In her past life, this person was a horse, so now that she has become a person in this life she still hasn't changed a horse's habits. She followed me for a long time, and I taught her the Buddhadharma, but she forgot all of it. Her karmic obstacles from past lives are very heavy, so she does many upside-down things, and she doesn't believe in cause and effect either. Why doesn't she believe in cause and effect? The *Sutra of Cause and Effect* says it very clearly: "Those who hear of retribution and will not believe or listen": you talk to them about the cycle of cause, effect, and retribution, and they don't believe it. "Have just escaped the animal realm and obtained a human body": they have just shed an animal body for the first time and obtained a human body, so they don't believe this is retribution. It isn't that she doesn't want to do what is right; it's simply her nature, and so she gets many things wrong.

Today I said to Upasika Tan, "I don't scold people anymore! All things go through formation, dwelling, decay, and emptiness, so what is there to scold about?" This is really a great pity, but that is how worldly affairs are: here something is formed, and there something decays; here something dwells, and there something becomes empty. If I were in Hong Kong, she would never have done such a thing, because I could keep an eye on her. Now that I have come here to America, I trust that people here in America won't do this kind of thing; after I'm gone, though, I don't know.

When I was in Hong Kong, in making Buddha images alone, I remember making more than 100 all told; I made them and then gave them to people. The Three Tathagatas, or perhaps the Three Sages of the West, now at the Chinese Buddhist Association of Hawaii were given to them by me. That was more than 20 years ago.

I hope that Buddha images will enter into everyone's heart, so that every person has a Buddha image in their heart and every home has a Buddha image too. This, too, is layer upon layer without end.

## 21. A Duckling Listens to the Dharma

In the first month of summer in the renchen year (April 1952), the Venerable Master lectured on the *Universal Door Chapter* at Western Bliss Gardens. On the day the lectures concluded, a little duckling suddenly squeezed through the fence into the monastery grounds and quietly crouched outside the door of the Buddha hall...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When I was lecturing on the sutras at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, a duck came to listen to the lecture. It kowtowed behind my bowing bench, kowtowing and quacking at the same time. Gan Guo Hui took a palm-leaf fan and shooed it out, but when she came back in, it came back in too, and it was still there quacking and looking as if it were kowtowing. She'd shoo it out, and it would come back again. After she had shooed it out three or four times, I finally told her not to chase it away anymore, and only then did she stop. It lay there and listened to a whole session of the lecture. When people went outside to have a photograph taken, this duck went along to be photographed too. The people were all standing up on the platform, and it couldn't get up there, so it had to stay below the platform. The photographer didn't take care to include it in the photo, so in the end it didn't make it into the picture.

Whenever the sutras are being lectured and the Dharma spoken, no matter what kind of living being comes in, you shouldn't drive it out. Even mosquitoes, flies, and moths that fly into the lamps are all here to listen to the Dharma. Do you remember, at Gold Mountain Monastery and at the Buddhist Lecture Hall, when there were bugs in my teacup? Those bugs had also come to listen to the Dharma. Those bugs belong to the dragon class; toads also belong to the dragon class.

## 22. An Arhat Falls

In the summer of the second year after Western Bliss Gardens Monastery was founded, a Guanyin session was held. During the morning bowing to the Buddhas, people discovered that a sword-shaped wooden board had been placed in front of the altar table. Most of the assembly were beginners in Buddhism, and someone, out of curiosity, asked the Master, "Shifu, is this a wooden sword?" The Venerable Master said, "This is an incense board. In monasteries it is used to strike people who don't follow the rules, who doze off, or who are lazy." A board that thick would surely hurt when it came down, and everyone trembled with fear, wondering who would be the first to try out the incense board. Yet as the Guanyin session was drawing to a close, the incense board was still lying where it had been. That evening, during the great dedication of merit, the Venerable Master picked up the incense board, put it on his shoulder, and strode out of Western Bliss Gardens. With astonished eyes, everyone followed the Venerable Master's figure as they looked outside. It turned out that a middle-aged monk had just come up the mountain; as soon as he saw the Venerable Master, he kowtowed to him and knelt on the ground repenting...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In the thirty-sixth year of the Republic (1947), a living Arhat appeared in China. This living Arhat was called Wu Jin. What abilities did this Arhat have? He could recite the Great Compassion Mantra to cure people's illnesses; no matter what the illness, once he treated it, it was cured. So at Mount Putuo, the Way-place of Guanyin Bodhisattva, he made a great display of spiritual powers. At that time, more than 500 monks had gone to Mount Putuo to take part in a precept-transmission ceremony. After the ceremony was over, because not many pilgrims went to Mount Putuo that year, the monks couldn't raise any money by seeking alms, so these 500-plus monks who had come to Mount Putuo had no way to get off the mountain and no way to go back to their own monasteries, because none of them had any money. So he cured people's illnesses there, and people came to believe in him especially strongly. In this way he raised enough donations for two boats and, free of charge, sent all of those 500-plus monks to Shanghai, Shenjiamen, and other places. As a result his fame grew very great; throughout Shanghai, Hangzhou, and Suzhou, there was no one who didn't know about this living Arhat. Some officials believed in him, ordinary people believed in him even more, and in Buddhist circles, monastics and laypeople alike believed in him.

At that time I was staying at the Buddha Recitation Hall on Lingyan Mountain in Suzhou, and once I met him in the recitation hall. I had heard this was the living Arhat. I took a look: his appearance wasn't bad; he looked like an Arhat and wore tattered clothes. But when I looked at him, I saw that this kind of Arhat was about to die. I asked him, "Are you the living Arhat?" He said, "I wouldn't dare claim it; people just call me that!" I said, "What abilities do you have, that you're called a living Arhat?" He said, "I just cure people's illnesses!" I said, "You cure other people's illnesses; you ought to cure your own illness! Have you cured your own illness yet?" He looked at me and said, "What illness do I have?" I said, "You have the illness of seeking fame, and in the future you will also have the illness of seeking profit. You cure other people's illnesses, but in the future there won't be anyone to cure yours!"

I said, "I'll tell you honestly: if you keep on curing people's illnesses and showing off your spiritual powers, you will certainly fall!" "Falling" means either committing offenses or no longer being a monastic; it's like dropping from a high place to a low place, and that is called "falling." When I said this to him, the living Arhat knelt down in front of me. I said, "What are you doing?" He said, "If I fall, I beg you to save me!" Actually, if he simply stopped curing people's illnesses, he could avoid falling. He didn't ask me how he could avoid falling; he just said that when he fell, he wanted me to come and save him. Ai! At the time I didn't think it over either, and I promised him, "All right, if you fall, I'll come and save you!"

This was very strange. When I got to Hong Kong, I met this living Arhat again, and sure enough, he was no longer a living Arhat. He had become an ordinary person, a layman rather than a monastic. When he saw me, I still recognized him. I said, "What's become of you now? Where has your living Arhat run off to? How did you end up like this?" He said, "It's all your fault! Your words were too efficacious! You said I'd fall, and I fell. If you hadn't said I'd fall, how would I have ended up like this!" I said, "Well, you've fallen, and I promised I would save you. Will you leave home again now?" He said, "Leave home? I don't have any money." I said, "If you have no money, I'll give you money; go and leave home!" And so I helped him to leave home again.

There was a Dharma Master who revered him like a living Buddha, bowing to him every day after morning recitation and bowing to him again after evening recitation. Originally that Dharma Master had wanted to invite me; he wanted to use me as his signboard, but I wouldn't go. This signboard of mine is simply not for sale to anyone. So that Dharma Master treated him as a living Arhat again instead. I said, "Be careful! If you revere him like this, he'll fall again!" Later, sure enough, this living Arhat ran off again, and nobody knew where. This living Arhat also never lay down to sleep and also kept the rule of not eating after noon, and still he could fall. So cultivating the Way is not easy.

**[Addendum 1]** When the Venerable Master met the living Arhat again in Hong Kong, the man was already married; his wife was the one who had been his chief Dharma protector for many years. The merit and virtue of his many years of cultivation had come to nothing, and his spiritual powers had vanished without a trace. When he saw the Venerable Master he was deeply ashamed and resolved to leave home once more. The Venerable Master gave him money for robes and other expenses, but in less than half a year he returned to lay life again. That day, he had come to Western Bliss Gardens to repent before the Venerable Master; it was the second time he had come to ask the Venerable Master to let him leave home, and the Venerable Master still helped him with money and encouraged him. Later his whereabouts became unknown; it was said that he had returned to lay life again.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master once said, "Once I said, 'I can know all the paths that others cultivate, but others don't know the Dharma-door that I cultivate!' Just because I said that one wrong sentence, my cultivation of the Way made hardly any progress afterward. Fortunately, at the time I asked my Dharma uncle Chang Ren for guidance, and only then did I realize I had misspoken. So in cultivation, remember: you must never be arrogant."

**[Addendum 3]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master said, "Cultivators must cut off desire and get rid of love! Cultivators who have not yet found their footing must be especially careful!" Looking back on our time in Hong Kong, the Master was very strict in disciplining his disciples, and following the Master meant observing many rules. For example, girls were not allowed to meet male Dharma Masters face to face; if they happened to pass each other, the girls had to lower their heads as they went by. The Master's instruction was, "Don't disturb others as they apply effort!" After coming to America, I once wrote to tell the Master that some young Dharma Masters from Honolulu had visited the San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall and that we had received them warmly. The Master wrote back reprimanding us: "Don't get close to them!" Although at the time I didn't dare go against the Master's wishes, in my heart I never understood what the Master meant. Only many years later did I learn that quite a few of these young Dharma Masters who came to America in the 1950s had been unable to withstand temptation and had returned to lay life and married.

## 23. Cultivation Is Not Easy

The virtuous ones of old often said, "Don't say that leaving home is easily attained; it comes from planting Bodhi seeds life after life." Leaving home is hard to attain, yet people don't realize that the tests after leaving home are even greater. For a monastic to maintain the resolve for the Way, proper knowledge and views and a firm, sincere, and constant mind are of vital importance.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

There was a monk who had been a soldier before he left home. Later he studied Buddhism and took refuge with the Triple Jewel, ate vegetarian food, recited the Buddha's name, and also led others to recite the Buddha's name. He saw others leaving home and thought it was good, so he left home too. He was a grand-disciple of Elder Hsu, very articulate and a smooth talker. After leaving home, he devoted himself to the Dharma-door of reciting the Buddha's name. I watched him receive the precepts at Nanhua Monastery. Later, when the ** Party came, he fled to Hong Kong. The situation in Hong Kong was very complicated. At that time monastics had nowhere to live in Hong Kong. It wasn't like now, when some monks in Hong Kong live in tall buildings and big mansions; back then, never mind a mansion, there wasn't even a little thatched hut. This monk couldn't get used to living in that one-story wooden building at Tung Po Tor. Probably he had been used to spending a lot of money before, so with no money in hand he found things hard, and he ran off to Tao Fong Shan.

At that place there was a pastor who made it his business to undermine Buddhism and entice monastics to return to lay life. If a monk didn't want to stay in Buddhism, all he had to do was go there, and the pastor would give you 30 or 50 dollars a month, or even 200 dollars. At his place, if you didn't want to eat vegetarian food, he had meat for you to eat; if you didn't want to be a monk, he would give you a wife. And what about Bhikshunis? It was the same for them: if you wanted to get married, he would find you a husband, and if you wanted to marry a monk, that was fine too. He used money and sex to entice monastics, and once they went to live there, they would "drift, linger, run wild, and go to ruin" [Tr.: a phrase from *Mencius*], "drifting" downstream. What is meant by "drifting, lingering, running wild, and going to ruin"? "Following the current downstream and forgetting to return is called drifting; following the current upstream and forgetting to return is called lingering; pursuing the hunt without ever being satisfied is called running wild; delighting in wine without ever being satisfied is called going to ruin." He said his place was the most convenient, and that in times like these this was how things ought to be. He also said, "Here we also recite the Buddhist sutras and do morning and evening recitation." In fact he was hanging out a sheep's head and selling dog meat: his whole purpose was to undermine Buddhism and to get people to stop believing in the Buddha and stop believing in monks. I once went there to take a look, and I got into a debate with this pastor.

I said, "What you are doing here goes against the heart of Jesus. You are not a Christian; you aren't qualified to be a follower of Jesus. Jesus built people up; he didn't ruin them. You don't build people up; you ruin them. You ruin monastics by making them stop being monastics; this is called destroying their pure conduct." I said, "As for me, I'm not a Buddhist either! If I were a Buddhist, I ought to be compassionate like the Buddha and save you. Unfortunately I can't save you; I don't have the power to save you, so I'm not a Buddhist." What I said left him staring at me wide-eyed with nothing to say. Afterward he wanted to have his photo taken with me. I said, "No photo! A photo of you, a disgrace to Christianity, together with me, a disgrace to Buddhism, would only bring shame on Buddhism and bring shame on Christianity too." So no photo was taken.

This monk went to that place and worked as a cook, at 300 dollars a month. But he wasn't cooking vegetarian food; every day he had to kill chickens, ducks, and fish. He said it didn't matter: every time he killed a chicken, he would recite as he killed it, "Namo Amitabha Buddha, be reborn in the Western Land of Ultimate Bliss! Namo Amitabha Buddha, be reborn in the Western Land of Ultimate Bliss!" He thought that by reciting "Amitabha Buddha" like this he could rescue the chicken and help it to be reborn in the Land of Ultimate Bliss. From morning till night he did nothing but kill chickens and kill ducks; he took a great many lives. I believe he may have killed 360 chickens. But then what happened? The retribution came! After he had been there a little over half a year, he went insane and couldn't stay there anymore, so he went back to Tung Po Tor, back to that place without money, to live. There, from morning till night, he raved: "Look, look and see whether the ducks I killed went to the Land of Ultimate Bliss! Did the chickens I killed get to the Land of Ultimate Bliss or not? Or did they become chickens again? Or will they kill me someday?" All day long he talked crazy talk like that.

Later he wanted to see me. Why did he want to see me? He knew that if he could see me, there might be hope of eradicating his karmic offenses, and then his illness would get better. How did he know that seeing me would make him better? Because in the past, he had brought several people with illnesses like his to see me, and they had all gotten better. Now it was his own turn. When he was lucid he would say, "I want to go and see Dharma Master An Tse. Which of you can help me and take me to see him?" But just when he wanted to go and see me, he would go mad again and talk to himself: "No! If you see him, what will become of us? You knowingly broke the precepts: you killed so many living beings. You, a monk, killed so many chickens and so many ducks. How could this possibly end well!" So he never did get to see me. About half a year later, he took a knife and killed himself. I saw this monk with my own eyes; so cultivation is not easy!

Why did this monk undergo this kind of retribution? Let me tell you. First, this was a great Bodhisattva manifesting this appearance for living beings to see: "Look, all of you: a monastic must not kill. A monastic who kills will undergo this kind of retribution!" He wasn't afraid of disgrace; he didn't shrink back and say, "This is too embarrassing; I don't want to manifest like this!" This is the same as Devadatta. That is putting it in a good light. I can't say he was a bad monk, because I am even worse than he was. Second, he lacked a firm resolve; after he left home he changed and didn't know how to cultivate. True, reciting the Buddha's name can rescue beings, but when you, a monastic, kill living beings, you are knowingly breaking the precepts, and your offense is three degrees heavier. What qualifications do you have to rescue them? You killed it; are you certain you can rescue it to rebirth in the Land of Ultimate Bliss? You're not certain, and you don't have that right either, and yet you tell yourself, "I think it should have gone to the Land of Ultimate Bliss!" That won't do. So when we hear this case, we must not mistakenly think that we can recite the Buddha's name while creating offenses at the same time. If you recite the Buddha's name, you must not create offenses; all the more should you do good. You should not do a single wrong thing, and you should not create a single offense.

## 24. The Sea of Suffering Is Boundless

On the vast ocean, people do not know where they are going or which way to head; thus those who awaken are few indeed! In his earnest compassion, the Venerable Master was like a lighthouse on the sea, skillfully offering a ray of dawn light, but living beings were often unable to understand the Venerable Master's great compassion...

In 1950, not long after the Venerable Master came to Hong Kong, Upasika Luo Guo Ming brought her daughter Huang Guo Song and her son Huang Guo Jun, and all three of them took refuge with the Venerable Master together. Luo Guo Ming had come to know the Venerable Master through an introduction from her neighbor, Wang Meigu. This Wang Meigu was the Master's first lay disciple in Hong Kong; her Dharma name was "Guo Hai." Her husband worked on ships and came home only once in a long while, and the family was not very well off.

In the 1950s, many people in Hong Kong believed in Catholicism or Christianity, because it made schooling easier for their children, and the churches all handed out relief goods, such as flour; there were things to eat and also things to use. Because of her family situation, she often went to Christian and Catholic places.

One day she came to Western Bliss Gardens wanting to tell the Master that she had already become a Catholic and would not come to the Way-place anymore, but she just couldn't bring herself to say it. That day, as she was taking leave of the Master to go home, the Master said to her, "Guo Hai, I'll see you down the mountain. You'll take the wrong road!" Guo Hai said, "I won't! I've come to Western Bliss Gardens so many times; how could I take the wrong road?" The Master said, "I'd still better see you down. You'll take the wrong road!" The devotees present all thought it very strange, because the Master never saw anyone down the mountain. After that, Guo Hai never came again.

## 25. Sincerity Wins the Buddhas' Protection

In the autumn of 1952, Luo Guo Ming ran into several misfortunes one after another. While she was depressed and troubled, she also caught an illness so serious that even the doctors were at a loss. Her daughter, Huang Guo Song, grief-stricken and not knowing where to turn, knelt before the Buddha...

Upasika Huang Guo Song wrote:

In the autumn of the renchen year (1952), because things were not going well for her, my mother was suddenly struck down by illness. Her condition was very serious and she was in terrible pain; her face was ashen, she couldn't get out of bed, and the doctors we called in brought no improvement. Later the doctor said my mother could die at any moment. The family was very distressed to hear this, and I felt even more lost and helpless, so I prayed to the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, begging them to protect my mother. Just then, a great light suddenly shone before my eyes, and there appeared a Dharma Master wearing a kashaya and seated in full lotus. Looking closely, I saw that it was actually the Master. Overcome with surprise and joy, I quickly recited the Buddha's name silently. Before long, my mother fell into a heavy sleep, but her complexion still showed no improvement. Then an idea came to me, and I quickly found someone to go with me to see the Master. As soon as I saw the Master, I burst into tears and begged him to save my mother. The Master told me, "I already knew about your mother's illness. Go home quickly, recite the Buddha's name sincerely, and contemplate; the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas will certainly aid your mother." I bowed to thank the Master and went home. Following the Master's instructions, I stayed up the whole night reciting the Buddha's name, praying for aid for my mother. By daybreak, my mother's illness was more than half gone, and her spirits had recovered a great deal. Through this experience I came to appreciate even more deeply the inconceivability of the Buddhadharma. I will never forget the Master's kindness!

From the Venerable Master's instructional talk of September 4, 1983

In their lifetime everyone must go through the four great sufferings of "birth, old age, sickness, and death"; nobody can escape these four kinds of suffering, unless you cultivate the Way and end birth and death, which is another matter. Ordinary people all have to undergo these four sufferings. At the time of "birth," it is like being squeezed between two mountains, so when a child is born, the first thing it does is cry, expressing suffering. At a birth, relatives and friends are turned by the state of birth and all come to celebrate; although they offer congratulations, it is actually suffering. In "old age," the eyes grow dim, the ears go deaf, the teeth fall out, the hair turns white, and the hands and feet no longer obey orders; they all go on strike together, and you can't move. That is very painful too. In "sickness," the four elements are out of balance and won't cooperate with each other, to the point that you lie in bed and can't get up; and if poverty and sickness strike together, it is suffering upon suffering. At that time the spirit is willing but the strength is lacking. Tell me, is that suffering or not? When it comes to "death," the suffering is even more beyond words; it is just like a cow being skinned alive. And with sons and daughters tugging at one's heart, deluded by the seven emotions and six desires, one still cannot let go even at death.

"Death" is in fact a road that every life must travel, so why be happy at birth and grieve at death? Unfortunately, ordinary foolish people cannot break through this attachment. Now we want to investigate the question of "where we come from at birth and where we go at death." If we investigate it until we understand it, we can escape the Three Realms and no longer undergo the cycle of rebirth. To investigate this question, Shakyamuni Buddha left home to cultivate the Way. He sought the Way for six years and practiced austerities for six years, yet he still did not find a way to resolve birth and death. Finally he sat in stillness beneath the Bodhi tree for 49 days, saw the bright star at night, and awakened to the Way, thoroughly understanding the wheel of life, "the Twelve Causes and Conditions."

Why am I talking about these principles today? Because when I first arrived in Hong Kong, the first disciple who took refuge with me was Upasika Luo Guo Ming. She was 81 this year, and she passed away yesterday morning, and this has moved me. All her life she was hard pressed by her circumstances, but she never surrendered to them. She endured suffering and toil and raised five children, and all of her children have achieved considerable success. So Upasika Luo Guo Ming can rest with a smile in the world beyond, or else be reborn in the Land of Ultimate Bliss.

**[Addendum]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Because Guo Song often helped with the chanting at Dharma assemblies together with me, and Upasika Guo Ming often joined my mother, Yuan Guo Lin, and Mrs. Luo, the four of them pooling money to handle the various expenses and affairs of the Dharma assemblies, I thought of Upasika Guo Ming as if she were my own mother. One morning at around four or five o'clock, I was awakened by the sound of a crisp, lively trumpet. While I was still bleary-eyed, I saw Upasika Guo Ming tell me very happily that she was liberated and was leaving. That afternoon, I received word from Hong Kong that Upasika Guo Ming had passed away the previous evening. When I worked out the time, it was exactly my morning in America.

## 26. Wondrous Accounts of Saving Lives (1)

Saving people's lives is the most important thing: this was the Venerable Master's principle in all he did. Whether in China, in Hong Kong, or in America, the Venerable Master saved many people's lives. Because Mrs. Wu had borrowed money to build a stone house...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Saving people's lives is the most important thing! In my more than ten years in Hong Kong, I saved many people's lives. Now that their circumstances have improved, they all come very sincerely to repay the Buddha's kindness, and wherever I go, they support me. In America, too, I have saved many people's lives; it isn't only lecturing on the sutras and speaking the Dharma. When I say this, don't go digging to the bottom of it: "Whose life exactly did you save? What good deeds did you actually do? You say you did this and that, but we don't know." You can organize an investigation team and go to Hong Kong to investigate.

I remember there was a man surnamed Wu whose wife took refuge with me. What did this Mr. Wu do? He was the village head of a small village, and he smoked opium. His wife borrowed 9,000 dollars from someone. What for? She planned to build a stone house and make money by collecting rent on it. Unexpectedly, as soon as the stone house was built, it was torn down by the authorities concerned, and in an instant everything was gone, and there was no money to repay the debt either. In her desperation, Mrs. Wu was going to hang herself.

Speaking of this, I'm not advertising myself. Let me make it clear to you first: what I'm telling you about are real people and real events. Just as she was about to hang herself, she saw Shifu come and say, "Don't hang yourself. Tomorrow this problem will be solved; someone will help you." At that moment she didn't know whether it was real or not, and when she looked again, Shifu was gone. The next day, sure enough, a friend came and lent her money, and her problem was solved. That is one case; as for the rest, you can understand what it's all about.

There was also a child who still couldn't walk at the age of 5. I don't know whether it was polio or something else, but he just couldn't walk. When he was 9, his family heard about me from someone and came to find me. I said to his mother, "Every day, bow to the Buddha all the way from the foot of the mountain to the top. Whenever you have bowed enough, your child will get better." His mother brought him to the Buddha hall to bow to the Buddha every day. Going up the mountain there were more than 300 stone steps, and at every step they made one kowtow. They did this once a day and kept bowing for half a year, and without taking any medicine, the child's illness was cured. His family had no money, so they bought 5 catties of oil and offered it to the monastery. These are two incidents that I remember.

**[Addendum]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

One night I was woken up. Bleary-eyed, I got up and saw my mother kneeling before the Buddha, murmuring something; I didn't know what she was reciting. Only the next morning did I learn that my mother hadn't slept the whole night, because my elder brother's old illness had flared up again; he was coughing up blood and couldn't stop, and the doctor was at a loss. The doctor said that if my brother kept on coughing up blood, last night might be his last. When my mother learned this, never mind that it was nighttime, she grabbed a servant and ran to Western Bliss Gardens to beg the Master for help.

After seeing the Master, my mother went back home and continued kneeling before the Buddha, sincerely reciting the Great Compassion Mantra and asking for Great Compassion water for my brother to drink. After my brother had drunk it two or three times, the bleeding lessened. My mother kept on reciting the Great Compassion Mantra without stopping, and my brother finally stopped coughing up blood and slowly fell asleep. The next day the doctor came to see my brother and was astonished to find that he had come through the critical period and had actually survived. My mother loved her son so much that, besides sincerely praying for Guanyin Bodhisattva's aid, she also vowed to eat less. Hong Kong people take eating very seriously; eating less is regarded as a very serious matter. A mother's love is truly great!

Our family's servant, Sister Yin, often went with my mother to help at the Dharma assemblies at Western Bliss Gardens, and gradually she too came to believe in the Buddha and recite the Buddha's name. In particular, when she saw the response in which my brother received the aid of Guanyin Bodhisattva and the Master, she also resolved to take refuge. Before long, she quit her job with our family and went to a nuns' monastery as a volunteer, and later she too shaved her head and left home. A few years ago I went back to Hong Kong to visit Sister Yin; she was still cultivating at the nuns' monastery.

Question: I earnestly beg Shifu to be compassionate and save my daughter. My daughter has "hereditary mucopolysaccharide metabolism disorder," which has affected her bones, tendons, and heart.

The Venerable Master: When I was in Hong Kong, there was a child of 5 who couldn't walk. His mother brought him every day to my Buddha hall up on the mountain to bow to the Buddha. After bowing for half a year, he got better without taking any medicine at all. As for this matter, you would have to ask the one who did the bowing; only that person knows. I don't understand it.

## 27. Wondrous Accounts of Saving Lives (2)

In the spirit of great kindness and great compassion, whenever people were in suffering, distress, danger, or illness, the Venerable Master, seeing their utmost sincerity, fulfilled their wishes without exception. Upasaka Li Guo Yuan's youngest daughter, Dieyan, accidentally fell, and several days later she ran a high fever all over her body...

Li Dahua, whose Dharma name was Guo Yuan, had a youngest daughter, Dieyan, who accidentally fell while walking. At the time nothing seemed wrong, but several days later her whole body was burning hot, and her leg was also in severe pain. Li Guo Yuan and his wife thought it was caused by the fall and took their daughter to a bonesetter, Li Zifei, for treatment. After examining her, Dr. Li told them that it was not an injury from the fall but was caused by a sore growing on her leg. After they returned home, they called in another doctor to treat her, but it did no good.

Later, through someone's introduction, Li Guo Yuan's wife carried her little daughter on her back to Western Bliss Gardens Monastery to ask to see the Venerable Master. The Venerable Master said that he was not familiar with medicine and could not treat her. Li Guo Yuan's wife would not give up and came again to beg him to save her daughter, but the Venerable Master again refused. The third time, Li Guo Yuan and his wife came to Western Bliss Gardens Monastery together. The two of them knelt before the Venerable Master and begged him to find some way to save their daughter. Seeing that the couple was utterly sincere, the Venerable Master made an allowance for them as an expedient and finally agreed to pray to the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas on their behalf for protection. After one week, Li Dieyan's illness was, astonishingly, cured. Li Guo Yuan's whole family deeply believed that this was due to the Venerable Master's virtue and the protection of the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, so all five members of the family took refuge with the Triple Jewel and became the Venerable Master's disciples.

In the Venerable Master's own words: There was a policeman named Li Dahua whose 4-year-old daughter couldn't walk after a fall, and the doctors couldn't cure her. Li Dahua went to Western Bliss Gardens and bowed to the Buddha a few times, and his daughter was able to walk; because of this, many people said I could cure illnesses. I also remember a very poor man whose mother was ill, and seeing doctors didn't help. Because this poor man's mother had heard that I could cure illnesses, she told him to come to me and ask. At the time I said, "If you want your mom to get well, you have to give 100,000 dollars." If he had asked why money was needed, I would have told him, but he didn't ask; he just left in a huff.

He went home and told his mom that I was a monk who was greedy for money. That night he had a dream in which someone told him, "When the Dharma Master told you it would take 100,000 dollars, he didn't mean for you to give him HK$100,000. He meant for you to spend 1 dollar to buy a 100,000-dollar note of paper money, the kind of money ghosts use, and burn it; then your mom's illness will get better." He really did just that, and his mother's illness got better. Why was that? There's no explaining it.

**[Addendum]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master showed me a letter from Mrs. Zhan Liwu. Her husband, Zhan Liwu, was a devout Buddhist; she too had taken refuge with Elder Master Hsu Yun, and the whole family lived in Canada. In the letter she mentioned that her daughter had polio. Because she had read about the responses described in *Records of Chan Master To Lun*, she bowed morning and evening to the Master's photograph in the *Records*. After only a few days, her daughter felt strength in her legs and could move about, and she soon recovered completely. She also enclosed a photograph of her daughter. Every day she recited the "Hsuan Hua Verse" that the Master wrote in the *Pictorial Biography of Elder Master Hsu Yun*.

## 28. A Deaf Woman Listens to the Sutras

The elderly Upasika Liu Guo Juan had been deaf for many years. In 1951 (the xinmao year), she took refuge with the Venerable Master at Western Bliss Gardens. After taking refuge, she suddenly brought forth the Bodhi resolve. Her hands never let go of her recitation beads, and every day she did nothing but recite the great six-character name "Namo Amitabha Buddha"...

Whenever Western Bliss Gardens held a Dharma assembly or the Venerable Master lectured on a sutra, Liu Guo Juan attended, come wind or rain. In the summer of 1953 (the guisi year), the Venerable Master lectured on the *Amitabha Sutra* at Western Bliss Gardens, and Liu Guo Juan came to listen as usual. She was over 60 years old, and her legs and feet were not strong; going up the mountain meant climbing more than 300 rugged stone steps. Even so, she insisted on climbing up every day; she was truly full of sincerity. But how could a deaf person listen to a sutra lecture?

In the Venerable Master's own words:

At Western Bliss Gardens Monastery in Hong Kong, I lectured on the *Shurangama Sutra* for 14 months. During the summer vacation of 1968, at the San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall, I lectured on the entire *Shurangama Sutra* in 96 days. That was really as fast as riding a rocket to explore the moon: it was finished in three months. At a sutra lecture assembly, whether or not you can understand what you hear, listening to the sutra here brings inconceivable benefits. I had an old disciple who was very strange: whenever I lectured on a sutra, she would insist on coming to listen. Could she hear? No! She was deaf and couldn't hear the lecture, but she still wanted to listen.

When others lectured on the sutras, she didn't go to listen, because she couldn't hear anything at all. Strangely enough, though she couldn't hear my lectures either, she still came to listen. She told people that if she didn't come to listen to the sutras, she would feel uncomfortable all over, and she felt that if she didn't come this time, she would miss the opportunity. A deaf person coming to listen to the sutras doesn't amount to much in itself. But she was 60 or 70 years old, and she wasn't put off by how high the mountain was, climbing more than 300 stone steps. Someone asked her, "What are you listening to?" She said, "I don't know what I'm listening to." It was just that wonderful.

Once, when I was lecturing on the *Universal Door Chapter*, she was deaf and couldn't hear the lecture, but she still came to listen. They listened and listened, and then one day, which happened to be the second day of the fifth month, before the lecture on the *Amitabha Sutra*, when the assembly began reciting "Namo Lotus Pool Ocean-Wide Assembly of Buddhas and Bodhisattvas" three times, in the snap of a finger she heard a line of "Namo Lotus Pool Ocean-Wide Assembly of Buddhas and Bodhisattvas." She heard it! From then on, her deafness was completely cured, and she was no longer deaf. Tell me, isn't that very mysterious? After she was no longer deaf, she believed in the Buddha even more sincerely. Whenever I lectured on the sutras she came, not lazy even once; she wasn't afraid of heavy rain or strong wind, and she would always come and listen. So this proves that she had a sincere mind in coming to listen to the sutras: even though she couldn't hear, she still wanted to listen, and in the end her deafness was cured.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master said, "In Manchuria I often came across many extraordinary things. But in Hong Kong there weren't any strange happenings. If there were, I didn't get involved, or I handled them in secret without letting anyone know." Guo Mi, who was beside him, answered, "There were! The case of Liu Guo Juan."

**[Addendum 2]** On March 10, 2013, Dharma Master Heng Gui said at the Dharma Realm Buddhist Books Distribution Society in Taipei:

Upasika Liu Guo Juan was the wife of my lay grandfather's elder brother. She had great affinities with the Venerable Master. Although she was deaf, she went to Western Bliss Gardens Monastery every day to listen to the Dharma.

Western Bliss Gardens Monastery no longer exists. Some 10 or 20 years ago, while the Master was still in the world, the government wanted to develop the area and widen the road, and the Venerable Master, being very compassionate, gave up Western Bliss Gardens Monastery for the public good. So now it no longer exists.

Western Bliss Gardens Monastery was in a slum, and to get there you had to climb more than 300 steps. I still remember going there as a child: halfway up there was a heap of garbage beside the path, but if you kept climbing up to Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, you found a whole other world; the atmosphere was completely different.

Every day Upasika Liu Guo Juan climbed up there, umbrella in hand, to listen to the sutras. How could a deaf person listen to the sutras? It must have been because she had those good roots! One day she heard people reciting "Namo Lotus Pool Ocean-Wide Assembly of Buddhas and Bodhisattvas," and from then on she was no longer deaf. Although she was no longer deaf, she was Cantonese, while the Venerable Master had a Manchurian accent, so it would have been very hard for her to follow the Venerable Master's Dharma talks. But whether she was deaf or not, she kept on going to apply effort.

## 29. Earnestly Exhorted, Yet She Did Not Awaken

The sea of suffering is vast, yet people take suffering for pleasure; they know full well that turning back is the shore, yet no one is willing to give up the red dust of the world. Two sisters often went to hear the Venerable Master lecture on the sutras, and each time they saw golden light radiating from the body of the Venerable Master on the Dharma seat...

When the Master was lecturing on the sutras at Tung Sin Tan, the sisters Yuan Guo Xiang and Yuan Guo Zhen often went to listen, and Yuan Guo Xiang saw with her own eyes golden light shining from the Master's body. From then on they had great faith in the Master, and later Upasika Liu Guo Juan introduced them to the Master. The first time Yuan Guo Zhen paid her respects to the Master, on the way home down the mountain she suddenly felt herself bobbing up and down in a vast, boundless sea. She was very frightened, but when she later steadied herself and looked again, the sea had disappeared. Afterward she mentioned this to the Master, and the Master said, "The sea of suffering is vast and boundless; turn back, and there is the shore." But being young at the time, she could not grasp what the Master meant. The Master said she would have no blessings to enjoy in this life unless she left home.
