Some time later, Yuan Guo Zhen's marriage ran into trouble, and before long she gave birth to a daughter. Once, when her daughter was seriously ill, Yuan Guo Zhen carried her daughter on her back up to Western Bliss Gardens. Halfway there they were caught in a heavy rain, and mother and daughter arrived to see the Master soaked to the skin. The Master said to her, "I knew long ago that you would come to this, but still you don't turn back, and still you don't wake up." The Master immediately recited a mantra for her daughter and rubbed the crown of the child's head to bless her, and only then did her daughter gradually grow strong and healthy.

For many years, Upasika Lu Tanzhen had been Yuan Guo Zhen's close friend. One day she told Yuan Guo Zhen to go up the mountain to pay respects to the Master, but Yuan Guo Zhen didn't want to go, because she was hard up and had no money to make an offering to the Master. So Lu Tanzhen lent her money to put in an offering envelope for the Master, and only then did the two of them go up to Western Bliss Gardens together. Once they were up the mountain, Yuan Guo Zhen offered the envelope to the Master. The Master said, "There's no need for an offering envelope here. Take it back." But she left the envelope on the table anyway. Later, the Master said to Lu Tanzhen, "When you go back, tell that lady surnamed Yuan not to give me an offering envelope again next time, because she has no money; the money she put in the envelope was borrowed." Truly inconceivable! Only Yuan Guo Zhen and Lu Tanzhen knew about this, and they never imagined that it could not be hidden from the Master.

**[Addendum 1]** Yuan Guo Xiang is the mother of Dharma Master Heng Gui, and Yuan Guo Zhen is Dharma Master Heng Gui's aunt and also the mother of Mei Miaoling. When Yuan Guo Xiang was pregnant with Dharma Master Heng Gui, she went to Western Bliss Gardens to see the Master, and the Master said to Guo Xiang, "She isn't born yet, but I already know her!" Dharma Master Heng Gui became the Venerable Master's disciple while still an infant. As a child, she often went with her mother to Western Bliss Gardens to bow to the Buddhas and make offerings. At least 40 or 50 people in her family took refuge with the Venerable Master.

## 30. A Way to Turn Back Heaven's Decree

The Venerable Master had deeply studied every school and thoroughly investigated the Chan sayings, yet in ordinary times he taught his disciples only to recite the great six-character name "Namo Amitabha Buddha." The Venerable Master's refuge disciple Tang Guo Shan had taken refuge with the Venerable Master early on and believed in the Buddha most sincerely...

When the Venerable Master learned of it, he comforted her with kind words, telling her not to worry, and told her that when she went home she should tell her husband to diligently recite "Namo Amitabha Buddha." If his lifespan was not yet over, then by the power of the Buddha he might recover. If his fate was inescapable, then by sincerely reciting "Namo Amitabha Buddha" he could still be reborn in the Pure Land at the end of his life. The Venerable Master also explained to her in detail the principle that "Heaven is hard to fathom, and life is impermanent," and again instructed her to go home and tell her husband.

When Luo Kangrui heard his wife relay the Venerable Master's words, he felt that they truly made sense, so he begged his wife to go and ask the Venerable Master to let him take refuge. Three days after taking refuge, Luo Kangrui dreamed at night that the Venerable Master was wearing a Five Buddha Crown and a red kashaya, followed by a host of Bodhisattvas of majestic appearance. The Venerable Master told Luo Kangrui, "Your illness will get better. Don't worry!"

When he woke up, he had diarrhea that would not stop. As it turned out, besides his lung disease, Luo Kangrui also had a lump growing in his abdomen. The lung disease alone had already left his whole family terribly worried, so to keep his family from worrying, he had hidden the abdominal mass even from his wife. As a result, during the diarrhea, the mass in his abdomen was actually expelled along with the waste. From then on, Luo Kangrui had even greater faith in the Buddhadharma. Whether walking, standing, sitting, or lying down, his hands never left his recitation beads, and he recited the Buddha's name tens of thousands of times a day. His sincerity evoked a response: within just a few months his body gradually recovered, and when he went back to the hospital for an X-ray, his lung disease had, astonishingly, completely healed.

## 31. Scalp Sores Heal and Hair Grows Back

From the time Huang Xuemei was 100 days old, she began to break out in scabby sores on her head, which oozed pus and blood and gave off a rank, foul smell that made people cover their noses. Especially in the intense heat of summer, the condition got worse and her hair kept falling out, until she was nearly bald, which was very unsightly. Her family took her to every doctor there was, but both Chinese and Western doctors were helpless. After that they tried every kind of folk remedy, things to eat, things to rub on, injections, every one of them, but none had any effect.

When Xuemei was 3 years old, her father died, and she depended entirely on her mother, who worked as a domestic servant, to raise her. Almost all of each month's income went to pay her medical bills, yet her scabby head was the same as ever. Whenever Mother Huang thought of her daughter's condition, she wept tears of blood, her heart pierced as if by an awl, and she lamented her own ill fate. When Xuemei was 10, the scabby sores on her head still showed no improvement. Then, through a fortunate turn of conditions, she met the Venerable Master and took refuge with him. After she took refuge, strangely enough, the sores on her head began to scab over and heal; even in the intense heat of summer they gave her no great trouble, and her hair gradually grew back. The scabby, festering sores that had troubled her for more than ten years were completely cured. Seeing her full head of thick hair, nobody could imagine what she had originally looked like.

## 32. Rich and Poor Alike

The Venerable Master's disciples were rich and poor, old and young. Some were in dire straits and hard up, even working for others as servants or cooks. Whatever a disciple's situation, the Venerable Master treated them all equally well...

Upasika Xu Xiangjun recounted:

My parents both came to Hong Kong from the mainland, and they came to know the Master through Tan Guo Shi's mother. My father was a disciple of Elder Master Hsu Yun, so the Master said he didn't need to take refuge again. When my mother took refuge with the Master, the Master struck her with the incense board and asked her, "Do you know why I struck you with the incense board?" My mother shook her head and looked at the Master. The Master said, "You are my disciple now. I struck you with the incense board to eliminate disasters for you." My mother still looked at the Master with puzzled eyes. The Master went on, "When your father passed away, you didn't go to his funeral, so I struck you with the incense board." That was during the War of Resistance against Japan, and my mother was pregnant, so she had not gone back for the funeral. My mother was astonished that the Master knew even this.

Because our family was poor, the children had to share the housework, so when we wanted to go to Western Bliss Gardens to take part in the group cultivation, we could only take turns going each week. Our family lived in an illegal squatter structure, shabby and run-down. The Master came to our home, and he didn't look down on us because we were poor.

Once my mother was very seriously ill, and the family all thought she would not recover. But the next day she said to the family, "I'm going to get better! Nothing will happen to me. Yesterday I dreamed that the Master came to see me. I'm definitely going to get better, really!" Afterward she really did get better day by day. Sometimes we would have a meal up on the mountain; the Master ate very simply, sometimes just chili peppers with steamed buns.

[Supplementary Note] Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

The children all loved the Master and weren't afraid of him, because he was very friendly to children. As for older disciples, such as my father, the Master never allowed my father to kowtow when he saw him. The Master said, "You're so old; there's no need! No need to bow." My family was not especially rich, but we were comfortably off. We had servants and a driver, so I didn't know how to do any housework; I didn't even know how to wash my own hair. Before I met the Master, I had a very bad temper. Even though the Master often taught us to treat everyone alike, I would still bully them, thinking of them as servants who were beneath me.

Later I met the Master. The Master taught me many things, I changed a great deal, and my temperament changed as a result. When we went to Western Bliss Gardens for Dharma assemblies, our family's maids and cook sometimes went along too. Mother had them help in the kitchen and also sit with us to listen to the Master lecture on the sutras and speak the Dharma. For me, this was a big turning point. I realized that all people are equal, whether rich or poor, high or low. As long as one truly cultivates, then no matter how poor one is, one will attain unsurpassed wisdom; if one does not cultivate, then even a rich person will die in the end just the same and be just as unable to escape the cycle of rebirth. Later, two of our family's servants, having heard the Buddhadharma, both took refuge with the Master, and afterward both of them left home. I truly feel I fall far short of them!

## 33. A Ghost Seeks the Five Precepts

At the end of 1952 (the renchen year), Western Bliss Gardens commenced a Buddha-recitation practice, holding a Buddha Recitation Dharma assembly that ran for seven days in a row. On the fifth day of the Buddha Recitation session, as the sixth incense period was ending, a ghost suddenly possessed Zhang Guo Yu...

In the Hong Kong of the 1950s, natural disasters and man-made calamities kept occurring, and society was turbulent and unsettled. The Venerable Master traced this to its root cause and revealed that it had been brought about by the karmic power of living beings; therefore, to turn back this calamitous fate, the strength of the multitude was indispensable. Accordingly, in the renchen year, taking the occasion of Amitabha Buddha's birthday (the seventeenth day of the eleventh lunar month), he held a Buddha Recitation Dharma assembly for seven days in a row (from the eleventh to the seventeenth day of the eleventh lunar month), from 8 a.m. to 7 p.m. every day. Western Bliss Gardens was located up on a mountain, and although the mountain was steep and the way long, many devotees still took part.

Several days went by like this. On the fifth day of the Buddha Recitation session (that is, the fifteenth day of the eleventh lunar month), toward evening, just as the sixth incense period ended, Zhang Guo Yu suddenly knelt before the Venerable Master. It turned out that a ghost had attached itself to Zhang Guo Yu and was earnestly begging the Venerable Master to give it the precepts. Only after the Venerable Master granted its request did Zhang Guo Yu come out of a stupor and regain clear consciousness. Those attending the Dharma assembly clicked their tongues in amazement: even ghosts knew to come and seek the precepts!

From the Venerable Master's instructional talk at the opening of the Amitabha Buddha Recitation session at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas, winter 1979:

The whole world is full of natural disasters and man-made calamities; it is dark and without light. This shows that the lives of all humankind are in danger. Such murderous, violent energy has never existed before. We know about atomic bombs, hydrogen bombs, and nuclear weapons, and now there are lasers as well. Once these lethal weapons are put to use, humankind may well perish all together. Therefore, at this point in time, only by cultivating according to the Buddhadharma can we wipe out disasters before they ever take form. The whole world is filled with black energy; wherever there are genuine cultivators, that place will have somewhat fewer disasters. When many people cultivate together, their combined strength can eliminate disasters and dissolve calamities, imperceptibly turning violent, hostile energy into auspicious, harmonious energy. But you must keep your feet on the ground, actually put it into practice yourselves, and cultivate according to the Buddhadharma. Everyone, bring forth your true mind and recite the Buddha's name. Each time you recite the Buddha's name once, a ray of light appears in empty space. If you can recite the Buddha's name earnestly and with utmost sincerity, this light will shine throughout the three thousand worlds, making the atmosphere of the three-thousand-great-thousand world system turn auspicious and transforming the polluted, violent, disaster-laden atmosphere.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Buddha Recitation session had six incense periods each day. By the time we did the dedication of merit, it was already six or seven in the evening; we were there all day long. The Master was very strict: not a single person could get out of morning recitation.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Because most of the devotees at Western Bliss Gardens were poor and could not read much, the Master taught them to recite the Buddha's name. During a Guanyin session or an Amitabha Buddha session, each time we would recite the Buddha's name for about an hour and then sit in meditation for half an hour. The Master gave an instructional talk every day, and sometimes he also had disciples go up on the platform and speak for a while. There were two instructional talks each day, one in the afternoon and one in the evening. The Dharma assembly went from morning until night, and after evening recitation we continued reciting the Buddha's name. After the Master finished his instructional talk, we did the great dedication of merit, and it ended at about nine o'clock.

## 34. The Departed Are Freed from Suffering

Out of great compassion, and as an expedient for all living beings, the Venerable Master transmitted the precepts for the deceased [Tr.: precepts transmitted on behalf of the departed] in order to rescue the spirits of ancestors, wronged souls, and other beings. The Venerable Master's disciple Li Guo He, in a moment of carelessness, forgot to receive the precepts for the deceased on behalf of his late mother...

After the Venerable Master built Western Bliss Gardens in Hong Kong, the devotees grew more numerous day by day, because there was now a fixed place. To meet the needs of living beings, he often held Dharma assemblies; he also set up memorial tablets for rescuing the deceased and transmitted the precepts for the deceased.

Once, when Western Bliss Gardens was going to transmit the precepts for the deceased, Upasaka Li Guo He went and signed up to receive the precepts for the deceased on behalf of his ancestors. Afterward he went down the mountain with everyone else, but halfway down, his legs suddenly hurt so much that he couldn't take a single step forward. Strangely, when he turned around and walked back up the mountain, he moved freely and his legs didn't hurt at all. Li Guo He tried this several times, and it was always the same, so he had no choice but to turn back to Western Bliss Gardens and report the matter to the Master. The Venerable Master smiled and asked him, "Did you forget to receive the precepts for the deceased on behalf of your mother?" When Li Guo He heard this, it suddenly dawned on him, and he hurried to sign up his mother to receive the precepts for the deceased. Once he had signed her up, he had no trouble going down the mountain.

**[Addendum]** Dharma Master Heng Sure, speaking on the evening of August 31, 2000, in the outdoor tent Buddha Hall at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas:

Requesting the precepts for the deceased is an act of filial piety and of repaying kindness; it is practicing great filiality. As you all know very well, the saying goes, "The tree wishes to be still, but the wind will not stop; the child wishes to care for his parents, but they are no longer here." A person's parents may already have passed away, and while they were alive he himself didn't understand how to be filial; when he wakes up and wants to be filial, there's nothing he can do, because his parents are no longer here. You originally had several decades in which you could have served them, been filial, and repaid your parents' kindness, but sadly you missed it. So what then? The precepts for the deceased can help you continue practicing filial piety: "The spirits on whose behalf I request the precepts have already passed away, but I am still willing to keep doing meritorious deeds for them and to dedicate to them the merit and virtue of the precepts for the deceased." That extends filial piety beyond the stage at which their bodies are no longer here. Isn't that wonderful? This enables the merit and virtue of the Dharma door of filial piety to continue. Why? Because although the people are no longer at our side, the relationship between us still continues in our hearts, so we use this relationship to do meritorious deeds and dedicate them to them. This is very wonderful. This is how the Venerable Master explained the precepts for the deceased at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas.

## 35. The King of Praises of His Generation

Dharma Master Xu Lang was often invited to Western Bliss Gardens and Cixing Chan Monastery to help with Dharma ceremonies. The Venerable Master praised his chanting as being among the very best in China; he was universally acknowledged as the "King of Praises"...

Dharma Master Xu Lang once wrote an essay in remembrance of the Venerable Master:

I came to know Dharma Master To Lun at Nanhua Monastery in Guangdong. At that time he had just resigned from his post at the Vinaya Academy and was in charge of the Sutra Library, and I got to know him because I went to the Sutra Library to borrow sutras. Since he was from Manchuria and I was also from Manchuria, he took special care of me. Unexpectedly, we later ran into each other again at Tung Po Tor Monastery in Hong Kong, but he left after staying only a few days. It was not until he had Western Bliss Gardens Monastery and often asked me to come and help perform Dharma ceremonies that we were in regular contact.

**[Addendum]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

The Master did not put emphasis on chanting; he often respectfully invited Dharma Masters to come and help with the Dharma assemblies. I remember that when I was little and went to Dharma assemblies at Western Bliss Gardens, what impressed me most was hearing the chanting voice of one Dharma Master. It truly "lingered around the rafters for three days, ringing on and on in the ears," and it filled those who heard it with joy. I had always loved singing, so I ran to ask the Master, "Who is that cantor Dharma Master?" The Master said, "That's Dharma Master Xu Lang. Did you know? Before the Liberation, his chanting was among the very best in China, and he was especially famous for chanting praises. If you said he was number two, nobody would dare claim to be number one. He's universally acknowledged as the 'King of Praises,' so of course he chants well!"

The Master went on, "I knew him when I was at Nanhua Monastery, and I never expected that we would meet again at Tung Po Tor Monastery, so I've invited him to help with the Dharma assemblies. If you're willing to learn from him, how about I ask him to teach you?" Before long, the Master announced to everyone, "Whoever wants to learn chanting from this 'King of Praises,' hurry and sign up! I don't know how to chant, so I can't teach you. Since you can learn from this 'King of Praises,' don't miss this good opportunity. That this 'King of Praises' is willing to teach you is your good fortune!" When they heard the Master say this, everyone rushed in a swarm to learn chanting from Dharma Master Xu Lang.

I remember that one day 20 or 30 laypeople came to class. Some were 60 or 70 years old and some were 40 or 50, and I counted as the youngest. At the beginning, everyone thought it would be great fun and very easy: imagine standing before the Buddhas, holding the Dharma instruments and striking away; how impressive! Who would have known that Dharma Master Xu Lang would be so serious about teaching, and especially meticulous in teaching how to play the Dharma instruments? Within a few days, fewer and fewer people came to class, until only the four or five of us young ones were left.

He said that what he had learned was the Shanghai "Sea Tide Sound" style of chanting. He wanted us to sing using the breath from the dantian [Tr.: the energy center in the lower abdomen]; every note had to be drawn out very long, rising and falling like waves, and every word had to be enunciated correctly with a full, round tone. He had us follow the beat, striking the table hard with our hands to keep time. He said the more it hurt to beat, the faster we would remember, and only after we had learned it thoroughly by heart and wouldn't make mistakes could we play the Dharma instruments. Dharma Master Xu Lang taught us to chant praises and to play the large Dharma instruments, and he also taught us the repentance ceremonies, reading out the memorials (shu), the Mengshan Food Bestowal, and all the ceremonial procedures for bowing to the Buddhas. He said, "Chanting praises, reciting sutras, and playing the Dharma instruments must all be dignified, because we are making offerings of sound to all the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas. If the chanting is not in accord with the Dharma and the Dharma instruments are played in a chaotic jumble, not only will it give the devotees afflictions, it is also disrespectful to the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas. What would originally have brought limitless merit and virtue ends up bringing no merit and virtue at all."

I remember the first time Dharma Master Xu Lang taught us to chant the *Medicine Master Repentance*. I don't know whether it had something to do with my past lives, but as soon as I looked at the text, I knew how it should be chanted. Sometimes the Dharma assemblies were led by Dharma Master Xu Lang, and sometimes by Dharma Master Heng Yue and Dharma Master Heng Zhi, with Guo Song, Guo Su, and me helping. Later on, I served as cantor for most of the Dharma ceremonies. In Hong Kong we bowed the *Great Compassion Repentance* and the *Medicine Master Repentance*. Most of the people who took refuge with the Master in Hong Kong spoke Cantonese, so apart from the texts of praise, which were chanted in Mandarin (Putonghua), the sutra texts and the repentance texts were all recited in Cantonese. Nowadays repentance ceremonies in Hong Kong are all chanted in Cantonese; that was something we started in the early days.

I once asked Dharma Master Xu Lang why his chanting voice was so beautiful. He said, "For this I have to thank Guanshiyin Bodhisattva! I left the home-life at 15, and when I heard a cantor whose chanting was extremely beautiful, I admired him tremendously. An elder Dharma Master learned what was on my mind and said to me, 'If you want a good chanting voice, go and pray to Guanshiyin Bodhisattva. If a monastic doesn't know how to chant, how is he going to make a living in the future?' You see, ours was a small temple, and the monastics mostly lived on the income from reciting sutras and bowing repentances for people. This elder Dharma Master said, 'Go buy a large bell. In front of the Buddha, first strike it three times, then recite Guanyin Bodhisattva's name once. After that, strike the bell once, bow once, and sincerely pray to Guanyin Bodhisattva to give you a good voice.' So I did as the elder Dharma Master instructed, and I kept bowing like this until I received full ordination at 18. One day the cantor was sick, so the abbot had no choice but to have me stand in for him. Because I had always paid very close attention to chanting, I completed the ceremony without a hitch. There were no loudspeakers in those days, yet everyone at a Dharma assembly of several hundred people could hear my voice just the same. From then on, whenever there was a big Dharma assembly, people would come and ask me to serve as cantor."

Elder Xu Lang was never the least bit careless about the chanting and recitation in Buddhist ceremonies. When he presided over the "Yoga Flaming Mouth" ceremony, there were a great many spiritual responses, which people passed on by word of mouth. At the Opening-the-Light ceremony of Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island, the Venerable Master invited Elder Xu Lang to help with the Buddhist ceremonies. One evening, during the "Yoga Flaming Mouth" ceremony, many people saw and heard the spirits of the dead and the beings of the ghost realm hurrying there to be rescued.

In 1995, when the Master entered perfect stillness at Long Beach Monastery in Los Angeles, Elder Xu Lang, considering it his bounden duty, guided the arrangements for the ceremonies so that they could be carried out in accordance with the Dharma and with proper ritual, and he performed a "Three Periods of Mindfulness" ceremony for the Master (one of the supreme practices of the Pure Land Dharma door). Afterward, at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas, Elder Dharma Master Xu Lang and Elder Master Ming Yang jointly presided over the Venerable Master's cremation ceremony.

[Appendix] In Respectful Memory of Elder Master Xu Lang (excerpted from the February and March 2010 issues of *Vajra Bodhi Sea*)

The Venerable Master's old friend Elder Master Xu Lang passed away in Los Angeles, U.S.A., on September 29, 2009, at the age of 90; with his passing the Buddhist world lost a Good and Wise Advisor. The Venerable Master said that his chanting and recitation were among the very best in China, and that he was universally acknowledged as the "King of Praises." Elder Master Xu Lang was born on April 27, 1919, in Liaoning Province, China. He left the home-life at 15 and received full ordination at 18; he had been a monastic for 75 years and a fully ordained monk for 72. Following Elder Xu Lang's final instructions, his disciples did not publish a notice in the newspapers, did not send out an obituary, and still less spent lavishly on an elaborate funeral. From the following memorial couplet offered by the Dharma Realm Buddhist Association, one can see that Elder Xu Lang's conduct and deportment are a model for us. We sincerely hope that his example will inspire those who come after, so that the Buddhadharma will flourish greatly.

> Radiant, radiant, the light of wisdom has left behind a legacy of love;  
> though on this day it has faded,  
> the light of wisdom ever remains,  
> shining everywhere, even beyond the Saha world. [Tr.: the two lines of this couplet open with the two characters of the Elder's name: Xu, "rising sun," and Lang, "bright, clear."]
>
> <small>旭旭慧光曾遺愛  此日雖銷  慧光常存  娑婆界外尤普照</small>

> Clear, clear, the Brahma sound long circled the rafters;  
> though this present body has passed away,  
> the Brahma sound seems still to be here,  
> heard from afar even within Maitreya's Court.
>
> <small>朗朗梵音久繞梁  今身已滅  梵音宛在  彌勒院中亦遙聞</small>

## 36. Guo Shi Takes Refuge

To teach and transform the Westernized young people of Hong Kong, the Venerable Master used expedient means to guide them and change their bad habits and faults, hoping to nurture Buddhist talent with proper knowledge and views. Upasika Tan Guo Shi grew up in Westernized Hong Kong. She came to know the Venerable Master when she was in primary school, and she took refuge with the Venerable Master during the Guanyin session in Western Bliss Gardens' second year...

Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

To me, the Master was like one of my own family, an elder; that's why I always went to Western Bliss Gardens after school. At school I would finish my homework first and then leave school a bit early to go to Western Bliss Gardens to see the Master. That way, every day I had almost an hour to read books aloud to the Master or listen to the Master tell stories. The Master had me read *Biographies of Eminent Monks* and *Stories of Jigong*. The book that made the deepest impression on me, and also the one I liked best, was *National Master Yulin*. After reading stories, I would go home for dinner.

When I first met the Master, I didn't take refuge right away. Even after I had learned to play the Dharma instruments, and even helped out at the Master's refuge ceremonies, I still never took refuge. To me it was just a formality; I didn't know that I needed to take refuge. Since he was my parents' teacher, I should properly have called him "Grand Master"; but to me he wasn't a Grand Master, he was "our" Master, so I always called him "Shifu."

Although I studied at a Christian school and could recite the whole Bible from memory, I didn't believe in Catholicism or Protestant Christianity at all. That was because I had asked my teacher: if God created human beings, why are there so many poor people and ugly people? Why did he create people so different from one another? Right up until I graduated, my teacher never answered my question. My elder sister went to a Catholic school, and she too never came to believe in Catholicism, because some of her questions never got answers. When I met the Master, I asked the Master the same question, and the Master's answer completely convinced me: "Whatever we do, that is what we will get."

I also asked the Master, "If I had past lives, then what was I like in my past lives?" The Master did answer me. I didn't ask these questions directly; instead I wrote a little poem whose last line was "May I ask about my roots and origins?" The Master said to me, "I've read your poem. Do you really want to know your roots and origins?" I said, "Of course! You've said we have all had many, many lives. I want to know what I used to be." He told me what I had been in a past life, but he said, "I've told you about your past life, but only you may know it. In the future you're going to help me; you will be my right and left hand." I said, "No problem!" At that time I was always thinking up very striking, unusual questions, and I was very competitive, so that's how I answered the Venerable Master. I never thought about taking refuge, and the Master never brought it up either.

In the sixth lunar month of 1953, Western Bliss Gardens held a Guanyin session. When I went to Western Bliss Gardens, I saw the Master lying on his bed, not saying a word, perfectly quiet. Some people were kneeling beside the Master; my mother, my elder sister, and Guo Song and her mother were kneeling there too. Some of them were crying, and some looked very sad. I asked my sister in a low voice, "What's happened? Why are you all kneeling here?" "The Master isn't eating. He won't eat anything; for the last few days he's only been drinking water. The Master is going to die!" Nobody dared say anything, and that silence was terribly sad. I ran to the Master's bedside, knelt, and said to the Master, "Shifu, why aren't you eating?"

"The people of Hong Kong are too hard to teach and transform. They're so stubborn, they won't listen to teaching, and I don't know how to teach them anymore. I'm thinking of leaving," the Master said.

"Shifu, I remember when you were speaking the Dharma, you said you wanted to save everyone. You want to cross over living beings!"

"Cross over what living beings? They're so obstinate, there's no way I can cross them over."

"Didn't you teach me that no matter what difficulties I meet, it doesn't matter, I must find a way to overcome them? Why are you giving up?"

"But no one is helping me."

"I'll help you." I blurted it out at once; even I didn't know why I said it.

"Is that so? You'll help me?"

"Yes. If you eat, I'll help you."

"But you're still an outsider. How can you help me?" At the time I didn't know what "outsider" meant, so I said, "What did you say?"

"I said you're an outsider."

"What's an outsider?"

"It means you're still standing outside the door."

"That's easy. I'm standing inside the room right now; I've already come in."

"No, that's not what I mean. I mean you haven't taken refuge yet, so you're still an outsider."

"Haven't I already been calling you 'Shifu'? I think taking refuge is just a formality; it doesn't mean anything."

"No! Without a compass and a square, you cannot make circles and squares."

"That's easy. I can become an insider: I'll just take refuge. If I take refuge, will you eat and not leave?"

In the end the Master did eat, and that same afternoon (the nineteenth day of the sixth lunar month) I formally took refuge.

Guo Jun took refuge before me, and Guo Mi took refuge after me. The Master composed a verse for each of us, working our Dharma names into them. I know that Guo Song had one, but I don't remember what the Master wrote for her. (Editor's note: The Venerable Master presented his refuge disciple Huang Guo Song with the lines "With the plum as the cause, prajna blossoms; with the pine as the fruit, Bodhi is certified." [Tr.: the lines pair yin, "cause," with guo, "fruit," and the plum (mei, from her name Huang Jiemei) with the pine (song).]) Using my Dharma name, the Master wrote this verse: "Vigorously cultivate the One Vehicle, / and be a model for ten thousand generations. / Universally transform your fellow beings, / so that together all ascend to the ground of Buddhahood. / Be a model! Be a model!" [Tr.: shi, the second character of her Dharma name Guo Shi, means "model, pattern."] What did it mean? The Master said he wanted me to set an example for everyone; I'm ashamed that I've never managed to do it! This treasured calligraphy of the Master's is still enshrined in the Buddha hall in my home today. After taking refuge, I often went to Western Bliss Gardens, and whatever the Master told me to do, I did my best to do, such as learning the Buddhist ceremonial procedures and helping out at Dharma assemblies.

Once, the Master took me to see Dharma Master Mao Feng. Before he left the home-life, Dharma Master Mao Feng had been a xiucai, a scholar who had passed the imperial examinations, and poetry flowed from his lips whenever he opened his mouth. After asking my name, the elder Dharma Master wrote two couplet scrolls using my name and my Dharma name: [Tr.: the lines begin with the characters of her birth name, Mu and Jie (Tan Mujie), and of her Dharma name, Guo and Shi.]

> First line: Admiring the sages and admiring the worthies, become a superior person;  
> purifying the mind and purifying the will, become a lofty one.
>
> <small>上聯：慕聖慕賢為上士，潔心潔志作高人。</small>

> Second line: If you can truly make the great Bodhi vow,  
> you will fittingly be a model teacher for humans and gods.
>
> <small>下聯：果能發大菩提願，式合人天模范師。</small>

Once, when the Master came to my home to receive a meal offering, he saw that I had hung these two couplet scrolls in the house, and he joked with me:

"Dharma Master Mao Feng thinks so highly of you, he's praised you all the way up to the heavens and made you a guiding teacher of humans and gods!"

"Shifu, didn't you say that when heavenly beings have used up their blessings, they fall? I don't want to go up to heaven and then fall back down," I answered.

"Don't worry. If you drop down (fall), I'll catch you (save you)," the Master said.

"All right, you have to keep your word. If I fall, you have to come and save me," I said.

We often joked like this. I learned a great deal from the Master, and I respected him very, very much, even though I sometimes joked with him.

## 37. Praying for Rain to End a Drought

Wherever the Venerable Master went, places that had no water came to have water. Once there was a drought in Hong Kong, and it did not rain for six months in a row. Many Way-places, monasteries, and viharas were praying for rain, but the drought still went on...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

One year Hong Kong had a terrible drought. From spring to summer it didn't rain for six months, so water use was restricted every day. All the Buddhist Way-places, monasteries, lecture halls, and viharas were praying for rain. With so many people praying for rain, I didn't get involved. I've never been willing to meddle in other people's business. With so many people praying for rain, their strength had to be greater than mine alone, so I stayed out of it. But after five or six months, I couldn't stay out of it even if I wanted to. Why? Because the water at Western Bliss Gardens was about to dry up too. Very little water was coming down from the mountain, and it had such a strong taste of sulfur that it was practically undrinkable. At that point I gave Liu Guo Juan an order: "Within three days, you have to pray for rain for me. You must recite the Buddha's name every day. If it doesn't rain, then don't you ever come to see me again!" Actually she had no special abilities; I said that to her on purpose.

Now Liu Guo Juan really panicked. What she feared most was not being able to see the Master; she could do without seeing anything else, but she had to come and see the Master every day. She was really very simple and honest. She took a string of recitation beads and recited the Buddha's name for all she was worth, even forgetting to eat, for fear that it wouldn't rain. I said to Cheng Guo Cheng, "They've been praying for rain for several months, and it still hasn't rained. Now I've given Liu Guo Juan three days to pray for rain; I figure it will rain in about two and a half days." She recited for two and a half days, and sure enough, it started pouring rain, and so Hong Kong lifted its water restrictions.

As soon as this rain came, all the temples and Buddha halls put advertisements in the newspapers, every one of them coming forward to claim credit and saying it was all thanks to them. This one said, "Our repentance bowing was efficacious; we're the ones who brought the rain." That one said their Buddha recitation was efficacious and that their Buddha hall had brought the rain. In reality, they had no idea how the rain came! All the Buddha halls got credit; only Western Bliss Gardens didn't claim any credit or report any virtue. Nobody knew that the rain had come from my old disciple's Buddha recitation, either. Why? Because she didn't put it in the news; we didn't publicize how this rain came about. Still, if this made them happy, I rejoiced along with them. I'm not saying these things at all to show off any power of my own, but to explain the principle that "where the mind is sincere, there is a response."

Why did I give Liu Guo Juan three days to bring down rain? I wanted this old disciple to recite the Buddha's name more, to pray for rain on everyone's behalf, and to accumulate a bit more merit and virtue. I'll tell you, never mind three days: if I told the heavens to rain that very day, the rain would come. Why? Because I have ten dragon disciples. If these ten dragons aren't lazy, whichever one I tell to make rain will make rain. Things like this have happened many times, so you must be sincere in your cultivation, and these dragon disciples of mine will all help you; if you don't cultivate the Way, they won't protect and support you.

Another time, during the first lunar month, we were making offerings to the heavens. Because there were too many people and not enough room inside Western Bliss Gardens, we set up the tables outside on the open ground. Just as we were about to make the offerings to the heavens, clouds rolled in as if it were going to rain, and everyone said, "This won't do, this won't do! We have to move into the Buddha hall; it's going to rain out here!" At the time I thought, it can't rain now, so I told them, "Don't move. It won't rain. If we're making offerings to the heavens and the heavens still rain on us, that would be a complete lack of response! We make offerings to the heavens, and the heavens weep anyway? There's no such principle."

In short, as their teacher I'm just this dictatorial, this autocratic: whatever I say goes. If what I say is right, it's right, and if it's not right, it's still right. So they didn't dare move. The offering to the heavens took about three hours. Because I didn't allow it to rain, the sky was just covered with dark clouds, and no rain fell. Only after we had finished making offerings to the heavens and carried the incense burners, candlesticks, and tables into the Buddha hall did it immediately start pouring outside. So some of my refuge disciples in Hong Kong had great faith in me; they knew that what I said was very efficacious and always came true.

Today I manifested a small spiritual power again. Early this morning I said to Guo Xiu (the American Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Chih), "Guo Xiu, go tell the deity, tell this God, that it's not allowed to rain before four o'clock this afternoon. If it rains, I'm going to lose my temper!" You all know that, sure enough, it didn't rain before four o'clock today. Guo Xiu, I didn't tell you a lie, did I? You heard it with your own ears! What I did doesn't count as a spiritual power; I haven't got a single power, not one. It's just that when I say something, it does it.

**[Addendum 1]** In the summer of the jiawu year (1954), since the nineteenth day of the sixth lunar month is the anniversary of Guanyin Bodhisattva's attainment of the Way, the Venerable Master held a seven-day Dharma assembly at Western Bliss Gardens (from the thirteenth to the nineteenth). On the eighteenth and nineteenth, as many as several hundred devotees came to Western Bliss Gardens. It was the height of summer; the weather was scorching hot and dry, and to save water the Hong Kong government supplied water for only three hours a day, so the people complained bitterly about the water shortage. Yet the pond at Western Bliss Gardens was the same as always, without the slightest sign of drought; water kept flowing in without end, fully supplying several hundred people with drinking water and everything else they needed. It could well be called "a miracle among miracles."

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Mei Miaoling recounted:

When I was little, about 8 to 10 years old, I went to Western Bliss Gardens with my mother (Yuan Guo Zhen), and the Master would always bring out lots of things for me to eat. Once when it was raining heavily, my mother brought me up the mountain while I was sick. When it was time for the meal, the Master told the kitchen not to serve the meal yet; only after we arrived did the Master begin to eat. Every time we went up to Western Bliss Gardens, my mother would tell the Master beforehand when we would next come up. Several times the Master told my mother to come up the mountain an hour early, and it turned out that an hour later it started pouring rain. The Master always gave me shiitake mushrooms to eat; the Master would chew them up first and then give them to me.

## 38. The Typhoons Vanish

During the Venerable Master's 12 years in Hong Kong, typhoon warnings were issued again and again, yet each time, just as the storm's circle was closing in, it would suddenly change course. In 1960, when the Venerable Master left Hong Kong for Australia, a powerful typhoon struck Hong Kong and caused heavy losses...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I'm not trying to scare you, and I'm not saying how great I am, but the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division, and all the ghosts and spirits, listen to me! At Western Bliss Gardens I planted lots of flowers, plants, and trees: papayas, bamboo, and fruit trees, and the papayas they bore were very sweet. The following spring, that is, the spring of my third year in Hong Kong, just when the papayas were ripe and the bamboo was at its most luxuriant, a typhoon suddenly came and tore the papaya trees, the pine trees, and the bamboo up by the roots, leaving everything scattered in a wreck.

I lost my temper, pointed my finger at the sky, and said, "Jade Emperor! If this typhoon has anything to do with you, I have something to say to you now: for as long as I live in Hong Kong, I won't allow you to blow any more typhoons. If Hong Kong gets hit by another typhoon, I'm telling you straight, I won't be polite about it! Later on, once I leave Hong Kong, then I won't concern myself with it!" After I said that, I thought, "Hey, how could I act like this? Losing my temper at Heaven is not right!" But even though I felt it wasn't right, the words had already been spoken.

Very strange! From then on, during the more than ten years I lived in Hong Kong, Hong Kong was never hit by a typhoon. Every time the observatory said a typhoon was going to strike Hong Kong, the typhoon, which had been heading straight for Hong Kong, would suddenly turn and go somewhere else when it was still 20 miles, or 15 miles, from Hong Kong. It was just that strange! But I had been in Sydney, Australia, for only one month when Hong Kong was hit by a typhoon: signboards flew all over the sky, a great many houses were smashed, and the losses were incalculable. In 1962 I went to America, and that summer a record-breaking great storm came; the mud on the hillsides of Sha Tin slid down too, and more than 150 people were killed or injured. How such a thing could happen, nobody knew.

These are things of the past. What I've said is absolutely true; believe it or not, as you like!

Because I don't tell lies, Heaven won't oppose what I say either; this is my own experience from the past. I hope everyone can refrain from killing, stealing, sexual misconduct, lying, and drinking alcohol. If you do, the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division, and all the Dharma-protecting spirits, will protect and support you, and everything will go as you wish and be auspicious.

**[Addendum 1]** As recorded in Wikipedia:

In the 1960s Hong Kong was struck by a number of severe typhoons, which caused major economic losses and heavy casualties. These led the Hong Kong Observatory to hoist the No. 10 Hurricane Signal as many as six times. Among them, Typhoon Mary, which struck Hong Kong in June 1960, left 45 dead and 127 injured and destroyed about 10,000 homes. Typhoon Wanda, which struck Hong Kong in 1962, caused 130 deaths and left 72,000 people homeless. It set Hong Kong's records for the lowest air pressure, the highest hourly mean wind speed, and the highest gust speed, and was one of the most disastrous typhoons ever to strike Hong Kong.

**[Addendum 2]** As reported at the time by Hong Kong's *Sing Pao*:

For Typhoon Wanda, the Observatory hoisted the No. 10 signal on September 1, 1962; it was the most powerful typhoon to strike Hong Kong since records began after the Second World War. Wanda brought serious destruction to the whole of Hong Kong. According to the authorities' subsequent tally, it caused 183 deaths and 388 injuries, and 108 people were missing. Because many residents at that time lived in squatter huts and rooftop wooden huts, the force of the typhoon completely destroyed these poorly built dwellings, leaving as many as 72,000 people homeless. The damage was most severe in the Sha Tin district, where about 3,000 squatter huts collapsed, and the roads were strewn with debris, corrugated iron sheets, scaffolding, broken windows, and the like. Wanda also set several meteorological records, including the records for the highest gust and the highest hourly mean wind speed, which no other typhoon has broken to this day.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

One year I went back to Hong Kong and spoke the Dharma for five days at a monastery. Since I hadn't been back to Hong Kong for a long time, returning felt like seeing my own family. Things I had never said while I was in Hong Kong, this time I wanted to talk over very sincerely with my elders and brothers there. I talked about how, during the more than ten years I lived in Hong Kong, Hong Kong was never hit by a typhoon. I said it honestly at the time, without the slightest lie, but then someone got people to write articles in magazines saying that what I said was not true. When I read them I just smiled. Who would have thought that even telling the truth would get you slandered? But this too is "the Dharma is naturally thus": "All dharmas arise from conditions; all dharmas cease from conditions." So I just treated it as wind passing my ears. But some of my well-educated refuge disciples were very unhappy and wrote letters to me, asking my permission to start a war of words with these people, so I wrote a few lines of verse for these people:

> Why must right and wrong be argued?  
> Given time, what is true and what is false become clear of themselves.
>
> <small>是非何須辯，真偽久自明。</small>

> The wise see what is true and real;  
> the foolish practice falsehood and pretense.
>
> <small>智者見真實，愚者行虛偽。</small>

> The good learn from the Bodhisattvas;  
> the evil dare even to scold the Buddha.
>
> <small>善者學菩薩，惡者敢罵佛。</small>

> With impartial, great kindness and compassion,  
> gather in all beings with consciousness everywhere.
>
> <small>平等大慈悲，普攝諸含識。</small>

"Why must right and wrong be argued?": If you say something is right, he can say it's wrong; if you say it's wrong, he'll say it's right. There's no way to get right and wrong sorted out. The evil world of the five turbidities is just this chaotic; good and evil are muddled together and can't be told apart. Only with the eye that discerns dharmas can you recognize them, so there's no need to go arguing over right and wrong. "Given time, what is true and what is false become clear of themselves": whether something is true or false, after a long time everyone will naturally know whether it was true or false. "The wise see what is true and real": people with wisdom see only what is true and real. "The foolish practice falsehood and pretense": foolish, ignorant people only go around deceiving others and promoting themselves everywhere.

"The good learn from the Bodhisattvas": although good people are not Bodhisattvas, they are willing to learn the Bodhisattvas' spirit of kindness, compassion, joy, and giving. "The evil dare even to scold the Buddha": never mind a poor monk, evil people would dare to scold even the Buddha to his face. Therefore, "With impartial, great kindness and compassion, gather in all beings with consciousness everywhere": we use a mind of impartiality, a mind of great kindness and compassion, and a mind of great joy and giving to gather in all creatures that wriggle and all that have spiritual awareness, all living beings.

After I gave these lines of verse, the war of words was quietly defused. It wasn't that I was afraid; it's just that there's no need to stir up so much right and wrong within Buddhism. So after that I seldom went back to Hong Kong, so as not to arouse people's jealousy and have them take the false for true and the true for false.

I'm telling you, whoever you may be, don't praise your teacher. As long as you don't scold your teacher or lose your temper at me, that's enough. There's no need to flatter me, and no need to praise me, because whether I'm good or not, I myself know perfectly clearly. I don't need others to tell me, and I don't want others praising me in any way. Your praising me like this may not seem to matter much, but some Buddhist figures in Hong Kong will write articles scolding me again, saying, "This Dharma Master and his disciples are putting on a show together! The disciples praise and praise their teacher, and the teacher praises and praises his disciples; they praise each other, putting on a show!"

Question: Since the Dharma Master was able to keep all typhoons from striking Hong Kong when he lived there, can he now stop all typhoons for the residents of Hong Kong?

Venerable Master: You should ask that nobody in all of Hong Kong will ever die!

Question: Most people in Hong Kong are anxious about what lies ahead. Could the Venerable Master please put the minds of the people of Hong Kong at ease?

Venerable Master: Rather than looking to the road ahead, better to look to the road behind. Whatever is behind is what will be ahead; and whatever is ahead, the same will be behind. "Originally nothing is wrong under heaven; ordinary people stir up trouble for themselves." "Upon those with virtue, blessings descend; upon those without virtue, calamities descend."

## 39. The Crab Harbors a Grievance

In the summer of 1953 (the guisi year), the lay Buddhist Dong Guo Qi and the Dharma Master who was manager of Chi Lin Nunnery at Diamond Hill in Kowloon respectfully invited the Venerable Master to lecture on the *Earth Store Sutra*. At that time a crab of flesh had grown on the manager's finger, causing her unspeakable pain, and she asked the Venerable Master for help...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In Hong Kong I once came across something. In the summer of 1953 I was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Chi Lin Nunnery. The manager was a disciple of Elder Hsu, and she had great merit and virtue from supporting Elder Hsu. No matter where Elder Hsu was establishing a Way-place, she would help him seek donations in Hong Kong, and she raised a great deal of money. She couldn't read, but she was a very good talker, and laypeople in general trusted her a great deal. Before she left the home-life, she worked for others, cooking meals, and she often bought chickens and ducks and brought them home to kill. Once she bought some crabs and brought them back, intending to prepare them for her employer to eat. One of the crabs clamped onto her middle finger with its pincer and wouldn't let go, so she chopped the pincer off with a knife and then cooked the crab to be eaten. Not long afterward, her finger hurt terribly from morning to night; the pain was simply killing her.

Later she bowed the *Water Repentance* for seven days at Fu Yung Shan. Although this crab of flesh became a little smaller, it still swelled and hurt. When I was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Chi Lin Nunnery, she knew I wasn't competing with her for Dharma affinities, so at that time she didn't yet have any jealousy toward me, and she told me about the causes and conditions of this crab and asked me to help her find a way to resolve the grievance. She asked me, "My hand often hurts. How can it get better?" I said, "Just go and repent!" She said, "I've bowed this repentance and bowed that repentance, I've bowed lots of repentances, but none of them has repented away this Big Brother Crab of mine." She called this crab "Big Brother Crab." "Big Brother Crab just won't leave!" I said, "It's going to leave you now. Have it take refuge, and you'll be fine." When she heard this, she signed it up to take refuge, and I gave it the Dharma name "Sheng Luo" [Tr.: "Sagely Conch"]. From then on, her Big Brother Crab ran off, and her hand stopped hurting. After her crab ailment was cured, she saw that her group of devotees all believed in me, so she began spreading rumors and slandering me. That's just how Buddhist circles are, and that's why they can't develop.

Even so, this person had some affinity with the Buddha and some affinity with ghosts too. How is it that she had some affinity with the Buddha and also some affinity with ghosts? If she had no affinity with the Buddha, she wouldn't have met Elder Hsu, nor could she have become a great Dharma protector supporting Elder Hsu. And how is it that she also had some affinity with ghosts? When she was cooking for people, she beat a cat to death, and this cat ghost kept coming after her to take her life, making her whole body ache from head to toe and keeping her sick from morning to night. Wanting to save her life, she said, "Big Brother Cat! If you make me suffer to death like this, this debt between us still won't be settled. Why don't you help me win the sweepstakes instead? Then I'll have money to rescue you so that you can be reborn. Wouldn't that be better?" When she said this, the cat ghost did as she said: she went to Macau and bought a sweepstakes ticket, and she really did win the grand prize. That's how she made her fortune. She used to sell plain congee; later she opened a vegetarian food shop in Hong Kong, and after she made her fortune she left the home-life. She did very well, and so she became the manager of Chi Lin Nunnery. That's why I say she had affinity with the Buddha and affinity with ghosts as well.

## 40. The Acharya Resolves a Difficulty

The Venerable Master was ordinarily frugal in providing for himself and was never willing to spend a cent on himself; yet for Bhikshus who truly cultivated, he would come to the rescue in times of danger and never begrudged his help. On the other hand, toward Bhikshus who did not truly cultivate and came begging, he was not the least bit polite...

In the spring of 1952 (the renchen year), Dharma Master Qin Jue bought a house at Diamond Hill in Kowloon. He had lived there only a little over a year when the landowner suddenly forced the house to be demolished and the land returned. Meeting with this heavy loss, Dharma Master Qin Jue was quite distressed, and his spirits sank unusually low. One night at 11 o'clock, the Dharma Master happened to be reading the "Contemplation of the Four Noble Truths" chapter of the *Treatise on the Middle Way*: "All dharmas produced by causes and conditions, I say, are empty; they are also false names, and they are also the meaning of the Middle Way. There has never been a single dharma that did not arise from causes and conditions; therefore, of all dharmas, there is none that is not empty." He contemplated its meaning and silently recited this passage over and over to relieve the afflictions he was mired in, and without realizing it he fell asleep and had a dream. In the dream he strolled into a mountain forest. While he was wandering about there, a violent storm of wind and rain suddenly descended, and thunder cracked across the sky, deafeningly loud. As he stood frightened and at a loss, a monk whose whole body emitted purple-burnished golden light descended from the sky, and the wind and rain instantly vanished; everything became still all at once. Dharma Master Qin Jue thought it was a manifestation of Guanyin and hurriedly knelt and bowed. Only when the monk came closer did he recognize him as one of the honored certifying Acharyas [Tr.: Sanskrit for "teacher"; at an ordination, one of the senior monks who confer or certify the precepts] from his ordination: Dharma Master To Lun. In the dream, the Venerable Master told him, "Don't worry. I'll help you resolve all your difficulties!" At that point in the dream, he woke up.

Not long afterward, Dharma Master Qin Jue found a plot of land and was preparing to build another house, but he was short of money. At that time the Venerable Master was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra* at Chi Lin Nunnery. When he heard about this, he immediately took out 1,500 dollars to help him acquire a new house, so that he had a place to live.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

At Tung Po Tor there was an old monastic who told people he was in closed retreat, yet he often went out to all sorts of places seeking alms, saying, "There's no oil in my retreat room to light the lamp. Won't you give me a little oil?" He would also say, "I've run out of incense. Why don't you buy me a little incense?" Or, "Dharma Master, please be compassionate and give me a little money so that I can buy some rice to cook a meal!" In this way he collected quite a lot of money, and he even thought of coming to Western Bliss Gardens to swindle me. When he came to my place, he put on his robe and his sash, fully unfolded his sitting cloth, and bowed down to me; then he knelt upright with his palms joined and said:

"Dharma Master, please be compassionate and save me. Let me become enlightened!"

"Why are you carrying on like this?" I said.

"I'm in closed retreat over at Tung Po Tor. As soon as I sat in meditation, I saw you come. You emitted great light and floated gently down through empty space to land in front of me. You struck me three times with the incense board, and I became enlightened. Today I've come here to fully unfold my sitting cloth before you in person and bow to you with nine kowtows. Please strike me three more times with the incense board so that I can have a bit more enlightenment!" he said.

Why was he doing this? He was thinking, "Dharma Master To Lun surely likes being flattered too. If someone says he's a Buddha, he'll be pleased and will certainly give some money as a reward." But never in his wildest dreams did he imagine that this time I really would give him three blows of the incense board. As he knelt there, I glared at him and knocked him over with one kick, and I said:

"Get out of here right now! How dare you come here and try to cheat me!" But I saw that this man was older than I was; his hair was white, and his beard was white too. I didn't know whether he had gone white from thinking about "before" (qian, which sounds the same as "money") or from thinking about "after," but I knew he didn't think about the present. I felt a bit bad toward him, so I took 5 dollars out of my pocket, threw it on the floor, and said:

"You want to get enlightened? You don't want to get enlightened; all you want is money! Now I'll give you an enlightenment about money! I don't have any more than this, just 5 dollars for your bus fare. Take it and get out of here fast, and don't you ever come here again!" He looked at me, took the 5 dollars, and ran off.

He had heard that Qin Jue dreamed of me and said he saw me emitting light and so on and so forth, and he wanted to copy him. He didn't know that copying is useless! The reason Qin Jue had said that was that earlier he had sought alms from me and I had given him HK$1,500. In those days everything was very cheap, and 1,500 dollars was worth a great deal then. This old monastic knew about it and wanted to use the same method to cheat me. Am I someone who can be cheated? So I gave him a kick to make him wake up: "When I kick you, you'll wake up! You'll wake up to the fact that a kick landing on your body hurts!" So cultivators should not tell lies. True is true, and false is false.

**[Addendum]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

A letter came from Bhikshu Qin Jue. He told the Master about something that had happened about nine years before: he had dreamed that he walked to a remote, secluded place. Rain was pouring down at the time, and he was terrified. Suddenly he saw a very majestic Bodhisattva, who said to him, "Don't be afraid. I am here." When he looked closely, it was none other than the Venerable Master!

## 41. The Wealthy Merchant Goes Back on His Word

In the sixth month of the guisi year (1953), a certain Zhang, suffering from incurable cancer, begged the Venerable Master to save him. The Venerable Master told him that he must take refuge with the Triple Jewel, and urged him to give medicines and bolts of cloth to establish affinities with the Bhikshus and Bhikshunis, so as to dissolve his karmic offenses...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In Hong Kong I had a bad refuge disciple. How was he bad? This refuge disciple had harmed people. His surname was Zhang, and his family ran a Western pharmacy. Who knows how much money he had; it was more than could be counted. How did he get his money? By harming people. After Japan surrendered, there were contagious diseases everywhere on the Chinese mainland and epidemics were rampant, because "after a great war there are bound to be years of calamity." He went into partnership with a very rich man to do business: they went abroad to purchase Western medicines and brought them back to Hong Kong to sell.

He made a lot of money, and when he had made money, he went and bought more medicine. On top of that, he also made imitation Western medicines and sold them in China, and from then on he was rich. After he got rich, what do you think happened? Probably because of grievance being repaid with grievance, he got cancer. At that time there were six doctors in Hong Kong who were the most famous, and all of them concluded that he would definitely die within 100 days, saying that there was no way to cure his illness. So he put a notice in the newspapers publicly begging for someone to save his life, saying that whoever could keep him alive would be given US$200,000. At that time US$200,000 was a considerable sum, but still nobody answered; nobody had a way to save his life.

Someone asked me if there was anything to be done. I said I would have to see the person to know. A certain Ye brought Zhang to Western Bliss Gardens to see me and asked me how his illness could be cured. I said, "If you want not to die, that's very easy; it's not a difficult problem at all. Since you've gotten this illness, first you must take refuge with the Triple Jewel, and then you must make offerings to the Triple Jewel. If you do this, perhaps your illness will get better!" He wanted to take refuge with me right then. I said, "If you take refuge with me, well, I don't have much virtue. But if you're willing to take refuge, I won't refuse you."

He came to take refuge on the eighteenth day of the fifth (lunar) month. During the refuge ceremony, he saw Guanyin Bodhisattva anoint the crown of his head with sweet dew, and he immediately felt much better physically. Right then, kneeling before the Buddha, he also made a vow: if he didn't die within 100 days, he would give me US$200,000 to build a monastery. After that, I told him to make offerings to the Triple Jewel and to establish affinities by doing good deeds. At that time there were probably two or three thousand Bhikshus and Bhikshunis who had fled to Hong Kong as refugees; none of them had anywhere to stay, and they didn't have enough clothes to wear. So I told him to give every monastic a bolt of American cotton cloth, enough to make two sets of monastic clothing, and also to offer each person HK$20. I said, "If you can do this, I guarantee you will certainly not die!" He agreed at the time.

There were many monks in Hong Kong with great reputations. In particular, when many of the elder Dharma Masters heard that Zhang had taken refuge with me, these monks all started displaying their spiritual powers. What spiritual powers did they display? They went through friends and relatives to talk to Zhang, wanting him to come to their temples to create merit and virtue. One elder Dharma Master knew that the wife of one of his refuge disciples was a relative of Zhang's, so he had her go and plead his case: "This Dharma Master To Lun is nothing much! This elder Dharma Master is his teacher; why don't you take refuge with his teacher?" When Zhang heard this, he thought, "This is his teacher, so of course he must be even more brilliant than he is." So he went to the elder Dharma Master's temple to create merit and virtue, and he created some merit and virtue at other temples too. This reputable elder Dharma Master had never been one to perform rescue ceremonies for people, but because Zhang gave him 5,000 dollars, he bowed the *Medicine Master Repentance* for him for seven times seven, or 49, days.

All six doctors had concluded that Zhang would surely die within 100 days. This time, when he passed 100 days without dying, some monks said, "It was our bowing repentances for you that made you well!" That elder Dharma Master said, "It was our sutra recitation for you that made you well!" Another elder Dharma Master also said, "We prayed for you before the Buddha every day; that's what made you well!" At this point every Dharma Master had a share of the credit. Only I, over here, had no credit at all, and I didn't care to go and explain to him what had really happened.

When I was building Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island, Zhang had a worker bring me 200 dollars for building Cixing Monastery. I picked up the 200 dollars and, without even looking at it, threw it out the door. I said, "Go back and tell him I won't use his filthy money. This money is too unclean! I don't just take people's money casually." Afterward, some monks slandered me: "What kind of monastic is that? Someone gives you money, and you go and throw it out the door right in front of them. That's wrong!" Zhang then asked the elder Dharma Master who had been currying favor with him to put in a good word with me, wanting to give me some more money. I said, "My construction work is finished now, so I don't need money. If he has money, he can create some other merit and virtue. There are so many Dharma Masters and so many temples; he can do it wherever he is willing to go."

## 42. Blessings Bestowed on Monks and Nuns

Zhang had earlier promised the Venerable Master that he would make offerings to the monastics of Hong Kong, but after recovering from his illness he went back on his word and reduced the amount of the offering, cutting it to 5 dollars and one bolt of cheap cloth per person. Faced with Zhang's breach of faith, the Venerable Master did not say a single word...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Originally I had told Zhang to give every monastic 20 dollars, but those elder Dharma Masters had courted his favor and drawn all that away; he went back on his word and gave only 5 dollars. He did give a bolt of cloth, but it wasn't American cotton cloth. Still, when I said to give each monastic a bolt of cloth, I hadn't said how big, so that part could still be justified. Originally a bolt of American cotton cloth was enough to make two robes (long gowns) and two sets of trousers and jackets, but he switched to Shanghai Dachen gray cloth, and there wasn't enough cloth to make them. Since I had told all the monastics in advance that someone was going to offer each person 20 dollars, and now he was giving only 5 dollars, what could I do? I never force people to do things; if he wouldn't put it up, so be it. So I went and borrowed money and added another 10 dollars. In addition, two big companies, Daguang and Daming, put in 25 cents each, so with difficulty we pieced together 15 dollars and 50 cents to offer to each Dharma Master. Some monastics, not knowing the ins and outs of it, even said that I had secretly kept 4 dollars and 50 cents for myself. Truly, getting things done is so hard! At that time I was the one who scraped together those 10 dollars, and I slowly paid back the borrowed money afterward. Even now nobody knows about it, and the matter just passed like that.

**[Addendum 1]** The Venerable Master personally supervised the distribution of the goods. He notified the monasteries and hermitages throughout Hong Kong and Kowloon in advance and registered the number of monks and nuns, and then bought bolts of cloth and medicines, such as Tiger Balm, Shiling Dan pills, lemon essence, and the like. On the ninth day of the sixth lunar month (July 19), the goods were first transported to Tsuen Wan for distribution; the first distribution points were Tung Po Tor Monastery, Chuk Lam Sim Yuen, Luk Ye Yuen in Kowloon, and other places. The next morning, distribution was made at Nam Tin Chuk, Tung Lin Buddha Recitation Hall, and Hongfa Vihara. On the eleventh day of the sixth lunar month (July 21), they went to Haichao Monastery at Castle Peak, Yuen Long, and other places. In Yuen Long there were few monasteries but many hermitages, so the Venerable Master notified the monasteries and hermitages to gather in one place for the distribution; among them were the Bhikshunis of Yuen Tung Monastery, Ling Wan Monastery, and others. A few days later they went to Sha Tin, where the monks and nuns gathered at Prajna Vihara: several dozen monks and nuns in all, from Sai Lam Monastery, Facheng Monastery, and elsewhere. After that, they went on to distribute at Dafo Monastery in Wong Tai Sin, Kowloon, and at Chi Lin Nunnery at Diamond Hill.

At every place, at each distribution point, the monks and nuns of the monasteries were first called together, and then, following the register, their names were called out and the goods distributed; each received one bolt of cloth, one packet of medicine, and HK$5. The Venerable Master also handed over, in full, the offering envelopes he had received in the ordinary course of things, to establish affinities with the monks who had joined the Sangha Fellowship Association, HK$10 per person. In addition, two lay Buddhists, Chen and Yang, joined in the good deed, and the Venerable Master also distributed 50 Hong Kong cents to each monk and nun. After each distribution, if any names had been left out or anyone had forgotten to sign up, they could register again and receive their share after it was confirmed. As for the distribution to the monasteries belonging to Lantau Island, that was entrusted to Dharma Master Fa Ke of Po Lin Monastery to handle. In this way, nearly all the monks and nuns of Hong Kong and Kowloon received offerings.

The Venerable Master handled matters clearly and in perfect order; on this occasion of establishing affinities with the monks and nuns, everyone admired him.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

I remember that once, when the Master was distributing cloth and money to the refugee monks from the mainland, the Venerable Master organized some people beforehand to help him distribute medicine and food, and also arranged for some people to help make clothes. At that time the headquarters for the giving was at Chi Lin Nunnery. That time the Master took us to different monasteries, and he also took my parents to meet the abbots of the monasteries.

**[Addendum 3]** Historical background:

In the 1920s, as unrest continued without cease in mainland China, many monks of great virtue and high repute came to Hong Kong one after another to propagate the Buddhadharma, greatly furthering the development of Buddhism in Hong Kong. By 1940 Hong Kong already had over a hundred Buddhist temples. After Hong Kong was occupied by Japan in 1941, Japanese-style Buddhism was forcibly promoted, and the development of local Buddhism was held back. During the War of Resistance against Japan and the civil war between the Nationalists and the Communists, the Chinese mainland was in ceaseless turmoil, and in 1949 in particular a historic upheaval took place on the Chinese mainland. Tens of thousands of people headed south from the Chinese mainland to Hong Kong, and Buddhist monks and nuns also migrated south to Hong Kong in group after group; this continued right on into the 1950s.

Facing the hardship of the monks from the north migrating south, the Buddhist community of Hong Kong, though in very difficult economic circumstances, still received them warmly, so that the monasteries, thatched huts, viharas, hermitages, wooden huts, and so on built early on in the Hong Kong countryside all became places of refuge. The Buddhist community of Hong Kong and the faithful devotees of Kowloon each gave support to the best of their ability, building bamboo sheds and donating grain and oil, sharing joys and hardships and getting through difficult times together. Together they ushered in several decades of stable development for Buddhism in Hong Kong, and monasteries sprang up one after another like bamboo shoots after a spring rain. (Sources: Wikipedia, Hong Kong Buddhist magazines, and others)

## 43. The Remarkable Story of Guo Mi (1)

Wen Guo Mi's father died early. He was the only boy in the family, and the sole male heir for nine generations. He had had a heart condition since childhood, and it was especially serious in the two years when he was 12 and 13; on doctor's orders, he had to rest at home...

One day, Wen Guo Mi discovered a secret of his sister, who was four years older than he was: keeping it from their elder sisters, who were Christians, she had taken refuge in Buddhism, and she had also brought home a copy of *Records of Chan Master To Lun*. In his heart he deeply admired this Dharma Master and asked his sister to take him to see her teacher, but his sister put him off, saying, "You have to climb more than 300 stone steps to get to Western Bliss Gardens. If you had a heart attack, I couldn't bear the responsibility!" From that day on, he secretly bowed and made prostrations to the Venerable Master's Dharma image in the *Records*. One day three months later, as he was bowing in his room, the person in the Dharma image actually walked out and rubbed the crown of his head.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When we are working, we should recite the Buddha's name as we work: either recite "Namo Guanshiyin Bodhisattva," or recite "Namo Amitabha Buddha," or recite "Namo Fundamental Teacher Shakyamuni Buddha." Don't recite "Namo Shifu." Don't be like that stupid disciple Guo Di, who starts reciting "Namo Shifu" the moment the Master comes into view. If you go "Namo Shifu" and your teacher goes "Namo disciple," with "Namo" going back and forth, all your time will be wasted. You'd do better with "Namo Bodhisattvas, Namo Buddha, Namo Dharma, Namo Sangha"; that's a bit better. Why does Guo Di recite "Namo Shifu"? Because Guo Di heard me tell how there was once a person who recited "Namo Dharma Master To Lun," and his illness was cured. Speaking of this, I'll have to tell this fairy tale once more:

I had a student in Hong Kong who came down with heart disease, an enlarged heart, at the age of six or seven. The doctor told him, "This illness of yours won't be easy to cure! If you want to get better, you can't go to school and you can't walk; you have to lie in bed every day, for at least five years. And you can't walk even from the head of the bed to the foot of the bed." The child followed the doctor's instructions and lay at home sleeping. But sleeping for one day, three days, or five days was all right; when he had to sleep like this every day, he couldn't sleep anymore, and when he couldn't sleep, he had idle thoughts. What idle thoughts did he have? He thought, "If I can't walk, it wouldn't be bad if I could fly!"

So he lay there for about a year or two. One day he saw a book that had a photograph of a monk in it. He asked people who this monastic was, and someone told him that this was a Chan Master named To Lun. His relatives and friends all recited "Namo Amitabha Buddha," but he, oddly enough, put the photograph on his bed, sat in full lotus facing the photograph of the monk in the book, joined his palms, and recited, "Namo Dharma Master To Lun, Namo Dharma Master To Lun, Namo Dharma Master To Lun..." He recited like this from morning till night.

He had never even seen me. It was rare for a child to have such perseverance, sitting on his bed every day reciting "Namo Dharma Master To Lun." After he had recited for more than 70 days, something strange happened. What strange thing? He saw the photograph turn into a person, who walked right out of the photograph, rubbed the crown of his head, and spoke to him. From then on, his heart disease was cured. When he went to see the doctor, the doctor said, "Huh? This illness of yours is completely better now!" These things sound very much like a fairy tale, but this was his own personal experience. As long as you are sincere, even a photograph can bring a response.

He first saw me at Chi Lin Nunnery, when I was lecturing on the *Earth Store Sutra*.
