When I saw this child, he seemed very familiar, and when he saw me, he immediately resolved to take refuge with the Triple Jewel. From then on, his wisdom opened up greatly. Before he fell ill, he had been very slow at his studies, but after taking refuge, no matter what book he read, he never forgot anything once he had read it. Within three years, he skipped ahead five grades in school. For example, he jumped from first grade to second grade; when he was in second grade, he jumped to fourth grade; and from fourth grade he jumped to fifth grade. Why could he skip grades? Because he understood the lessons of the higher grades as soon as he heard them, so the teachers let him skip ahead.

This young disciple of mine was very unusual; he was always wanting to see me. In Hong Kong I didn't teach people to investigate Chan and sit in meditation; whoever wanted to investigate Chan and sit in meditation applied effort on their own. When others were playing, he would sit in meditation. He would go up the mountain and sit in meditation on a rock, or, like the Venerable Rahula going into the toilet to sit in meditation, he would sit in the toilet; that place stank, yet he could sit there in meditation and enter samadhi.

All you Good and Wise Advisors, don't go hearing me tell this story and then all start reciting "Namo Shifu" too, until you've recited this teacher into having no response at all. Because if too many people recite it, I won't know which one to help. You should know that the intertwining of stimulus and response is the response to a single thought of true sincerity; if you can do it at the level of the primary meaning, there will definitely be a response! Nobody taught this child to recite like that, and he had never heard my story either; he had this response because he made the resolve himself. If you hear about it and then copy him, you've fallen to the level of the secondary meaning: half true, and half heard from others. He recited for 70 days and had a response; you'd have to recite for seven years, and then perhaps you might achieve a little something; otherwise there won't be any response. Someone says, "Everything we have now is learned." That's right, all of it is learned. If you can understand without learning, that is enlightenment; you've attained awakening!

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

I knew Guo Mi before I took refuge with the Master. Guo Mi's father had died early and his mother was quite old. He was the only son in the family and had had heart trouble since birth, and his elder sisters all looked after him well. When he became the Master's disciple, at first he didn't dare let his family know; only later, when his health was getting better and better, did he tell them. He was very intelligent and could sit in Chan meditation for a long time; the Master said Guo Mi had some attainment in Chan samadhi. He didn't go to Western Bliss Gardens or the Buddhist Lecture Hall very often, only on weekends or holidays, but his sister (Guo Shu) went often. He was a very good speaker and often gave talks at the Sunday Dharma assemblies.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

My classmate Margaret and I often went swimming and bicycle riding together. Then for a time she suddenly found she couldn't find me and didn't know where I had gone. When she learned that I had a teacher, she asked me to take her to see the Master, and she too took refuge with the Master, with the Dharma name "Guo Shu." Her family were all Christians, and at the time they didn't know about it. She had a younger brother called Guo Mi (Wen Songyi), who, because of a heart problem, had been out of school for a year or two. He knew what his sister Guo Shu had done, and Guo Shu even showed him the records of the Master's life. Every day he bowed to the Master's photograph, and unexpectedly the Master in the photograph appeared; from then on his heart disease was cured without any medicine.

In 1961, the Master once said at the Buddhist Lecture Hall, "Guo Mi can be said to be the foremost Dharma protector of the whole Lecture Hall!" The Master also said to me, "When I say these things, I hope you can listen with a straight mind. I'm not saying this deliberately; the Bodhisattvas can all certify what I say. The reason Guo Mi has been able to achieve something in his cultivation so quickly has to do with his previous lives. If, before he took refuge with me, his capacities had not been suited to the teaching, then no matter how I taught him, it wouldn't have had much effect, and his progress would have been limited."

I understood why the Master said these things. At that time I hadn't believed in Buddhism for long, and I thought of myself as one of the top students at school. I was very angry that the Master wouldn't teach me to cultivate Chan samadhi and spiritual powers, so I tried to find ways to make the Master angry. I knew the Master very much hoped that we would cultivate vigorously, so I deliberately did the opposite. Within a month or two, I joined the drama club, went to night school in the evenings, and very soon I had a boyfriend too. When the Master found out, he told my mother, and my mother had a big fight with me. The Master had my classmate Guo Shu (Wen Guo Mi's elder sister; Guo Mi did not yet know the Master at that time) pass on a message that I was not allowed to come to Western Bliss Gardens anymore. At the time I was just about to sit for the public examination for graduation from secondary school, and I said to Guo Shu, "Fine, if I'm not to go, I won't go. I don't have time anyway!"

The public examination was finally over. Although my name was on the list of those who passed, I felt very depressed; my boyfriend could not make up for the unease I felt at being without the Master. One day at noon, unable to stand it any longer, I ran up the mountain alone and rang the bell at the bamboo-fence gate of Western Bliss Gardens. Dharma sister Guo Ping came out to answer the gate, but as soon as she saw it was me, she turned and walked away. After a while she came out again and said to me quietly, "The Master says you're to kneel before the Buddha." I don't know how long I knelt. It had already gotten dark and everyone who had come to the Buddha hall had left before I heard the Master call my name. Weeping and sobbing, I knelt before the Master and poured out everything that was in my heart. The Master told me, "The straight mind is the Way-place," and told me about the principles of cause and effect.

## 44. Young Disciples

During his time in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master had more than ten young disciples who attended him closely, such as Wen Weibin (Guo Shu), Wen Songyi (Guo Mi), Tan Muzhen (Guo Zheng), Tan Mujie (Guo Shi), Huang Jiemei (Guo Song), Huang Ketai (Guo Jun), Cai Shuqing (Guo Su), Xu Xiangjun, Xu Lijun, Wu Xiangcai, Wu Yingcai (Guo Dong), Zhong Meiwei (Guo Jia)...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When I was in Manchuria, I had many young disciples. Some were eight or nine years old, and there were also students in elementary school and in middle school. When they took refuge with me, they first had to observe my conditions. What was my first condition? They had to be filial to their parents and respectful to their teachers and elders. Disciples who took refuge with me had to bow to their father and mother every day; if they didn't bow to their parents, I wouldn't accept them. You want to take refuge? Then you have to practice filial piety. If you don't practice filial piety, then don't take refuge. That was my condition for entering the door.

When I got to Hong Kong, I stopped enforcing this condition, because Hong Kong was neither Chinese nor Western. The people there only half-understood Chinese, and they only half-understood English too. They wanted to learn modern things, things with a bit of a foreign flavor, and they thought Eastern ways of thinking were too clumsy. If you taught a child to bow to his parents, his father and mother would be completely baffled: "Why is he acting like this? Has the child gone mad?" They might even take him to see a psychologist. So in Hong Kong I didn't enforce this rule. When I came to America, even less so. Especially in the early days, I opened wide the door of expedient means—you want to take refuge? I accepted people's refuge with no conditions at all. That's why so many of the finest people in this world came to study the Buddhadharma. If there had been conditions, I'm afraid these Westerners couldn't have accepted them.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasaka Huang Guo Jun recounted:

In junior high I attended the Salesian English School in Shau Kei Wan, which had been founded by the Catholic Church. The school wasn't far from Western Bliss Gardens, so every day at noon I would run over to Western Bliss Gardens for lunch, and the Master would even peel apples for me to eat. After lunch it was my time to sit in meditation. When the Master saw that the time was up, he would tell me to get up from my sitting and go back to school.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

We went to see the Master almost every day, not just on weekends. In the summer, the English-language schools in Hong Kong held classes for only half the day, and school let out after one o'clock in the afternoon, so every afternoon we would go to Western Bliss Gardens. Even though we had to climb more than 300 steps, we never tired of it. The Master spent a good deal of time on us. We would sit around him chatting with him, and we even asked him for things to eat. We were very close to the Master and to his disciples, just like one big, happy, harmonious family.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Guo Jun, Guo Mi, and I were all about the same age. I loved to talk, and Guo Mi loved to talk too. Guo Jun was very bright and didn't like to talk much; his Chinese was better than mine. Guo Mi was extremely bright, but he didn't go to Western Bliss Gardens or the Buddhist Lecture Hall very often, only on weekends or holidays. The Master changed us, especially me. Before I met the Master, I cried a lot. It wasn't because I was in poor health; it was because I was afraid of seeing so many poor people, so I often cried. The Master asked why I was always crying, and I said, "To me, there's nothing in this world that is good or that makes people happy. People are so miserable!" The Master taught me a great deal, and after that I seldom cried.

I remember the Master once told me that I was a very stubborn person. If it weren't for him, no one could have changed me; no one could have made me yield. The Master really changed me a great deal! When the Master told the story of how he had bowed to his parents, to the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, and to everyone, I came to realize the respect one ought to show one's parents. So I went home and bowed to my parents. This is what a son or daughter ought to do, and I wanted to learn to do it too. Before long, my mother couldn't take it anymore and told me to stop right away and not bow anymore. She said that bowing to the Buddha and being a really good child was enough.

### (1) Joy in Hearing the Dharma

The Venerable Master especially admonished his young disciples not to listen attentively only when someone spoke the Dharma well, and then let their minds wander when someone else spoke it poorly...

The Venerable Master especially admonished his disciples to pay careful attention to every word and deed, every action and movement, of anyone at all, and not to let any of it pass by in vain. They should use the Wonderful Contemplating Wisdom to observe whether what was said was right or not, and whether it accorded with the Dharma or not, rather than use the conscious mind to discriminate and calculate. "If it is the Way, advance; if it is not the Way, retreat." Only in this way could they gain something and open their wisdom. Opening wisdom is not something achieved in a single step; one must be at it morning and evening, studying without interruption from dawn to dusk. Only then can one accomplish something.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

The Two Vehicles are elementary school, the Bodhisattva Vehicle is middle school, and the Buddha Vehicle is university. So once in Hong Kong I said to people, "The students I teach are university students, not elementary school students." With elementary school students, you have to tell them some stories to make them happy; only then will they make progress in their studies. With middle school students, you have to explain some principles to them. When they reach university, you have to make them really learn something before they can pass. Originally there were no Two Vehicles; the Buddha set up the Two Vehicles. Originally there was no need for elementary school, but because people are so small, an elementary school has to be set up. When people grow a bit older, a middle school is set up. After they turn 18, they enter university, where the degrees are further divided into bachelor's and master's, and there's also the doctorate.

So to become a Buddha, you first have to enter elementary school, and then middle school. Middle school is practicing the Bodhisattva Path; university is becoming a Buddha. In Buddhism, the most important thing is to become a Buddha. But if you suddenly tell someone, "Come study Buddhism and become a Buddha," he'll be scared off. What is a Buddha? Why should I become a Buddha? He doesn't understand. It's like suddenly giving a child the highest degree, a doctorate: the child doesn't even know what a doctorate is, because he's too young and his intelligence hasn't developed yet.

Listening to sutra lectures depends on the individual, on whether or not people have affinities with you. If someone has affinities with you, even if you scold him, he's happy. If someone has no affinities with you, then no matter how you flatter him and tell him how wonderful he is, he still won't be pleased. Refuge disciples who have faith in me believe whatever I say, without the slightest doubt; even if I deliberately speak an unreasonable Dharma, they still believe it. People who have just taken refuge for the first time don't have such firm faith; even those who have taken refuge for one life, two lives, three lives, or five lives don't have such deep faith. All those who have faith in their teacher took refuge with me in previous lives as well. Yu Guo Man is one of them. Guo Li also has faith in her teacher: even if she were told to die in my place, she would be willing!

At that time there were several places where Dharma Masters were lecturing on the sutras, but strangely enough these young disciples of mine wouldn't go to listen. I told them to go and listen, and they still wouldn't go. I said, "Don't just listen to my lectures all the time. Other Dharma Masters lecture better than I do; you ought to go and hear them!" "No matter how well he lectures, I'm not going anywhere else." Wu Guo Dong was like that. He said, "Why should I listen to them? Even I lecture better than they do." I said, "Don't be so arrogant!" He said, "It's true, there's just no comparison!"

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

While the Master was at Western Bliss Gardens, he also lectured on the sutras in other places. He was often invited to lecture at Chi Lin Nunnery and Tung Lin Kok Yuen. The founder of Tung Lin Kok Yuen was not a monastic, yet established a Way-place for nuns and at the same time built a Buddhist school for the poor. The Master was also invited to lecture at Tung Sin Tan, and I remember he went there more than once. Some Dharma Masters in Hong Kong always lectured by simply reading out the text and explaining the literal meaning, but the Master didn't lecture that way. He would weave stories into his lectures: some were stories from the scriptures, some came from the Master's own experiences, and some were stories the Master had heard. So every time the Master lectured, lots of people came to listen. The Master was very humorous, just as he was in America, and everyone loved his classes, especially young people.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

Wu Xiangcai (Guo Sen's eldest son) had been baptized for more than ten years, but after meeting the Master he took refuge with him. In the past he had had many questions that no one could answer; after he came to know the Master, the Master answered them for him one by one. From then on, his faith grew twice as firm. Wu Xiangcai waited for the Master to return from Australia before leaving to study in England. Before he went abroad, for several days, starting a little after six in the morning, the Master explained the *Sixth Patriarch's Dharma Jewel Platform Sutra* especially for him. The Master forgot himself for the sake of the Dharma and spared no pains in teaching his disciples.

After Wu Xiangcai arrived in England, he read the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra* and *Records of Chan Master To Lun* every day. The Master said, "It's cold in England and it snows heavily, but Xiangcai says his neck feels warm and he doesn't feel the cold. That's because he often sits in meditation and also reads the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra*." The Master remarked with feeling, "It looks as though he has had some response." The Master had taught him for only three months, but he was very vigorous, so he progressed remarkably fast.

### (2) Teaching as a Strict Teacher

Many disciples had the experience of receiving the Venerable Master's instruction in person. The Venerable Master would, as the occasion arose, at any time and in any place, and when they least expected it, give a disciple a pointer...

With his disciples, the Venerable Master would sometimes scold and sometimes remind, to make them correct their bad habits. The disciples who were scolded, while repenting and reflecting on themselves, all felt a kind of refreshing coolness. More importantly, this priceless teaching often became a force that helped them break through a bottleneck in their cultivation of the Way, and a turning point on the road of their wisdom-life. This is precisely the skillful expedient means of great wisdom and great compassion that appeared in the Venerable Master's way of teaching.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

You've never seen me lose my temper in a big way. If you had, you wouldn't be so unruly and wouldn't ignore the rules like this! In Manchuria and in Hong Kong, I really carried myself with the dignity of a teacher. Never mind my telling you to do something: if I merely hinted at it without saying it and you didn't go do it, I would already be displeased! That was my style. My disciples didn't dare to talk casually in front of me; they were very afraid of me. Sometimes when the kids were naughty and didn't follow the rules, I would give them a scolding, or even a beating. In Hong Kong I really did beat my disciples, and not fake beatings either; people were beaten by me until they were swollen.

Some people say, "Shifu, don't be angry with us!" I'm not angry with you. Whether people treat me well or not, I don't get angry. Why? You huff your air and I puff mine, so why should I get all huffy with you? Because everything is false; everything is empty and illusory. But sometimes I have to use expedient methods to teach and transform people. You're someone I ought to lose my temper with. If I don't flare up at you a bit, you'll never be vigorous; you'll always be lazy, always thinking, "I'll wait till tomorrow to cultivate, I'll wait till tomorrow to cultivate, I'll wait till tomorrow to cultivate..." Ah! You wait and wait until you're old, and even until you're dead, and you still haven't cultivated. So I have to really lose my temper with you, and even give you a beating. In Hong Kong I often beat people.

Now I'll tell you again: I have a big temper. Sometimes when I scold people it really is unreasonable, and when I beat people that's unreasonable too. But it's not that I'm unreasonable with just anybody; you have to be at that level. If you're not at that level, I'll be reasonable with you; only if you're at that level can I be unreasonable. In teaching and transforming living beings, sometimes, when you ought to be reasonable, you are reasonable; sometimes, when you ought to be unreasonable, you are unreasonable. Scolding can be reasonable and can be unreasonable; beating can be reasonable and can be unreasonable. If you're truly at that level, there's no reason to speak of at all! The Buddhadharma is inconceivable like this. You see it's this way and that way, and then it's neither this way nor that way, and it gets you all muddled. When you're muddled to the extreme, that's when your good news is coming. And if you can be unmuddled while you're muddled, that's good news too.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master said, "Guo Mi often recites 'Namo Dharma Master To Lun.' He truly has faith in me, and that moves me. The Patriarchs of the past would put their disciples through torments to see whether they really had faith, and I too have deliberately tested him several times. Once he went hiking in the mountains with Guo Jun, and as punishment I made him kneel for four hours; he knelt until he could hardly stand up. Guo Shu saw it but didn't dare make a sound; she only wept in secret. Another time, at Cixing Monastery, over some small matter, I deliberately drove him down the mountain. I told him, 'Tonight you go sleep in the guest hall!' Having no choice, he took his pillow from my room and went to the guest hall. Someone asked him why he had come to sleep there, and he just sighed and said, 'I'm going down the mountain tomorrow!' Only after half an hour had passed did I call him back to my room."

On the seventeenth day of the eleventh lunar month of 1991, the Master said, "My vow is to undergo suffering on behalf of living beings. Especially in these last few years, I want to undergo suffering for living beings. I had a great many offenses, but now they are all gone. Even offenses as vast as the heavens vanish with a single repentance." Bewildered, I asked, "Shifu, why do you say you have so many offenses?" The Master said, "I often scold people, and I take the offenses of living beings upon myself. The ones I scold are good people, not bad people."

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Those of us Dharma brothers and sisters who were close in age were very happy following the Master, because he would often give us some "sweet treats." Even though we couldn't go eating, drinking, and having fun with our other friends, it was worth it. Whenever any of us disciples slacked off a little or made a mistake, it could never be hidden from the Master. We all quickly learned to behave: before doing anything, we would first report it to the Master, and only then would we dare go ahead. Sometimes when we did something wrong, the Master would rebuke us, even in front of everyone, and punish us with the incense board. I was the only one the Master never hit with the incense board; perhaps the Master knew I was a very sensitive person.

**[Addendum 3]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master said to me, "At home no one can keep Ah Ying (Wu Yingcai, Guo Sen's second son, whose Dharma name is Guo Dong) in line; only when I scold him does he not dare to argue back. He told his mother, 'When Shifu scolds me, I'm glad to accept it.'" Hearing this, I laughed and said, "Hitting is love, and scolding is love too." The Master said, "If one day I hit you, it won't be lightly; I'll hit you hard. Are you afraid?" I had no answer. The Master said, "For someone who believes in me, even if I were going to kill him, he would say it was liberation."

**[Addendum 4]** Upasaka Huang Guo Jun recounted:

The Master had a very special way of teaching according to people's potentials! At that time I was attending a Catholic school. The good schools in Hong Kong were run by either the Catholics or the Protestants, and good Buddhist schools were as rare as phoenix feathers and unicorn horns. I was about ten at the time, and one day I said to the Master, "If I have money, when I grow up I'll open a Buddhist school." The Master said, "Good! It's very good that you can make this vow." A few months later the Master said to me, "Now I have a Dharma-door: if you cultivate it for just seven days, you can go to the Western Land of Ultimate Bliss." I immediately knelt down and said, "Shifu, please teach me this Dharma-door." The Master said, "And what about your school?!" With that one test, the Master exposed the vow I had made as false. My vow wasn't sincere, so my school never got started, and I didn't get to go to the Land of Ultimate Bliss either. Everyone knew the Master could foretell things, so when I was 15 or 16, I once asked the Master, "What will become of me later on?" The Master looked at me and said just one short sentence: "Just be kind-hearted!" He said nothing more, and I didn't press him. Those four short words became the motto of my whole life.

**[Addendum 5]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

Wu Yingcai was Wu Xiangcai's younger brother; we all called him "Ah Ying." He and his mother had taken refuge long before. The Master told me that Ah Ying used to come to hear the sutra lectures every evening, and then at a little after seven the next morning he would first come to the Lecture Hall to bow to the Buddhas before going to work. Christmas is a social occasion for young people, and because he went to dances, he didn't come to the Lecture Hall for several days. The Master was afraid something had changed or some accident had happened at his home, so he sent someone to go and find out. The next day, Ah Ying came to the Lecture Hall, and the Master said sternly, "None of my young disciples celebrate Christmas. If they were all like you, wouldn't that mean no one would protect my Dharma?" Ah Ying was so ashamed that he wished the earth would swallow him up, and he begged the Master to beat him. The Master said offhandedly, "I won't beat you. Why should I beat you?" Yingcai felt even worse, so he slapped his own cheeks until they were swollen, begging the Master's forgiveness.

### (3) Always Learning at the Master's Side

The Venerable Master's wisdom was deep and profound, and he was thoroughly versed in ancient Chinese history, literature, poetry, and calligraphy. Every weekend, when the young disciples came up the mountain, the Master would teach them classical Chinese...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When we study Chinese literature, we have to practice our characters and write them very neatly. As they say, "Not one stroke done carelessly." Don't just carelessly dash off a character in one sweep; you can't be careless about this. In China, no matter how well you wrote your essays, if your handwriting was poor you couldn't become the top scholar in the imperial examinations, so Chinese characters must be written well. With the writing brush there's small regular script and large regular script: large regular script is for writing big characters, and small regular script is for writing small characters. "Fly's-head characters" are each only as big as a fly's head, yet every one of them is written without a single careless stroke, perfectly clear. I'm also glad for you to learn all kinds of languages and scripts, because I don't know that many languages, and I get pushed around wherever I go. When I go to Australia, the Australian kids push me around; when I come to America, you American kids push me around. Wherever I go, people take advantage of my not understanding the language. That's something I really regret!

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

On weekends the Master taught us classical texts such as *Mencius* and *Laozi*. If anyone wasn't paying attention, the Master would write a little poem on the blackboard, and as soon as that person read it, they would know the Master was talking about them. Reciting a poem is different from reading it; reciting a poem is like singing it. In the old-style schools of ancient times, the teacher would wag his head back and forth as he chanted poetry. The Master didn't do that, but his intonation was very similar. Later on the Master stopped chanting poems that way. Every time I saw the Master, he would give me a book to take home and read. Most were Buddhist books, but not all of them. I remember that the first book he gave me was *Poems in Imitation of Hanshan*, written by Layman Meicun, Zhang Shouyue. The Master often wrote phrases freely on old newspapers, and we would copy them after him. To this day I still keep some of the newspapers the Master wrote on.

One day the Master wrote the following verse:

> With the nature settled and demons subdued, every morning brings joy;  
> With false thoughts not arising, everywhere is peace.
>
> <small>性定魔伏朝朝樂，妄念不起處處安。</small>

> Flowers lovely and the moon full, people do not age;  
> With mind settled and spirit whole, the body grows ever stronger.
>
> <small>花好月圓人不老，意定神全體更堅。</small>

> Reach out a hand and pluck the moon from the edge of the sky;  
> Close your eyes and see right through to the sky within the water.
>
> <small>伸手摘來天邊月，閉眼澈見水中天。</small>

> Fine lines I chant toward a place where no one is;  
> The true mind lies bare as it was before birth.
>
> <small>好句吟向無人處，真心披露未生前。</small>

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Saturdays didn't have a full day of group cultivation the way Sundays did, so on Saturdays we studied classical Chinese, Japanese, and calligraphy. Back when the Master was young, his calligraphy was superb. He would first write on the blackboard and teach Guo Shu, Guo Song, Guo Jun, and me how to move the brush. My elder sister's Chinese was good, because our father had once hired an old-style private tutor to teach her and my elder brother classical Chinese, so both of them wrote quite good calligraphy. In Hong Kong I attended a Christian school that emphasized English, so I didn't learn much Chinese. We also studied Japanese, taught by Mr. Pei (Pei Youming; Pei Guo Jian; Pei Zhenghui), one of the Master's disciples from Manchuria. My sister studied it for a while before she went to America.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Zheng recounted in 1995:

The Master put a great deal of effort into teaching our band of hard-to-manage teenagers, finding every possible way to do so, without complaint and without asking anything in return, in the hope that we would become citizens of good character and sound thinking. The Master taught each person according to who they were, using a different method for each of us, so that amid the glittering, pleasure-seeking society of Hong Kong our minds still did not waver and our resolve did not retreat, and we held firm to our faith in studying Buddhism. This was truly no easy thing.

To nurture a younger generation of Buddhist disciples, the Master racked his brains and wore himself out in mind and body. He once talked with us all through the night until dawn; he saved the tastiest pine nuts for us; and he once even neglected his honored guests because he was telling us Chan cases. To keep us in line, the Master would sometimes put on a stern face and go the whole day without eating or drinking; and when we went astray, he also punished us severely. Only today do we truly understand the pains he took for our sake.

Wherever the Master went, there was always a group of young followers. He nurtured the younger generation without ever counting the cost; even if nine out of ten turned out to be good-for-nothings, he never minded. His hope was to pass on and develop the meaning and essence of the Buddhist sutras. Even if only one became a vessel of the Dharma, it could be handed down from generation to generation, so that Buddhism would flourish and spread. The Master required his disciples to practice earnestly, both in the precepts and in cultivation, never carelessly, and to establish the Way-place of their own minds.

### (4) Putting On Performances

The Venerable Master encouraged his disciples to discuss and dig out the truth, to apply the Buddhadharma in their lives, to awaken to the true meaning of the Buddhadharma in everyday affairs and human relationships, and to discover for themselves what was right and what was wrong. It was precisely the Venerable Master's blend of strictness and kindness that inspired his disciples...

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Chinese believe that Guanyin Bodhisattva was Princess Miaoshan, the daughter of King Miaozhuang, who wanted to leave the home-life and, after many hardships, finally attained the Way. But this story does not come from the Buddhist sutras. From the *Shurangama Sutra* and the *Great Compassion Heart Dharani Sutra* we know the true story of how Guanyin Bodhisattva attained the Way. Because we didn't understand this at the time, we once put on a play about Princess Miaoshan. I think it was probably my idea, but the Master didn't tell us we were wrong; he wanted us to figure out for ourselves what was right and what was wrong. I did many kinds of foolish things, and only now do I know that I was wrong. For instance, when I was helping with the *Gold Mountain Newsletter* at Gold Mountain Monastery in San Francisco, I once tried to explain the verses the Master spoke when he ascended the hall to speak the Dharma. After the newsletter was published, I reported this to the Master, and the Master told me that the verses for ascending the hall to speak the Dharma carry the flavor of Chan; they are for people to understand for themselves and are not to be explained. A wise person may become enlightened on hearing a single word or a single line.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

We were all very close to the Master. He was like a loving father to us; he taught us everything and also took us out to have fun. At the end of the Dharma assemblies, we would also put on some plays and performances, all of them related to Buddhism. I remember that the first play was about the Master's own life experiences. My sister's Chinese was good, and she put a lot of effort into writing the script and preparing it. Most of the programs were things she worked on wholeheartedly.

### (5) Little Soldiers of Translation

The Venerable Master encouraged his young disciples to take part in Dharma assemblies and in the work of translating the sutras and his instructional talks. He intended for them to be tempered through translation, and he taught them not to be turned by states...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

As for translating the sutras, some of you have learned a bit by now. What have you learned? You've learned that you say I translated it wrong, and I say you didn't translate it well. What counts as good? What counts as not good? If you translate well, go on translating in the direction of good; if someone else translates poorly, he'll gradually get better too. Whoever is translating, we all hope to do our very best at the work of translation. There's no need to be overjoyed when someone says it's good, or to get afflicted when someone says it's not good.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

When I was little, I spoke with the Master in Cantonese. We communicated very well; the Master understood Cantonese. Only after we came to America did we speak with the Master in Mandarin (Guoyu). I remember the first time the Master asked us to translate Mandarin into Cantonese: there were no tape recorders then, so you had to listen and take notes with full attention. Nobody wanted to do it, so we drew lots to decide. The Master lectured on the sutras every evening at Western Bliss Gardens, and I often went to translate. The Master's Mandarin was easy to understand, but other Dharma Masters had local accents that were very hard to understand, especially Dharma Master Ming Guan.

After Dharma Master Ming Guan came to Hong Kong, the Master often invited him to Western Bliss Gardens. At first, one of the Master's disciples from Manchuria helped translate. For some reason, Dharma Master Ming Guan thought he wasn't translating correctly and got a bit angry. He said to him, "No, no, you step down!" Then he pointed at me and said, "You come be my translator." I told the Master that I simply couldn't understand what Dharma Master Ming Guan was saying and couldn't do the translation. The Master said, "Don't worry! Remember, I'm right behind you." I had no choice but to scratch my head and go up on stage, and to my surprise I actually understood what Dharma Master Ming Guan said. Afterwards I told the Master, and the Master said, "I told you I was right behind you!" From then on, the Master trained us to translate sutra lectures and instructional talks.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Zheng recounted in 1995:

I remember that when we were young in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master required us young Dharma brothers and sisters to take turns translating his sutra lectures into Cantonese. At first, because of stage fright, my hands shook with fear when I translated, and I couldn't even take decent notes. But a teacher's command is hard to disobey, so I had no choice but to steel myself and go up. After this kind of training, though, I benefited endlessly, and it also laid the foundation for my later career in education.

### (6) The Key to Quiet Sitting

Where does the wonderful meaning of Chan lie? Is sitting Chan? How should one practice quiet sitting and Chan meditation? The young disciples once asked the Venerable Master about the key to quiet sitting...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

As for sitting in Chan meditation, only if you like it can you sit in Chan; if you don't like it, then even if you do sit in Chan, it won't do much good.

By sitting in Chan, you gain samadhi power. Then is Chan a matter of sitting? It is a matter of sitting, and it is also not a matter of sitting. Before you sit, it's not sitting; after you finish sitting, it's not sitting; while you're sitting in Chan, it's sitting. In reality, with this Chan, standing is Chan, sitting is Chan, walking is Chan, and sleeping is Chan. If you understand this "Chan," then walking, standing, sitting, and lying down are all Chan. However, until you understand this "Chan," you must first sit in Chan. Once you have obtained Chan, you can't be separated from Chan. Whether walking, standing, sitting, or lying down, this Chan is you, and you are Chan; Chan follows you, and you follow Chan, like a shadow following a form, and even if you wanted to get away from it, you couldn't! That is samadhi power. This is sitting in Chan.

Having talked about it back and forth like this, what is this "Chan," after all? It isn't anything. If you think it is something, then you're attached! It isn't anything: that means being without attachment. Someone says, "That's vague talk that doesn't touch any edges!" Of course: if it had edges, it wouldn't be Chan! Chan has no edges at all; it is simply empty. Out of that emptiness your samadhi power is born, and samadhi is born.

Chan is half of a Sanskrit word; in full it is called dhyana. Dhyana is Sanskrit, and in Chinese it means "cultivation of thought"; it is also called "stilling of thought." That is, you sit there cultivating thought and stilling thought. If you don't know how to apply effort, you'll just sit there having idle thoughts; if you can apply effort, perhaps you'll drive the idle thoughts away, and then there won't be any idle thoughts.

Don't think "having idle thoughts" is a bad expression [Tr.: the Chinese expression for having idle thoughts, da wangxiang, literally means "hitting idle thoughts"]. Hitting idle thoughts is fine. If you can hit your idle thoughts and drive them off, if you fight with them, then as soon as an idle thought arises, you give it a blow with the demon-subduing pestle and kill it. Once it's killed, after a while another one arises; you use the demon-subduing pestle again and kill it too. Another one arises, and once more you use the demon-subduing pestle and kill it as well.

But when you hit them with the demon-subduing pestle, sometimes after you kill them they come back to life. What then? Use the demon-slaying sword. As soon as you see an idle thought come out, you cut it down with one stroke of the sword, and it won't come back to life again! So the demon-slaying sword is very formidable. Once you have slain your idle thoughts, your wisdom comes forth. Wisdom is the demon-slaying sword. If you have wisdom, you have the demon-slaying sword; if you don't have wisdom, you don't have the demon-slaying sword.

What is the demon-subduing pestle? It is your samadhi power. If you have samadhi power, you have the demon-subduing pestle; if you don't have samadhi power, you don't have the demon-subduing pestle. Do you understand now? When you sit in Chan, samadhi power comes forth; when samadhi power comes forth, it beats your idle thoughts to death. If you keep sitting in Chan, the power of wisdom comes forth. The power of wisdom is the strength of wisdom, and it slays those idle thoughts too.

If you know how to sit in Chan, then "Walking is Chan, sitting is Chan; speaking or silent, moving or still, the substance is at peace," and "In dreams, so clearly, there are the six destinies; after awakening, all is empty, with no great thousand world system"! If you become enlightened, even this great thousand world system is gone, let alone anything else! If you don't investigate Chan and don't sit in meditation, then where did you come from at birth? Where will you go at death? You don't know. Not knowing, you are born in a muddle and die in a muddle. To pass a whole life like this, how pitiful! Those who apply effort and investigate Chan become enlightened. Once enlightened, they recognize their original face before their parents gave birth to them, and they suddenly break through, so that there is nothing in the outer and inner, the fine and coarse aspects of all things that they do not reach, and nothing in the whole substance and great function of their minds that is not clear. They attain a state of tremendous good fortune, and in the future they can accomplish Buddhahood and attain the position of unsurpassed, proper, and equal right enlightenment.

**[Addendum 1]** In his *Impressions of Master Hsu Yun*, Dharma Master Le Guan mentions: I remember that year, Elder Yun held the winter Chan sessions with us before he went to Chongqing. During the Chan sessions, he once gave an instructional talk in which he said something that made people laugh. He said, "To investigate 'Chan,' you have to investigate with all your might; only then will you have 'Chan.' To study the 'teachings,' you also have to study in earnest; only then can you penetrate the principles of the 'teachings.' I often see people who investigate Chan crossing their legs to investigate for a while when they're in the mood, and dropping it again when they're not in the mood, like someone 'shaking with ague' (malaria), hot one moment and cold the next, now slack and now tight. If you investigate 'Chan' like that, then even if you gain something, it will be no more than the 'chan' that sits on a tree branch [Tr.: a pun: chan 'meditation' sounds the same as chan 'cicada']. People who study the 'teachings' are the same. When the whim strikes, they summon up their energy and gnaw away at their books from morning till night; when their laziness flares up, they toss the sutra on the bed and use it as a pillow, or sometimes put it up on a high shelf and let it get eaten by bugs. When they're tense, they won't even stop to pee or pass gas, wishing they could awaken to the Buddha's knowledge and vision on the spot; when they're slack, they're like a limp 'fried dough stick.' If you study the 'teachings' like that, then even if you study for eight or ten years and become a glib talker, you'll have learned no more than to 'jiao' like a chicken [Tr.: a pun: jiao 'the teachings' sounds the same as jiao 'to cluck']." Although these words border on the comical, their meaning is well worth pondering.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master telephoned, and I talked with him about quiet sitting. The Master said, "When the mind is clear, the moon appears in the water; when the thoughts are settled, there are no clouds in the sky." The Sixth Patriarch said, "Not thinking of good, not thinking of evil." I remember the Master once saying, "A cultivator of the Way must not doubt; once doubt arises, he loses his way." He also said, "Having intention is false thinking; having no intention is a response." The Master emphasized, "A cultivator of the Way is allowed only one mind; having two minds will not do."

### (7) Disciples with Opened Eyes

In real life we often see that some people are blind, while others can see the different states of ordinary beings and sages, such as Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, spirits and ghosts, and past lives. Is this true?...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In Manchuria, more than ten of my disciples could see Buddhas, Bodhisattvas, ghosts, and spirits. There were some in Hong Kong too, but I seldom had them talk about it to everyone. Why? Because Hong Kong was a very complicated place. Most people there didn't believe what was true and liked to run after what was false. If you told them something true, they said it was false; if you told them something false, they said it was true. They couldn't tell true from false. Especially some people who thought themselves clever turned right and wrong upside down even more. Why are some people blind? Why do some people have eyes? And why do some people see the different states of ordinary beings and sages? This all comes from their cultivation in the past. People who can see the different states of ordinary beings and sages mostly cultivated the Forty-two Hands and Eyes in their previous life, or in the life before that. Because by cultivating the Forty-two Hands and Eyes, one can obtain the Thousand Hands and Thousand Eyes. Even if they haven't formally obtained the Thousand Hands and Thousand Eyes, they can still see the different states of ordinary beings and sages. Why do some people have very good eyes? Because in previous lives they offered incense and flowers and lit lamps to the Buddhas, so their eyes are bright too. Why are some people blind? Precisely because they didn't believe in anything. When others said they had seen something, they would say, "That's fake; there's no such thing!" Afterward they couldn't see anything at all. This kind of retribution is truly inconceivable.

**[Addendum]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Cai Guo Su truly had good roots; perhaps it came from her cultivation in past lives. She was able to enter samadhi. One day we were holding a Buddha Recitation session. We sat and recited the holy name of Amitabha Buddha for 10 to 15 minutes, then sat in silence reciting silently, until the Dharma Master struck the bell; then everyone stood up for walking meditation. Guo Su didn't get up. Someone was about to give her a push, but the Master said, "Don't push her, don't touch her." She was in samadhi for about half an hour. If the Master hadn't struck the bell, she could have stayed sitting in samadhi indefinitely. Guo Su could see what had happened in past lives. Sometimes the Master would have her talk about her experiences, and sometimes he would have her enter samadhi to look into other people's problems.

## 45. Training Disciples to Speak the Dharma

The Venerable Master gave the Dharma to people in a style all his own and was a model for monastics in speaking the Dharma. Not only did he lecture on the sutras and give instructional talks, spreading the essence of the Buddha's thought to his refuge disciples and devotees, but with earnest compassion he also trained his disciples to lecture on the sutras and speak the Dharma...

The Venerable Master taught people according to who they were. He encouraged young, educated disciples to study Buddhism, understand its principles, and awaken to the Way, so as to become successors in propagating the Dharma. Elderly, uneducated lay Buddhists he likewise encouraged to study the Buddhadharma and share what they felt, even if it was only one thing or one sentence.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When Dharma Masters generally lecture on the sutras, one person lectures and everyone else listens. But when I lecture on the sutras, I bring two helpers along, and before I speak I have them talk first for three to five minutes. Why do I do this? Because my principle is to bring up young people to become talented propagators of the Dharma. I want to cultivate new blood for Buddhism; I want to nurture a new generation of young Buddhists. We need to train a group of genuine cultivators and teach a group of the most capable speakers. Whoever likes giving talks can practice first, and then come out and speak publicly at the large Dharma assemblies. Don't fall into the habit where you don't dare to speak, I don't dare to speak, and we don't even want anyone else to speak, but only know how to hide behind people's backs gossiping about right and wrong. That is a great and grievous mistake.

Making offerings to the assembly with the Dharma is the giving of Dharma! No matter who is lecturing on the sutras, I rejoice and praise it. As long as you dare to speak, it doesn't matter if you say something wrong; that's my principle. If you speak wrongly and there's an offense, the offense is mine. The Buddha is compassionate: if you have faults and can correct them, he forgives you, and besides, it's not as if you're deliberately speaking wrongly. Take my sutra lectures: I'm not afraid of anyone criticizing me. If what I say isn't right, then you come and lecture; that's my principle. When you're giving a talk, there should be no one in your eyes. Don't see anyone, don't be afraid of people laughing at you, and don't be afraid of what people might say; as long as what you say makes sense, that's fine. Some muddle-headed people say, "Don't speak! If you say something wrong, you'll commit an offense!" Even if you speak wrongly, it's still better than not speaking. If you don't speak, you yourself won't know whether you're right or wrong, and no one else will know either. We're all here to study the Buddhadharma. If, as just now, someone says something wrong, someone who knows can help correct it. All of you who are learning to lecture on the sutras should hold respect in your hearts for those who came before you; you may not call the Patriarchs and senior figures directly by name. For example, when referring to the Sixth Patriarch, don't just say "Huineng"; you must add "Great Master." "Great Master" is a title of respect. Don't study the Buddhadharma on the one hand while creating offense-karma on the other. You are an ordinary person, and calling a Patriarch directly by name is the most disrespectful and impolite behavior of all; it shows that you haven't yet truly understood the Buddhadharma.

As for what I'm telling you now: in the future, once you understand the principles of the sutras, you don't necessarily have to lecture the way I do; you can speak according to the principles you yourselves have awakened to. Speaking the Dharma should be concise: don't say one word too many, and don't say one word too few. Don't be like embroidery, and don't go on at great length as if you were writing a composition. Don't talk too fast, and don't talk too slowly; don't speak too broadly, and don't speak too narrowly. Sutra lectures should be such that everyone can understand. They should be plain and accessible, so that ordinary people all understand; only then are they useful. If you lecture too profoundly, so that people don't understand, that isn't of much use either.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

After Western Bliss Gardens Monastery was completed, every weekend there was a Buddha Recitation Dharma assembly and an instructional talk by the Master, and the Master also had each of us take turns speaking the Dharma. After the adults finished speaking, we young people would go up after them and speak. Actually, it didn't matter what you said; you could even just say your name. The Master would remember who had spoken this week and whose turn it was next week. He didn't let us prepare beforehand; he wanted us to say whatever we were thinking. This was the Master's method for teaching us public speaking. He told us, "Don't be afraid. Just go up on stage and say what you're thinking, and that will do."

At that time I wasn't afraid to go up and give a talk, because at school I often took part in debate competitions and had faced more people than there were at Western Bliss Gardens, but I didn't know what to talk about. The Master told me, "Don't worry! Just talk about your ideas, tell what's on your mind, and that's enough!" I don't remember what I talked about the first time, but everyone thought I spoke quite well. I remember an elderly Upasika in her sixties or seventies who couldn't read; she recited the Buddha's name vigorously every day, and the Master had her go up and speak too. She said, "Shifu is so compassionate. I don't know what to say; all I know is reciting Amitabha Buddha!" The Master said, "That's exactly right!"

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master often had us come up and speak, but we never knew when the Master would call our names; this was his method of training. The Master not only trained young people to speak the Dharma, but also encouraged older lay Buddhists to study the Buddhadharma; the Master truly hoped that everyone could speak the Dharma. There was once an elderly lay Buddhist who came up every week and recited the same verse, the same four lines, but the Master said it didn't matter, as long as we were willing to open our mouths and speak. I remember that once the Master said in an instructional talk, "After seeing through, you also have to put down." Xue Guo Su restated the main points of the Master's talk. Guo Sheng explained "precepts, samadhi, and wisdom: the Three Non-Outflow Studies." People like Ye Guo Qing and Yu Guo Man, both past fifty, spoke the Dharma full of their faith in the Master. Ye Guo Qing went on to explain the lines from Great Master Hanshan's *Song of Wasted Effort*: "Cultivation is easy, but meeting a teacher is hard; if you don't meet an enlightened teacher, it's all wasted effort." Yu Guo Man said, "The Master always urges us to recite the Buddha's name. I just recite the Buddha's name; there's nothing else for me to do. To begin with, your mind-ground must be bright. When your mind-ground is bright, everywhere is bright."

---

# Part Three: Cixing Monastery and Providing for the Sangha

*Ages 36 to 39*

*1953 to 1956 CE*

## 46. Cixing Chan Monastery

After building Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, the Venerable Master vowed to found a Way-place that could accommodate more members of the Sangha to apply effort and cultivate the Way. At the time, the political situation was turbulent and unsettled, and a great many monks and nuns from mainland China had drifted to Hong Kong. Because there was no suitable place for them to settle, their cultivation of the Way was going to waste...

Seeing this, the Venerable Master felt that building Buddhist Way-places in Hong Kong was a matter that could not be put off for a moment. But alas, his resources fell short of his intentions: the Venerable Master's Way-place, Western Bliss Gardens, was close to a busy district and small in area, and there was simply no spare room to house the monastics who had fallen on hard times.

It was not until the autumn of the guisi year (1953) that conditions finally ripened, when the lay Buddhist Dong Guo Qi, after taking refuge with the Venerable Master, generously donated a two-story stone villa located halfway up Ling Wui Shan at Man Cheung Po, in the southwest of Lantau Island in the New Territories, as an offering to the Buddha to serve as a Way-place. As it happened, the villa's neighbor on the right was the abandoned Guoqing Chan Monastery, founded by Dharma Master Fa Chuan in 1930. When the gunfire of the Pacific War reached Lantau Island, Guoqing Chan Monastery was bombed and destroyed by the Japanese army, and its abbot, Dharma Master Fa Chuan, died a martyr's death. From then on, the Sangha scattered in all directions, and only broken walls remained, half-buried in heaps of rubble.

(Editor's note: Japan launched the Pacific War on December 7, 1941. On December 8 it made a surprise attack on Kai Tak Airport in Hong Kong, and on December 25 Hong Kong fell. The Hong Kong–Kowloon Brigade continued to wage guerrilla warfare against the Japanese army. In the Battle of Midway in June 1942, the American forces defeated the Japanese and regained command of the sea and the air in the Pacific. At the time, the Allies planned to land along the coast of China to wipe out the main force of the Japanese army. Lantau Island, situated on high ground, was the best place to establish an Allied beachhead, so a Lantau Island guerrilla unit was quickly formed, opening up the Lantau battlefront. Guoqing Chan Monastery was one of the operational bases of the Hong Kong–Kowloon guerrillas, and the Japanese army killed many people on the Lantau battlefront.)

Because Guoqing Chan Monastery had long been in disrepair, it was overgrown with weeds, and wherever one looked there were broken bricks and shattered tiles. There were no doors, no windows, and no roof, and even the surrounding walls had crumbled beyond repair; the desolate, ruined scene was unbearable to look at. Facing this scene of ruin, the Venerable Master could not help feeling a deep sorrow: this was a place where disciples of Shakyamuni had cultivated, so how could it be left abandoned like this for long? So he obtained the consent of the people concerned with Guoqing Chan Monastery, and the ruins of the monastery were handed over to the Venerable Master to deal with.

In fact, Ling Wui Shan at Man Cheung Po (whose name is also written with the character for "cloth" in place of "waterfall") is a secluded mountain place where birds sing, with peaks gathered all around in a majestic sweep. But because the mountain paths were rugged and the ravines steep and perilous, it was hard to get to, and so people seldom came. Its natural environment lay hidden and unexposed, and so, on the contrary, it had escaped damage at human hands. Guoqing Chan Monastery lay right in the midst of these clustered peaks and ridges, strangely shaped jagged rocks, and secluding green forests. The Venerable Master was overjoyed to obtain this sacred site. Following the contours of the mountain and its slopes, he planned to combine the ruins of the monastery and the villa into one and renovate them as a monastery where the Sangha could apply effort and cultivate the Way.

Although the lay Buddhist Dong's villa had been built after the war, it had been abandoned for so long that all its doors and windows were gone. The only way it was better than Guoqing Chan Monastery was that its roof wasn't open to the sky! Dilapidated to this degree, it was practically a ruin itself. So how did the Venerable Master restore it?

Because funds were limited, the Venerable Master decided to repair the villa first and use it temporarily as the Buddha Hall; this later became the Left Courtyard of Cixing Chan Monastery. In the spring of 1954 (the jiawu year), the repairs to the villa and the guest hall were completed, and he named it "Cixing Chan Monastery, Spirit Mountain Way-place." The monastery was divided into an East Courtyard and a West Courtyard. The East Courtyard was the site of the ruins of Guoqing Chan Monastery and served as the place of cultivation for men; the West Courtyard, made from the villa, served as the place of cultivation for women. Besides the Buddha Hall, the Left Courtyard, and the guest hall, a dining hall, a retreat room, and a thatched hut were also added, one of each.

To build Cixing Chan Monastery, the Venerable Master worked day and night, forgetting to sleep and eat, rushing about everywhere. Whether hiring workers or buying materials, making images or laying in provisions, there was nothing he did not see to personally.

The Venerable Master wrote a verse:

> Night falls on Ling Wui Shan;  
> I step out of Cixing Monastery.  
> A lone moon shines on a poor monk;  
> The host of stars encircles the Pole Star.
>
> <small>夜落靈會山，步出慈興寺。孤月照貧僧，眾星拱北極。</small>

> Worldly affairs are like dreams and illusions;  
> Those who know it, alas, are few!  
> Don't be turned by floating dust,  
> And I promise your six faculties will become one.
>
> <small>世事如夢幻，知者歎幾希。勿為浮塵轉，許汝六根一。</small>

In the early days of building the monastery, there were difficulties everywhere. Every time the Venerable Master took the boat to Lantau Island, he had to carry 50 or 60 catties of rice and flour, or oil and salt, on his back up the mountain. Only after Dharma Master Heng Yi cleared the wasteland and began farming was there no longer any need to buy rice. At first, the monastery's residents were Dharma Master Heng Yi, Tang Guo Yun, and an elderly woman, Guo Quan; later, people came one after another to help: three worked in the kitchen, four farmed, and two did the cleaning and tidying.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I, your teacher, was also a laborer doing hard work, hauling materials myself. Someone said, "You must like doing manual work, right?" No! I'm just like you: I don't want to do manual work either, and when I work I get very tired too. But for the sake of establishing a Way-place, I wasn't afraid of hardship. When I was building on Lantau Island, I wore myself out in mind and body, until my hair turned completely white from exhaustion! At that time I didn't have disciples to help me, so I had no choice but to hire workers.

I had to carry rice, oil, and salt on my back. From Hong Kong I'd take the 2:00 p.m. boat and reach Tai O at 5:00 in the evening, then carry 50 or 60 catties of rice, or oil or salt, and walk for two hours up the mountain. On the mountain there were lots of people working, and I carried these things up for them to eat. I'd reach the mountain at 7:00 in the evening, and early the next morning, a little after three, I'd hurry back down the mountain, because I still had to lecture on the sutras at Western Bliss Gardens in Hong Kong. Although I could have hired someone to carry the things up, that would have cost money, and I was saving every cent to buy building materials; I didn't waste a single cent! In this way, in two and a half years, I built three places. Why was there such an accomplishment in just two and a half years? Simply because I worked hard, wasn't afraid of any difficulty, and did the work myself, in person.

At Dajue Monastery on Mount Yunmen, rice was hulled by water power, and the rice it hulled was enough for the monastery's needs; villagers from nearby also went there to hull their rice. There was a pestle-and-mortar for hulling on Lantau Island too. I hulled rice there, though not often; sometimes I'd hull for an hour or two, and then others would come and take over. At that time there was a lot of grass on Lantau Island; some of the grass grew taller than a person. I saw "hundred-legs" (centipedes) three or four inches long; I saw them at Western Bliss Gardens too. Before that I had never seen centipedes that big.

At that time there were lots of discarded bricks, tiles, and stones piled up on the spot where the Buddha Hall was to be built, and to build the Buddha Hall they had to be cleared away first. Guo Li (Dharma Master Heng Yi) was there helping with the work, farming the fields, and managing the place. I told her to move the stones, bricks, and tiles away, but three times I told her and she ignored me, so I started moving them myself. Normally I just carried rice, flour, and oil on my back and seldom did any work on the mountain, so when Guo Li and the others saw me doing it too, they all got flustered and hurried over to clear it together. If I had just told them to do it, they couldn't have finished in five days; but when I led them and we all did it together, how did it go? It was all done in half a day.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi's oral account, given on January 7, 2001: When we first took over Cixing Monastery, I hadn't yet left the home-life; I was just living on the mountain, helping with the farming during the day and bowing to the Buddhas and cultivating in the evenings. The Master came back once every two or three days, always arriving very late. The Master's life on Lantau Island in the early days was very hard. There was no stove; to cook, we had to build a stove out of stones. There were no beds to sleep on either. From this you can get a glimpse of how hard it was. Every time, the Master would carry some food back with him. Ordinarily there was nothing good to eat; we ate congee all the time, and if on some day there was kelp, that counted as a fine dish.

One day, as night was falling, Guo Yun and I were still working in the fields when we saw a figure in the distance. When it came closer, we saw it was the Master! The Master was carrying a big sack of cement on his back and was doing three steps, one bow. The Master did a great deal of practice every day, and went down the mountain at three in the morning. (Upasika Tan Guo Zheng said, "The Venerable Master hardly ever slept at night; he spent the whole night sitting in meditation and applying effort. The Venerable Master's way of cultivating was unknown to others.")

**[Addendum 2]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi's oral account, given on September 25, 2000:

Western Bliss Gardens was built before Cixing Monastery.

After the Opening-the-Light ceremony at Western Bliss Gardens, someone came on Guanyin's birthday and said they would give a piece of land to the Master; that's what is now Cixing Monastery.

During the occupation, the Japanese killed many people here at Guoqing Monastery, the predecessor of Cixing Monastery, so when Cixing Monastery was being rebuilt back then, a lot of skeletons were dug up.

I remember that when the Master was about to build Cixing Monastery, I said to him, "Shifu, you want to put up a building here? This place is so high up, with no water, no sand, and no stone. How can we build a house?" The Master still wanted to build right there, so we had no choice but to dig the earth and quarry the stone ourselves, and carry the cement ourselves (we called it "red-hair mud," or "English mud"). At that time a bag of cement cost 25 cents, but hiring workers to carry it cost 60 cents a bag. That was so expensive that, to save money, we had no choice but to carry it ourselves.

It took three years to build the two places. First was the Hall of the Three Sages of the West. After the Opening-the-Light ceremony for the Three Sages of the West, we built the Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero. Back then the Buddha Hall was built with big stones; where there was no concrete poured, horse-dung mud was poured instead. In the early days nobody was making offerings yet, so we had no choice but to go down to the fields and farm. At first there was only me and one elderly person; later, nine people came to help: three worked in the kitchen, four farmed, and two did the cleaning.

After Cixing Monastery was completed, at least ten Chan sessions were held there every year, and sometimes as many as 20. At that time many Dharma Masters who had fled from mainland China lived at Cixing Monastery. That was just when the Communist Party was liberating the mainland.

In those days the Master would leave at 4:00 every morning and take the boat to Lantau Island, carrying lots of fruit, vegetables, soy sauce, and so on, on his back. By the time he reached the mountain, it was already past 9:00 in the morning. These things were all for the Dharma Masters on the mountain to eat, and we would work in the fields until dark. Tell me, wasn't that hard for the Master?

At that time, the Master's conditions had ripened for him to go to America to propagate the Dharma. At first I lived at Cixing Monastery and made my living by farming. Later I went to live at the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley, and once I was there, I stayed for 11 years; the Master, too, was in America for 11 years before he came back to Hong Kong. I phoned the Master and told him that I wanted to go back to Cixing Monastery: I couldn't live in Hong Kong too long, or I would die. After the Master had been gone for 11 years, I went back to the mountain.

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Xing recounted on September 25, 2000:

Cixing Monastery is in the northern part of Lantau Island, with its back to the south and facing northwest, so whenever a typhoon comes, it often gets hit very hard. It takes the northwest wind head-on. On ordinary days, when nothing happens, it's fine; but when something does happen, the wind can blow a whole awning up into the air, and the awnings over the corridors get damaged. Even the roof covering of the kitchen was once lifted up by the wind, but then it fell back into its original place without being damaged. Though it has been patched for leaks many times, it can still be used, because it's made of asbestos tiles, which hardly ever break; but even so, as we patched it, it kept leaking. In the end it leaked for a whole week: as we ate, water dripped in; it was raining outside, and raining inside too.

Cixing Monastery's predecessor was Guoqing Monastery. The land of the Three Sages of the West (the villa) was given to the Master by a lay Buddhist named Dong. When the Hall of the Three Sages of the West was being built, Guoqing Monastery, the villa's neighbor on the right, was extremely dilapidated. Seeing such a fine place, the Master didn't want it to go to waste, so he asked the people then in charge of the place and brought up rebuilding it. So the Master used the foundations of Guoqing Monastery to build what is today's Cixing Monastery.

Today's Cixing Monastery consists of two parts: there are about six buildings, but none of the spaces is very large.

Building Cixing Monastery and the Hall of the Three Sages of the West was extremely difficult. According to Dharma Master Heng Yi, the walls put up back then were built of big stones. Where did those big stones come from? It turns out they were dug out of the mountain. Workers were hired to cut the stone, and they cut it into rectangular blocks. At the time, the price was 25 cents to cut one stone, and each stone weighed on average 60 to 80 catties. The stone-cutting was done by hired workers, because we didn't know how to cut stone ourselves. Our own people did the hauling, because the stones had to be carried back to the building site; they hadn't come out of the original buildings. So what could be done? There was nothing for it but to haul them ourselves, carrying them back on our shoulders. Just imagine: one stone weighed 60 to 80 catties, so carrying two came to over a hundred catties, and you had to walk a long way, sometimes over mountains and across ridges, crossing two hilltops or two gullies and ridges, before you could get them back to Cixing Monastery. Back then they really had a very hard time building it. As for sand, it wasn't bought at the foot of the mountain, not like now, when it's bought by the truckload. So how did they get it? They took sand from the mountain gullies and the banks of streams and washed it, panning it the way people pan for gold. Sand was very precious, because without sand you can't put up a building. And there was no cement, only yellow mud slurry; cement was very costly. Because it had to be carried up the mountain (ordinarily, even going up the mountain empty-handed took a few hours), the cement had to be carried all the way up. By the time I came to Cixing Monastery, it still had to be carried up the same way. Although later on vehicles could get to Cixing Monastery, it was still just a bad road. Hiring people to carry things up the mountain was very expensive. As the saying goes, "The servant girl is bigger than the mistress" [Tr.: a Cantonese saying: the incidental cost exceeds the cost of the thing itself]. Because building Cixing Monastery was so very difficult, the Master was extremely worried.

When the Master lectured on the *Shurangama Sutra*, he mentioned that back then, because he worried so much about building Cixing Monastery, his hair turned white; later, when he put it down, his hair turned black again. So it's not hard to imagine how much the Master had on his mind back then.

Because no one knew whether the construction could be completed smoothly, the Master vowed to burn a finger as an offering to the Buddha, in the hope that the rebuilding of Cixing Monastery would soon be completed. That was because at that time many Dharma Masters from the mainland had come to Hong Kong and had nowhere to live, and many monastics had to beg. At that time the abbot of Tung Po Tor Monastery, Elder Master Mao Feng, was very compassionate. His own monastery didn't have enough food to eat, but seeing the monks suffering so much, he opened the monastery gates and took in monastics who had fled to Hong Kong. It was just as the saying goes: "If there's congee, eat congee; if there's rice, eat rice; if there's one meal, eat that one meal." The Master too had stayed at Tung Po Tor as a visiting monk at that time. So the Dharma Masters of Hong Kong called Elder Master Mao Feng the King of Compassion.

To come back to Cixing Monastery: at that time the Master very much wanted to finish building Cixing Monastery quickly, so that we monastics would have a place to settle. So the Master vowed to burn a finger as an offering to the Buddha, in the hope that the construction of Cixing Monastery would be completed soon. The Master asked Dharma Master Yong Xing to help him bind up his finger in preparation for burning it. Unexpectedly, his disciples found out and became alarmed; besides, the rebuilding work was extremely urgent. The disciples said to the Master, "Shifu! If you burn your finger and injure your body, how can the work be finished? This will hold up the construction!" Everyone knelt and begged the Master not to burn his finger. The Master's heart softened, and he had no choice but to grant his disciples' request: according with living beings, he gave up burning his finger. Then, with tears in his eyes, the Master asked Dharma Master Yong Xing to unwind the cloth wrapped around his finger. The Master also wrote two large characters meaning "Dead!" on the ceiling above the head of his bed.

Later, the Master thought of another way to get Cixing Monastery finished soon. He boarded a boat at Aberdeen (at that time there was no Star Ferry, nor any Tsing Ma Bridge): he took a fishing boat from Aberdeen to the Tai O pier, and starting from the Tai O pier, he did three steps, one bow all the way up to Cixing Monastery. Even on an ordinary day, walking from the Tai O pier to Cixing Monastery took two or three hours, let alone doing three steps, one bow. In the end his wish was fulfilled, and Cixing Monastery was built.

From Dharma Master Heng Yi I learned that Cixing Monastery at that time was extremely dilapidated, and there were also lots of skeletons. That was because during World War II the guerrillas used Cixing Monastery as a base. The Japanese army fought its way up from the Tai O pier to Guoqing Monastery and battled the guerrillas, leaving behind many skeletons as relics of that time. Even long after the war, no one cleared them away, until Cixing Monastery was rebuilt.

I remember that around 1982 or 1983, when Cixing Monastery was being renovated again, I saw that there were still bullet marks on the walls of Cixing Monastery, and during the renovation the bullet marks on the walls were covered over with plaster.

Back then, when Cixing Monastery was being rebuilt, the Master spent a lot of time rescuing those who had been killed in battle and those who had perished. The lay Buddhists who lived at Cixing Monastery at that time often saw ghosts; Cixing Monastery was frequently haunted, and the ghosts often played jokes on the people in the monastery. What kind of jokes? There was a cook (in the old days, cooking was done on kerosene stoves or with coal) whose fire would often be blown out just as it was lit for cooking, though there was no one else around, so it often took a long time to get a meal cooked. When the Master was at Cixing Monastery, he would often recite mantras facing one particular direction. We often saw the Master not saying a word, and we knew he was rescuing them.

Dharma Master Heng Yi also said that the Master didn't spend much time at Cixing Monastery; he went back to Cixing Monastery once a week. The Master spent more of his time at Western Bliss Gardens. The Master lived at Western Bliss Gardens, or lectured on the sutras at the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley, because the Lecture Hall was a place for receiving and guiding living beings, whereas Cixing Monastery was a place for cultivation. Those who wanted to cultivate lived at Cixing Monastery, so at that time about 20 people lived at Cixing Monastery.

From the Buddhist Lecture Hall you went to Aberdeen to take a boat, and you had to ride a fishing boat for five hours to reach the Tai O pier. The boats were what were popularly called "big-eyed chicken" fishing junks; nowadays it's a hovercraft. After landing, you still had to walk two or three hours to reach Cixing Monastery. Every time the Master went up the mountain, he carried lots of fruit, vegetables, soy sauce, and so on, on his back; rice wasn't needed, because they grew their own rice on the mountain.

At that time, a boatman told a lay Buddhist, "Your Master is very special. Once he (the Master) was on the boat when it started pouring rain. Everyone ran into the cabin to get out of the rain; only your Master stayed sitting at the bow." The Master stayed out in the rain the whole way until he went up the mountain.

Once the Master went down the mountain at two in the morning. Someone wanted to give him a flashlight to light his way, but the Venerable Master didn't want it. One of the others, however, said the Master didn't need a flashlight; he had eyes on his hands and feet! It was pouring rain at the time, and the Master got soaked in the rain all the way to the Tai O pier. After boarding the boat he sat in meditation, getting rained on all the way to Hong Kong. When he got back to Western Bliss Gardens, the lay Buddhist who looked after the Master was heartbroken to see him like that. It was already seven in the morning by then.

**[Addendum 4]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Every time I went to Western Bliss Gardens, most of the time I saw the Master sitting with his legs crossed. One day I noticed the Master was walking a little strangely, so I asked him, "Are you sick?" He said he wasn't; the Master wouldn't say anything. Suddenly I noticed that the Master's knees were bleeding. I said, "Shifu, what happened to you?" The Master said, "I wasn't careful!" Later I asked the Master's attendant, the elderly Upasika Guo Ping, and she told me, "Don't you know the Master goes to Lantau Island every night?" I said, "Yes, I know. Why?" She said that after the Master vowed to build a monastery there, he did three steps, one bow every night, and it was only after he had been bowing for a long time that we found out about it.
