Once I went with the Master to Cixing Monastery. From Hong Kong the boat took three hours; then we crossed over Pine Mountain (there were lots of pine trees there, so we called it "Pine Mountain") and walked another two or three hours on a rugged mountain trail before reaching Cixing Monastery. On the way we passed Man Cheung Po; looking down from above, it seemed bottomless. Every time we passed there, we had to walk in single file, because the path was very narrow, only wide enough for one person; walking two abreast was impossible. We carried flashlights to light the way, but we still couldn't go as fast as the Master, who didn't use a flashlight. I remember that the Master once set a record of reaching Cixing Monastery in less than two hours.

When the Master decided to rebuild Guoqing Chan Monastery, at first no one was willing to carry the bricks and timber. It wasn't a question of how much they would be paid; they thought it was an impossible job. Because the path was narrow, building materials could only be carried on the shoulders, and then you had to walk three hours on mountain trails, so no one was willing to do it. One disciple was a construction worker, and he said no workers were willing to go there, because it was up on a mountain, there wasn't anything to eat, and life there was dull and hard. Dharma Master Heng Yi hadn't yet left the home-life then; she stayed there to help the Master put the place in order.

Fortunately, there was an elderly lay Buddhist named Ye (Guo Qing) who worked for a construction company. Ye went to Lantau Island to survey the terrain and found another route. Because Lantau Island backs onto the sea, the heavier building materials could be transported by water, and then people could be hired to carry the materials up the mountain. Every time we went, we brought some materials up with us, and for the rest we hired villagers to help carry them; but what we carried was much lighter than cement and big timbers. This is the history of how Cixing Monastery was built: because of the Master's sincere and firm determination, the monastery was finally rebuilt.

**[Addendum 8]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi's oral account, given on September 25, 2000:

Later on (in the 1980s), Cixing Monastery had to be renovated again, because many places were badly dilapidated and there was no choice but to repair them. I phoned the Master and said, "Shifu, it's so vexing! Cixing Monastery has to be repaired again!"

"What's vexing about it? Put it down!" the Master told me.

"How can I put it down, Shifu?" I asked the Master.

"I can put down the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas; why can't you put this down?"

"After I put it down, where will I go?"

"Everywhere is a Way-place!" the Master said.

## 47. The Divine Dragon Comes to Life

In the autumn of 1953, early in the reconstruction of Cixing Chan Monastery, an episode took place. A venomous snake with a red head and a green body often appeared there. When the residents saw it, they caught it in a large iron bucket and carried it several li [Tr.: a li is about half a kilometer, or a third of a mile] away to release it...

Strangely, though, before the people who took the snake away had even returned, the snake was already back ahead of them, appearing right where there were lots of people and it would be easily seen, with an air of "What can you do to me?" Each time they took it away, it came back, over and over again. The strangest time was when they took it far away to release it. Just as they opened the lid of the bucket, they found the snake wasn't inside. They turned the bucket over and tapped it lightly, and suddenly half a snake's body dropped out of the bucket. By the time the people who had taken the snake away got back, it had become a whole venomous snake again, head raised and tongue flicking, slithering about in front of the Buddha Hall.

Snakes belong to the dragon family, and only a true dragon can keep venomous snakes in check. So the Venerable Master carefully studied the lay of the land at Man Cheung Po on Ling Wui Shan. Cixing Chan Monastery lies at the source of Man Cheung Po; nearby, mountain streams join together, majestic as a rainbow, and seen from afar it looks like a bolt of silver cloth ten thousand zhang long [Tr.: a zhang is about 3.3 meters] falling from the sky. Amid this sacred scenery, the Venerable Master chose three towering rock faces to the rear left of the monastery and set up a Dragon-Subduing Rock upon them. He then had Upasaka Chen Guo Sheng make use of the natural terrain to sculpt a golden dragon. The golden dragon is about four meters long, its scales tumbling and its claws soaring, splendid in seven colors and astonishingly lifelike. After the golden dragon was finished, the Venerable Master recited the Shurangama Mantra for seven days, and from then on the venomous snake never appeared again. Truly, it is no empty saying that virtue can subdue demons.

After the golden dragon was built, a spring welled up from a crevice in the rocks behind the monastery and flowed all year round, never drying up even in times of drought. The Venerable Master called it "Dragon's Mouth Water." A water-quality specialist once took the spring water to be tested and found it extremely pure, entirely free of impurities. (Editor's note: This great golden dragon has become one of the scenic sights of Lantau Island.)

In the Venerable Master's own words:

The man who made the dragon on Lantau Island in Hong Kong was the one who made all the sculptures in the Aw Boon Haw Garden. I hope this man can come to America; I don't know whether he'll be able to come. He said that if he could come, he'd be willing to make a Guanyin Bodhisattva image 36 Chinese feet (3 zhang 6 chi) tall on the vacant land at the monastery.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasaka Pei Zhenghui recounted (reproduced from *Mind Dharma* (*Xinfa*) magazine):

Cixing Chan Monastery as it is now (1956): besides the Buddha Hall, the Left Courtyard, and the guest hall, a dining hall, a retreat room, and a thatched hut have also been added, one building each. On the left is the Dragon-Subduing Rock, and on the right is the Tiger-Taming Stream. The layout is superb, spread out like stars and chessmen. Its white walls and red pillars stand out within the embrace of countless mountains. Truly it can be said to capture the spiritual energy of heaven and earth and to surpass the hidden workings of creation. Its secluded beauty is in no way inferior to that of the Four Great Famous Mountains and the Eight Great Lesser Mountains of China; even compared with Nanhua Monastery, the Way-place of the Sixth Patriarch, it is not the least bit inferior!

**[Addendum 2]** Lay Buddhist Liu Mianzhai recounted (reproduced from *Mind Dharma* magazine):

Adornment Peak holds Azure Dragon Ridge at its left; on the ridge towers the Dragon-Subduing Rock, and on the rock a golden dragon has been sculpted, made by craftsmen at the Venerable Master's command. Its scales tumble and its claws dance, so lifelike it seems alive. Bright as lightning, it twists and changes; rumbling like thunder, it roars and bellows. Its might strikes fear into goblins and sprites, and its spirit cows evil demons. Awe-inspiring, it bears the air of the "Dragon's Swift Vigor Samadhi."

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Xing recounted on September 25, 2009:

The stone dragon at Cixing Monastery was made by a lay Buddhist. Although Cixing Monastery is a very good place, a Buddha-land, it also has a flaw. What flaw? In terms of fengshui, the Azure Dragon is on the left and the White Tiger is on the right, and here the White Tiger position is higher than the Azure Dragon position. This harms people and livestock: people living there are prone to injuries or accidents, and it's hard for people to keep living there. So the Venerable Master had a dragon made at the Azure Dragon position, so that the Azure Dragon position would be higher than the White Tiger position. After the dragon was finished, the Master told everyone that now they could live there in peace. Those were the causes and conditions that brought about this stone dragon.

Below the dragon there are rocks on which you can sit in meditation.

**[Addendum 4]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Wei's oral account:

Back then a dragon really did often appear around Cixing Monastery. With my own eyes I saw a dragon cultivating together with the assembly in the Buddha Hall.

**[Addendum 5]** Upasaka Huang Ketai recounted:

This dragon was built on the hillside at Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island. After the dragon was finished, a pool of water appeared on the slope below, right where the dragon's mouth was facing. This was truly very unusual. From this you can see that it is absolutely true that the Master had gods and dragons as Dharma protectors.

## 48. Miraculous Foresight for a Hundred Tables

In the spring of 1954 (the jiawu year), the restoration of the villa of the Left Courtyard and of the guest hall at Cixing Chan Monastery was completed, and preparations were made to hold the Opening-the-Light ceremony for the Three Sages of the West on the sixteenth day of the third lunar month (April 14 in the Gregorian calendar), the birthday of Cundi Bodhisattva...

Starting on the thirteenth day of the third month, the *Great Compassion Jeweled Repentance* was bowed for three days in a row, and on the evening of the fifteenth the *Three Great Masters' Flaming Mouth* ceremony was performed. It was just at the turn of spring and summer, and it had been drizzling for days on end; the land seemed shrouded in a layer of white gauze, and beyond ten paces people were just blurry shapes. Dark clouds had blanketed the sky for many days in a row, and it looked as though the rain would go on for a good while yet. Everyone was worried that the great ceremony would be spoiled by the rain. Who would have thought that on the evening of the fifteenth the clouds would suddenly scatter and the rain stop? On the day of the Opening-the-Light ceremony there was neither sun nor rain; the breeze was fresh and the air crisp, and it was very comfortable.

That day, from morning until noon, people came from Lantau Island, from Castle Peak and Tsuen Wan, and even from Hong Kong and Kowloon, arriving in a continuous stream, crowding in front and behind. Local gentry and merchants, schools and organizations, fishermen, farmers, woodcutters, and peddlers, faithful devotees from all walks of life, more than 1,000 people in all, attended the Opening-the-Light ceremony, and all 100 tables of vegetarian food that had been prepared were filled.

At ten o'clock there was a Universal Buddha Ceremony, and at noon the Opening-the-Light ceremony was held. The Buddha Hall was a sea of heads, packed so tightly inside and out that not even a drop of water could have trickled through. Local people said that this Opening-the-Light ceremony for the Left Courtyard of Cixing Chan Monastery broke the island's attendance record for any celebration in several decades.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I've never had much affinity with people, but I do have a bit of affinity with heaven. In Hong Kong, when I held the Opening-the-Light ceremony on Lantau Island (Cixing Monastery was preparing to hold its Opening-the-Light ceremony on the sixteenth day of the third lunar month), on the fourteenth and fifteenth of the third month it rained very heavily. It poured for several days in a row, the ground was soaked (it was all damp), and the robes people wore got completely wet, as if they had been washed in water.

The day before the Opening-the-Light ceremony, I had prepared a hundred tables of vegetarian food. That Liang Guo Xiao said to me, "Shifu! It's raining today, and you've prepared so much food. What if nobody comes? With the rain, nobody will come, and won't all this food spoil and be left over...?" When she finished saying this, I gave her a scolding. I said, "You don't know what you're talking about! Whether it spoils or not, even if it does spoil, I won't make you eat it! Why do you care whether it spoils or not? Why do you have to meddle so much?" I scolded her until she didn't dare say anything more. Because "The Dharma does not arise on its own; it arises in dependence on circumstances," since a disciple had come raising this kind of request, I had to find a way to make it clear up! I sat down there and called these dragon disciples of mine: "Dragon disciples, hurry up and stop the rain! I'm holding an Opening-the-Light ceremony here; how can you keep on raining? Then people won't come!" I was still sitting there in meditation when they saw the ground dry up on its own; the water on the ground was all gone, and it stopped raining. That was on the evening of the fifteenth. The next day was the sixteenth. There was neither sun nor rain, and going up and down the mountain, people didn't sweat too much.

Normally, to come from Hong Kong to Lantau Island by boat, you had to catch the boat at 6:00 in the morning. By noon that day, who knows how many people had come. We had 97 tables seated, at 12 people a table, so about 1,000-odd people came. After the Dharma assembly ended, everyone left, and strangely enough, as soon as everyone was on the boats, it started raining again, and it went on raining for several days. As for the three tables of food that were left over, there were still 50 or 60 people staying on the mountain that night, and they ate it all up in one meal. So all the vegetarian food I had prepared got eaten.

Speaking of this, it was truly inconceivable!

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

When people attended Dharma assemblies at Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island, because it was such a long way, everyone would stay overnight at the monastery, so the rooms and beds were usually overcrowded, and many people slept sitting up in the Buddha Hall.

I remember that during the Dharma assembly for the Opening-the-Light ceremony of the Left Courtyard of Cixing Chan Monastery in 1954, many of the Dharma brothers and sisters stayed overnight on Lantau Island, and the young girls all stayed in the same room. Early in the morning, the person assigned to strike the board ran into the room and beat it loudly, and soon the resonant sound of the bell drifted to our ears from the Buddha Hall.

One of the girls said, "I can't get up; you all go to morning recitation!" The quilts were nice and warm, and once someone had taken the lead, everyone was glad to laze about in comfort a little longer, so no one got ready to get up for morning recitation.

Suddenly Guo Ping, the Dharma sister who lived at the monastery, rushed into the room in a fluster and cried in a low voice, "Hurry up and get up! The Master has just lost his temper. He said that whoever doesn't get up for morning recitation will be driven down the mountain! The Master also said, 'One good disciple is enough!'" In less than ten minutes, everyone in the room was standing in the Buddha Hall, wearing their robes, with their lay precept sashes on. From then on, I was afraid the Master would expel me from the monastery, and I didn't dare skip morning recitation.

Below Cixing Monastery there was a campsite where people often camped and had fun. One night the campers heard the sound of a horn in the middle of the night. The next day they came up to the monastery and asked why the horn had been blown so early. Someone answered, "No one blew a horn; we don't even have a horn." It is said that the mountain spirits had blown their horns to gather their retinues to attend morning recitation.

**[Addendum 2]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Ding's oral account:

When the Master invited Dharma Master Xu Lang to perform the Flaming Mouth ceremony on Lantau Island, ghosts were heard talking, and there were also male and female ghosts who came rushing to get the food.

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Xing recounted on September 26, 2000:

Below and in front of Cixing Monastery there was a campsite where people often camped and had fun. One night, at three in the morning, the campers heard the sound of a horn. The next day they came up the mountain to ask why the horn had been blown so early. Dharma Master Guo Yi said that no one had blown a horn; we didn't even have a horn. It is said that the mountain spirits had blown their horns to assemble their retinues and followers to attend morning recitation.

In the year after the Master entered perfect stillness (1996), we respectfully escorted the Master's sharira [Tr.: relics; the crystalline remains found after the cremation of an accomplished cultivator] back to Hong Kong. They went first to the Buddhist Lecture Hall and were then taken up to Cixing Monastery. When we reached the foot of the mountain below Cixing Monastery, Dharma Master Heng Xiang, another lay Buddhist, and I drove up the mountain together. As we were going up, an elderly lay Buddhist at Cixing Monastery was looking out from the open courtyard and saw many people holding large five-colored banners and carrying a sedan chair coming to welcome the Master. So the Master had actually arrived back at Cixing Monastery before us. This lay Buddhist later told us that those people were probably mountain spirits.

## 49. Sagely Conditions in the Yiwei Year

The Right Courtyard of Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island, built by the Venerable Master, was largely completed by the end of the yiwei year (1955), though the interior decoration still had to be done and Buddha images and Buddhist implements added. Lantau Island is quiet, secluded, and vast; one has to cross mountains and ridges to reach it. Its scenery is extraordinary, and it is truly a superb place for cultivation...

Although Guoqing Chan Monastery had been cruelly ravaged during the Japanese occupation, the revival of the monastery now was truly a sagely condition. For this reason, at the Spring Festival of the yiwei year, the Venerable Master composed a verse:

> Joyfully we meet the year yiwei in the cycle;  
> A thousand blessings gather like clouds, and the multitudes rejoice.  
> Bells ring and drums resound, reaching through to the mind-ground;  
> Kites soar and fish leap, revealing the heaven of the nature.
>
> <small>欣逢歲次乙未年，千祥雲集萬眾歡。鐘鳴鼓響通心地，鸢飛魚躍見性天。</small>

> Atomic and hydrogen bombs add to the calamities of the kalpa;  
> The Dharma ship sailing the seas rescues women and men.  
> Formation, dwelling, decay, and emptiness are nothing to marvel at;  
> Kindness, compassion, joy, and giving have sagely conditions.
>
> <small>原子氫氣增劫運，航海法*救女男。成住壞空無足怪，慈悲喜捨有聖緣。</small>

## 50. The Jeweled Hall Shines Anew

In the summer of 1956 (the bingshen year), the Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero in the Right Courtyard of Cixing Chan Monastery was completed. On the nineteenth day of the sixth lunar month (July 26), the anniversary of Guanyin Bodhisattva's attainment of the Way, a ceremony was held for the completion of the Jeweled Hall together with the Opening-the-Light of the golden images of the Three Tathagatas...

After the Opening-the-Light ceremony for the Buddha Hall of the Left Courtyard, the Hall of the Three Sages of the West, had been brought to a successful close, here amid the fine mountains and waters of Ling Wui Shan the Venerable Master spent another two years of arduous effort, personally drawing up the plans and selecting the timber and stone, and one after another completed the reconstruction of the Right Courtyard (the site of the ruins of Guoqing Chan Monastery): the Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero and the Patriarchs Hall on the first floor, and the Sutra Library and the living quarters on the second floor. He also gradually added a number of facilities. Although the Jeweled Hall is not grand in scale, it has a particularly majestic air. In particular, the solemnity of the golden Buddha images, the wonder of the sounding bells, drums, and chimes, and the marvel of the beams and pillars that seem to hold up the sky all came about through the Venerable Master's virtue, and display its full majesty.

On this day, the Buddha-sun shone more brightly and the ancient monastery shone anew. It was the anniversary of Guanyin Bodhisattva's attainment of the Way, and also a day of great rejoicing for Lantau Island. From the surrounding villages near and far, and from as far away as the monasteries of Hong Kong and Kowloon, venerable Elders and countless patrons and devotees, and even representatives of the police station in Tai O, came on hearing the news. They presented calligraphy to celebrate the happy occasion and mirror plaques to offer congratulations, and took part in the ceremony for the completion of the Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero together with the Opening-the-Light of the golden images of the Three Tathagatas. The Venerable Master invited the Elders of the various monasteries to preside together over the purification by sprinkling, the ceremony celebrating the completion, the Opening-the-Light Dharma assembly, and the life-liberation ceremony, and to pray for blessings: favorable winds and timely rains, a nation at peace and a people secure, abundant harvests of the five grains, and good fortune in all things. Dharma Master Xu Lang chanted in a lofty Brahma voice; the presiding Dharma Master offered incense and sprinkled water for purification, then, holding a mirror, dotted the eyes to open the light, as Buddhist disciples from the ten directions bore witness together to the Jeweled Hall shining anew.

**[Addendum 1]** The mirror plaques presented by the greatly virtuous ones have, over half a century, been badly damaged by damp, and only a few traces of writing remain. The few mirror plaques that are still faintly legible are now recorded below: (omitted)

**[Addendum 2]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi recounted on September 25, 2000:

After the Opening-the-Light ceremony at Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island, we began holding Guanyin sessions and Amitabha sessions, and every session drew more than a hundred people. In the evenings there were sutra lectures until 10:00, and every time, those who came to hear the sutras had to climb several hundred stone steps up the mountain to listen. At that time there were always more than 40 or 50 people who came to listen to the sutra lectures.

After the Buddhist services each day, I would shuttle back and forth between the two places, the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley and Cixing Monastery. Every day I went up the mountain at 4:00 in the afternoon, stayed until 2:00 in the middle of the night, and then started back down the mountain. Every single day like that.

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi's oral account, given on January 7, 2001:

A fengshui master said that the fengshui here was very good and that Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island would produce Arhats in the future; the places where the Master had sat in meditation would all emit light.

**[Addendum 4]** Cixing Monastery (reproduced from *Buddhist Temples of Hong Kong* (*Xiangjiang Fanyu*)):

Cixing Monastery, halfway up Man Cheung Po in southwestern Lantau Island, is an ancient monastery. The original monastery was founded in 1930, built by Great Master Fa Chuan, and was formerly called "Guoqing Monastery." Sadly, when Hong Kong fell, it was burned down by the Japanese army, and Great Master Fa Chuan also died a martyr's death. In the autumn of 1953, Dong Guo Qi, a lay Buddhist devoted to the work of Buddhism, gave Dong's two-story villa, which stood beside Guoqing Monastery on its left, to Dharma Master To Lun for use in enshrining the Buddha. Through this opportunity, the Dharma Master consulted with the people concerned with Guoqing Monastery and, after obtaining their consent, planned and carried out the combined reconstruction of the old monastery and the villa. First the villa was repaired and named the Left Courtyard of Cixing Monastery. The Triple Jewel is enshrined within it, and it remains intact to this day. Later, Dharma Master To Lun raised more funds and built the present Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero, and the Kuomintang elder statesman Yu Youren specially inscribed for Dharma Master To Lun the plaque reading "Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero." In 1962 Dharma Master To Lun went to America to spread Buddhism widely, and he founded the Dharma Realm Buddhist Association.

While Dharma Master To Lun was away from Hong Kong, he entrusted Master Fa Zang with serving as acting abbot of Cixing Monastery, while the women's section was run by Shi Guo Li and Shi Guo Ren (that is, Dharma Master Heng Yi and Dharma Master Heng Qi). Dharma Master Fa Zang was a Dharma heir of Elder Master Tan Xu, a greatly virtuous monk of the Tiantai School. Dharma Master Fa Zang spent over a year repairing the monastery and completed living quarters and guest rooms, and Elder Tan personally inscribed a plaque reading "Abode of Pure Joy," which is still there today. Through the repairs carried out by the monastery's residents, the Buddha Hall for the first time acquired the grandeur of a glazed-tile roof and a majestic hall.

In America, Dharma Master To Lun accepted many American monastics as disciples. At the end of 1972, three of his American disciples came to Hong Kong to stay at Cixing Monastery and carried out work on the English translation of the *Dharma Flower Sutra*, and Cixing Monastery thus became the first Buddhist monastery in Hong Kong to have foreign monks. Dharma Master Fa Zang left Cixing Monastery in 1974. After Dharma Master To Lun entered perfect stillness and was cremated, he left behind thousands of sharira, some of which were specially brought back to Cixing Monastery to be enshrined.

Encouraging the Chan Cultivator Heng Bo in Helping to Translate the *Dharma Flower Sutra*

Composed by Hsuan Hua on May 22, 1972

> Affinities formed in former lives have now borne fruit;  
> In the same boat we help one another across, ferrying those lost in confusion.  
> When waves surge up to the sky, the ship sails steady;  
> When winds come sweeping the ground, do not be alarmed.
>
> <small>結緣前生果已成，同舟共濟度迷蒙。沖天浪起船行穩，卷地風來且莫驚。</small>

> The burden is heavy and the road is long as you promote the teaching;  
> Patience, precepts, vigor, and Chan: practice the rules in person.  
> On the day the translation of the Wonderful Dharma is complete,  
> At the Dragon Flower Assembly your special merit will be celebrated.
>
> <small>任重道遠倡教化，忍戒精禅律實躬。妙法翻譯圓滿日，龍華大會慶殊功。</small>

> Born with a wholesome nature,  
> Not deeply stained by worldly conditions,  
> You met good friends early on  
> And leapt out of the ford of confusion.  
> Making light of wealth and valuing righteousness,  
> You resolutely left the dust.  
> Upholding the precept against money,  
> You break through the mind of greed and anger.
>
> <small>天生善性，染緣未深。早逢良友，跳出迷津。輕財重義，毅然離塵。持金錢戒，破貪嗔心。</small>

> Helping to translate the *Dharma Flower*,  
> You polish the text in search of the truth.  
> Donning the armor of vigor,  
> Your courage surpasses all your peers.  
> Ascend to the ground of non-retreat  
> And pay homage to the World Honored One.  
> Strive on, strive on,  
> And do not fail my words.
>
> <small>助譯法華，潤色求真。披精進甲，勇冠群倫。登不退地，朝觐世尊。勉勵勉勵，勿負吾言。</small>

Sending Off and Encouraging the Chan Cultivator Heng Jing as He Goes to Taiwan and Hong Kong to Propagate the Dharma: In Commemoration

Composed by Hsuan Hua on December 12, 1971

> In the space age, the Proper Dharma rises to meet the times;  
> Win glory for the teaching and do not shrink from hardship.  
> East and West are nothing but a single unity;  
> North and South have never left the divisions of the six consciousnesses.
>
> <small>太空正法應運興，為教爭光莫辭辛。東西不外一元化，南北未出六識分。</small>

> The mind, the Buddha, and living beings are not different;  
> In beings endowed with spirit, the enlightened nature is shallow or deep.  
> Patience, vigor, and the perfection of prajna;  
> Shila and dhyana: be diligent at every moment.
>
> <small>心佛眾生無差別，性覺含靈有淺深。忍辱精進般若度，屍羅禅定時刻勤。</small>

Sending Off and Encouraging the American Bhikshu, the Chan Cultivator Heng Shou, Who Is Going to Propagate the Dharma at the Invitation of Hong Kong Buddhists

Composed by Hsuan Hua on November 3, 1972

People can propagate the Dharma; it is not the Dharma that propagates people. The Dharma has no Proper or Ending Age; it is people who are good or bad. Habituated to things, people no longer notice them, and one after another they follow along until it becomes the prevailing custom: they treat the Buddhadharma as idle theorizing and do not put it into practice themselves, regard cultivation as empty talk, and even go so far as to put on a hypocritical show of solemnity. This truly deserves attention. The worthy should strive to turn back this decadent custom, reform the standards of the Sangha, and handle affairs with care. By no means let down the earnest intent behind my years of teaching and guidance. Be determined that Buddhism shall flourish and spread, and that the Proper Dharma shall abide in the world. Remember this well! I also present you with a verse:

> Cultivate blessings, cultivate wisdom, cultivate Chan contemplation;  
> Plant many good causes and form few enmities.  
> Kindness enters the three periods of time, constantly handed on;  
> Transformation began in the Six Dynasties, and truly it came first.
>
> <small>修福修慧修禅觀，多種善因少結冤。慈入三世恆相授，化始六朝果然先。</small>

> The Saha world and Ultimate Bliss appear only from the mind;  
> Hells and heavens are contained within the Dharma Realm.  
> With no thought and no birth, you are always at ease;  
> The mani jewel is perfect from the very beginning.
>
> <small>娑婆極樂惟心現，地獄天堂法界含。無念無生常自在，摩尼寶珠本來圓。</small>

Behind Cixing Monastery, on the hillside, there are three rock faces in a row. A lay Buddhist named Guo Sheng made use of the natural terrain to sculpt a flying dragon of reinforced concrete. The dragon is about four meters long, baring its fangs and brandishing its claws, lively in form and splendid in seven colors, and it has become one of the scenic sights of western Lantau. Unfortunately, the mountain path is now overgrown with weeds and creepers, so it can only be viewed from afar and not admired up close. Behind the monastery a spring wells up from a crevice in the rocks, flowing all year round and never drying up even in drought, which is inconceivable. Dharma Master To Lun named it Dragon's Mouth Water. A water-quality specialist once had the spring water tested and found it extremely pure, entirely free of impurities.

Cixing Monastery is a Buddhist monastery that is not very open to the public. At present the men's section of Cixing Monastery is managed by Bhikshu Shi Heng Xing and other monastics sent by the Dharma Realm Buddhist Association, and the monastery still keeps the rule of eating one meal a day, at midday.

## 51. The Monk's Bowl Subdues a Dragon

Not long after Cixing Chan Monastery was built, a green snake, neither large nor small, often appeared. So as not to alarm the assembly, it was taken far away and released, but as soon as it was released it came right back. One day the snake appeared again, and there was the Venerable Master, setting his bowl on the ground...

Not long after the Opening-the-Light ceremony at Cixing Chan Monastery, several laywomen left the home-life one after another. The Venerable Master gave his consent for Dharma Master Heng Yi and Upasika Tang Guo Yun to bring back under cultivation the rice paddies left behind by the long-abandoned Guoqing Chan Monastery (Cixing Chan Monastery's predecessor), so as to lighten the monastery's burden of supplying rice and grain. Every day they went to work at sunrise and rested at sunset. After coming back from the fields, they still had to recite the Buddha's name ten thousand times and recite the Great Compassion Mantra 49 times. At night they went to bed very late, and they rose at three o'clock in the morning.

One evening, as they were returning to the monastery, they suddenly saw a green snake crawling on the open ground in front of the women's dormitory, and everyone got a fright. Unexpectedly, a few days later it appeared again. Dharma Master Heng Yi caught the snake in a cloth bag and took it out into the wilds to release it. But no sooner had she stepped through the main door of the Buddha Hall than she saw the same snake crawling on the open ground again. This time, after catching the snake, she sealed it in an iron can with the lid shut tight and took it to a place very far away. But when she undid the lid to let it out, the snake had vanished without a trace, and no one knew where it had gone.

It had clearly been inside the iron can, so how could it have disappeared? As it turned out, the snake had gone back to the monastery yet again. This happened again and again, time after time; the snake simply could not be sent away. Everyone was terrified and at their wits' end, so all they could do was wait until the Venerable Master came back to Lantau Island and report the facts to him. The Venerable Master said, "If it comes out and disturbs the assembly again, put it in my bowl and send it to Cha Kwo Ling to be released."

One day, when the Venerable Master was at Cixing Monastery, the snake appeared again. The Venerable Master set his bowl on the ground, formed a mudra, and recited a mantra several times. Before long the snake crawled into the bowl and lay coiled up inside it, perfectly still. Then Dharma Master Heng Yi put the bowl into a cloth bag, and a layperson was found to take it down the mountain to a place very far away. From then on the snake was never seen again.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi's oral account, given on January 7, 2001:

On the hill behind Cixing Monastery there are a Snake Cave, an Immortals' Cave, a Monkey Cave, and a Buddha Cave. One day, as I was passing the Monkey Cave, a monkey knelt and bowed down to me. But this monkey had not met the Master, so I told the Master that there was a monkey who wanted to take refuge. (The Master held a refuge ceremony on the tenth day of every lunar month.) Later, when we returned to Lantau Island, we saw this monkey holding a refuge certificate in its hands and bowing over and over.

**[Addendum 2]** Snakes Come to Hear the Dharma

On Sunday evening, June 20, 1982, a Dharma assembly was in session at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas, and the disciples of the fourfold assembly were circumambulating the Hall of Ten Thousand Buddhas, reciting "Namo Amitabha Buddha" in unison. When they returned to the hall, they were startled to discover a green snake with mottled markings, about three or four feet long, lying beside the front door of the Buddha Hall. Someone wanted to remove the snake, but the Venerable Master smiled and said, "There's no need. All living beings have the Buddha-nature. This snake has come to draw near to the Triple Jewel and to listen to the Dharma. It should not be driven away."

What was strange was that this snake was unlike ordinary snakes: it lay stretched out straight on the ground. Although nearly a hundred people were coming and going in front of it and behind it, it showed no fear at all and was gentle and docile. Shortly afterward the Venerable Master ascended the Dharma seat and said, "Tell it that it may come in now to listen to the Dharma!" At once the snake crawled from the front door into the hall, then crawled from the left side over to the right side, as though circumambulating the Buddha once, and then lay down in front of the Dharma seat, for all the world as though it were kneeling before the Buddha listening to the Venerable Master speak the Dharma. (Reproduced from the editor's note preceding the article "Snakes Come to Hear the Dharma" in *Venerable Master Hua's Talks on Dharma*, Volume 4.)

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Today, as everyone was entering the Buddha Hall, you saw a snake. It too is one of the living beings. But because it manifests in the body of a snake, people take fright at the sight of it; they see it but do not recognize it, and some are even scared stiff. But this snake has come to draw near to the Triple Jewel, to listen to the sutras and hear the Dharma. Otherwise, how could it lie so well-behaved on the threshold without moving at all?

Although at first glance it looks like a small snake, in fact it knows how to undergo a thousand changes and ten thousand transformations. It can make itself invisible, appear small or grow large, and it can also ride the clouds and mount the mists, flying freely through the sky. It's just that we can't know what it is capable of. In the future, when this snake succeeds in its cultivation, it too will be able to attain the Way. The Buddhist sutras speak of "the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division," and it belongs to one of these divisions, the Mahoraga, which is a great python.

Looking into this snake's origins: it may have been a high minister of a small state during the Zhou dynasty. But he harbored treacherous schemes and plotted to usurp the throne. He hid in his sleeve a dagger that had been steeped in poisoned wine and stabbed the king. But not only did this deed fail to win him the throne, it brought on the wrath of Heaven and the resentment of the people. As he lay dying, the minister gave rise to great hatred, and after death he fell into the body of a snake and turned into a giant python.

In the world there are two forces: one is yin and one is yang; you could also say one is good and one is evil. Those who follow the good cultivate both blessings and wisdom to the full, and they can become immortals, become sages, become Bodhisattvas, and become Buddhas. The power of the evil ones is also great, but they devote themselves to dark dealings that cannot bear the light of day, taking revenge on one another and killing one another, and so they brew up an incomparably huge miasma of resentment. The world is everywhere full of venomous snakes and venomous dragons. These venomous snakes and venomous dragons cannot be seen with ordinary physical eyes, yet they truly pervade the land. These venomous snakes take shape from the greed, hatred, and delusion accumulated in people's minds, and they wreak havoc everywhere. The natural disasters and man-made calamities of the world, flying corpses and deviant ghosts, goblins, demons, and nightmare-spirits, the spread of plagues, and all kinds of strange incurable diseases are all the mischief of these venomous snakes. These evil dragons and plague ghosts specialize in releasing poisonous vapors that confuse and disturb people's minds; or else, as mountain sprites and water goblins, they steal people's vital essence, leaving people terrified and deranged. Therefore all the kinds of mental illness so common nowadays, such as schizophrenia, madness, and phobias, as well as all kinds of stubborn physical ailments, are nothing other than these demons, ghosts, and goblins stirring up waves and wreaking havoc on living beings. Modern psychology and medicine do not deeply understand the principle behind this and cannot prescribe the right medicine for the illness, so their cure rates are far from satisfactory. Of course, only the Buddhadharma is the most ultimate miraculous medicine, the wondrous prescription for dispelling calamities and quelling disasters!

This venomous snake, driven by its hatred, strove hard at its cultivation and then became a spirit creature. Its magic arts grew stronger day by day, until in time it could penetrate heaven and earth, and it always took pleasure in tormenting and harming the common people. These days, who knows how many cases there are like this snake's? I hope they will quickly give rise to repentance and no longer act as evil monsters bringing calamity upon the people.

Later, by the time of the Song dynasty, this snake had become a venomous dragon. In a great river it whipped up winds and overturned the rains, killing countless fishermen and ferrymen. At that time the people of the area lived in fear and dread, in extreme anxiety. Fortunately, a certain sagely monk of great Dharma power came especially to rescue them. Holding his monk's staff, he stood on the riverbank and loudly rebuked the venomous dragon. Hearing his voice, the venomous dragon flew into a rage and brought out its powers, manifesting the body of a giant python several thousand zhang long. But this Dharma Master was sustained by the power of the Vajra Samadhi and had no fear at all. Using the methods of summoning and subduing, after a life-and-death struggle lasting three days and three nights, he finally brought the venomous dragon to submission. Evil cannot overcome the proper: the venomous dragon's evil arts were broken, and having no other choice, it had to surrender. The Dharma Master spoke the Dharma for it: everything is suffering, empty, impermanent, and without self, and only karma follows one. He also admonished it that it should bring forth a compassionate heart and use its magic arts to benefit living beings, and must not harm living creatures. On hearing the Dharma, the snake suddenly awakened and obtained the pure eye. The Dharma Master then transmitted to it the Three Refuges and the Five Precepts and ordered it to go to a certain mountain cave to cultivate and to stop disturbing the people. The venomous dragon gladly obeyed. From the Song dynasty until now, several hundred years have passed. By now this dragon has become one of the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division, with spiritual powers and transformations at its command.

(Editor's note: At this point a Bhikshuni in the women's section said, "Several weeks ago we saw this snake near the women's Buddha hall at Joyous Giving House. Because we were afraid it might be a rattlesnake, we put it in a can, covered it with a hard wooden board, and got ready to take it to a fairly distant grove to release it, so that it wouldn't disturb the residents. But as soon as we turned around and opened the iron can, the snake was no longer there. Logically, there was no way it could have escaped. No wonder! It turns out it has spiritual powers.")

I've known this snake for a long time. More than 20 years ago, at Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island in Hong Kong, it often came to the monastery to listen to the sutras. Although it never troubled or harmed anyone, living beings were all afraid of its snake form. Once, someone also put it in an iron can and carried it several miles away to the mountain forest, intending to release it there, but when they opened the can and looked, the snake had vanished into thin air! And now that the Buddhadharma has come to the West, it too has not shrunk from crossing the oceans to come to America to protect the Dharma.

Living beings who study Buddhism must deeply believe in cause and effect. Do not plant evil causes, or you will reap evil fruits; if you plant good causes, you will certainly reap good fruits. Cause and effect do not err by a hair's breadth: "If you kill another man's father, others will kill your father; if you kill another man's elder brother, others will kill your elder brother." Do not be so unscrupulous as to kill people just to gain some advantage. If you harm others, in the end it is yourself you harm. Heaven has the virtue of cherishing life; Heaven does not want anyone to do things that trouble and harm living beings. So do not get cause and effect wrong. As the sayings go, "One slip of the foot brings a thousand ages of regret," and "Once the human body is lost, it is not regained in ten thousand eons." You can disbelieve anything else, but you cannot disbelieve in cause and effect. To deny cause and effect is extremely dangerous.

"Bodhisattvas fear causes, not effects." On the causal ground they are very careful to plant good causes, and as for the evil causes they planted without knowing it, they calmly accept the evil fruits. They face reality and accept their retribution, never blaming heaven or other people. On the other hand, "Living beings fear effects, not causes." When planting causes they are careless and muddled, acting recklessly and wildly. When it comes time to undergo the retribution, they are afraid, and they blame heaven and other people, saying, "Why do I have to suffer this torment? Why do I have to undergo such an unfair retribution?" But they never stop to think: if they had not created such causes in the past, how could they be undergoing such retribution?

Today we have met with this causal condition: one of the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division has appeared in person to speak the Dharma, so I am taking this opportunity to say a little about its causes and effects. "Just one wrong move, and the whole game of chess is lost." With one mistaken thought, it is not easy to turn back a calamitous fate. Fortunately it has been able to take refuge with the Triple Jewel, and in the future it will still have the chance to understand the mind and see the nature.

If any of you don't believe this account of causes and conditions, you're welcome to go try it for yourselves. If you believe in cause and effect, you should not harbor the slightest doubt.

## 52. Guo Man Takes Refuge

In 1956, Upasika Yu Guo Man broke a bone in her hand. The bonesetter wrote a prescription that used dragon bone [Tr.: fossilized animal bone used in Chinese medicine] as medicine. Guo Man said, "I can't use this medicine. I'm a vegetarian!" The lay Buddhist Yao Guo Bai, who was standing nearby, heard this and asked, "Who is your teacher?"...

"I haven't taken refuge yet, but at Guanyin Cave on Fu Yung Shan I once met a Dharma Master who had real cultivation, and now I have no way to find him," Yu Guo Man replied. "Who is he?" "He burned a string of recitation beads and a swastika onto his body with incense." "Ah! He's my teacher!"

When Yu Guo Man heard this she was overjoyed. Although she was stout, she wasn't daunted by the more than 300 stone steps, and she hurried to Western Bliss Gardens to pay her respects to the Venerable Master and ask to take refuge.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

After I left Guanyin Cave on Fu Yung Shan, Yu Guo Man looked for me everywhere, asking everyone she met. Some people knew but wouldn't tell her. Why? They were jealous of her, so they wouldn't tell her my address. Yu Guo Man searched for two years before she found me. She was very happy and said, "I've been looking everywhere for you, Dharma Master. I searched for two years and couldn't find you, and now I've finally found you!" After that she went to Western Bliss Gardens, and after going a few times, she took refuge together with her son and her daughter-in-law, Xue Peilan.

This person had a special strength. What strength? She didn't spend money on herself. Her sons, daughters, and grandchildren often gave her money, and she used all of it to do works of merit and virtue. So when I was in Hong Kong, she was the most generous-hearted of my disciples and did a great many works of merit and virtue every year. She didn't put up money of her own either; she would see which of her grandsons and granddaughters had money and then seek donations from them, having them do works of merit and virtue. That was her clever method.

## 53. The Insects in the Tree Move Away

At Western Bliss Gardens there was a tree covered with insects, too many to count, and the insects crawled all over the place. Yu Guo Man said she would never dare come again, because as soon as she saw insects, she couldn't even walk anymore...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

After Yu Guo Man took refuge, around the nineteenth day of the sixth month she went to Western Bliss Gardens to see me. She saw that there was a peach tree at Western Bliss Gardens with no telling how many insects on it; on every leaf there were three or four crawling about. It frightened her so...

Ever since she was born she had been afraid of monks, and also afraid of crawling insects. As soon as she saw insects, her hands and feet went limp; she couldn't take a step and couldn't walk.

She liked to recite the Buddha's name. I said, "Now that you've found me, you can come often to bow to the Buddhas!" She said, "Shifu, I'd like to come here to bow to the Buddhas, but I'm terribly afraid of insects. On this peach tree of yours there are three or four insects on a single leaf, and the whole tree has thousands upon thousands of insects on it. I'm scared to death! That's the one thing I'm afraid of. With so many insects here, how can I dare to come?"

Then and there I said to her, "Don't be afraid! Tomorrow they'll be gone." The next day she came here full of faith, and sure enough, there wasn't a single insect left. It was a very big tree, as tall as two people, with thousands upon thousands of insects on it, and in a single day they had all moved away. They were gone, and no one knew where they had moved to. From then on she had even more faith.

And don't go saying, "Oh! I know. Shifu must have sprayed some insecticide and killed them all." Ha ha! I would have liked to use insecticide, but I had no money to buy any, so I just blew a few breaths at them, "Whoo, whoo, whoo!" and they all ran off! Who knows where they moved to.

If I didn't tell you, none of you would know. You see so many insects, but in fact they aren't insects at all. These insects are dragon disciples who have taken refuge with me. They came to the monastery to bow to the Buddhas. Otherwise, how could it be that when I said one sentence, "Come tomorrow and they'll be gone!" they were gone? These insects were also manifestations of the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division, displaying a bit of their spiritual efficacy. From this you can see that the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division are manifesting their bodies all the time, but when people see them they don't know it; if no one points it out, they don't recognize them. Today I'm explaining it clearly for you; if I didn't tell you, you wouldn't understand a thing.

Once, a fellow countryman from Manchuria came to see me. He was also one of my former refuge disciples, and we were very happy to meet again in a strange land. As he was leaving, I told him to come to the Way-place often from then on. He said, "I'm not going to come anymore. Look at all those big grubs on the leaves of the peach tree at the monastery gate. How frightening!" I said to him, "Dragons and snakes all mixed together! If you're afraid of them, I'll tell them to leave. I guarantee that when you come tomorrow, you won't see a single one." Sure enough, when he came the next day to attend the Dharma assembly, there really wasn't a single one left. They were just manifesting a form for you to see, that's all.

## 54. A Weasel Takes Refuge

Before long, whenever Yu Guo Man heard the drumbeats of a repentance ceremony, her head would ache. No matter who was beating the drum, not only did her head ache as though it would split, but fear arose in her mind; above all, she was most afraid of seeing strangers. Even the doctors were helpless before her strange illness...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

This illness of hers was very strange. It was simply fear. Fear of what? She was afraid of people and afraid of strangers. No matter where she was, she would always run off and sit in a corner where no one could see her. She was terribly afraid of people, and she also had a big temper and was always flying into a rage. She was also afraid of sounds, and above all she was afraid of me, so terribly afraid of me that she didn't even dare look at me. She went to doctors for this illness, but after one or two months of treatment she still wasn't better. One day she said to me, "Shifu! I used to have great faith in you, but now, I don't know why, I'm so terribly afraid of you. Neither Chinese doctors nor Western doctors can cure this illness of mine. What's going on?"

Now I'll tell you: the reason she was afraid of everything was that a weasel was making mischief. That weasel wanted to take refuge with the Triple Jewel. When she first told me about it, I saw that the conditions hadn't arrived yet. She was supposed to be ill for 100 days, and at that point she had only been ill for 30-some days. I said, "Wait a while longer. After some time passes, you'll gradually get better! For example, if you're supposed to get better after 100 days, then when you reach 80 days I can make you better. Being 20 days short is all right, but now you're still half the time short, so it can't be done!"

On the afternoon of the fourteenth day of the second month (lunar calendar), I saw her and said, "Guo Man, today your illness should get better! Go kneel before the Buddha and bow, and don't stop bowing until I tell you to stop." So she bowed and bowed and bowed and bowed... for about half an hour. After this weasel had taken refuge with me, I said, "Now your illness is cured; you're all right!" With just that one sentence she immediately stopped being afraid, and her illness was completely gone. That's why it's said, "Dharma or no Dharma, it all comes down to one sentence before the altar." It was just that one sentence: I said she was well, and she was well; she had no more illness. From then on she believed in me deeply without doubt and had complete faith.

**[Addendum 1]** Yu Guo Man, whose name was Yu Shaochen, was born in 1906 into a Buddhist family in Shanghai, China. Her father was a vegetarian for many years, but Guo Man did not become a vegetarian until she was 41. Because the family tradition was honest, kind, and good, Guo Man was always glad to help others, and she taught her children that they must never gain without labor. During the hard years of the war, when a food rationing system was adopted, supplies were limited, yet Guo Man still shared her food with unfortunate people. Guo Man met the Venerable Master on Fu Yung Shan when he had just arrived in Hong Kong, and from time to time she offered him new clothes and food. After taking refuge, she often went to Western Bliss Gardens Monastery to take part in group cultivation and to listen to the Buddhadharma.

Guo Man's whole family believed deeply in the Buddhadharma and encountered many inconceivable things; they were the Venerable Master's loyal disciples. In the sixth year after taking refuge with the Venerable Master, Guo Man fasted on the first and fifteenth days of every lunar month, drinking only water. Once, on a fasting day, she planned to go to Western Bliss Gardens, but her son Guo Xing tried to dissuade her, worried that, as old as she was and having eaten nothing, she could not walk such a long mountain path. Heeding her son's words, Guo Man changed her mind and turned back home. Guo Man's home was on the sixth floor, and since there was no elevator, she had to climb the stairs. That day, just as she walked into the apartment building, she found herself already on the sixth floor without having climbed a single stair. She thought this very extraordinary and hurried back downstairs to go to Western Bliss Gardens.

Once, her daughter-in-law contracted severe tuberculosis for the third time, and there was almost no medicine that could cure it. Although her son and daughter-in-law were Catholics, they still asked their mother to seek the Venerable Master's help. The Venerable Master said, "Although the tree is sick, it will still blossom again." Before long, Guo Man's daughter-in-law did indeed recover, and afterward she gave birth to three more children. Because of this, they too came to believe in Buddhism.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master never wanted people to talk during meals, so at the noon meal he often spoke the Dharma for the assembly; sometimes Wen Guo Mi or Huang Guo Jun would speak in his place, expressing the Master's intent. Once, after listening to the Master speak the Dharma, Xue Guo Feng (Yu Guo Man's daughter-in-law) said to the Master, "I feel that every word and every phrase you say, Shifu, is the speech of a sage. It's a pity that among those present there may not be anyone who truly understands." The Master smiled and said, "Since you know what it means, aren't you the one who truly understands?"

## 55. Strange Happenings During Chan Sessions

After the Opening-the-Light at Cixing Monastery, a hundred or more people came every time a Guanyin session or a Buddha Recitation session was held. The sutra lectures in the evening went on until ten o'clock. Most of those who came to hear the sutras were lay Buddhists in their forties and fifties or older, and they had to walk a long way to reach the mountain...

Cixing Monastery held at least ten Chan sessions every year, and as many as twenty. Many Dharma Masters who had fled from the mainland also cultivated there. During the Chan sessions the Venerable Master was very strict: except to go to the toilet, no one could take a single step out of the Chan Hall.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I had an old refuge disciple in Hong Kong whose way of applying effort was very special. How so? Other people investigated "Who is reciting the Buddha's name?" and she investigated too. What did she investigate? She investigated "Eighty-four years old, eighty-four years old." She said she wanted to live to 84.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi recounted on September 27, 2000:

There is an old disciple in his nineties who is still alive today. In this life he is wealthy. In his cultivation he was always preoccupied with building a big house to live in himself, and when he got sick he insisted on eating bird's nest. Because he was always ill, the Master wanted him to make a vow, but he refused to make one. Once, during a Chan session at Cixing Monastery, in the third incense period, everyone, men and women alike, heard the clanking of iron chains and also heard the ghost of impermanence calling his name (Liang Guixian), and then they heard him let out a great cry. In a former life, because someone would not pay back money they owed him, he killed that person. Only after the Master had him repent and vow to protect and support the Way-place was he all right.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

All the Buddha Recitation sessions, celebrations, and chanting gatherings were held at the Buddhist Lecture Hall; before there was a Buddhist Lecture Hall, they were held at Western Bliss Gardens. We never held Chan sessions at Western Bliss Gardens or at the Buddhist Lecture Hall; the Chan sessions were all held at Cixing Monastery. The Master often invited Chan masters who were quite famous at the time; the only one I remember is Dharma Master Ming Guan, who presided over several Chan sessions there. During Chan practice, talking was not allowed, and you couldn't sway from side to side when you walked. If you dozed off while sitting in meditation, the Dharma Master would hit you with the incense board. If you go to Cixing Monastery now, you can still see many incense boards; these wooden boards were all used in the Chan sessions back then.

The Chan sessions went from early morning until night. The Master gave instructional talks twice a day, once in the afternoon and once in the evening, and sometimes he would have a disciple go up and speak for a while. The Master told us that when sitting in meditation we shouldn't close our eyes completely; the eyes should be fixed on the tip of the nose, "the eyes contemplating the nose, the nose contemplating the mind," and that way we wouldn't doze off. The Master also said that our thoughts should be concentrated on one thing, investigating a meditation topic (huatou), such as investigating "Who is reciting the Buddha's name?"

Because many of the disciples in Hong Kong were elderly people, housewives, and people without education, the Master said, "If some people can't use the Chan method, it's also fine for them to silently recite the name of Amitabha Buddha." The first time I met the Master, he taught me how to sit in full lotus. For the older disciples, never mind full lotus, even half lotus was hard, but for us children it was no problem.

By the year-end Chan session my winter vacation had already begun, so I was able to take part. Every day we got up at three-thirty and did the morning recitation at four. After the morning recitation we had breakfast, rice porridge and pickled vegetables, and then we began sitting in meditation. During a Chan session no dinner is eaten, but the Master still compassionately had dinner prepared for the lay disciples.

I remember that once, during a Buddha Recitation session, we were reciting "Namo Amitabha Buddha" continuously. There was a Mrs. Luo from a very well-off family who couldn't read but was very diligent; whenever there was a Dharma assembly, she would come. At that Buddha Recitation session I was the cantor and Guo Song struck the wooden fish. While we were circumambulating and reciting, Guo Song suddenly smiled and asked me, "Did you hear any unusual sound?" I said, "What?" She said, "Listen!" I followed the sound of the assembly to track it down, and I couldn't help laughing too. Everyone else was reciting the Buddha's name, but Mrs. Luo was reciting, "Eighty-four years old, eighty-four years old, eighty-four years old, eighty-four years old..." After the Dharma assembly, I told the Master that Mrs. Luo kept reciting "eighty-four years old" and asked whether that meant she could only live to 84. The Master laughed too.

**[Addendum 3]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

Today (December 25, 1961), at around three o'clock, Mother and I went to the Lecture Hall. The Master again told us the story about reciting "Om mani padme hum." The story goes like this: There was a man who didn't recognize the character "hum," so he read the "hum" in "Om mani padme hum" as "niu" [Tr.: niu means "cow"]. Because he was very diligent, on the dry, barren hilltop where he lived, many brilliant flowers and plants actually bloomed. One day an old cultivator passed by this hilltop and found the sight strange. After asking about it in detail, he taught the man to correct his pronunciation. But from then on, no matter how hard the man worked, it no longer had any efficacy, and the beautiful flowers and plants disappeared.

The Master told us how important a single thought of utmost sincerity is. Because the man later began to have doubts, suspecting that he had been reciting the mantra wrong, idle thoughts and scattered thoughts flew about in profusion, and so it no longer worked. The Master told this story because Huang Guo Song had mentioned how Mrs. Luo turned "Who is reciting the Buddha's name?" into "eighty-four years old."

## 56. I've Been Waiting for You a Long Time

With his wisdom eye, the Venerable Master observed the causes and conditions of living beings and compassionately guided lost wanderers to awaken early and return home to the Buddha, their compassionate father. Once the opportunity and conditions had ripened, the Venerable Master would use skillful means to receive them...

Tan Xiuzhen (Tan Guo Qi)

Spoken at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall on January 12, 2001:

At that time I was in my twenties (I'm 71 now) and lived on the same floor as Guo Chen. I saw that she had a photo of the Master with the words "A Living Buddha in the Human World" written on it, so I asked her why it said "A Living Buddha in the Human World" about this Dharma Master. Guo Chen said, "It's very efficacious! I'll take you to bow to the Buddhas!" And she took me to bow to the Buddhas. At that time my children were still little, and Western Bliss Gardens was high up, so it was very inconvenient to take the children up there; so I always went to bow to the Buddhas on the first and fifteenth days of the lunar month. The Master was the only monastic there. The Master lectured on the sutras in the evening, and the Dharma assemblies were all led by lay Buddhists; there were Guanyin sessions, Amitabha sessions, and other Dharma assemblies.

In 1954, something suddenly grew on my son's body. I took him to the hospital to see the doctor and had X-rays taken, but they couldn't find the cause. Guo Chen said, "Why don't you go and ask the Master for help!" So I went to the Master and knelt to beg him for help.

The Master said, "Why are you kneeling here?"

"Sharp spines have grown on my son's body. Every time I bathe him, they prick my hands," I said.

"I don't know how to heal people."

"You do. Guo Chen's whole family has said that you know how to heal people."

"Do you have great faith in me?"

"I have great faith in you!"

"Very well, then! Then take refuge!"

"We don't have any money. How can we take refuge?" I said.

"Does taking refuge cost money?"

"How can you take refuge without money?"

"It doesn't cost any money." "But I'm young, and I don't understand anything."

"You don't need to understand. As long as you know to come back and bow to the Buddhas, that's enough."

After I took refuge, my Dharma name was "Guo Qi." The Master then had me carry my son over to him, and the Master blessed him. In less than a week, the spines on my son's body were completely gone.

## 57. Taking Refuge Equally

Guo Li took refuge with the Venerable Master because she had read the Venerable Master's *Records* and also wanted to ask the Venerable Master for help in curing her sick husband. And to seek blessings and wisdom for her children, she brought her own two children to take refuge with the Venerable Master, but her husband's several children by his former wife did not get the chance to draw near to the Venerable Master...

Upasika Guo Li

Spoken at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall on January 12, 2001:

I took refuge with the Master when I was in my twenties; now I'm 75. I took refuge at Western Bliss Gardens. My causes and conditions for taking refuge came through a worker I had hired, who had taken refuge with the Master and showed me the Master's *Records*. At that time my husband was ill, and because the *Records* contained true accounts of real people the Master had saved, I too wanted to take refuge and ask the Master for help. I set out from Prince Edward Road, asking the way as I walked, and walked a very long way before I reached Western Bliss Gardens.

When I saw the Master, I said I wanted to take refuge together with my husband. The Master said, "That will have to wait until your husband wants to take refuge!" When I heard that, I wanted to leave right then and there. A lay Buddhist who was helping cook in the kitchen said to me, "Take refuge yourself first, and later bring your husband to take refuge." So that day I took refuge. I loved wearing pretty clothes, so my Dharma name was "Guo Li" [Tr.: li means "beautiful"]. After I took refuge, whenever the Master spoke to me I always listened on my knees, but I couldn't understand what the Master said. Also, because I lived very far away and the road was out of the way, I gradually came to Western Bliss Gardens less and less.

Once when I went to Western Bliss Gardens, the Master said, "Why don't you have a Dharma ceremony done!" So I had a Dharma ceremony done for my husband. Later, when Cixing Monastery was built, the Master told me, "That time you gave 200 dollars, so now you have a longevity plaque at Cixing Monastery."

In 1956 my husband passed away. At that time I was too young, and I had a very complicated extended family, so I seldom went to Western Bliss Gardens. Only after the matters of the estate had been settled, wanting to seek blessings and wisdom for my children, did I return to the Buddhist Lecture Hall, where I helped out with the work and took part in Dharma assemblies reciting the *Earth Store Sutra*.

Both of my children took refuge with the Master. Because I was the second wife, I didn't take the five sons and two daughters my husband had had before to take refuge with the Master. The Master once said to me, "You should have all the children come and take refuge together. It isn't right that only the ones you gave birth to take refuge. You have no mind of your own; you just listen to what other people say..." Whenever I think of the Master's words, I feel very uneasy and feel that I was rather selfish, so I often bow to the Buddhas and repent of this matter.

## 58. The Departed Break into Smiles

After the Ullambana Dharma assembly, at the request of the faithful in general, the Venerable Master spared no effort to open a Dharma assembly for rescuing the departed. Monastic and lay disciples received the precepts for the deceased on behalf of relatives and friends who had passed away, planting in them the seeds of the vajra precepts. This was an act of great filial piety and great compassion...

The fifteenth day of the seventh lunar month of 1954 (the jiawu year) was the Buddha's Day of Joy. At Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island, the Venerable Master held an Ullambana Dharma assembly lasting three days (the thirteenth, fourteenth, and fifteenth of the seventh month), and quite a large number of monastics and faithful devotees took part. Those who attended the Dharma assembly wanted nothing other than to use its merit and virtue to benefit their relatives, hoping that the karmic obstacles of the living would be eliminated and their good roots would grow, and that the departed would be reborn in good places. At that time Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Yue and the Upasikas Guo Gen, Guo Quan, and others, more than 30 people in all, made a request to the Venerable Master, hoping he would transmit the precepts for the deceased on the concluding day of the Dharma assembly. Some of these people were seeking the precepts for the deceased on behalf of their parents, some for their siblings or spouses, and some for clan relatives and family members, hoping to free them from the sufferings of the hells and of the hungry ghosts.

The Venerable Master granted their request, and at nine-thirty on the night of the fifteenth, an altar was set up in the guest hall downstairs to transmit the precepts for the deceased. At around ten o'clock, when the invitation of the sages had been completed, at the very instant the Venerable Master returned to his seat, suddenly a great hubbub of voices arose all around within the boundary of the ritual area. Dharma Master Heng Yue, who was kneeling before the altar holding his father's memorial tablet in his hands, at that very moment discovered that his father had suddenly appeared in front of him.

At that time, Tan Guo Zheng and Wen Guo Shu had gone back to their room early to rest, because they had to go down the mountain at four o'clock the next morning to return to Hong Kong. They had only just lain down when suddenly they heard a commotion of voices in the corridor outside the door and a confused trampling of footsteps, as though many people were passing back and forth. When they opened the door to look, there was no one there at all; when they closed the door, the sound of scrambling footsteps came again, making their hair stand on end.

Dharma Master Heng Ding was cultivating in quiet on his own in a small thatched hut several hundred meters from the Buddha hall. This thatched hut had been built beside the path, and anyone who wanted to take the shortcut down the mountain had to pass in front of its door. That night, at around ten-thirty, he heard from inside the hut the footsteps of many people going down the mountain. These people talked as they walked, and every now and then someone would burst out laughing; it was a great uproar. The next morning, when Dharma Master Heng Ding and the two laywomen each told of what they had experienced the night before, everyone exclaimed in wonder with one voice.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Yi recounted on September 26, 2000:

Cixing Monastery always had a lot of ghosts. Once the Master told me to write a memorial tablet. I said, "No one in my family has died. What memorial tablet would I write?" That was because during that period I kept falling down, even when going up the stairs. The Master said, "There's a big, tall ghost following behind you." But I still didn't know who this ghost was. After the Buddha Recitation session had been going for three days, the Master blessed me, and only then did I learn that a clansman of mine had died of a high fever not long after coming to Hong Kong. The Master said, "There are a lot of ghosts at Cixing Monastery, but when they see you, every one of them is frightened away."

**[Addendum 2]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Xing's supplementary account, given on September 26, 2000:

Once, when Cixing Monastery was holding a Buddha Recitation session in which the precepts for the deceased were transmitted, during the last three days Dharma Master Heng Yi kept falling down for no reason at all, and no one knew why: she fell when walking, and she fell when going up the stairs. The Master told her to write a memorial tablet, but no matter how hard she tried, she couldn't think of any relative who had died recently. At that time she was still a laywoman. Later the Master had her come into the Buddha Hall and blessed her, and only then did she learn that her clansman had died of a fever. She immediately wrote a memorial tablet to rescue this clansman of hers to rebirth. The Master once said that the ghosts were all afraid when they saw Dharma Master Heng Yi. Cixing Monastery always had a lot of ghosts; other people could see them, and only she couldn't.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

There were many beings in that area, and they all placed their hopes on Cixing Monastery to bring them liberation. My elder sister Guo Zheng and the sister and brother Wen Guo Shu and Guo Mi all saw them, and they also heard voices saying, "Go to Cixing Monastery and we can be rescued!" On the second night of the Ullambana Dharma assembly, Dharma Master Xu Lang performed the Flaming Mouth ceremony. Dharma Master Xu Lang performed the Flaming Mouth ceremony very well, so the Master had invited him to perform this ceremony. That night my younger brother, my cousin, and I, the three of us, stood behind the Dharma Master fanning him; because there was no electricity there, we used fans to stir up a breeze and drive off the heat.

That night my elder sister and Guo Shu were in a room on the second floor of the villa when they suddenly heard some voices. My sister said to Guo Shu, "What are you saying to me?" Guo Shu said, "I thought you were talking to me!" "Did you hear something too?" "Yes, I heard it!" They had both heard "people" talking, saying, "Hurry, hurry, we have to hurry and go seek rescue!" Only then did it suddenly dawn on them that it wasn't "people" talking. The two of them were so frightened that they went pale and ran downstairs as fast as they could. Later my sister told me, "We also heard someone talking, saying, 'Come, come, come, hurry and come!'" It wasn't only them: Dharma Masters Heng Ding and Heng Yue, who lived in thatched huts by the roadside next to the monastery, heard it too.

## 59. Reflections on Propagating the Teaching

Although Western Bliss Gardens had been built, there were few educated people who believed in Buddhism at that time, and most people regarded Buddhism as superstition. At that time it rained heavily day after day; the roof of Western Bliss Gardens cracked, rainwater leaked in, and the Buddha Hall became a watery marsh...

The Venerable Master lamented the listless and dispirited state of Hong Kong's Buddhists. Eager to propagate the teaching, he composed a verse to make his resolve clear:

> Five autumns I have passed in vain since coming to Hong Kong;  
> Others call it cause for joy, but I call it cause for worry.  
> A single patched robe, the same as it has always been;  
> Sitting in lotus in a humble room, I carry on with new sorrows.
>
> <small>來港空度已五秋，人道可喜我道憂。衲衣一襲仍舊貫，陋室趺坐續新愁。</small>

> The censer in the Buddha Hall is drenched by the rain;  
> In the guest hall, you could sail a boat up to the meditation bed.  
> Among the faithful devotees and disciples, who pays any heed?  
> The Dharma protectors leave the Triple Jewel to stay or go as it may.
>
> <small>佛殿香爐遭雨淋，客堂禅榻可行舟。善信弟子誰關注，護法三寶任去留。</small>

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Let me tell you, at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery in Hong Kong, a single bed had six places where the rain leaked through. Now tell me, wasn't that hard? But hard as it was, I didn't repair it. I didn't care whether my own house leaked or not; if I had money, I wanted to use it to help others. Tell me, is that foolish or not?

There was a monastery that was making Buddha images, and I donated more than HK$1,500. It would have taken only 200 dollars to repair the house, but I couldn't bear to spend that money. Spending more than 1,500 dollars to help others make Buddha images was no problem. When I was in Hong Kong, all told, I had more than 100 Buddha images made and gave them away. For example, the Three Sages of the West at the Chinese Buddhist Association of Hawaii were a gift from me.

There was a monastic who often came to Western Bliss Gardens to bring me gifts, biscuits, milk, and powdered milk. There was a reason for this. Earlier, when he was building his Buddha hall, I had donated several thousand dollars. After the Buddha hall was washed away by floodwaters, he made Buddha images, and I also gave him an image of Amitabha Buddha. It was the seventh day of the first lunar month when the elder Dharma Master from that place came to Western Bliss Gardens and said to me, "I've been waiting for you to come and pay a New Year's call on Amitabha Buddha. I waited until today and you still hadn't come, so I came over to see you."

"Where is Amitabha Buddha?" I asked him.

"It's the Amitabha Buddha you gave to our Buddha hall."

"That Amitabha Buddha was made by me. Since I made him, why should I go and bow to him? I have an Amitabha Buddha here too, and Amitabha Buddha and Amitabha Buddha are not separate. Why are you telling me to forsake what is near and seek what is far?" My question left him with nothing to say.

Next he tried to win me over with sentiment, saying, "I think of you every day. Do you think of me?"

"Me? I don't know how to think of anyone, and I don't know how to miss anyone either," I said.

"The Dharma you speak is higher than mine!" he said, looking at me.

"Why don't I think of anyone or miss anyone? Because I have no knowing and no awareness."

"The Dharma you speak gets higher with every step!" He said that the more I spoke, the higher it got, higher with every step.

"The *Vajra Sutra* says, 'This Dharma is level and equal, with no high or low.' Elder Dharma Master, why did you come out with the word 'high' today? Explain!"

In the end he couldn't say a single word. He turned to the Dharma Master who had come with him and said, "He's really formidable!" The Dharma Master who had come with him was someone who studied the precepts and Vinaya, but in fact he didn't understand any of the precepts and didn't keep any of them either.

He brought me biscuits, milk, and powdered milk because he knew I was a very stupid, very foolish person; he gave me gifts hoping I would help him out again. I probably don't know how to do arithmetic. I can't tell other people's affairs apart from my own, and I often handle other people's affairs as if they were my own. That's why this poor man kept on living in a house that leaked.

**[Addendum 1]** Reproduced from *Mind Dharma* magazine, Issue 3, news item "Our Society's President, the Venerable Master To Lun, Generously Donates Images of the Three Sages of the West to the Honolulu Buddhist Association":
