Our society's president, the Venerable Master To Lun, seeking to help the Buddhadharma flourish and spread in America, has recently and generously donated three large images of the Three Sages of the West to the Honolulu Buddhist Association in the United States. However, because the U.S. government is enforcing an embargo policy, it is rather difficult to arrange their import, and the Honolulu Buddhist Association is currently having someone here in Hong Kong handle the procedures for shipping them to the United States.

**[Addendum 2]** Reproduced from *The Origins and History of the Founding of Tung Lin Buddha Recitation Hall*, by Dharma Master Shi Kuan Yun:

On the fifth day of the eighth month of 1952, the "Tung Lin Pure Abode" was nearly half destroyed when a mountain flash flood broke out. After the disaster... Dharma Master To Lun, Dharma Master Xu Lang, Upasaka Zhao Ren'an, and others sponsored images of the Three Sages of the West, and their Opening-the-Light ceremony was scheduled for Amitabha's birthday in the eleventh lunar month. On the seventeenth day of the first lunar month of the following year, the Pure Abode was renamed "Tung Lin Buddha Recitation Hall."

## 61. It Really Happened

Some people came down with strange illnesses for no reason at all. Neither Chinese nor Western medicine could cure them, and secret remedies and folk prescriptions had no effect either. At their wits' end, one after another they sought the Venerable Master's help to resolve their troubles...

Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

I've heard people ask the Master to hit them: "Hit me, hit me, and I'll get better!" When the Master was in Manchuria and in Hong Kong, he cured a great many strange illnesses. The Master had vowed that whenever he came across illnesses caused by ghosts and goblins, he was willing to resolve them for these people, and because of this he drew all sorts of ghosts that wanted to contest their powers against the Master. I really liked talking with the Master, because I loved hearing these stories. I completely believe that the stories the Master told about dragons and about ghosts really happened. In fact, several times when the Master encountered ghosts and goblins, I happened to be there.

That day I went to Western Bliss Gardens Monastery. The Master was sitting solemnly in full lotus, and Guo Song was also in full lotus. With a very serious expression she told me, "Sit down and recite this section of the Shurangama Mantra!" I said, "What's happening?" "The Master told us to recite this section of the mantra to help him subdue a demon!" That was the first time I saw the Master encounter a ghost or goblin at Western Bliss Gardens. Later the Master subdued that goblin, but I don't know what the goblin looked like. After a while the Master rose from his seat and said, "All right!" He told us that a very fierce and vicious goblin had made a sneak attack on him, trying to wreck his cultivation.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When ordinary people get sick, it is always caused either by karma or by demons. If someone's karmic obstacles have caught up with him and you make his illness go away, that's still not much of a problem. But if his is an illness of demonic obstacles and you want to cure it, the demon on him will come after you and fight with you. If your power in the Way isn't sufficient, that is, if you don't have much power in the Way, you'll enter the state of the demon king. If you do have power in the Way, you'll still form enmity with the demon. It will always be looking for a chance to fight with you and make you lose to it.

When I was in my twenties and treated people's illnesses, whatever illness someone had, I was determined to make them well. If they didn't get well, I would even go without dressing, eating, or sleeping until I had cured their illness. No matter what kind of demon, ghost, or goblin it was, I would subdue it and bring it under control. The fierce demonic obstacles I ran into were too many to count. In one person's house, teacups would fly up by themselves and float about in the air like this, and the water in the cups wouldn't spill; or a book, like this one here, would burst into flames all by itself for no reason. I've encountered all these kinds of things; they really were that fierce. Whether you believe it or not is your business! Because I was always treating people's illnesses, I offended a great many mountain goblins and water monsters, deviant demons and externalists. They all came to oppose me, but there was nothing they could do to me, because in my every move there was not the slightest fault.

## 62. Hungry Ghosts Hidden in the Belly

As the saying goes, "When you want to learn to be good, your karmic foes come looking for you; if you want to become a Buddha, you must first undergo demons." The elderly Upasika Liu Guo Juan came down with a strange illness: she had to eat more than ten meals a day. If she didn't eat, she got terribly hungry. Why was she so terribly hungry?...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Here I want to tell you all a story. Perhaps many people won't believe it, but this story is absolutely true. In a former life Liu Guo Juan also encountered something that she refused to believe, so in this life she has had to repay the retribution for her doubt. Therefore Buddhists must pay attention to cause and effect, be careful and cautious in everything, and never slander others carelessly!

It wasn't just that Liu Guo Juan's deafness was cured; she also had a peculiar, strange illness. What strange illness? When I tell you about this illness, nobody believes it, because it's just too strange. How was it strange? One night, as though dreaming and yet not dreaming, she dreamed that three chubby little children, two or three years old, burrowed into her belly. From then on she had to eat more than ten meals a day; every hour she had to eat a big meal, and she always felt she couldn't get full. But if she didn't eat, her belly wouldn't stand for it, and she felt terribly hungry. Why was she so terribly hungry? Let me tell you: with this illness of hers, although she was one person, there were three hungry ghosts in her belly, and everything she ate was eaten by those hungry ghosts. She found this illness of hers very strange, so she went to see doctors. The Western doctors examined her and said she wasn't ill; the Chinese doctors didn't know what illness it was either. None of them could detect anything, and they all said she wasn't ill. The doctors still gave her medicine, but no matter what medicine she took, she didn't get better.

Two or three years went by like this. By then she could already hear, and whenever there was a sutra lecture she still went to listen. When I lectured on a sutra for two hours, she would listen for half of it and then have to run to the kitchen and soak some biscuits in boiling water to eat; that's how it was. I knew she had this illness, but I didn't want to meddle in this business. Whenever I wasn't at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery, Guo Ping, being afraid to stay there alone, would ask Liu Guo Juan for company, and the two of them would look after Western Bliss Gardens.

On the seventh day of the second month I came down from Lantau Island, and at around ten-thirty in the morning Liu Guo Juan said to me, "Shifu, someone is talking inside my belly." I said, "How can someone be talking in your belly? Are you going to have a baby? You're already in your sixties; can you still have a baby? What does it say?" She said she heard it loud and clear: there really was something in her belly that could talk.

She said, "When I got up early this morning, I had just made some glutinous rice cakes and was about to eat them when my belly spoke. It said, 'I don't like eating this!' So I said to my belly, 'If you don't eat this, what will you eat? As long as you get full, that's good enough!' Then my belly stopped talking."

I told her, "All right, never mind! The child in your belly can already talk, so it ought to be ready to be born. Tonight I'll make this illness of yours better. When you get home tonight, remember: at twelve o'clock midnight, light a stick of incense before the Buddha and keep reciting the Buddha's name, and then see what happens."

That night, following my words, she lit a stick of incense before the Buddha at twelve midnight. She recited the Buddha's name until she was in a state as though asleep and yet as though dreaming, when suddenly she saw Weituo Bodhisattva coming, holding a steaming bowl of fragrant noodles, which he set down in front of her bed. Then three plump, fair little children, some four or five years old and some five or six years old, the very same three children she had seen before she fell ill, instantly burrowed out of her belly and scrambled to eat the noodles. As they were scrambling to eat, Weituo Bodhisattva grabbed them by the ears and hauled them away. Once they had been hauled away, she felt there was nothing left in her belly; it was completely empty. From then on Liu Guo Juan's belly felt as if a heavy burden had been lifted; she was never hungry again and never again needed to eat so many meals.

What were these three strange creatures in her belly? Let me tell you: two big geckos and one big frog. As for a case like hers, never mind foreigners not believing it; even Chinese people don't believe it.

But you should understand why she got such an illness. It's because in her former life she came across someone with the same illness, and later a Dharma Master cured that person. When people told her about it, she said, "I don't believe it!" Because she didn't believe it and said one wrong sentence, in this life she got this illness so she could try it out for herself and see whether she believed it or not. That's how formidable cause and effect are! Fortunately she was sincere in listening to the sutras, and so there was a response from the unseen realm that resolved this calamity.

**[Addendum]** Dharma Master Heng Gui, speaking at the Dharma Realm Buddhist Books Distribution Society in Taipei on March 10, 2013:

The Master told me this story himself back then. Some time after Upasika Liu Guo Juan was no longer deaf, because she was often hungry, every time she went to hear a sutra lecture she had to bring along some bread and biscuits, and every little while she would have to go out, take a couple of bites, and come back in; she went in and out like that. Later, after she had eaten something, a voice in her belly told her, "Don't like eating this." (This was said in Cantonese; in Mandarin it would be: "I don't like eating this.") Not knowing what else to do, she reported it to the Master. Later she did as the Master told her, and only then was the problem solved.

When I heard this at the time, I asked, "Shifu, why did she get this illness?" The Master said that in her former life she had a friend who had this very illness, and later the friend was cured by a monastic. This friend told Guo Juan, "So-and-so, a monastic cured my illness for me." Right then Guo Juan got cause and effect wrong and said one wrong sentence! She said, "There's no such thing! You've been fooled!" It was just because of that one sentence that in this life she herself had to get a taste of this illness of hunger. So I asked:

"Shifu, who was that monastic?"

"Probably me!" the Master said with a smile.

## 63. Editing the *Records*

The lay disciples Tan Guo Zheng, Guo Jue, and others felt that the Venerable Master's *Records* had a profound and far-reaching influence, that readers were deeply moved by them, and that they helped the propagation of the Buddhadharma. So that more of the faithful could benefit, they joined forces to re-edit the Venerable Master's *Records*...

"Though freezing to death, stand facing the wind; though starving to death, walk with your belly thrust out. Be the sturdy grass in the gale, the true gold in the blazing fire." The white mountains and black waters of Heilongjiang forged the Venerable Master's tenacious and resolute character. The Venerable Master was born into turbulent times of social upheaval, the Japanese invasion of China, and the war between the Nationalists and the Communists, and he went through all kinds of suffering, including poverty, hunger, and the loss of his mother. When Manchuria was occupied by the Japanese army, the Venerable Master was in his youth. Although he could not fulfill his dream of joining the army to serve his country, he realized the true meaning of life in another way: awakening people from their deluded dreams, then pointing directly to the mind-ground, and even to seeing the nature and becoming a Buddha.

When the Venerable Master was in Hong Kong, he was only a little over 30, yet everything he did moved people to the depths of their hearts and earned the reverence of humans and gods. Whether in favorable or adverse circumstances, the Venerable Master was always consistently vigorous, setting an example of cultivation for his disciples. The Venerable Master's deeds, teaching by word and by example and transforming people through virtue, have a wondrous power to rescue those in peril and save the world.

In the fifth month of the yiwei year (1955), Upasaka Tan Guo Pu wrote in his article "Starting from How I Came to Study Buddhism":

One day I happened to obtain a book, *Records of Dharma Master To Lun*, respectfully written by his disciple Guo Jue: "It gives an outline of how the Master, in Songjiang Province in Manchuria, entered the Way in childhood, journeyed far into China, and then went on to Siam. Reading it, I was moved. At the same time I learned that in serving his parents, the Master fulfilled the worldly dharma to the utmost; in helping people, he manifests the conduct of a Bodhisattva. His vows are such that even empty space cannot contain them; his worthiness and wisdom stand revealed through the passing springs and autumns. Such resolve and such words are worthy to be a model for others; such conduct and such virtue can revive the style of the lineage. I am old, and moreover unlearned and unlettered, and I am ashamed that I have been unable to set down the Master's deeds in a book to benefit the ways of the world and the hearts of the people." Now, fortunately, several lay Buddhists have come to share this view and have joined forces to re-edit the Master's *Records*, removing errors and supplying omissions in the hope of making it complete, and adding printed photographs to inspire reverent admiration. Moreover, because Chinese writing is profound in diction and meaning and not easy for people of other lands to read, someone with the knowledge has translated it into English. Without dwelling on the merits of the wording, and at the risk of being laughed at by the learned, this is done simply so that the book may circulate widely. For this book has not been compiled to make anyone's name known; it is truly because we see that the Master's deeds have the power to proclaim the Buddhadharma, rescue those in peril, and save the world. May Buddhist disciples and faithful lay devotees, Chinese and Western alike, kindly give it their attention and read it.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

One day the Master gave me a biography of himself. It was printed in the style of early Chinese editions, long and narrow, with only 10 to 15 pages. After the Master gave me that biography, I tried adding what I had seen and heard in Hong Kong, to complete that booklet.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Much of the Master's first *Records* was compiled by my elder sister on the basis of the original biography, with what she had seen and heard in Hong Kong added in; some parts were also written with the help of the Master's disciple Upasaka Pei Guo Jian.

## 64. Founding *Mind Dharma*

In March 1956, the Venerable Master founded *Mind Dharma* magazine. Running a magazine could propagate the Dharma and benefit living beings, and it could also encourage the disciples to publish articles and turn their reflection back upon themselves. The Venerable Master encouraged his disciples to write down the principles they had heard in the form of articles, poems, or songs, so that more people could understand Buddhism...

The following is the prologue, "Written at the Outset," from the inaugural issue of *Mind Dharma* magazine:

Buddhism's teaching and transformation takes all living beings as its object, rescuing and saving them equally, without any prejudice or discrimination of high or low, great or small. This is where the greatness of the Buddha lies. But today, in an age when science is flourishing, Buddhism ought to be advancing shoulder to shoulder with science, so why is Buddhism still maliciously misunderstood? It is because we have failed to propagate it. The blame for this should be laid on the fact that we Buddhists have not truly shouldered our responsibility.

Fellow practitioners, let us all press forward bravely together! Let us win glory for our great Buddhism and stand up for the spirit of the Buddha, which is gradually being neglected by people. *Mind Dharma* was born with this mission, and its four great aims have already been set out at the front. Although its strength will not be very great, we will advance toward our goal.

We will certainly overcome all difficulties, never shrinking back, and wholeheartedly propagate the lofty and supremely good teachings of our Buddhism. We hope that fellow practitioners will support us with all their strength, continually favoring us with their writings and donating toward printing costs, so that, through the concrete actions of fellow practitioners everywhere who cherish *Mind Dharma*, we may, besides being grateful, gain a great deal more confidence and courage. In this Dharma-Ending Age, it is truly rare to see such a hopeful new sprout. This is not self-importance or arrogance on our part; we truly hope that *Mind Dharma* can grow strong. We believe that all Buddhists, and all who are unwilling to look on indifferently as Buddhism heads toward the fate of decline, will come together to help *Mind Dharma* flourish and spread, so as to bring forth the supreme, perfectly interfused, and wonderful teachings and spread them to every corner of the human world. We earnestly hope that fellow practitioners everywhere will not stint in giving us their guidance and will join together in supporting this undertaking. We would be most grateful and look forward to it with the utmost hope!

The following is an excerpt from the "Editor's Afterword" in the inaugural issue of *Mind Dharma*:

In Hong Kong, writings about Buddhism can be said to be extremely scarce. Yet under these circumstances this magazine has at last come into the world. Not only are we, the colleagues at this society, overjoyed beyond our expectations, but the greatly virtuous ones everywhere, monastic and lay, who have placed their hopes in this magazine will surely all rejoice as well...

The following is an excerpt from the "Editor's Afterword" in the second issue of *Mind Dharma*:

The year before last, this magazine's publisher, the Venerable Master To Lun, proposed founding a Buddhist periodical; at the time he said this to several of our present colleagues at the society. But we all felt that we had no foundation at all in Buddhist studies and no experience in founding a periodical, so we didn't dare act rashly, and in the end the idea ran aground. In fact, we had overlooked the enthusiasm for propagating the Dharma among the greatly virtuous ones, monastic and lay, and the capable writers everywhere. Now the words the Venerable Master To Lun told us at the time have been proven true: "As long as it is wholeheartedly for the sake of propagating the Dharma, not only will people help, but even the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas will lend their aid!" Truly, not long after the inaugural issue of *Mind Dharma* went into circulation, we received one after another nearly ten manuscripts from Taiwan, Indonesia, and other places, and greatly virtuous ones, monastic and lay, who cherish this magazine wrote from Indonesia, Manila, Vietnam, Taichung, and elsewhere, offering to set up distribution centers on our behalf. Some praised this magazine to the skies; of course, there were also people who wrote to point out its shortcomings. As for praise, we will never become complacent; we will be all the more vigilant and strive all the harder, so as to live up to the kind hopes of those who wrote to us. As for advice, we accept it with complete sincerity and will strive to improve.

In this issue, English has been added to both the cover and the table of contents, because we hope to extend *Mind Dharma*'s distribution network to every corner of the world and let the light of the Buddha shine upon all living beings under heaven. We therefore earnestly hope that Buddhist organizations and individuals in all the countries of the world will get in touch with our society, exchange experiences, and work together to propagate the Buddhadharma...

Should Buddhism undergo some reform and renewal? This is a major matter, so we only have this idea and hold no subjective preconceptions. On this question, this magazine plans to solicit differing opinions from all quarters and publish them in successive issues. Without doubt, correct opinions will be accepted by Buddhists; conversely, we can also publish the others, so as to stir up responses and serve the purpose of "tossing out a brick to attract jade"...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In Hong Kong I put out a magazine called *Mind Dharma*. It came out once a month, and three issues were published. All three issues were given out to establish affinities; we never collected a single cent from anyone, and they were all given to people to read for free. Later, because of financial constraints and personnel problems, it was not published anymore.

Everyone should learn to write articles, articles in Chinese and articles in English. The more you write, the better you'll write. You can take the principles you've heard and write them up freely as an article, or compose a song or a poem, to propagate Buddhism, and send it to all the newspapers and magazines; it doesn't matter which one publishes it. Letting more people in the world understand Buddhism and shaking them awake from their habit of sleeping brings limitless merit and virtue. All of you should understand what I mean. Don't keep retreating. Buddhists should go forward with courageous vigor; don't always be retreating.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

The Master's *Mind Dharma* was produced by my elder sister together with Upasaka Pei Guo Jian. Upasaka Pei was from Manchuria and was also the Master's disciple. The Master also had him teach us Japanese.

*Mind Dharma* published articles of ours: articles by Guo Song, Guo Jun, my elder sister, and me. I remember publishing an article that had a poem in the middle, which the Master corrected for me. The articles appeared under either our Dharma names or our precept names. When we received the five precepts, the Master gave us precept names, which began with the character "Zheng." My precept name was "Zheng He," my elder sister's was "Zheng Wu," and Guo Jun's was "Zheng Ren."

**[Addendum 2]** Copyright page of *Mind Dharma* (omitted)

## 64. Demonic States in the Pratyutpanna Practice

One day the Venerable Master suddenly heard his Dharma nephew from Manchuria, Dharma Master Heng Yue, reciting the holy name of Amitabha Buddha very loudly, his voice growing faster and faster, as though he were gasping for breath. The Venerable Master understood what was happening, so he immediately headed for the little hut...

Hong Kong was densely populated, and everywhere there were places of luxury and dissipation. Many young monastics who did not yet have enough samadhi power, after coming to prosperous Hong Kong, began to grow lax without realizing it, and some even went bad, forgetting why they had left the home-life.

The Venerable Master had a Dharma nephew who had come from Manchuria. Although he had donned the robes and shaved his head to leave the home-life, he was usually not serious about his cultivation and not strict in keeping the precepts, and the Venerable Master often admonished him. This time, in order to thoroughly correct his past mistakes, he asked the Venerable Master to let him hold a Pratyutpanna session [Tr.: the Pratyutpanna Samadhi practice consists of constant walking and standing, without sitting or lying down, for a set period]. What the Venerable Master liked best was a cultivator who applied effort, so he had a retreat room built especially for him near Cixing Chan Monastery. On March 30 (the nineteenth day of the second lunar month) he entered the retreat room and began the 90-day practice of the Pratyutpanna Samadhi.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When Heng Yue came to Hong Kong, at first he didn't cultivate much. He smoked cigarettes, and he had other faults too, which I won't go into, and he didn't dare come to see me. Then, because he had no place to live, he brought forth the resolve for the Way and told me he wanted to hold a Pratyutpanna session on Lantau Island.

What is the Pratyutpanna Samadhi? "Pratyutpanna" is Sanskrit; here it is translated as "the Buddhas stand," so it is also called the "Samadhi of the Buddhas Standing." In this practice, a person walks constantly inside a room, so it is also called the "Samadhi of Constant Walking." How many days do you go on like this without sitting or lying down? Ninety days. You just walk like this inside the room and don't sit down anywhere. Apart from eating meals and going to the toilet, you simply don't sleep; this is specifically to counter the demon of sleep. Sometimes you walk until your feet swell up and your legs swell up too, and all kinds of ailments arise, but you still have to keep walking and must not fall down. If you fall down, the session is broken, and you can't attain the Pratyutpanna Samadhi. You see, in Buddhism there's this kind of method of cultivation, a method of cultivating with such courageous vigor.

Here we are now holding a Buddha Recitation session, walking for a while, sitting for a while, reciting aloud, reciting silently, getting up at three-thirty in the morning and resting at ten o'clock at night. That still doesn't count as too hard!

At that time (February 1956) I had a small house built for him, a room of about 20 feet by 30 feet, to help him cultivate the Pratyutpanna Samadhi. I also found someone to protect his session, boiling water for him, bringing him his meals, and looking after him there. Heng Yue recited the Buddha's name until he saw Amitabha Buddha! Then he ran for all he was worth, reciting for all he was worth, "Namo Amitabha Buddha, Namo Amitabha Buddha, Namo Amitabha Buddha...," louder and louder, louder and louder. I was up on the slope, and he lived down below, about 200 paces from where I was. When I heard him reciting so loudly down there, I knew he had entered a demonic state, so I went to see him. I saw him running very fast in that room, reciting the Buddha's name as he ran, running extremely fast, running around like a madman.

What do you say was going on? In his former life this Bhikshu was an ox. Because he had worked for a monastery plowing the fields, in this life he was reborn as a human and became a monk, but his ox nature hadn't changed, and he always had a big temper. Why did he run like that? Because he saw Amitabha Buddha coming, and he was chasing after this Amitabha Buddha. But what was really happening? He had fallen into a demonic state. It was actually a water buffalo from the sea: a sea monster had come! This water buffalo was running in front, and he thought Amitabha Buddha had come, so he chased after it. It was luring him on like this, wanting to drag him off to be one of its retinue. How could this water-buffalo spirit manifest the form of Amitabha Buddha? Not only can it manifest the form of Amitabha Buddha; all heavenly demons and externalists have this kind of spiritual power. If you don't recognize it, at a time like this you won't be able to keep your samadhi!

When I got there, I used Dharma power to break up this demonic state. He said, "Just now I saw Amitabha Buddha coming to receive me. Where did Amitabha Buddha go?"

"First settle down. Don't be so flustered! What are you in such a hurry for? When you recite the Buddha's name, you have to recite according to the Dharma!" I said.

"Just now I saw Amitabha Buddha coming to receive me."

"And now?"

"As soon as you came in, Amitabha Buddha ran away," he said.

"You've mistaken it! That wasn't Amitabha Buddha coming. That was a demonic state coming to lure you," I said.

That's how he managed to escape that state. If I hadn't gone, he would have gone off the rails and been possessed by demons. So sometimes even reciting the Buddha's name can lead to demonic possession. In short, in cultivation you must have virtuous conduct, you must have the Way and virtue; without the Way and virtue, even if you recite the Buddha's name, you'll still run into demons.

And what became of Heng Yue after he finished that Pratyutpanna session? He stopped cultivating again. All day long all he thought about was eating. He ate three meals a day and ate a great deal, and he got very, very fat, so fat he could hardly walk. Because he ate too much, he often went to the toilet, and unexpectedly, he breathed his last in the toilet. When he died he was not yet over 40. This is an example of someone who cultivated and then stopped cultivating!

**[Addendum 1]** *Mind Dharma* magazine, Issue 2, news item "A Dharma Master Holds a Pratyutpanna Session at Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island":

At Cixing Chan Monastery on Ling Wui Shan at Man Cheung Po on Lantau Island (its abbot is this magazine's publisher), a retreat building was specially constructed in the second month of this year (1956) for the convenience of those who resolve to cultivate the "Pratyutpanna Samadhi method." A certain Dharma Master, hoping to accomplish his work in the Way early, entered the retreat room on the nineteenth day of the second lunar month and began cultivating the "Pratyutpanna Samadhi" method for a period of 90 days.

"Pratyutpanna" is Sanskrit; translated into Chinese, it is "the Buddhas stand." "The Buddhas stand" has three meanings: first, the awesome power of the Buddhas; second, the power of samadhi; third, the power of the practitioner's own merit and virtue. During the 90-day Pratyutpanna period, one walks around the room, not sleeping or resting day or night, only reciting the great six-character name. In the room there is no bed, table, chair, or the like. If a monk cultivating this ascetic practice suddenly feels drowsy, he may stand still for a little while and reach up to hold on to a cloth strap hanging in the air. After a short rest, he continues walking around the room. Cultivating vigorously like this, with the three karmas in accord, one can see the Buddhas of the ten directions in samadhi. After seeing the Buddhas, one understands the mind and sees the nature, and one can open up understanding and awaken to wisdom.

**[Addendum 2]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Ding's oral account:

Over on Lantau Island there is a retreat room, which was built when Dharma Master Heng Yue did the Pratyutpanna Samadhi practice there. At that time he saw many states, not just Amitabha Buddha.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

The Master was very respectful toward genuine cultivators and toward the older generation in the Sangha, but if someone in the Sangha did not behave properly, the Master would also criticize them sternly. I remember that the Master scolded Dharma Master Heng Yue more than anyone. Dharma Master Heng Yue and Dharma Master Heng Zhi were two of the Master's Dharma nephews. When they first came to Hong Kong, they stayed at Western Bliss Gardens most of the time. Western Bliss Gardens was originally a small house; when they came, it was enlarged with an extra room for them.

At the monastery, the Master didn't allow us to talk much, and this was even more so for the Sangha. The monastics did not talk casually with laypeople, and above all could not talk with women, except when giving instruction. Dharma Master Heng Zhi always sat in full lotus, fingering his recitation beads as he recited the Buddha's name, whereas Dharma Master Heng Yue would talk with us, and sometimes with other people too. In 1993, when the Master came to Hong Kong, Dharma Master Heng Zhi paid the Master a visit.

## 66. Receiving Dharma Transmission in the Weiyang Lineage

In 1946 the Venerable Master set out from Manchuria and trekked more than 3,000 li, enduring hardship all along the way, and in 1948 he arrived at Nanhua Monastery at Caoxi in Guangdong to pay his respects to Elder Master Hsu Yun. Elder Hsu, who was 109 years old at the time, gave the Venerable Master certification as soon as he saw him, saying, "So it is! So it is!"...

On May 18, 1956 (the ninth day of the fourth lunar month), in the Dharma Hall at Yunju, Elder Hsu transmitted the Dharma to the Venerable Master, making him the ninth-generation Dharma heir of the Weiyang lineage [Tr.: one of the five houses of the Chan School], and gave him the name "Hsuan Hua." From Mount Yunju he specially sent the Venerable Master the record of the origins and lineage of the Weiyang patriarchs, and he also wrote a "Verse of Certification" [Tr.: The four lines begin with the characters of the names Hsuan Hua and To Lun.]:

> Proclaiming the wondrous meaning of Wei, he revives the family name;  
> Transforming, he inherits from the Spiritual Peak, and the Way of the Dharma flourishes.
>
> <small>宣沩妙義振家聲, 化承靈岳法道隆,</small>

> Crossing beings over with the "four and six," he transmits the mind seal;  
> The wheel turns without rest, rescuing the suffering multitudes.
>
> <small>度以四六傳心印, 輪旋無休濟苦倫。</small>

Elder Hsu transmitted the Dharma to the Venerable Master, making him the forty-fifth generation counting from the Western Land of India, and the ninth-generation Dharma heir of the Weiyang lineage in the Eastern Land of China. Nevertheless, the Venerable Master's monastic and lay disciples have always carried on the Dharma lineage of the "Golden Summit Vairocana" branch of the Chan School. It was only after the Venerable Master went to America in 1962 that he began to use the name "Hsuan Hua" and raised the banner of the Dharma far and wide.

In 1956 the Venerable Master received from Elder Hsu the scroll of Dharma transmission, *The Treasury of the True Dharma Eye: The Origins and Lineage of the Buddhas and Patriarchs*, which lists the successive patriarchs since Shakyamuni Buddha and the successive patriarchs of the Weiyang lineage. On the scroll was written: "The Old Man Deqing Hsu Yun, eighth generation of the orthodox Weiyang lineage, now entrusts the Treasury of the True Dharma Eye to the ninth-generation Chan practitioner Hsuan Hua To Lun. Guard and uphold it well."

In 1958 Elder Hsu sent the Venerable Master a set of the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs (Expanded Edition)*, which Elder Hsu had revised, together with a letter. After each patriarch's page in the book, the Venerable Master added a verse introduced with the words "Again I speak a verse, which says," and after the fourth volume he appended the lives and verses of several dozen eminent monks of recent times. This became the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs (Further Expanded Edition)*, which was later printed and distributed.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When I built Cixing Chan Monastery, I made a statue of the Venerable Yun while he was still living, to show my reverence. I wrote to the Venerable Yun: "I have been entrusted with the Treasury of the True Dharma Eye, the origins and lineage of the Buddhas and Patriarchs: the Dharma of sealing mind with mind, the special transmission outside the teachings, the wondrous mind of Nirvana, the real mark that is without marks, true emptiness that is not empty. Above, I shall carry on the intent of the Patriarchs; below, I shall transform living beings. Such profound kindness in nourishing me with the milk of the Dharma is in my thoughts at every moment."

**[Addendum 1]** The Weiyang lineage is one of the five houses of the Chan School (Linji, Caodong, Weiyang, Yunmen, and Fayan). From the Venerable Kashyapa the Dharma was passed down to the Patriarch Bodhidharma, the Twenty-eighth Patriarch of the Western Heaven (India), who was also the First Patriarch of the Eastern Land of China. From the First Patriarch it passed down through five transmissions to Great Master Huineng, the Sixth Patriarch. Great Master Huineng transmitted it to Nanyue Huairang; Chan Master Huairang transmitted it to Mazu Daoyi; Chan Master Daoyi transmitted it to Baizhang Huaihai; and Chan Master Huaihai transmitted it to Chan Master Weishan Lingyou, the thirty-seventh generation counting from the Western Land. Chan Master Weishan then transmitted it to Yangshan Huiji. At that time their Dharma affinities flourished greatly, and so it became the Weiyang branch of the Chan School. The line runs from the first patriarch of the Weiyang lineage, Chan Master Weishan; the second, Chan Master Yangshan; the third, Chan Master Xita Guangmu; the fourth, Chan Master Zifu Rubao; the fifth, Chan Master Baoci Deshao; the sixth, Chan Master Sanjiao Zhiqian; and the seventh, Chan Master Xingyang Ciduo, down to Elder Master Hsu Yun, the eighth patriarch of the Weiyang lineage in the Eastern Land. Elder Hsu transmitted the Dharma to the Venerable Master, making him the forty-fifth generation counting from the Western Land and the ninth-generation Dharma heir of the Weiyang lineage in the Eastern Land.

**[Addendum 2]** From "The Origins and Lineages of the Five Schools of Chan," in Volume 8 of the *Collected Dharma Talks of Elder Master Hsu Yun*, respectfully recorded by Hsu Yun Deqing:

While I was living at Nanhua, in the summer of the jiaxu year [1934], Upasaka Guo Hanzhai (Kuan Hui) of Changsha, Elder Baosheng of Nanyue, Head Seat Jiucheng Liaozhao, and others came one after another from Great Mount Wei to earnestly request that I rebuild Great Wei. Their thinking was that this school ranked as the eldest of the five houses, but sadly it had lacked heirs, so that the flame at its ancestral monastery had gone out, and the monastery now kept the bell and board of the Linji school. This time it had been burned down by bandits and reduced entirely to ashes, so they had resolved to make a fresh start and restore the Weiyang line. Because I was advanced in age and senior in monastic years, and was respected and trusted by the monasteries everywhere, the assembly asked me to carry on and revive the Weiyang lineage. Since I could not lay down my responsibilities at Nanhua, yet could not decline, I did my best to follow the records. On investigation: this school began with Patriarch Lingyou and after four transmissions reached Bajiao Huiqing, whose disciple Jiche began a lineage verse of twenty characters; the character following "Ji" should be "Miao," and so on in order, to carry on the earlier lineage. However, the records stop at the two masters of the Song dynasty, Sanjiao Zhiqian and Xingyang Ciduo. One account says the two were brothers who both succeeded Patriarch Shao of Baoen as the sixth generation; another says that Zhiqian was the sixth generation and Ciduo the seventh. Since very little of this school's transmission has come down to us, I have kept both accounts, taking Xingyang Ciduo, as heir to Sanjiao Zhiqian, to be the seventh generation. After that there are no records to go by. Now I have taken the first character of Venerable Ciduo's name and of my own name, and continued them by unfolding fifty-six characters, awaiting worthy ones of later times to carry on the work without end. The verse says [Tr.: Each character of such a verse serves in turn as the generation character in Dharma names; this one begins with Ci and De.]:

> Words of virtue proclaimed and spread, the Way greatly flourishes;  
> The fragrance of the precept censer pervades, the fivefold incense made new.
>
> <small>詞德宣衍道大興  戒鼎馨遍五分新</small>

> The flame of wisdom spreads through worlds as many as the sands;  
> Clouds of incense shelter all, shining through past and present.
>
> <small>慧焰彌布周沙界  香雲普蔭燦古今</small>

> With kindness and compassion saving the world, the vows are endless;  
> The light shines like sun and moon, making the great heavens bright.
>
> <small>慈悲濟世願無盡  光昭日月朗太清</small>

> Reviving the flower once held aloft, we spread it wide from Wei;  
> The circle-form, the lamp of the mind, shall shine forever bright.
>
> <small>振啟拈花宏沩上  圓相心燈永昌明</small>

**[Addendum 3]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master once took out the Dharma scroll of the ninth generation of the Weiyang school, which Elder Master Hsu Yun had transmitted to him, and showed it to me. The Master also had the walking stick and the chopsticks that Elder Hsu had used in his daily life. The bearing of that greatly virtuous one lingers in my heart.

**[Addendum 4]** Note appended to the second enlargement of the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs (Expanded Edition)*:

In the summer of the yihai year [1935], I, Yun, took the images of 69 patriarchs that I had collected over several years, attached a biography and a eulogy to each one, and added them to the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs* compiled by the virtuous ones of the past, naming the result the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs (Expanded Edition)*. New printing blocks were carved, and it was distributed from Gushan. In the twenty years since then, I have obtained 27 more, again attached biographies and eulogies to them, and had them carved and added as a further supplement, for a total of 330 images. Yet the true face of the patriarchs is surely not limited to these. Long ago Wenxi asked Manjushri how many were in his assembly. Manjushri replied, "Three three in front, three three behind." [Tr.: A well-known Chan dialogue; the phrase points to a number beyond counting.] Now I, Yun, have added, and added again, completing the "three three in front." As for the "three three behind," that awaits the wise ones to come, who will add and add without end. This is my record.

Buddhist Era 2982, the yiwei year [1955], on the Double Ninth, the ninth day of the ninth lunar month

Recorded by Hsu Yun at his thatched hut on Mount Yunju, at the age of 117

**[Addendum 5]** Preface to the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs (Further Expanded Edition)*

The Way fundamentally has no image; what has an image is not the Way. The Buddhas and Patriarchs have no marks; what has marks is not the Buddhas and Patriarchs. Because living beings are attached, images are shown where there are no images, and marks are shown where there are no marks. If only one can leave marks while in the midst of marks, and let go of images while in the midst of images! That is why the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs* has been expanded once more; the causes and conditions lie here. Now I have also added ten eminent monks, greatly virtuous ones of recent times, so that seeing the worthy, we may strive to equal them and take them as our models. Perhaps this comes close to the principle of no image and no marks!

November 28, 1985

Preface written by the Monk of Changbai Mountain at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas

**[Addendum 6]** *Vernacular Commentary on the Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs*

The *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs*, as its name suggests, records the practice of the Way and the Dharma images of the successive patriarchs of the Western Heaven and the Eastern Land, so that they may be handed down in China and abroad as a standard for later cultivators. It serves the purpose of "seeing the worthy and striving to equal them."

To continue the mind-lamp of the Buddha, the Venerable Master also added seven patriarchs of the Pure Land School, naming the work the *Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs (Further Expanded Edition)*. In 1986 the original text was published first, as a thread-bound set of four volumes, which is of great value for research into the history of the Chan School.

Because the original book is written in classical Chinese, people today find it very hard to understand the virtuous conduct of the greatly virtuous ones of old and the deep and secret states of their enlightenment. The *Vernacular Commentary on the Images and Biographies of the Buddhas and Patriarchs* consists of lectures the Venerable Master gave from 1972 to 1985, over a span of 13 years, 346 sessions in all. After the Venerable Master entered perfect stillness, his disciples, mindful of the painstaking efforts of those before them to protect and preserve the sagely teaching, transcribed and edited the Venerable Master's vernacular explanations of those years step by step, and published them volume by volume.

March 11, 2004

Buddhist Text Translation Society

## 67. Deluded People, Deluded Dreams

Hong Kong was a society of vanity and glitter. Although a group of young people followed the Venerable Master, they had all received a Westernized education. The Venerable Master went to great pains to teach them, and he wrote a song of social realism expressing his pity for wayward youth...

> Living beings in the Dharma-Ending Age have scant blessings and heavy karma;  
> Defiled thoughts easily blaze, while pure virtue is hard to achieve.  
> Ignorance stirs falsely, and where there is karma, deeds follow;  
> Drifting through birth and death, now they float, now they sink.
>
> <small>末法眾生，福輕業重。染心易熾，淨德難成。無明妄動，有業斯行。流浪生死，或浮或沉。</small>

> They take suffering for pleasure, and mistake a thief for their own son;  
> So used to it they no longer notice, numbed by the rain and drunk on the wind.  
> "Freedom" and Europeanization dazzle their eyes and blur their sight;  
> They vie in prizing falsehood, while the truth grows dimmer day by day.
>
> <small>以苦為樂，認賊作子。習然不察，麻雨醉風。自由歐化，眩目迷睛。競尚虛偽，真實日蒙。</small>

> Social life is warped, and bizarre behavior runs rampant;  
> One cheats and another swindles, each showing off his wondrous skills.  
> Men seduce and women beguile, with far-reaching schemes and deep designs;  
> Sparing no money, they try to win each other's favor.
>
> <small>畸形社交，怪態橫生。彼欺此詐，各顯神功。男誘女惑，計遠謀深。不惜金錢，討好對方。</small>

> Once they succeed, they become a resentful and sorrowful couple;  
> Without any joy at all, it ends in desolation.  
> Living beings so upside down are truly to be pitied—  
> Alas, how painful!
>
> <small>及其成功，怨偶悲傷。毫無樂趣，結果淒涼。顛倒眾生，實堪憐憫。嗟呼痛哉！</small>

Before the Venerable Master left the home-life, he read widely and was well versed in the Taoist classics. When lecturing on the sutras and speaking the Dharma, the Venerable Master often quoted the texts and principles of Laozi's *Tao Te Ching* and the *Most High Scripture of Clarity and Stillness*. The following verse on cultivating the Way, written on the sixth day of the ninth lunar month of 1956, draws on stories from the Taoist *Biographies of the Seven Perfected* to encourage his disciples to emulate the firm and sincere resolve for the Way of those in the stories, and to leave the mundane world behind to cultivate the Way.

> Randeng resigned as chief minister and entered the mountain forests; (Note 1)
>
> <small>燃燈辭相入山林，（注一）</small>

> Bu'er, in Luoyang, went deep in the work of the Way. (Note 2)
>
> <small>不二洛陽道業深。（注二）</small>

> Zhongli of the Han house sealed away his marshal's seal; (Note 3)
>
> <small>漢室鐘離封帥印，（注三）</small>

> Li Er of the Shang era abandoned the red dust. (Note 4)
>
> <small>商時李耳棄紅塵。（注四）</small>

(Note 1) Liu Haichan, whose original name was Liu Cao and whose Taoist name was Haichanzi, was a patriarch of the Northern School of Taoism who lived during the Five Dynasties. He served as chief minister to Liu Shouguang, the Prince of Yan, but later abandoned his office and followed Zhengyangzi to live in seclusion and cultivate on Mount Zhongnan.

(Note 2) Sun Yuanzhen (also written Yuanzhen, with a different first character) was one of the Seven Perfected. She took Wang Chongyang as her teacher and was given the name Bu'er. She disfigured her face, left home, and, disguised as a madwoman, went to Luoyang to cultivate the Way.

(Note 3) Han Zhongli, that is, Zhongli Quan, was a great general of the Eastern Han, styled Zhengyangzi, and is one of the Eight Immortals.

(Note 4) Li Er is Laozi.

**[Addendum]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

I remember that in Hong Kong the Venerable Master often spoke the Buddhadharma to us. He not only explained to us what the "Triple Jewel" is, but also told us stories from the Taoist *Biographies of the Seven Perfected*, such as those of Qiu Changchun, whose cleverness led him astray, and Sun Bu'er, who disfigured her face to study the Way, as lessons for our own cultivation.

---

# Part Four: The Lecture Hall and Crossing Over Living Beings

*Ages 39 to 45*

*1956 to 1962 CE*

## 68. The Buddhist Lecture Hall

Within a few years of arriving in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master had built two Way-places one after the other: Western Bliss Gardens Monastery and Cixing Chan Monastery. However, because both were hard to reach and the round trip took a long time, it was difficult for them to draw in more devotees, so some laypeople resolved...

With Western Bliss Gardens Monastery and Cixing Chan Monastery, the Venerable Master had refuge disciples all over Hong Kong, Kowloon, and the New Territories. But Western Bliss Gardens Monastery was on a ridge at Ma Shan Village in Shau Kei Wan on Hong Kong Island, where the hillside was steep and the path winding; the place was cramped and crude, and there were more than 300 stone steps to climb up and down. Cixing Chan Monastery was even farther from the city, halfway up the mountain at Man Cheung Po on Lantau Island, and the round trip meant several hours of climbing mountains and crossing water. To help the Venerable Master spread the Buddhadharma widely, and to make it easier for more devotees to come to a Way-place to hear the Dharma and cultivate, Yu Guo Man and some other laypeople resolved to buy a floor of a building in Happy Valley, Hong Kong. In 1956 another Way-place established by the Venerable Master, the Buddhist Lecture Hall, came into being.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

It was because the building at Western Bliss Gardens leaked. In the space of just one bed there were six leaks. Yu Guo Man said, "Shifu, how can we let you suffer like this? I'll buy you a floor in Happy Valley, and you can move to Happy Valley!" I said, "If you want to buy it, then buy it!" She went home and told her son, and then they bought a floor to be a Way-place. That floor originally cost HK$45,000. She put up HK$40,000, and I and some others made up the rest; we also bought another floor next door. When I was at Western Bliss Gardens I was very thin, but after I moved to Happy Valley I put on weight. Now that I'm here in America I've gotten thin again; I've lost more than 20 pounds.

When I was about to come to America, I had no money, so I thought of selling one of the floors so that I could come to America and establish a Way-place. Yu Guo Man said, "Shifu, don't sell this floor to anyone else! Once it's sold, you'll never be able to buy it back!" I said, "I won't be buying it back anyway! I have no money, and I have to take a little money with me to America." "Oh, then I'll buy it!" So she had her son once again put up about 60,000 dollars, and she bought the floor back. She said, "Never mind! Whenever you come back, Shifu, I'll give it back to you. Right now you need the money!" She also said, "If I told my sons to give you money, they wouldn't be happy about it. But if I say it's to buy your floor, they're fine with it. When you come back in a few years, it will still be yours." I said, "Then do as you like!" That's how I went from Hong Kong to America.

**[Addendum]** Because Yu Guo Man had great faith in the Venerable Master, she came several times from Hong Kong to San Francisco, America, to see him. When she came to San Francisco in 1973, she resolved to leave the home-life. Through the Venerable Master's compassion, on November 27 she shaved her head and left the home-life at Gold Mountain Monastery together with five other disciples, Westerners of American nationality. The Venerable Master gave her the Dharma name Guo Man and the Dharma title Heng Fu. She was already 67 years old at the time. Being simple-hearted, she loved reciting the Buddha's name more than anything, but she was old, and as she recited she would fall asleep. The Venerable Master said: "Guo Man has left the home-life at such an advanced age, and she is so sincere about it. Look at her—the Buddha is always aiding her, aiding her to sleep a bit more. She eats her fill and sleeps soundly; she lies down there and falls right asleep. She can do that because she doesn't have idle thoughts. If she were full of idle thoughts, she couldn't sleep. For instance, when she was in Hong Kong about to come to America, she said she didn't sleep for two days. Of course: 'When will I get to America? What will it be like sitting on the plane? When will I see Shifu?' She had these idle thoughts from morning till night, so she couldn't sleep. Yesterday when she arrived here, she fell asleep as soon as she lay down on the bed, because her idle thoughts had stopped, and so she could sleep."

Yu Guo Man had left the home-life. The Venerable Master always made allowances for elderly people, especially in the Buddha Hall. The Venerable Master said: "Don't make the elderly bow so many times! Standing up like this and kneeling down takes a lot of effort. When she does it on her own, she'll be careful; but when she bows along with everyone else, she gets flustered—she sees these young people so nimble and quick, bowing so neatly and crisply, and she gets very tense there. So you should pay attention to this and not make old people feel rushed. Especially when bowing to the Buddhas, you should bring a bowing cushion for the elderly. Take Guo Man: I told her to use that bowing cushion, to make things a bit easier for her, because she has lumps on her legs again, and kneeling down on the floor is very hard for her. Young people are agile, and their hands and feet are nimble; even if they take a tumble while kneeling there, it doesn't matter—they won't get hurt. But if an old person falls, then there's trouble! Their old bones and old flesh aren't very sturdy. You all need to understand this!"

In the Venerable Master's own words:

She really should have left the home-life long ago, but she had three sons. This eldest son of hers was especially filial: every time he came back to Hong Kong, he bought her good things to eat, bought her clothes, and gave her money. All the money she contributed came from her son. Her whole family took refuge with me. Now that she wants to leave the home-life, I'm very happy too. In the future her skill in reciting the Buddha's name will be very good; she doesn't have many idle thoughts, and she works very hard.

Someone once asked me, "Why doesn't Guo Man leave the home-life?" That has to wait until the time is right! When the time comes, she'll leave home even if she doesn't mean to. If the time hasn't come, telling her to leave home won't work; even if she did leave home, she might sometimes regret it! Guo Man is so old now that she no longer has any problems between men and women to bother her. But her family is very wealthy, and there are many of them: her husband, three sons, and three or four daughters as well, and her daughters-in-law and grandchildren are all very good to her. That she could leave the home-life shows she has truly been able to let go! I'm not saying this for others to hear; I'm saying it for you to hear!

## 69. Better Still to Put It into Practice

Once the Buddhist Lecture Hall was established, it lived up to its name: the Venerable Master often lectured on the sutras and spoke the Dharma there, and every weekend he trained his disciples to take turns speaking the Dharma. It goes without saying that the young disciples who constantly followed the Venerable Master received this training; even...

...an elderly Upasika over 60 years old, Hu Guo Wei, took part every time. She once recited this Pure Land poem for everyone:

> Far off I point to the West, beside the setting sun;  
> The road home runs straight as a bowstring.
>
> <small>遙指西方落日邊，一條歸路直如弦。</small>

> When I go, I'll need no shoes or socks:  
> With every step, lotus flowers will bloom.
>
> <small>去時不用穿鞋襪，步步蓮花朵朵生。</small>

This elderly Upasika was Li Guo Yuan's mother (see the chapter "27. Wondrous Accounts of Saving Lives (2)"). After taking refuge with the Venerable Master, she never let her recitation beads out of her hand. One day, probably some time after 1962, she suddenly took it into her head to go around visiting relatives and friends she had not seen for a long time. Three days later she passed away peacefully at home. The Venerable Master once said, "Those who can speak are not as good as those who can listen, and those who can listen are not as good as those who can practice." She is truly someone worth emulating!

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Take listening to the Dharma: people may hear a great deal, but they understand very little; or they may understand a lot, but very few can put it into practice. That's why I often say, "Those who can speak are not as good as those who can listen; those who can listen are not as good as those who can practice; those who can practice are not as good as those who can realize it." People who can speak may speak so well that heavenly flowers rain down in profusion and golden lotuses well up from the earth, but if they don't put it into practice, if they don't carry it out in their own conduct, that's called "talking about food and counting others' treasure." It's like a stone man: the stone man can talk, but he can't walk, because he's made of stone. So it's said, "Speaking well, speaking wonderfully—if you can't practice it, it isn't the Way." And how do people who know how to listen listen to the Dharma? It's "Coarse words and subtle speech all return to the primary meaning."

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshu Elder Zhi Ding wrote in his article "In Memory of Elder To Lun": After Elder To Lun returned from Thailand to settle in Hong Kong, he immediately set about the work of propagating the Dharma and benefiting living beings. First he founded Western Bliss Gardens Monastery in Sai Wan Ho, Hong Kong, and drew in beginners with the Dharma door of reciting the Buddha's name, which reaches those of all three kinds of faculties. Later he bought the 12th floor of a building in Happy Valley and established the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall. As its name suggests, it was for propagating the great sutras, broadly benefiting those of superior faculties and sharp wisdom while also reaching the general public. Moreover, it gave monks one more place to lodge. Besides accepting monastics to stay and lecturing on the sutras and expounding the teachings in Hong Kong, Elder To Lun also printed sutras for free distribution and circulated Dharma treasures.

**[Addendum 2]** Tan Guo Shi recounted:

The elderly Upasika Hu Guo Wei was illiterate, but after taking refuge she attended every Dharma assembly and sutra lecture at Western Bliss Gardens without fail. At every break she would sit off to one side with her recitation beads in her hand, reciting the Buddha's name. When I greeted her, she always smiled at me. At the weekend Dharma assemblies, every time the Master asked her to go up on the stage to speak, she would only say, "My name is Hu Guo Wei. I don't know how to give talks. Amitabha!" and then step down, all smiles. But one day she suddenly recited the verse above, and everyone was astonished!

According to her daughter-in-law, for three days before she passed away she went everywhere visiting her relatives and friends. On the morning of the day she passed away, she bathed, changed into her robe and lay precept sash, sat in full lotus on the bed in her room, and passed away while reciting the Buddha's name! Only when her daughter-in-law went to see her at lunchtime did she find out that she had already passed away.

**[Addendum 3]** Tan Guo Zheng recounted in 1995:

To spread Buddhism, one must lecture on the sutras and speak the Dharma. Every weekend there were Buddhist talks at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall. The Buddhist Lecture Hall was set up not only for great Dharma Masters, but also to train the little Dharma Masters of the future.

The Venerable Master required his disciples, male and female, old and young, all to speak. Even the elderly Upasika Hu Guo Wei, who was in her sixties, would go up to speak, reciting the same verse every time. Expounding the Buddhadharma without notes, speaking straight from the heart and saying whatever came naturally—this was the Venerable Master's unique method of training his disciples to lecture on the sutras, and it has continued to this day.

**[Addendum 4]** *Nature Daily* (*Ziran Ribao*), Monday, November 10, 1958 (the twenty-ninth day of the ninth month of the wuxu year):

Buddhist Lecture Hall Teaches the Mahayana Dharma

(Special report) The Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall, on the top floor of 1A Shing Woo Road, Happy Valley, in this territory, held a Dharma-propagation lecture assembly yesterday (Sunday), presided over by the eminent monk Dharma Master To Lun, and the audience turned out enthusiastically. Dharma Master To Lun formerly served as dean of academic affairs at the Nanhua Buddhist Academy. Since coming to Hong Kong, he has founded three Way-places in succession: Western Bliss Gardens in Shau Kei Wan, Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island, and now the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley. His refuge disciples are found throughout Hong Kong, Kowloon, and the New Territories. From now on, every Sunday from 2:00 to 5:00 p.m., Dharma Master To Lun will lecture on the Mahayana sutras to save the people of the world. Good men and faithful women are welcome to come and hear the Dharma.

## 70. Pilgrimage to Burma

In July 1957, Chen Zhenfu, the Burmese consul, and Judge Yu Zhendong invited the Venerable Master to go to Burma to take part in the Opening-the-Light ceremony of the World Peace Pagoda. The Venerable Master's long-cherished aim was to unite Mahayana and Small Vehicle Buddhism into one, so that Buddhism in the world...

The Venerable Master went to Burma accompanied by Wen Guo Mi. They visited the Buddhist university at Pegu, paid homage at the Great Golden Pagoda in Rangoon, led several hundred Rangoon Buddhists in circumambulating the pagoda while reciting the Buddha's name, and at the Reclining Buddha Temple sat in meditation, investigated Chan, and studied together with Burmese Bhikshus. The Venerable Master set an example himself, guiding Mahayana and Small Vehicle practitioners to cultivate together, advance together, and develop together, so that Buddhism throughout the world would be united as one, with no views of "Great" and "Small" Vehicles.

While in Burma, the Venerable Master stayed at the World Buddhist Society. After the Dharma assembly concluded, he went from Rangoon to Penang in Malaysia, where he stayed at Kek Lok Si Monastery, and then went on to Singapore. When the Venerable Master left Burma, the laypeople could not help kneeling and weeping at the airport as they saw him off. Although his time there was short, it let them see the bearing and virtue of a true cultivator.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

The Buddhadharma originated in India and later spread to every country, and many of these countries regard the Buddhadharma as their own. Burma has Burmese Buddhism, Cambodia has Cambodian Buddhism, Vietnam has Vietnamese Buddhism, Thailand has Thai Buddhism, China has Chinese Buddhism, Japan has Japanese Buddhism, and Korea has Korean Buddhism. When the Buddha first spoke the Dharma, did he speak it for just one country? No. The Buddha observed the potentials of his listeners and taught accordingly, and the Buddhadharma suits the people of every country. The Buddhadharma itself is not divided into so many national Buddhisms. But these many countries are themselves unwilling to let Buddhism spread and flourish widely, so they take it as their own and say that this Buddhism belongs to their country.

For this reason, several decades ago (1957), when I went to Burma and visited a Buddhist Way-place, they brought out a guest book and asked me to write some words to commemorate the visit. I wrote: "Our vision should take in the whole world; our resolve should embrace the nature of the Dharma Realm. Promote the inherent, concrete, and perfect Buddhism, so that the teachings and vehicles are unified, and the Great and the Small return to the source. May everyone together reach the other shore, and each one see the nature and become a Buddha." My reason for saying those few lines at that time was that if Buddhism is to spread and flourish, our vision must reach far. We must spread Buddhism to every country and every corner, even into every mote of dust, turning the great Dharma wheel there to teach and transform living beings, so that everyone can leave suffering and attain bliss, and end birth and death. That's why I boldly said, "This Buddhism of ours is not the private Buddhism of any one country. It is the Buddhism of all humankind, the Buddhism of all living beings. We should not treat Buddhism as a private treasury; we should make it available to the whole world, doing as much as we can."

The Shurangama Mantra has Dharmas for averting disasters. For example, if you're clearly headed for some accident, it turns a big disaster into a small one, and a small one into none at all, so that most of the time things turn out to be a scare without real harm. Someone who was supposed to fall into the sea and drown won't drown once they recite the Shurangama Mantra. Or if you take a boat and the boat is supposed to sink, when you recite this mantra, the boat won't sink. Or if you're on an airplane that's supposed to explode in midair, as soon as you recite this mantra, it won't explode. This is truly miraculous beyond words! The leg of the flight from Burma to Thailand was very dangerous, yet the plane I was on didn't shake or sway the whole way. Even the pilot said, "How come the plane is so steady this time?" He didn't know that at the time the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division, and all the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, were protecting the plane on the left and right, in front and behind.

In the countries of Southern Buddhism, such as Siam (Thailand), Burma, and Ceylon, the monastics all go on alms rounds to beg for their food; only in China do they cook for themselves in the monasteries. When monks beg for food in Siam and Burma, a monk comes to the house of a donor, and the donor kneels on the ground, raises the bowl of food above their head, pours it into the monastic's bowl, and then bows three times. That is how they make offerings to the Triple Jewel. Every household makes offerings to the Triple Jewel in this way.

I saw that the bowls of the monastics in Siam and Burma were very big; when full, they held enough for several people to eat. Their monastics eat with their hands, using neither spoons nor chopsticks. This is called "eating by the handful," and they say that eating this way makes the food taste more fragrant. Since I'm not used to it, I've never tried it.

The Burmese are in the habit of putting cooked onions and garlic on the dining table. The smell of the onions and garlic gets breathed right down into your stomach. Even without eating any, I couldn't stand it; that whole day I felt as if the smell were in my stomach and intestines. Onions and garlic are among the five pungent plants. The five pungent plants are onions, garlic, chives, shallots, and asafoetida. Eating them gives you a very unpleasant odor, so those who keep a vegetarian diet should abstain from them.

In India, Siam, Burma, and Ceylon, they still follow to this day the system of wearing the sash that existed when the Buddha was in the world. The kashayas that monastics wear are all yellow and have no hook and ring. Why do Chinese kashayas have a hook and ring? This is an expedient adaptation suited to the circumstances. When Chinese monastics put on the sash, they also leave the right shoulder bare; this too follows the system established by the Buddha. Because China's climate is rather cold, they had to wear padded cotton clothes and simply put the kashaya on over their clothes. If the kashaya slipped off, it was hard to notice, and it could easily be lost. The Patriarch Bodhidharma was very clever, and he was also an inventor: he invented the use of a hook and ring on the kashaya, which both preserved the system the Buddha established and allowed for expedient adaptation. That is the origin of the hook and ring in China.

After I left the home-life, I asked many elder Dharma Masters about the hook and ring. These Elder Masters all shook their heads; they didn't know. It's a very small question, so no one knew. So who actually told me? No one told me. When I compared the climate of China with the climates of India, Siam, and Burma, I realized that the patriarch who first came to China put on clothes because the climate was cold, and later worked out a way to fasten the kashaya with a hook and ring, so that when it was draped over the body it wouldn't slip off. When I mentioned this reason to some elder Dharma Masters, they all said that was how it was.

**[Addendum 1]** Dharma Master Le Guan mentions in his *Impressions of Master Hsu Yun*:

Elder Hsu asked me, "Have you come to request lodging and stay on here, or are you just passing through?" I said, "I came specially to pay my respects to you, Elder Master, and right after that I have to go on to Guangzhou." He said, "You've gone to the trouble of coming all the way here to see me, so you must stay a while longer; only then will I be at ease. However, life in the monastery is plain, and we have no fine tea or food to offer you." I replied, "I'm very grateful for your compassion, Elder Master, and I would like to stay close to you longer, but unfortunately I didn't bring my robes with me, so it's inconvenient to stay in the monastery." He said, "In times of war and chaos, don't be too particular about that. The round-collared robes that we Chinese monks usually wear are not, according to the precepts and Vinaya, the clothing a monk ought to wear. Only the kashaya is the true monastic garment. If you feel it's inconvenient, then don't follow the assembly to the Buddha Hall or to the formal meals in the dining hall; just take your meals in the guest hall every day."

**[Addendum 2]** The World Peace Pagoda is located in the northern suburbs of Rangoon, Burma, and is a famous Buddhist structure in the city. In 1954, in order to convene the "Sixth Council," the Burmese government held a two-year World Buddhist Scripture Council at this site (Auspicious Hill). At the foot of Auspicious Hill is a cave called the Great Sacred Cave, which can hold an assembly of more than 10,000 Bhikshus. The cave has six entrances and six stone pillars; on the walls are six relief carvings, and on the platform are mounted six candle-shaped electric lamps, symbolizing the Sixth Council. The World Peace Pagoda stands to the right of Auspicious Hill.

The architecture of the Peace Pagoda also embodies the symbols of the Sixth World Buddhist Scripture Council: the pagoda is 35 meters high, its base is 35 meters in circumference, and its body is hollow. The whole pagoda is supported by six great pillars and has six doors, and at each entrance a huge statue of the Buddha is enshrined; all of these symbolize the Sixth Council of the scriptures. At the very center of the pagoda is a small treasury, where a Buddha image cast of pure silver and weighing nearly a ton is enshrined in the sharira shrine; it is the treasure that guards the hall and is extremely precious. The sharira of the Buddha's two great disciples, the Venerable Shariputra and the Venerable Maudgalyayana, are also enshrined here. In the niches on the round pillars inside the pagoda there are 28 small statues of the Buddha. Inside the pagoda there is also a museum of Buddhist art, which has gathered a wide range of Buddha images and Buddhist scriptures from many places. Its collection includes two sharira originally enshrined in the Potala Palace in Tibet, presented by a Chinese Buddhist delegation in 1955; a photographic facsimile of an 800-year-old Chinese Tripitaka; and Chinese and Tibetan monastic robes, bowls, monks' staffs, and other items, all displayed in the pagoda's display cases. Although the World Peace Pagoda is the youngest pagoda in Rangoon, it holds an extremely important place in Buddhist history and culture, and it is also the center of Burmese Buddhist culture.

(Editor's note: [The Sixth Council] On Vesak Day (World Buddha Day), May 17, 1954, Burmese Buddhism, with state sponsorship, convened the "Sixth Council of the Buddhist Scriptures" on a hill at Yegu in the northern suburbs of Rangoon. This council took as its basis the 729 marble slabs inscribed at the Fifth Council, and it consulted the Pali editions of Ceylon, Thailand, and Cambodia and of the Pali Text Society of London, as well as the various Pali editions of Burma, carrying out a detailed critical collation. The council also invited Bhikshus from all the countries of Southern Buddhism to take part, and Bhikshus from the countries of Northern Buddhism were also invited to observe. In all, more than 2,000 people from the Buddhist world took part. It took more than two years and was completed in 1956 (Buddhist Era 2500), and the materials of the council were printed and distributed. The significance of this council lay in uniting Buddhists and furthering the flourishing of Theravada Buddhism. This is called the Sixth Council.)

**[Addendum 3]** The Great Golden Pagoda of Rangoon, also called the Shwedagon Pagoda, stands on Singuttara Hill in Rangoon. It is the highest point in Rangoon and the most famous pagoda in Burma. It is also the most sacred pagoda in Burma, because it enshrines relics of four Buddhas: the staff of Krakucchanda Buddha, the water filter of the Perfectly and Equally Enlightened Kanakamuni Buddha, the robe of Kashyapa Buddha, and eight hairs of Shakyamuni Buddha. It was first built in 585 BCE. The main stupa is 99.36 meters high. The entire pagoda is covered with gold from top to bottom; jeweled bells hang from the golden umbrella at its summit, and the very top is set with 5,448 diamonds. The Great Golden Pagoda of Rangoon, together with Angkor Wat in Cambodia and Borobudur in Indonesia, is acclaimed as one of the three great ancient monuments of Southeast Asia. It is a world-famous pagoda and a symbol of the Burmese nation.

**[Addendum 4]** The Reclining Buddha Temple

The Chaukhtatgyi Pagoda enshrines the largest reclining Buddha in the world. It was first built in 1907 and rebuilt in 1966. The image is 75 meters long and 30 meters high. On the soles of its feet are 108 designs, which represent the 108 rounds of rebirth that people undergo and also symbolize that all things in heaven and earth lie beneath its feet. The Great Golden Pagoda and the Reclining Buddha are the most representative symbols of Burmese Buddhism.

**[Addendum 5]** Wen Guo Mi recounted on September 13, 2010:

In July 1957, the Master went to Rangoon, Burma, at the invitation of the Chief Justice of Rangoon; the invitation was delivered through Mr. Chen Zhenfu, the Burmese consul in Hong Kong. When we arrived in Rangoon, devout local Buddhists of Chinese descent came to the airport to meet us. The Master stayed in the back courtyard of the World Peace Pagoda. During the day the Master attended meetings, discussing the development of the Buddhadharma in various regions with participants from Burma and neighboring countries; in the evenings, the Master went to a nearby Chinese temple to give instructional talks to local Buddhists, and the audience was very respectful toward the Master.

We stayed in Rangoon for about two weeks; our next stop was Penang in Malaysia. Shortly after takeoff, the twin-engine plane we were on ran into a storm. The plane shook violently and dropped sharply, things on board rattled, and everyone was terrified. The Master began to sit in meditation and pray to the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division to steady the plane, and I did the same. A few minutes later the plane stabilized, and the rest of the flight was smooth.

In Penang the Master stayed at the renowned Kek Lok Si Monastery, where he was warmly welcomed by the monastery's abbot and the local Dharma Masters. The Master met with local Buddhist devotees, many of whom were students. The Master stayed in Penang for a week and then continued his journey. Singapore was the last stop before returning to Hong Kong, and our host there was Ms. Bi Junhui, president of the Singapore Buddhist Association. She was a devout Buddhist and very respectful toward the Master. While in Singapore, the Master held several meetings with people from the Buddhist association and gave an instructional talk to the students of the Bodhi Primary School.

**[Addendum 6]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master praised Guo Mi, saying, "When Guo Mi went with me to Burma, he was only 16, and he handled affairs and interpreting extremely well." Guo Mi had a heart condition and had never dared to fly before. When Guo Mi went to Burma with the Master, on the flight from Burma to Penang the plane ran into turbulence and bounced up and down, yet he came through safe and sound; he believed it was the Bodhisattvas' protection. After Guo Mi returned to Hong Kong, a doctor's examination confirmed that his heart was normal and fine! When Guo Mi went to Burma with the Master he was still very short, but not long after coming back he grew taller—he even became taller than the Master!

## 71. The Remarkable Story of Guo Mi (2)

Once, when the Venerable Master was lecturing on a sutra at the Buddhist Lecture Hall, the Venerable Master sat on the Dharma seat without saying a word the whole time, and only Guo Mi spoke. People whispered to one another, resentful, and everyone was thinking, "We came to hear the Venerable Master speak the Dharma. How can he let this teenager talk nonsense!"...

Everyone was thinking this. When the lecture was over, the Venerable Master said earnestly: "We ordinary people very easily attach to outward appearances. As soon as we see that it isn't a Dharma Master speaking the Dharma, we immediately give rise to discriminating thoughts, regardless of whether the sutra is being explained correctly or not. What Guo Mi said is also what I wanted to say. What came out of his mouth was just what I had in mind to say."

In the Venerable Master's own words:

This little disciple wasn't tall, but he spoke English very well. In Hong Kong, whenever Westerners wanted to see me, he was the one who interpreted.
