But when foreigners saw how small he was, they didn't take what he said very seriously. I told him, "Hurry up and grow taller! You're so short that no matter how well you speak, people see you as a little kid and find it hard to have faith in what you say!" He was very obedient: he grew three inches in one week, and now he's taller than me. That too is quite an inconceivable thing! He was the disciple I cherished most in Hong Kong. He had originally promised me he would leave the home-life, but now I don't know whether he'll leave home or not.

After about a year, he opened the Buddha Eye. He was extremely clear about everything, understood a great deal of the Buddhadharma, and had great faith in me. When I lectured on the sutras in Hong Kong, he interpreted into Cantonese. Whatever I wanted to say, I'd say just one sentence, and before I had finished, he could say it all for me, explaining the principles very completely. Some people objected, saying, "Shifu didn't say any of this. How come he's saying so much?" Actually, he said that much because I told him to. Because he had the Buddha Eye, he knew I wanted him to say more. Why did I have him say more? Because what he said was the same as what I would say. Some ignorant people became very jealous of him, so much so that it was as fierce as when Shenxiu wanted to kill the Sixth Patriarch.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

Once the Master and his disciples took the Tai O ferry to Lantau Island, and Guo Mi was among them. There were more than 300 people on the boat, more than half of them Buddhists, so the Master told Guo Mi to speak the Dharma on board. The topic the Master gave was "One, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine, ten." Guo Mi did as he was told and spoke with unobstructed eloquence, and the listeners were full of praise. Afterwards the Master said to Guo Mi, "You spoke very movingly, but it's a pity you're so short; people don't notice you or take you seriously. It would be good if you were a bit taller!" After the Master said this, Guo Mi grew three inches in one week.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

I went to America at the end of 1957. Before I left, I transferred to a Catholic school and was in the same class as Guo Mi. At that time Guo Mi was very short. In the 1970s, when the Master returned to Hong Kong from America for the first time, I went back with him, and when I saw Guo Mi then, he was already taller than the Master. He was working on a cruise ship that sailed between Hong Kong and Australia.

## 72. Unforgettable Kindness

In those turbulent times, although the Venerable Master was unable to return to Yunmen to be near Elder Hsu again, he often exchanged letters with Elder Hsu and supported Elder Hsu with all his heart and strength. Below are letters from Elder Hsu:

To the Benevolent One, To Lun, for your wise perusal:

Yesterday Guang Miao came up the mountain bringing a great deal of gold leaf. Apart from the HK$1,500 that you, Benevolent One, kindly contributed as a meritorious donation, HK$1,298.40 of the price is still owing. You earlier resolved to have a statue of Amitabha Buddha made, so I very much hope that you will hand over the amount owing, HK$1,300, to Guang Miao. I would be most obliged. Furthermore, this monastery is now in urgent need of funds for construction, and I very much hope that you, Benevolent One, will continue to resolve to support it. I also hope that when it is convenient you will lead overseas Chinese laypeople on a visit to the mountain. This is my hope. With this I wish you

Dharma joy.

Hsu Yun

Zhenru Chan Monastery, Mount Yunju, the second day of the fourth month

To Dharma Master To Lun, for your perusal in the Way:

Several years have suddenly passed since we parted, and I often think of you. Some time ago the layperson Guo wrote to say that your Dharma work in Hong Kong is flourishing, which greatly comforts me! Just now Taiping Lotus Lodge in Guangdong forwarded your kind letter, together with your gift of HK$600, and I was glad to learn of everything. Your resolve is extraordinary: you are restoring an ancient monastery, and you have also kept us in mind from afar and kindly given meritorious donations. I cannot help sighing at how rare this is. It is now almost three years since I, Yun, came to Yunju and built a thatched hut. This too is a famous Way-place of the patriarchs in this country, but it had long lain in ruins, its halls completely reduced to rubble. After I came here, monks from all over heard of it and came too, so the year before last we managed to build a Dharma hall to house the assembly so they could dwell in peace. This spring we are building the Buddha Hall, the Hall of the Heavenly Kings, and other structures. But our means are very strained; the resident assembly numbers over a hundred, and provisions for the Way are also hard to come by. Your Dharma affinities are very extensive, and I very much hope you will do your best to help us and bring this meritorious work to completion. That would be most fortunate! As for what you said about making more than ten holy images, these are all extraordinary deeds. But as for making an image of me, how could I deserve it? That is quite out of the question! Please don't do so! You are earnest for the sake of the Dharma and continue the wisdom-life of the Buddhas and Patriarchs, so your wish should be fulfilled. Enclosed I send the lineage record, so that you may carry on the patriarchal line and the Way of the Patriarchs may be revived through it. This is my utmost hope! This reply cannot say all. With this I wish you

Dharma bliss.

With palms joined, the patched-robed Yun, the ninth day of the fourth month

Letterhead of Zhenru Chan Monastery, Mount Yunju

In the Venerable Master's own words:

The Elder wrote asking me to do meritorious deeds, so I vowed to donate a dozen or so images, such as the Buddha images for the Jeweled Hall of the Great Hero, to Zhenru Chan Monastery on Mount Yunju. I also went to Burma to buy gold leaf to gild the Buddha images: more than 300 boxes of gold leaf in all (large boxes at that). The Elder was very pleased and wrote several times to thank me. This shows how deep and far-reaching his regard for the younger generation was. In his humble virtue he never let up; he was sparing toward himself and generous toward others, and he set aside his own way to follow others. His great spirit, unsurpassed compassion, lofty virtue, and utterly true equality won people's heartfelt and sincere admiration. He also wrote summoning me to Yunju. In my Chan contemplation I knew that he wished to entrust me with the heavy responsibility of Zhenru Monastery, but because of various causes and conditions I was unable to answer his call, which I regret deeply to this day. And once I had established the Buddhist Lecture Hall, I was busy all day long propagating the great Dharma, and I felt all the more that I could not split myself in two. I had intended to put affairs fully in order and entrust them to someone, and then go back to be near the Elder and serve at his side...

## 73. The Dhuta Left Speechless

Once, a dhuta with long hair down to his shoulders, who had burned off a finger, came to the Buddhist Lecture Hall to visit the Venerable Master. The Venerable Master said to him, "Dharma Master, I have the first line of a couplet. If you can match it, I'll call you a seasoned cultivator."...

The Venerable Master went on, "If you can't match it, I'll call you an old demon king. Listen! 'White and water make a spring; within, one and great make heaven.'" For half an hour the old dhuta scratched his head and ears but couldn't match it, so the Venerable Master spoke a verse to admonish him. (Editor's note: The original text has been lost; it is translated here freely from the English rendering.)

> Old demon king with tangled hair and grimy face,  
> Everywhere you go, you set people gossiping.  
> In the end, will you let go or not?
>
> <small>蓬頭垢面老魔王  到處令人說短長  究竟放下不放下</small>

Why did the Venerable Master give him such a hard time? Because when this dhuta first came to Hong Kong, he went around proclaiming that he was the teacher of Elder Master Hsu Yun. Many of Elder Hsu's disciples believed it was true, and so they supported him and made offerings to him.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

A man came to Hong Kong with long hair and a long beard, looking very scruffy. When people said, "I've taken refuge with Elder Master Hsu Yun," he would say, "Oh! He's my disciple." When Elder Hsu came to Hong Kong, his refuge disciples said, "Our Grand Master is now..." Elder Hsu said, "Who is your Grand Master?" "So-and-so—he says he's your teacher!" Elder Hsu just smiled and said nothing. When I was in Hong Kong, there was also someone who passed himself off as my teacher, though I didn't even know him. So in Buddhism there are plenty of strange people and strange happenings.

**[Addendum 1]** Coming up with a good matching line for a couplet is certainly not easy, but composing a good first line is even harder. Take this first line, "White and water make a spring; within, one and great make heaven": it is a character-splitting puzzle, and it also subtly contains a Chan meaning. The first two characters, "white" and "water," must combine to form the third character, "spring"; and the fifth and sixth characters, "one" and "great," combine again to form the last character, "heaven."

**[Addendum 2]** Professor Yang Fusen recounted on August 14, 1995:

Once, in a class on couplets, the Master said, "I'll give you a first line, and we'll see whether you can match it with a second line." I nodded in agreement. The Master said that the first line was: "White and water make a spring; within, one and great make heaven."

I immediately grasped that the cleverness of this first line lay in taking the two characters "spring" and "heaven," splitting them apart, and then putting them together into a line. The second line would have to do the same.

So I immediately wrote down the four characters for "mountain," "stone," "crag," and "below" as the first half of the second line. To match "one" in the first line, I thought of the number "two." Then it suddenly occurred to me to use the character "husband," because "husband" and "heaven" are almost identical: "heaven" is made up of "one" and "great," and "husband" should be made up of "two" and "man." But how could I match "one, great, heaven"?

While I was still pondering it, the Master, watching from the side, knew I was stuck, and couldn't help giving me a hint: "Wouldn't 'two, man, husband' do it?" Suddenly it dawned on me: "two, man, husband" means "the husband of two people," which matches "one, great, heaven" perfectly. So I was the first one present to hand in an answer, and the couplet became:

> White and water make a spring; within, one and great make heaven,
>
> <small>白水泉中一大天，</small>

> Mountain and stone make a crag; below, two and man make husband.
>
> <small>山石巖下二人夫。</small>

The Master looked at it, nodded, and smiled without a word.

## 74. The Brahma Net, Layer upon Layer

In 1958 the Venerable Master invited Dharma Master Sheng Yi to lecture on the *Brahma Net Bodhisattva Precepts Sutra* and the *Vajra Sutra* at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall, over a period of three months. The couplet inside the Buddhist Lecture Hall reads:

> The Brahma Net, layer upon layer: the Buddhas of the ten directions transmit the mind-ground;
>
> <small>梵網重重十方諸佛傳心地</small>

> Its sound, clear and distinct: all living beings awaken to the heaven of their nature.
>
> <small>聲音了了一切眾生悟性天</small>

—Composed and inscribed by To Lun, a Bhikshu in the mountains

**[Addendum 1]** *Wah Kiu Man Po*, Friday, January 2, 1959 (the twenty-third day of the eleventh month of the wuxu year):

Buddhist Lecture Hall Holds Lectures on the *Vajra Sutra*

Dharma Master To Lun, abbot of the Buddhist Lecture Hall on the 12th floor of Yu Yuen Mansion, Happy Valley, together with many Buddhist devotees, has respectfully invited Chan Master Sheng Yi to come down from the mountain to the Hall to expound the Buddhadharma and lecture on the entire *Vajra Prajna Paramita Sutra*. The lectures began on the evening of the nineteenth day of this lunar month and are held every evening from 7:30 to 9:00. People from all walks of life are welcome to come and listen and share in the benefits of the Dharma.

**[Addendum 2]** From *A Brief Biography of Elder Master Sheng Yi*, published by Po Lam Monastery:

At the Buddhist Lecture Hall run by Dharma Master To Lun, Master Sheng Yi lectured on the *Brahma Net Bodhisattva Precepts Sutra* and the *Vajra Sutra*.

**[Addendum 3]** Elder Dharma Master Sheng Yi, the retired abbot of Po Lam Monastery on Lantau Island, Hong Kong, passed away peacefully on August 3, 2010. Elder Dharma Master Sheng Yi was a native of Xinhui in Guangdong. He lived to the age of 92, had been a monastic for 69 years, and had held the precepts for 67 autumns. Besides restoring Po Lam Monastery, Elder Sheng Yi served from 1983 to 1990 as the fourth-generation abbot of Po Lin Monastery at Ngong Ping. In March 1958, at the age of 36, he went to Mount Yunju to pay his respects to Elder Hsu, received the mind seal, and was given the name Xuan Xuan, becoming a ninth-generation heir to the Dharma lineage of the Weiyang school. His Verse of Certification says [Tr.: The four lines begin with the characters of the names Xuan Xuan and Sheng Yi.]:

> Proclaiming and spreading the wondrous meaning, he carries on the ancestral school;  
> Mystery upon mystery, traces vanish, and all affairs merge and fuse.
>
> <small>宣宏妙義繼先宗  玄玄泯跡事镕融</small>

> Sagely understanding and worldly feelings are but shadows of flowers in the sky;  
> Let him be one who roams free and at ease.
>
> <small>聖解凡情空花影  一任逍遙自在人</small>

## 75. Dharma Master Heng Yi

Bhikshuni Heng Yi (Dharma name Guo Li) was born in 1924; her ancestral home was Shunde County in Guangdong Province. In 1954 she went to Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island to study the Buddhadharma. After taking refuge with the Venerable Master, she worked hard at clearing land and growing crops...

In 1961, deeply distressed by the shortage of hands, she got gasoline and rice straw ready, intending to burn herself on Amitabha Buddha's birthday. Fortunately the Venerable Master became aware of it, and on the day before Amitabha's birthday he told her to come down the mountain. The Venerable Master asked her, "When the fire burns your body, will you run or not?" She replied, "Heaven knows! I've never been burned before." The Venerable Master told her to let it go and go recite the Buddha's name, and to stop farming.

That day she happened to come across a copy of the *Treatise on the Vajra Mind Dharani* and read Chan Master Zuyuan's section on the ten demons that disturb the proper; only then did she recognize her own demonic obstacle. So she decided to shave her head and leave the home-life at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery on the day of Amitabha's birthday. In the early days after she left home, Dharma Master Heng Yi missed her lay father very much. The Venerable Master instructed her, "Amitabha Buddha is your father!" At that one word from a good teacher, she woke as if from a dream and suddenly understood. From then on she devoted her thoughts wholeheartedly to her Dharma family, and for the rest of her life she supported the Way-places.

In September 1963, Po Lin Monastery held a Triple Platform Ordination, and the Venerable Master had Dharma Master Heng Yi take part in this transmission of the precepts, and so she became a Bhikshuni.

**Dharma Master Heng Yi's own account, September 25, 2000:**

I was at Cixing Monastery for such a long time, and I didn't know I would leave the home-life; I only knew to help the Master with the work. It was the Master who brought me across to leave home! When the Master told me to leave home, I didn't know that the Master wanted me to leave home. At that time I was afflicted, and only when I read about the ten demonic obstacles in the *Treatise on the Vajra Mind Dharani* did I realize that this obstacle of mine was a demonic obstacle, and then I thought of leaving home. The Master said, "I didn't know you wanted to leave home either." I said, "You're my Master, and you didn't know I was going to leave home? Then what did I bow to you as my Master for?" Then the Master asked me, "When do you want to leave home? I'll give you three dates to choose from: Amitabha Buddha's birthday, Guanyin's birthday, or Shakyamuni Buddha's birthday."

I shaved my head and left home at 37, and received the precepts at 39 (in September). After I left home I stopped farming and recited the Buddha's name. The year after I left home, in February, the Master went to America. The Master brought me to the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall and wouldn't let me go back to Cixing Monastery; he wanted me to stay at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall. I hadn't realized that, as soon as I had left home, the Master was going to leave. That year, when the Master was about to leave Hong Kong for America, I cried so hard I nearly fainted. The Master said to me then, "You've just left home, and I'm going away. I'm afraid that after I've gone, you'll change your mind and give up the monastic life." I told the Master, "I won't change my mind. I still have kindness to repay: I have to repay the Buddha's kindness and my teacher's kindness. I have to repay your kindness, Shifu!"

We accompanied the Master all the way to see him off on his flight, and the whole way the Master kept saying he was afraid we would change our minds, because there was someone else who had also left home under the Master, who was still very young then. Once the Master went to America, he was gone for 11 years; only after 11 years did he come back to Hong Kong.

At that time it was very hard for the Master to establish Way-places in America; he had nothing at all, so I saved up supplies and sent them to the Master. Now the Master has built so many Way-places, and you must resolve to support them! Don't be afraid of hardship! Don't let the Master's painstaking efforts go to waste.

**Dharma Master Heng Yi's own account, September 26, 2000:**

Back then, when I was clearing the fields at Cixing Monastery, I accidentally killed a lot of earthworms while digging. One day I saw a great many earthworms come to demand my life in payment. I said to them, "I didn't mean to kill you when I was digging; I couldn't see you. If you want to take my life now, then go ahead, but please let me tell my fellow cultivators first. When I come back, you can come for my life!" So I told my fellow cultivators about it, and when I came back, I said to the earthworms who had come for my life, "Come on!" Then I saw them, their whole bodies glowing, circle three times above my head, and then they flew out the window and were gone.

There was also a kind of insect that ate only flowers and leaves. Because its head was red and its body black, it was called the "Red-Headed Army." They often came to eat the flowers and leaves. We caught them and took them several hills away, but they came running back; we sent them away and they came back again, always like that. Each time, we caught them in cans, mooncake tins, and boxes like that, and then took them away to liberate them. The Master once taught us to recite the Great Compassion Mantra to tell them not to come, but that didn't work either; they always came back. As long as there was something to eat, no matter what you did to them, they wouldn't leave. Later, when the Master came back to Cixing Monastery, he asked me to take him around the fields to have a look. At the edge of the fields, the Master nudged the cans there with his foot and asked me what they were. I said they were Red-Headed Army. The Master opened them and saw that they were all full of dead Red-Headed Army insects. The Master asked why we hadn't liberated them. I answered the Master, "Sometimes when we're too busy we forget." The Master said, "These past few nights, tens upon tens of thousands of Red-Headed Army insects have been at the head of my bed, weeping and complaining that you killed them." The Master had us kneel before Guanyin Bodhisattva and repent. While we were repenting, I saw many Red-Headed Army insects and other insects and ants appear, and we prayed to Guanshiyin Bodhisattva to rescue them to rebirth. That was a fault committed knowingly.

**[Addendum]** Supplementary account by Bhikshu Dharma Master Heng Xing, September 26, 2000:

When Dharma Master Heng Yi and the others were farming, they piled up a lot of rice straw and burned it to make fertilizer, intending to use the burned straw as fertilizer. Who would have thought that for a moment they couldn't keep watch over it, and the fire got completely out of control. In the end it burned several hillsides and killed many animals and insects. When the Master found out, he said, "You've created bad karma again!"

**Dharma Master Heng Yi's own account, September 26, 2000:**

I remember that once when I was small, I chased a cat away, and it accidentally fell from the second floor and died. After I took refuge I had many spiritual responses. Not long after taking refuge, my chest started to hurt badly. Later the Master saw a cat whose body was glowing, clawing at my chest. The Master asked me whether I had ever killed a cat. I said no, but I remembered chasing a cat away; maybe when I chased it away, it fell from the second floor and was killed. Afterwards the Master rescued it to rebirth.

**Dharma Master Heng Yi's own account, January 7, 2001:**

When I left the home-life, a snake came to settle accounts with me, because once when I was farming, I dug into a snake's nest and accidentally sliced a snake into three pieces, killing it. While I was sitting in meditation, the snake emitted light and circled me three times, coming to settle accounts with me. I explained to it that I had been farming at the time and had no intention of harming it, and I repented to it. Then the snake went away.

Another time, at New Year, the Master told me not to beat the ox. I said, "But the ox won't move if I don't beat it!" The Master said, "Don't beat it. In the past it was an official; now it's an ox plowing the fields. Look, sometimes it even kneels on one knee. It's already repenting, so don't beat it." So I explained to the ox that I beat it because it wouldn't work, but we had to plow the land and grow vegetables in order to have food to eat; I didn't mean to beat it. After that the ox became obedient.

**Dharma Master Heng Yi's own account, September 26, 2000:**

The Master said to me, "I don't know how long I've been looking for you."

One day I saw a very dirty monk come out from the cattle pen. I knew this was the Master.

Dharma Master Heng Yi's own account, January 7, 2001:

Why did I leave the home-life? It was while we were holding ten Buddha Recitation sessions. During the sixth session, the Master sent someone to tell me to come down the mountain to Hong Kong. I said, "Let me finish the ten Buddha Recitation sessions first, and then we'll see!" After I finished the ten sessions, while I was sitting in meditation, I saw a bird appear, and it told me to go buy gasoline to burn myself to death. I also watched it being burned to death, and from then on I planned to burn myself to seek liberation. That day I went down the mountain to see the Master, and I happened to come across a copy of the *Treatise on the Vajra Mind Dharani* with the ten demonic obstacles written by Chan Master Zuyuan. It mentioned my very kind of demonic obstacle. I knew I had an obstacle from past lives, and I got rid of it right away, and then I left the home-life. At that time I was very busy every day, and I had never read or studied any other scriptures. I don't know how I happened to come across this book that day; I think the Master was helping me. If the Master hadn't sent someone to call me down the mountain and told me that he knew my situation, I'm afraid I would already have burned myself to death.

The method of cultivation the Master taught me was to sit in meditation every day for the time of three sticks of incense. That way I could become a Buddha in ten years. But my mind wasn't settled, so I still haven't become a Buddha.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Jin Run recounted:

Because Dharma Master Heng Yi was very old and in poor health, I was sent to Hong Kong to take care of her. When the Venerable Master came to America, he entrusted her with looking after the three Way-places in Hong Kong. She usually spent most of her time at Cixing Monastery up on the mountain. Life on the mountain was very inconvenient, transportation was poor, and there were large fields on the mountain that she had to tend. She was very strong and could wield a very big hoe to till the fields. Once she carried a very big, heavy refrigerator up the mountain, and she was much faster than the rest of us. After she got up the mountain she even cooked a meal, and only after the meal was cooked did the laypeople behind her arrive. While I was looking after her, she told me many of her stories.

When I first went there, Dharma Master Liang told me that in the very early days, Dharma Master Heng Yi did seven Great Compassion Repentances a day, as well as 20,000 recitations of the Buddha's name. She was always farming, and she made use of that time, and of the time going up and down the mountain, to recite the Buddha's name. She spoke very little, especially when she was older, so I could only learn from her conduct and bearing. She was a very good model for me. Although I went there to take care of her, I can say that she took care of me more than I took care of her; she was very concerned about my health and my cultivation.

Dharma Master Heng Yi was very tall and sturdy. She was very genuine, very plain and simple, and very patient. Her vigorous cultivation and her sense of responsibility moved me, and so I respected her. She taught each of us cultivators to talk less; to do the recitation services in the Buddha Hall, and to work diligently at the Way-place; and to be honest and down-to-earth and not keep running outside, because when we go out, our minds become scattered.

I remember that Dharma Master Heng Yi's cremation was held at Elder Master Sheng Yi's Po Lam Monastery. The Dharma Master in charge of the cremation furnace said that in all the time she had been doing this work, she had never seen anyone whose fire burned so brightly and so densely. She also said that from the way the fire burned, one could tell that Dharma Master Heng Yi was a cultivator of hidden practices. Po Lam Monastery specially offered her a very thick, large piece of sandalwood, about the height of a big tree, which was placed beside the coffin and burned together with it at the cremation.

**[Addendum 2]** In the second year after Dharma Master Heng Yi left the home-life, the Venerable Master went to America to propagate the Proper Dharma. At that time only she and a few nuns lived in the Hong Kong Way-places. The Venerable Master worried that their resolve for the Way might not be firm enough. Dharma Master Heng Yi said, "Shifu, don't worry. We won't retreat!"

The Venerable Master had three Way-places in Hong Kong, and Cixing Chan Monastery in particular had many places that needed rebuilding, repair, and upkeep. Usually, as soon as she finished her daily recitations, she went out to work, reciting the Buddha's name as she worked; in addition she had to lead Dharma assemblies. The hardship involved is not hard to imagine. Even when she was advanced in years, on the birthdays of the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas she would come down the mountain to the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley to attend the Dharma assemblies. Every time she returned from Happy Valley to Cixing Monastery, she had to carry daily supplies weighing over a hundred catties on her back onto the ferry, change to a bus, walk more than ten li of mountain path, and climb over a hundred stone steps to get back to Cixing Monastery. In this way she lived at Cixing Chan Monastery for more than 40 years.

**[Addendum 3]** Dharma Master Heng Yi passed away peacefully on December 28, 2004 (the seventeenth day of the eleventh lunar month), Amitabha Buddha's birthday. She was the Venerable Master's first female monastic disciple to receive full ordination; her Dharma name was Guo Li, and her Dharma title Heng Yi. Therefore all the female monastic disciples whom the Venerable Master accepted after he came to America honored her as their eldest Dharma sister. After the Dharma Master passed away there were a great many elegiac couplets; only a few are selected here as a sample.

Elegiac couplet 1 [Tr.: These couplets weave in her names (Guo Li, "fruit of benefit"; Heng Yi, "constant benefit") and the names Ling Wui Shan and Cixing.]:

> Guo Li, of keen roots and nature:  
> Her benefit runs throughout Ling Wui Shan,  
> Her benefit gathers where spirit and grace converge;  
> All sentient beings of the ten directions look up to her.
>
> <small>果利根性  利貫靈會山  利集靈秀會聚  十方有情皆仰止</small>

> Heng Yi, ever benefiting living beings:  
> Her benefit shines clear through Cixing Monastery,  
> Her benefit adds to the ship of compassion and the flourishing of the teaching;  
> There is nowhere in the three realms the wheel does not turn.
>
> <small>恆益眾生  益澈慈興寺  益增慈航興教  三界無處不轉輪</small>

Elegiac couplet 2: With respectful condolences

> Guo Li, whose fruits benefited the altars and halls:  
> A lifetime of practice and upholding, her merit and virtue complete;
>
> <small>果利壇宇  一生行持功德滿</small>

> Heng Yi, ever benefiting living beings:  
> The Three Sages come to receive and guide her, amid the fragrance of sandalwood.
>
> <small>恆益眾生  三聖接引旃檀熏</small>

Elegiac couplet 3: In memory of our eldest Dharma sister, Dharma Master Heng Yi, who has returned to the West

> Thirty years ago,  
> A newly ordained one first visited the Buddhist Lecture Hall,  
> And was amazed to see our elder Dharma sister's unbending backbone;
>
> <small>三十年前  新戒子初訪佛教講堂  驚見釋兄硬骨</small>

> An instant later,  
> Our old fellow cultivator has indeed returned to the Land of Ultimate Bliss,  
> Forever a model for those who come after.
>
> <small>一剎那後  老同參果歸極樂世界  永為晚輩楷模</small>

## 76. Achievements of Eight Years

Western Bliss Gardens was completed at the end of 1951, and its Opening-the-Light ceremony was held on the Buddha's birthday in 1952. From then on the Venerable Master lectured on the sutras and spoke the Dharma there every day, held all kinds of Dharma assemblies, and transmitted the Three Refuges, the five precepts, and the precepts for the deceased, using skillful expedient means to draw in living beings. The Guanyin Dharma assembly of 1958...

Instructional talks by the Venerable Master during the Guanyin session at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery in 1958

Morning of the thirteenth day of the sixth lunar month, wuxu year (excerpt):

The consistent aim of Western Bliss Gardens has always been to promote the "Pure Land Dharma door" and to urge everyone to recite the Buddha's name vigorously. Therefore every year, on the nineteenth day of the sixth lunar month and the seventeenth day of the eleventh lunar month, we routinely hold recitation assemblies: a Guanyin session and an Amitabha Buddha session. But you mustn't just "attend as a matter of routine," going through the motions carelessly and leaving it at that; you should be more vigorous each year than the year before. Keep it constantly in mind: during these seven days, everywhere and at every moment, you should diligently recite the Bodhisattva's name and not be lax.

We should be mindful of the Bodhisattva, rather than making the Bodhisattva mindful of us. Why do I say we shouldn't make the Bodhisattva mindful of us? Because during this session, if you're taking part in the session in name only, with scattered thoughts swarming, and not diligently reciting Guanyin's great name, then the greatly kind and greatly compassionate Bodhisattva will surely feel sorry for you, pitiful living being that you are, and worry because you are not sincere in the session. So everyone should recite with utmost sincerity, and further, should have a heart of kindness, compassion, joy, and giving. If you can do this, I can say with certainty: "The Bodhisattva will definitely aid every one of you!"

Moreover, Western Bliss Gardens has never sent an invitation card to anyone; everyone comes to the session of their own accord. This shows that you are not lacking in sincerity, and this spirit of coming of your own accord is very praiseworthy. That being so, all the more we shouldn't let this sincerity go to waste. We should all vow to recite until "the water recedes and the rocks appear," until the Bodhisattva appears in person and speaks the Dharma; only then will we not have taken part in this session in vain.

Today is the first day of the session. On this opening day, I wish you all in advance some accomplishment this year. Otherwise, I'm going to settle accounts with you all, and if the accounts can't be squared, don't come to regret it! I won't say any more idle words; let's recite the Bodhisattva's great name a few more times!

**Afternoon of the thirteenth day of the sixth lunar month (excerpt):**

In this hot weather, you've all braved the heat and, undaunted by the high mountain and the long road, hurried here to take part in the session. What benefit is there in it, after all? The real benefit can't be put into words. It's like drinking water: only the drinker knows whether it's cold or warm. Only those with a true mind can naturally comprehend the wonder of it.

How can you obtain the benefit? It's nothing else but this: as I said just now, you need only recite the Bodhisattva's name with a true mind. A true mind, in other words, is a concentrated mind. As the saying goes, "Concentration brings efficacy; division brings obscurity." If you can be single-minded, then naturally the response and the Way will intersect. The power of this intersection of the response and the Way is inconceivable. But you have to apply the effort yourself; no one else can do it for you, and still less can you count on luck.

During these seven days we also recite the Great Compassion Mantra. The merit and virtue of the Great Compassion Mantra is inconceivable. Without considerable good roots, you couldn't easily get to hear even the three words "Great Compassion Mantra." Now you are not only able to hear its name, but also able to receive, uphold, read, and recite it, which shows that all of you have great good roots, having planted good roots in the times of many Buddhas. Since you have great good roots, you must not lightly let this life pass in vain.

I remember that when we first held sessions at Western Bliss Gardens, eight or nine out of ten laypeople couldn't recite the Great Compassion Mantra. But now, more than eight out of ten can recite it. This is a marked improvement on the part of you laypeople.

The *Great Compassion Heart Dharani Sutra* says, "Those who recite and uphold the Great Compassion Mantra can dispel all difficulties: fire will not burn them, and water will not drown them." So I urge the laypeople who can already recite it to recite it at least three times every day, and those who can't yet should hurry to learn it. The merit and virtue of reciting the Great Compassion Mantra not only drives off thieves and robbers, but also cures a hundred illnesses and quells all demonic difficulties, so we should recite and uphold it sincerely.

On this opening day of the session, the atmosphere of the Dharma assembly is very good, and everyone is taking it very seriously. I hope you will keep up your efforts and strive to be even more vigorous!

**Morning of the nineteenth day of the sixth lunar month (excerpt):**

Today is the day the Bodhisattva attained the Way. Although Western Bliss Gardens is built on a mountain, up more than 300 steps, people have turned out in great numbers to take part in the session. This shows that everyone who has come to celebrate the birthday has a true mind and is courageously vigorous.

In terms of numbers, over these eight years the number of people taking part in the session has grown year by year. What's more, many people who have come here to bow to the Buddhas say that everything has gone more smoothly for them than before. For example, ever since Upasika Yuan Guo Lin started coming to Western Bliss Gardens, business has been especially prosperous. Upasaka Tan Guo Pu, after taking refuge, has also increased his blessings and prolonged his life; he is over 80 this year, yet his health is still excellent. There was also someone with bad legs who could not walk from the age of four until nine, but after bowing to the Buddhas here was able to walk. Besides that, Upasaka Li Guo Yuan's daughter injured her leg in a fall, and neither Chinese nor Western doctors nor bonesetters could cure it, but after bowing to the Buddhas it healed too. So some people have spread the rumor that I can cure illnesses. In fact, it's nothing more than the merit and virtue of bowing to the Buddhas.

Today everyone is so happy and so enthusiastic, but such a grand gathering doesn't happen often; it won't be held again until next year. For next year's Guanyin session, please come early. Moreover, you should vow to attend the full seven days. And at the Amitabha Buddha session in the eleventh month, you should also be courageously vigorous, and you must not be lax or lazy.

**[Addendum 1]** *Nature Daily* (*Ziran Ribao*), Thursday, December 18, 1958 (the eighth day of the eleventh month of the wuxu year):

Amitabha Buddha's Birthday: Dharma Master To Lun Lectures on a Sutra

(Special report) The seventeenth day of the eleventh lunar month is the anniversary of Amitabha Buddha's birthday. To bring salvation to people everywhere, Dharma Master To Lun, a native of Jilin Province, will hold a Buddha-recitation assembly for a full week, from the eleventh to the seventeenth, at the Buddha hall of Western Bliss Gardens at the hilltop of Ma Shan Village, Shau Kei Wan, and will lecture on the Mahayana *Amitabha Sutra*. Devotees are welcome to come and hear the sutra.

**[Addendum 2]** *Wah Kiu Yat Po*, Sunday, December 21, 1958 (the eleventh day of the eleventh month of the wuxu year):

Buddhist Lecture Hall Transmitted the Three Refuges Yesterday; Western Bliss Gardens Begins Amitabha Session Today

At the request of Buddhists, Dharma Master To Lun, abbot of the Buddhist Lecture Hall on the 12th floor of Yu Yuen Mansion, Happy Valley, transmitted the Three Refuges at the Hall yesterday at 2:00 p.m. Those who took refuge included (list omitted). At Western Bliss Gardens, Ma Shan Village, Sai Wan Ho, Dharma Master To Lun, beginning yesterday evening, led elders of the various monasteries, such as Dharma Masters Fa Yun and Ru Xiu, in perfuming the altar and purifying it by sprinkling. From this morning an Amitabha session Dharma assembly is being held for seven consecutive days. With this merit and virtue, they pray for world peace and the happiness of humankind. All are welcome to rejoice and share in the benefits of the Dharma.

## 77. A Maze Hard to Escape

During his time in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master put all his heart and strength into training a group of young disciples who were only in their early teens. In their spare time after school, he taught them culture and history, sutra study and mantra recitation, Chan meditation and sitting, translation and public speaking. Whether it was...

...the wonderful principles of the sutras, moral cultivation, or the rules for disciples relying on a teacher and the small details of daily life, the Venerable Master spared no effort in guiding them with expedient means. In those years the Venerable Master exhorted them earnestly and patiently, combining strictness with kindness, in the hope of fostering a group of successors who would cultivate wholeheartedly, have penetrating wisdom, and stand tall between heaven and earth, and so of preparing a group of pillars for Buddhism. However, faced with the glitter and Westernization of Hong Kong, how many could truly awaken to the real meaning of life and not be deluded by the vain dust of the world, willing to cast off the illusions of the dust and return to the original simplicity and truth?

One disciple took refuge with the Venerable Master when she was about 14 or 15. Her good roots were deep, and she cultivated seriously, so she made amazingly rapid progress and often had extraordinary states. When she was about to get married, the Venerable Master, lamenting how hard it is to save living beings, wrote two verses on the night of August 1, 1958 (the sixteenth day of the sixth lunar month):

> Rising early and retiring late, for whom am I so busy?  
> Living beings are so hard to save—it's truly heartbreaking.
>
> <small>夙興夜寐為誰忙？眾生難度頗堪傷。</small>

> Lost in the wearisome dust, their natures turned upside down;  
> I take them by the ear and earnestly exhort them, yet find no way to transform them.
>
> <small>迷諸塵勞性顛倒，耳提諄諄化無方。</small>

> Glory in a dream—what is there to rejoice in?  
> Riches and rank before your eyes are not real either.
>
> <small>夢裡榮華何足喜，眼前富貴亦非真，</small>

> Why are the foolish always so deluded,  
> Living drunk and dying in a dream? Truly pitiful.
>
> <small>緣何癡人總迷昧，醉生夢死實堪憐。</small>

In the Venerable Master's own words:

There's someone who used to be a little layperson and now (1971) has become a big layperson. He took refuge with me when he was in elementary school, and after that he always wanted to see Shifu. If his mother didn't bring him to see me, he'd get angry at home and cry. As for him, if he hadn't taken refuge with the Triple Jewel back then, he would certainly have become a bandit when he grew up. It's because he took refuge with the Triple Jewel that he didn't become a bandit.

One of my American monastic disciples said to me, "I've been a monastic for over two years, and I haven't accomplished anything." Look at this disciple of mine who took refuge as a child. He originally wanted to leave the home-life, yet decades have gone by and he hasn't accomplished anything. Compared with him, how are you doing? Maybe he saw that there were so many Westerners in America who hadn't left home, and thought, "I'll wait for them!" So he still hasn't left home, and he let all of you leave home first!

This disciple of mine who came from Hong Kong knows that gambling isn't proper, but probably because he hasn't had enough money since he got married, he went off to Reno (America's gambling city) to gamble, hoping to win a little money. As a result, not only did he not win any money, he wore himself out, and when he got home he had to sleep for a whole day, so he couldn't come to the sutra lecture today either. He's very intelligent, but he goes and does some muddled things. He used to say that marriage was a lot of trouble, and he was going to cultivate the Way as a lifelong celibate and never marry. Now he's married, and he's run right into that maze himself. Wouldn't you say that's foolish! Knowing something is wrong and not doing it is wisdom; knowing something is wrong and doing it anyway is foolishness. Like that, it's not easy to obtain the Five Eyes. If you want the causes and conditions for obtaining the Five Eyes, you must be very clear and very conscientious in what you do; you can't be casual and sloppy.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

When we received the five precepts, the Master asked Dharma Master Sheng Huai to teach us how to kneel and bow, how to arrange our robes, and how to unfold the sitting cloth (the nishidana). Because we had all received the precepts, we all had the sash, the robe, and the sitting cloth. Our sitting cloths were the same as those used by monastics; only our sashes and robes were different. Every year when we celebrated the Master's birthday, the disciples used their sitting cloths to perform the great ceremony, fully unfolding the sitting cloth and bowing nine times. So after receiving the precepts, many of the Hong Kong disciples knew how to use the sitting cloth. We were young and loved the Buddhadharma so much that people often asked us, "Do you want to leave the home-life?" Several of us said, "We're all going to leave home someday!" I said too, "Yes, I want to!"

Once Guo Song asked the Master about her future, and the Master said she would become a very wealthy person. A few years later she married, and she really did become very wealthy. At that time I also asked about my prospects, and the Venerable Master told me that I would enjoy a life of quiet ease and wouldn't need to worry about making a living, but I wouldn't be as wealthy as Guo Song. My elder sister asked too at that time, and so did Guo Shu, but the Master didn't say any more. He only said, "Don't you have any other questions to ask?"

Guo Su was a few years older than me. She had very good roots and often entered samadhi and saw things, but unfortunately she didn't leave the home-life. Her family was very poor, and she didn't have many friends, but she and I were very close. After I went to America, I lost touch with her. When I came back to Hong Kong in the 1960s, she had already died, and I set up a memorial tablet for her to rescue her to rebirth. Her mother told me that not long after she married, she gave birth to a girl, and soon after that she passed away.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

After I came to America for university, the more than ten of us Dharma brothers and sisters who were close in age all went our separate ways: Huang Guo Jun and one of his elder sisters went to Africa, and Wen Guo Shu and Wen Guo Mi went to Australia. In the end, all of us dozen or so young disciples who used to gather around the Master in those days got married. I remember that the Master once explained the "Twelve Causes and Conditions" to me:

"Ignorance" is love and desire. From desirous thoughts, "activity" arises. From activity there is the awareness of "consciousness." From the not-yet-formed individual of "name and form," the "six entrances" of eye, ear, nose, tongue, body, and mind are produced. With the six entrances there is the chance of "contact," and with contact there is "feeling." When there are selfish thoughts of love, one must "grasp" and "possess" the other. Having emotional desire is ignorance beginning all over again, and so the cycle of birth and death is created.

Unfortunately, I let the Master's teachings go in one ear and out the other. Leaving the home-life truly takes firm faith and the determination to give up one's life for the Dharma. That is why the Master encouraged everyone to uphold the precepts strictly and avoid contact; then one can break the chain of the Twelve Causes and Conditions.

In 1961 my younger sister (Guo Shi) wrote to say that she was about to get married. I asked whether I should write to my sister, and the Master, composing on the spot, told me to write down the following verse:

> It's long since a letter came from you;  
> At the ends of the earth, my thoughts are with you.
>
> <small>久未見來函，天涯任念懸。</small>

> Weary of travel, I've returned from Australia;  
> Dharma affairs are many and complex.
>
> <small>澳洲倦游歸，法務頗紛繁。</small>

> Now, through Muzhen's letter,  
> I send you a few words.
>
> <small>今因慕貞箋，聊以寄片言。</small>

> Esteeming the worthy can replace love of beauty;  
> Following the Way can turn back heaven's course. [Tr.: The first line echoes a saying in the Analects; the second plays on the opening of the Tao Te Ching.]
>
> <small>賢賢能易色，道道可回天。</small>

> At all times, be diligent and vigorous,  
> So the Dharma's transformation pervades the billion worlds.
>
> <small>時時勤精進，法化遍大千。</small>

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Zheng recounted on February 11, 2015:

Back at Western Bliss Gardens, there was a girl with us named Zhong Meiwei (Guo Jia), who was two years younger than me. She came to America to study around 1960 and soon got married. When the Master arrived in America in 1962, her husband was stationed far away in Texas for military service, so they had no way to be in touch with the Master, which they have always regretted. They once told me, "In the 1980s, when we had to choose one of three houses in different locations, we asked the Master for advice. The Master said, 'Buy the one in Millbrae.' We followed the Master's instructions, and now the house has gone up in value more than tenfold. Everything has gone smoothly for us living here." As they spoke, the couple's gratitude was plain to see.

As for the two sons of Lin Guo Sen (Dharma Master Jin Ci), the elder son went to England. When Dharma Master Jin Ci passed away, her elder son in England came specially to the City of the Dharma Realm to see his mother off on her final journey. As for Ah Ying, I have not been in touch since I left Hong Kong in 1968. Also, two sisters went to Taiwan with their parents. The elder sister, Xu Xiangjun, is still in Taipei, and when she comes to America she sometimes chats with us for a while; the younger sister, Xu Lijun, may still be in Australia, but we're not in touch.

In the early 1980s, Huang Guo Jun and his whole family immigrated to America and settled here. In 1993, with the Venerable Master's consent, he and some laypeople in Silicon Valley, such as Hu Dawen and Wang Hongyi, founded the Hearing, Contemplating, and Cultivating Lay Buddhist Society in San Jose, which still holds various activities today.

Huang Guo Song and her whole family also immigrated, at about the same time in the early 1980s, to Calgary (Canada), and later returned to settle in Hong Kong. Before the Venerable Master entered perfect stillness, she went to Los Angeles to pay her respects to him.

In 1982 Wen Guo Mi brought his two daughters to America to see the Master, and I saw Wen Guo Shu at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas. In 1983 I saw Wen Guo Shu again in Hong Kong; after that, I don't know what became of her. Wen Guo Mi now lives in Australia. After the Venerable Master entered perfect stillness, he came to America and went to the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas to pay homage to the Venerable Master. Guo Jun and I met with him. Life is like a play; the past is too painful to look back on!

## 78. The Opening-the-Light Ceremony at the Lecture Hall

On March 1, 1959, an Opening-the-Light ceremony was held for the image of the Thousand-Handed, Thousand-Eyed Guanshiyin Bodhisattva of Great Kindness and Great Compassion enshrined in the Buddhist Lecture Hall. For three days before the Opening of the Light, the "Jeweled Repentance of the Medicine Master for Eradicating Disasters and Lengthening Life" was held, and for three days afterward the "Jeweled Great Compassion Repentance" was bowed...

In his 14 years in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master devoted himself to propagating the Buddhadharma and established three Way-places: in the winter of 1951 he founded Western Bliss Gardens Monastery; in 1954 he built Cixing Chan Monastery; and in 1956 he established the Buddhist Lecture Hall.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I built several places in Hong Kong. Western Bliss Gardens Monastery in Shau Kei Wan faced north; Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island also faced north, with its back to the south; and the Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley also faced north. Now that I've come here to America, I thought that perhaps things would shift and change direction, but turn this way and that, it just won't turn around: now this Way-place faces north again. Ah! I think that, in some unseen way, this has a little to do with Guanyin Bodhisattva, because Guanyin Bodhisattva doesn't want to face south; the Bodhisattva sits facing north. Why does Guanyin Bodhisattva sit facing north? Because the people of the world don't know to turn back! They all just keep going forward and don't know to go back. So it is said, "Why does Guanyin of the Southern Sea sit facing backward? Because the people of the world will not turn back!" The Buddha faces south and Guanyin Bodhisattva faces north precisely because the people of this world are unwilling to turn back. "The sea of suffering is boundless; turn back, and there is the shore." But they won't turn back! So Guanshiyin Bodhisattva sits facing backward, hoping that living beings will awaken soon and turn back to the shore.

**[Addendum 1]** The lay Buddhist Hong Guo Hong's oral account, given on January 12, 2001, at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall:

I am 75 this year. I took refuge with the Master at Western Bliss Gardens in 1954. We often went to the Buddhist Lecture Hall to hear the Master lecture on the sutras, and the Master also invited other Dharma Masters to come and lecture. I remember that when the Buddhist Lecture Hall held its Opening-the-Light ceremony, there was a vegetarian banquet of more than 30 tables at a vegetarian restaurant. We told the Master we were afraid there wouldn't be enough money to pay for it, and the Master said, "Don't worry!" It was truly wonderful: when it came time to settle the bill, there really was just exactly enough money to pay it.

**[Addendum 2]** *Wah Kiu Man Po*, Wednesday, February 25, 1959 (the eighteenth day of the first lunar month of the jihai year):

Buddhist Lecture Hall to Hold Opening Ceremony

Dharma Master To Lun, abbot of the Buddhist Lecture Hall on the 12th floor of Yu Yuen Mansion at the Happy Valley bus terminus, is an eminent disciple of Elder Master Hsu Yun of Nanhua Monastery in Shaoguan. Since he arrived in Hong Kong, ten years have passed in the blink of an eye. He founded Western Bliss Gardens Buddha Recitation Hall in Ma Shan Village, Sai Wan Ho, and Cixing Monastery at Man Cheung Po on Lantau Island. Since returning to Hong Kong after propagating the Dharma in Siam, with a view to expounding the great Dharma and benefiting and bringing joy to living beings, he has established this Buddhist Lecture Hall in Happy Valley. He has repeatedly invited the Elders of the various monasteries, greatly virtuous ones of the ten directions, and eminent monks of various countries passing through Hong Kong to come and expound the essentials of the Dharma, in the hope that people from all walks of life will hear much of the Buddhadharma and understand the wonderful principle of True Suchness in the teaching bequeathed by Shakyamuni, the World Honored One. At the same time, the Hall has printed many kinds of tracts on the true meaning of Buddhism; they are not for sale, and all are welcome to take copies to read. The Hall will hold its opening ceremony on March 1 (that is, Sunday, the twenty-second day of the first lunar month), and has invited Elder Dharma Master Ding Xi to preside over the Opening-the-Light of the true-body image of the Thousand-Handed, Thousand-Eyed Guanshiyin Bodhisattva. A vegetarian meal will be served all day, with guests served as they arrive. People from all walks of life are welcome to attend.

**[Addendum 3]** *Wah Kiu Yat Po*, Monday, March 2, 1959 (the twenty-third day of the first lunar month of the jihai year):

Record of the Grand Opening of the Buddhist Lecture Hall, Yu Yuen Mansion, Happy Valley

Dharma Master Ding Xi Presides over the Opening-the-Light of the Thousand-Handed, Thousand-Eyed Guanyin

More Than 1,800 Faithful Devotees, Men and Women, Attend the Grand Event

(Special Report) The Buddhist Lecture Hall on the 12th floor of Yu Yuen Mansion in Happy Valley held its opening ceremony yesterday and invited the greatly virtuous Buddhist Elder Dharma Master Ding Xi to perform the Opening-the-Light ceremony for the Hall's newly sculpted image of the Thousand-Handed, Thousand-Eyed Guanshiyin Bodhisattva of Great Kindness and Great Compassion. The abbot, Dharma Master To Lun, laid on more than 100 tables and received distinguished guests from all walks of life and Buddhists all day long. The program began at 10:00 a.m.:

(1) All assembled in the Buddha hall and bowed three times; (2) purification by sprinkling: the "Willow Branch and Pure Water" praise, "Great Compassion Guanshiyin Bodhisattva" recited three times, the "Great Compassion Mantra" recited, and the *Heart Sutra* chanted, with "Maha Prajna Paramita" recited three times; (3) the Opening of the Light; (4) speeches; (5) praise to the Buddha; (6) praise to Guanyin; (7) the verse of dedication; (8) the meal offering; (9) bowing to the Buddha, followed by the vegetarian banquet.

(2) Mirror plaques, flower baskets, and gifts were sent from all quarters, a dazzling array; the rest is omitted.

**[Addendum 4]** A great many pieces of calligraphy were presented by people from all walks of life, but most of them have been damaged. A few of these pieces of calligraphy have been selected and are recorded below (omitted).

**[Addendum 5]** This image of the Thousand-Handed, Thousand-Eyed Guanshiyin Bodhisattva at the Buddhist Lecture Hall was made by the well-known sculptor Wang Taisheng. In the 1970s this Bodhisattva image and its shrine were moved from Hong Kong to Gold Mountain Sagely Monastery in San Francisco, USA. In the winter of 1983, when DRBA's Way-place in Vancouver was established, this Bodhisattva image, the shrine, and the altar table were respectfully moved again, from San Francisco to Gold Buddha Monastery, to receive offerings there, and from then on the Bodhisattva has resided there.

## 79. Praying for a Grandson to Carry On the Family Line

After Western Bliss Gardens was established, a laywoman, Mrs. Luo, often came to bow to the Buddha. Mrs. Luo's son had been married for many years without having any children, and she was anxious to hold a grandson in her arms, so time and again she asked the Venerable Master to grant her wish. Although she deeply believed that the Venerable Master could fulfill people's wishes to their hearts' content, she did not pass the test the Venerable Master gave her...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

I'll tell you about the filthiest thing I've ever done. I'm not afraid to tell it; anyway, you're all my disciples, and if you want to laugh at me, go right ahead. What was it?

When I was in Hong Kong, there was a woman surnamed Luo, whom people called Mrs. Luo; her family was very wealthy. After she took refuge with me, she was always coming to ask me to grant a wish. She would ask one time, and I wouldn't agree; she would ask another time, and I still wouldn't agree. What wish was she asking for? She wanted a grandson, because her son had been married for many years and still had no children. She wanted a grandson. One time she came again, just when I was washing my feet. In Hong Kong it wasn't so convenient to bathe, so sometimes I would wash my feet every three or five days, or perhaps once a week. When I had finished washing my feet, she knelt in front of me again to make her request, asking me to grant it. I said, "All right! Whatever you ask for, I'll fulfill your wish. But right now, can you drink this water I washed my feet in? If you can drink my footbath water, then whatever you ask for, I can fulfill your wish." I handed her the basin of footbath water and said, "Go on, drink it!"

And this time, what do you think happened? Right there on the spot, sweat broke out on her forehead. Sweating, she just knelt there, staring wide-eyed at this footbath water. Ah! It was so filthy; how could she drink it? "Shifu, you must be joking! It's so filthy, how could I drink it?" I said, "Well, are you going to drink it or not?" Sweat was pouring off her whole body, and she said, "You can tell me to do anything else, but this I can't do."

I said, "You can't do it? Then I'll do it!" I picked up the footbasin, a basin of water this big, and somehow or other I drank all of it, without leaving a drop. As soon as I drank it, she started crying and said, "Oh! How could you do that?" I said, "Why couldn't I? For more than two years now you've been asking me for this: today asking for a grandson, tomorrow asking for a grandson again. Today I wanted to fulfill your wish, but you weren't sincere enough, so your hope of getting a grandson is gone. Don't ask me for it anymore from now on!"

When I finished speaking, she burst out sobbing, terribly regretful, and said, "What am I going to do?" I said, "There's no 'what am I going to do' about it. Whatever wishes you have, don't come asking me again. I asked you to do this one little thing and you couldn't even do it, so what's there to do? If you had drunk the water at first, you'd have gotten whatever you asked for; I'd have guaranteed that you'd get it. But now that's past, and even if you want to drink it now, there's no way to fulfill your wish." After that she would often start crying; she cried again and again, until my heart softened once more, and I said, "All right. Whether you drink it or not, I'll fulfill your wish. Go home, and before long you'll have a grandson!" Not long afterward, her daughter-in-law became pregnant, and sure enough she gave birth to a boy. At the time I didn't tell her what good would have come of drinking my footbath water; my aim was simply to fulfill her wish for a grandson. I wanted to use this method to see whether she really wanted a grandson, whether she had a bit of sincerity, and the experiment showed the result. Although she wasn't sincere, she cried so much that I had no way out, and I agreed to grant it after all.

Let me tell you: don't think I'm the same as you. The things I can do are things you can't do. Let me tell you something else: when I eat, whether it tastes good or bad, it all seems to me to be one flavor. I could even eat excrement and urine, simply because to me it's all one flavor. You say, "Ugh, this Dharma Master is too dirty and too smelly. We don't want to be near you anymore!" Amitabha! Whoever wants to stay far away from me, that's fine; whoever wants to slander me, that doesn't matter either. There's nothing good about me; I'm just the kind with no abilities. Do you understand now?

Let me say a bit more. Why can I eat things that taste so awful? Why can I drink water that is so impure? And after drinking it, I don't get sick. What's the reason? It's nothing else: it's just putting oneself aside and not feeling that one is drinking such foul, dirty water, so in the end there's no problem at all. If each of you can truly get rid of this "self," then there won't be any problems at all. If you want to give it a try, perhaps you too can attain this kind of state; if you don't want to try, that's no problem either. I absolutely won't force you; I won't force you to cultivate this method.

**[Addendum]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Mrs. Luo wanted a grandson. She went to the Buddhist Lecture Hall to see the Master, and I happened to run into her when I went there. We had never seen the Master wash his feet in the Buddha Hall of the Buddhist Lecture Hall, since the Master's room was just at the back. That day, when Mrs. Luo arrived, the Master was washing his feet in a basin of water. After the Master finished washing his feet, Mrs. Luo said to the Master:

"Shifu, please be compassionate. I want a grandson. My son still hasn't given me a grandson."

"I don't know how to give you a grandson," the Master said.

"You can do it. You have spiritual powers!"

"Do you really want a grandson?"

"I really do!"

"All right, then. If you really want a grandson, drink this basin of footbath water right now, and you'll have a grandson."

"Shifu, are you joking? How could I drink this water?"

Just then I had come into the Buddha Hall, and I too saw that the basin of water was murky and dirty, but I didn't dare say anything. The Master said:

"Didn't you say you wanted a grandson? If you really want one, drink this basin of water."

"No, no, I can't drink it!"

"If you won't drink it, I can't promise to give you a grandson." Then the Master lifted up the basin and, gulp, gulp, drank down the whole basin of water. Besides me, there were some other people there too, and we all watched the Master in amazement. When the Master had finished drinking the whole basin of dirty water, he said to Mrs. Luo, "Now your grandson is gone! Even if you ask me again, there's nothing I can do." I remember that Mrs. Luo began to cry right then. I saw this with my own eyes.

In Hong Kong, some things seemed very strange and very funny, but they really and truly happened.

## 80. Elder Hsu's Pictorial Biography

In 1959 the Venerable Master commissioned painters to depict the life and deeds of Elder Master Hsu Yun as a biography in pictures: 208 Chinese-style paintings in all. The Venerable Master wrote the text and composed verses for the events of the pictorial biography, running to tens of thousands of words. Before long, the *Pictorial Biography of Elder Master Hsu Yun* was published...

Before the pictorial biography was published, in the seventh lunar month of that year, the Venerable Master learned that Elder Master Hsu Yun was in poor health. He immediately invited greatly virtuous ones of the ten directions to commence the *Jeweled Repentance of the Medicine Master for Eradicating Disasters and Lengthening Life* for Elder Hsu, together with a Universal Buddha Ceremony for prolonging his life. Not long afterward, a letter arrived from Mount Yunju saying that Elder Hsu had recovered, and the Venerable Master was greatly relieved and gladdened!

In the Venerable Master's own words:

There was a Chinese man to whom I gave the name "Hu Nao" [Tr.: hunao means "acting foolishly, making mischief"; Hu is also a surname], because like a little child he did nothing but make mischief. He didn't understand the Buddhadharma at all. He said that Elder Hsu wasn't 120 years old, and some ignorant people followed him; foreigners who considered themselves scholars also chimed in, saying, "So-and-so said so." Actually, the hardships and difficulties of Elder Hsu's whole life are truly more than words can tell! I believe that no one else could have withstood such torment. In the course of this century, the Elder crossed over himself and crossed over others, benefited himself and benefited others, and the auspicious events of his life, sublime and miraculous and beyond what others could attain, are too many to list.

Whenever anyone bowed to Elder Hsu, even the youngest person, he would always join his palms and return the bow, and it was the same whether they were monastics or laypeople. Therefore, at no time may monastics put on airs toward monastics or laypeople. Even more must Bhikshus not put on airs toward shramaneras, shramanerikas, or Bhikshunis. The more you put on airs, the more you stink. A monastic should not be the first to make a half-bow to a layperson, but neither should a monastic stand there like a block of wood or a lump of clay, putting on airs: "I'm a monastic!" without even a word of greeting. When a layperson bows, the monastic should sincerely recite the Buddha's name and dedicate the merit to that person, wishing that he or she will soon realize the Buddha Way.

**[Addendum 1]** (omitted)

**[Addendum 2]** (omitted)

**[Addendum 3]** *Pictorial Biography of Elder Master Hsu Yun*, Volume One:

The book *Records of the Responsive Manifestations of Shakya Tathagata* has been in circulation for a long time. It is a great work within the teaching and a precious raft for the realm of living beings. Everyone delights in reading it and wishes to look at it, and it is easy to understand. It is truly an excellent text suited to its readers, a wonderful treatise in accord with principle.

Now, out of reverent admiration for the Venerable Elder Hsu Yun—for his virtue, his precepts and conduct, his learning, wisdom, ascetic practice, power of vows, actual practice, kindness and compassion, joy and relinquishment, impartiality, giving, patience, vigor, Chan samadhi, and all the doors of the paramitas—and for the extraordinary deeds of his life, which are worthy to be a compass for the Dharma-Ending Age and a savior for this century, we have spared no expense in engaging experts, Buddhists of talent, to paint over 200 exquisite Chinese-style paintings. After several years of painstaking thought and design, it has now at last been completed and published. Through it we will spread the Elder's virtue throughout the whole world, so that everyone has a copy in hand and it becomes known to every household; so that by means of it people will bring forth the Bodhi mind, by means of it accomplish the unsurpassed Way, and by means of it turn the ship of compassion back to teach and transform living beings; and so that all will attain irreversibility in the position of Bodhi enlightenment.

On the birthday of Maitreya, in the first month of the gengzi year,

respectfully prefaced by this mountain monk, To Lun, in the Avatamsaka Seat Room of the Buddhist Lecture Hall on the island of Hong Kong

**[Addendum 4]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

All the creative content, text, and verses of the pictorial biography of Elder Master Hsu Yun were written by the Master. In the pictorial biography there is one verse written to refute the criticisms of Elder Hsu made by so-called intellectuals and scholars. Among the Master's disciples, Xue Guo Feng could recite all the verses of the entire book from memory.

**[Addendum 5]** Reported in *Mind Dharma* magazine, Issue 3, December 1956:

Dharma Heir of the Venerable Elder Master Yun Vigorously Promotes the Chan School in Hong Kong

(Report from this magazine) After the Second World War, our society's president, the Venerable Master To Lun, out of reverence for the Venerable Elder Master Yun, the sole greatly virtuous master of the Chan lineage, came south from Manchuria to fulfill his long-cherished wish. Arriving at Nanhua Monastery in Maba, Guangdong, he paid his respects to the Venerable Elder Master Yun and was held in high regard by the Elder Master, who soon appointed him proctor of the Nanhua Vinaya Academy; later he was transferred to the post of dean of academic affairs. Not long ago, the Elder Master transmitted to him the mind seal, the Treasury of the True Dharma Eye, the wondrous mind of Nirvana, so that he would carry on and make flourish the Weiyang lineage and continue the line of the Chan School.

Seven years ago, the Venerable To Lun left Guangdong for Thailand, and soon afterward he came to Hong Kong, where he immediately resolved to promote and revive the Chan School. In the winter of the year before last, at Cixing Chan Monastery at Man Cheung Po on Ling Wui Shan, Lantau Island (of which the Venerable To Lun is the abbot), he specially held Chan sessions lasting 70 consecutive days, attended by a great many Buddhists of the fourfold assembly.

Embodying the aspirations of the Venerable Elder Master Yun, the Venerable To Lun vigorously promotes the Chan School. He has also had sculpted a full-body image of the Sixth Patriarch and a four-foot-high statue of the Venerable Elder Master Yun, made while the Elder Master is still living, and has enshrined them at Cixing Chan Monastery to receive offerings. Beginning on the fifteenth day of the tenth lunar month of this year, he plans to hold another ten Chan sessions.

## 81. Elder Hsu Enters Perfect Stillness

The telegram from Mount Yunju read: "The Elder entered perfect stillness peacefully at Zhenru Chan Monastery on Mount Yunju at 1:45 p.m. on the twelfth day of the ninth month of the jihai year (1959). He instructed those who come after him to 'diligently cultivate precepts, samadhi, and wisdom; extinguish greed, anger, and delusion; forget yourselves for the sake of the Dharma; and respect and love one another.'"...

On October 16 (the fifteenth day of the ninth lunar month), the Venerable Master held in his hands the telegram from Zhenru Chan Monastery on Mount Yunju in Jiangxi Province. To hear the news so suddenly was for him nothing less than as if the mountains had crumbled and the earth had quaked, as if the human world had been destroyed and the fire at the end of the kalpa were blazing through everything. He could not tell whether he was dreaming or awake, whether it was real or unreal; he was like a dull, senseless wooden puppet, like a clay image without consciousness. Only after a long while did his awareness return, and he was overwhelmed with grief!

On the day after the telegram arrived (the sixteenth of the lunar month), the Venerable Master called together his disciples and the lay Buddhists and established the "Permanent Memorial Committee for the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun." It was resolved to begin a 21-day Buddha Recitation session the following day (the seventeenth), and to hold a 120-day Great Prajna session beginning on the tenth day of the tenth lunar month.

They sent out a hundred telegrams to notify Buddhist organizations around the world, and released the news in Hong Kong, calling on Elder Hsu's disciples and Buddhists to come and take part in a memorial assembly reciting the 600 fascicles of the *Great Prajna Sutra*.

> We grieve for our supreme teacher, teacher of gods and humans,  
> Who has become a sage among sages in that Pure Land.
>
> <small>痛我天人師上師，成彼淨土聖中聖。</small>

> Our only hope is that he will pity the living, those subject to birth,  
> And come again to save and transform the sentient, mired in sentiment.
>
> <small>惟望憐憫生有生，再來度化情中情。</small>

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Of the three places I built, Lantau Island could house several hundred people, and it could also house 20 or 30; at Happy Valley, more than 20 monastics lived at one time. After Elder Hsu entered perfect stillness, I held a memorial Dharma assembly for him at the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall: a 120-day Dharma assembly reciting the *Great Prajna Sutra*, plus three Buddha Recitation sessions, for a total of more than 160 days. Every day 20 or 30 people were living there, and at mealtimes there were so many people that they couldn't all sit down in the hall; they had to stand and eat wherever they could. Every day it cost a great deal of money, but I never said to any disciple, "Help out a bit with this Dharma assembly; put up a little money!" Never! I just toughed it out! With me it's "Though freezing to death, stand facing the wind; though starving to death, walk with your belly thrust out." I would never tell anyone that I couldn't manage and beg them to come and help!

After Elder Hsu entered perfect stillness, the Buddhist Lecture Hall did more than 160 days of merit and virtue, reciting the *Great Prajna Sutra*. Nothing like this had ever been done before. From the time of Shakyamuni Buddha until now, whenever a Patriarch entered perfect stillness, no one had ever recited the *Great Prajna Sutra* for him or held such a great Dharma assembly. This is called a great offering.

The memorial assembly organized by Elder Hsu's Dharma disciples and refuge disciples in Indonesia invited me to write a message of condolence. I sent my message, and it was printed in the commemorative publication of the Indonesian memorial assembly for Elder Hsu. I heard that someone had actually spent several thousand dollars to have paper effigies of a large mansion, a steamship, and a car made for Elder Hsu. I hope that Buddhists will abolish such superstitious and backward customs, and think more about whether what I say makes sense.

**[Addendum 1]** *Sing Tao Daily*, Sunday, October 18, 1959 (the seventeenth day of the ninth lunar month of the jihai year):

Having Lived in the World 120 Years, Elder Master Hsu Yun Enters Perfect Stillness; Buddhists Held a Dharma Assembly Yesterday

Elder Master Hsu Yun—who sat through the reigns of five emperors and four eras, scarcely noticing how many times the seas turned into mulberry fields; who endured to the full nine ordeals and ten difficulties; and who thoroughly understood the impermanence of worldly affairs—passed away peacefully, entering perfect stillness at the venerable age of 120, on the twelfth day of this lunar month at Zhenru Chan Monastery on Mount Yunju in Yongxiu County, Jiangxi Province.

Yesterday, at the Buddhist Lecture Hall on the 12th floor of Yu Yuen Mansion in Happy Valley, Dharma Master To Lun, Dharma heir of the Venerable Hsu, on learning that the sad news had arrived, immediately called together the Hall's refuge disciples and other Buddhists, and beginning yesterday evening they first held, at the Buddhist Lecture Hall, a Buddha recitation Dharma assembly of three consecutive Buddha Recitation sessions. After that Dharma assembly is completed, it will be followed on the tenth day of the tenth lunar month, the fourth seven-day period after the Venerable Hsu's entry into perfect stillness, by a "Prajna session" lasting 120 consecutive days.

The telegram that the Buddhist Lecture Hall received yesterday from Mount Yunju read as follows: "To the Dharma seat of the Venerable To Lun: Elder Hsu Yun passed away peacefully, entering perfect stillness, at Zhenru Chan Monastery on Mount Yunju on the twelfth of this month by the old calendar. He instructed those who come after him to strive vigorously, diligently cultivate precepts, samadhi, and wisdom, extinguish greed, anger, and delusion, forget themselves for the sake of the Dharma, and respect and love one another. Respectfully sent on the thirteenth by the old calendar by your precept disciple Kuan Chang."

Those who took part yesterday evening in reciting the Buddha's name and dedicating the merit to the Venerable Hsu numbered more than 100, including such Elders of the various monasteries as Dharma Masters Bao Xian, Ren Guang, Rong Xiu, and Fa Yun, as well as Li Kuan Zhong (the rest omitted). Buddhists are welcome to come, rejoice, and offer incense.

In addition, the lay Buddhist Yang Kuan Zhuang of the Daoci Buddhist Society in Sai Wan, having received the news of Elder Master Hsu Yun's entry into perfect stillness at the same time, sent a circular to all the members of that society to hold a Buddha Recitation session beginning at 10:00 a.m. today, dedicating its merit to the Venerable Hsu. All Buddhists are welcome to take part in the recitation, so it was announced.
