Daily schedule of the Great Prajna Dharma assembly: (omitted)

**[Addendum 3]** Bhikshu Dharma Master Zhi Ding wrote in "In Memory of the Venerable Elder Yun":

(Excerpted from the *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun*, published by the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall in the winter of 1959)

On the afternoon of October 17, I had gone to Halona Blow Hole on some business. When I came back, I received a telegram from Dharma Master To Lun in Hong Kong, saying that the Elder had already entered perfect stillness on the 13th (the twelfth day of the ninth lunar month)... It was truly like a bolt from the blue, and it shocked me exceedingly! Had the Elder really left us, this boundless multitude of suffering beings, and gone to another world of peace and nurture? A string of question marks appeared in my mind; I could hardly bring myself to believe it. The next day I received another telegram, from Dr. Liang—a telegram that amounted to an obituary notice—so the fact was a fact after all, and I could not but believe it. When I received this news, my heart was filled only with desolation, only with grief! I had been away from the Elder for ten years. When I left him ten years ago, I could never in any way have imagined that ten years later, today, he would leave us forever!

**[Addendum 4]** Zhan Liwu wrote in "Mourning Elder Hsu Yun":

That day I was informed by a newspaper office of the news of the Elder's entry into perfect stillness, and in the evening I also received a telegram from Dharma Master To Lun in Hong Kong.

Hong Kong Buddhists Unite to Mourn Elder Master Hsu Yun; Memorial to Be Held This Sunday at Tung Lin Kok Yuen

Yesterday, when the sad news arrived that the great Buddhist figure Elder Master Hsu Yun had manifested stillness and returned to the West, the Buddhist fourfold assembly of Hong Kong, monastic and lay, immediately called together Buddhists from all over Hong Kong, Kowloon, and the New Territories, and at 2:00 p.m. yesterday, in borrowed premises at the Ching Kok Lin Association in Happy Valley, they convened a Dharma assembly to remember Master Hsu Yun. A great many Elders of the various monasteries and laymen and laywomen attended. Dharma Master You Tan was elected on the spot to chair the meeting, and Upasaka Chen Jingtao took the minutes. **[Addendum 6]** Elder Dharma Master Shao Yun, "Glimpses of Elder Master Hsu Yun's Life on Mount Yunju":

After 1958, the Elder Master said to us, "I'm going to leave." We were very sad and asked him, "Venerable Elder, why do you want to leave now?" He said, "You don't know. There will be ten years of suffering ahead, and it will be terribly hard to bear!" At the time we didn't understand. Later came the ten years of catastrophe of the Cultural Revolution.

Starting in 1958, the Elder often had minor illnesses, so he handed over his affairs one by one to those who would come after him, and he distributed all his belongings to the assembly.

On the afternoon of the tenth day of the ninth (lunar) month of 1959, the Elder Master gave the assembly his final instructional talk and his last testament. The Elder Master said, "My last words are just these: 'Diligently cultivate precepts, samadhi, and wisdom; extinguish greed, anger, and delusion.'" After a while he said further, "With proper mindfulness and a proper mind, cultivate a spirit of great fearlessness, and save people and save the world." The Elder admonished us to transmit the precepts and cultivate well.

At twelve noon on the twelfth day of the ninth (lunar) month, the Elder Master said to his attendant, "Just now, in my sleep, I dreamed that I saw an ox step on and break the stone slab of the Foyin Bridge, and I also saw that the water of the Bixi stream had stopped flowing." Then he closed his eyes and said nothing. At 12:30 the Elder Master called his attendants to come in together and said to them, "You have served me for many years, and you have all worked hard. I won't say much about all these matters. For nearly ten years now I have endured every bitterness and hardship, living every day amid danger, suspicion, and upheaval, suffering every slander and injustice, and I have been willing to bear it all, wishing only to preserve the Way-places of the Buddhas and Patriarchs within this country, to uphold the virtuous pure rules of the Patriarchs for the monasteries, and to preserve this one great robe for monastics in general [Tr.: the formal robe (sanghati), standing for the monastic tradition itself]. I fought with my life to win back this one great robe. You are all disciples who have entered my room; you know what happened. From now on, whether you have a bundle of thatch to cover your heads or go out in all directions in response to conditions to teach, you must likewise firmly uphold and preserve this one great robe. But how can it be preserved forever? There is only one word for it: precepts." When the Elder Master had finished speaking, he joined his palms and bade everyone take good care. Everyone withdrew in tears and kept watch under the eaves outside the room.

At 1:45 p.m., in his thatched hut on Mount Yunju, the Elder lay on his right side in the auspicious reclining posture and peacefully entered perfect stillness. During the month and more before he entered perfect stillness, many of the monks had seen a patch of light come out of the thatched hut and go toward the Buddha Hall. They saw only a bright ring of light and no figure of any kind; once it entered the Buddha Hall, the ring of light gradually faded away. After a month and more, the Elder Master had arranged everything properly and written a will in his own hand; then he told his two attendants to leave, and he stayed behind in the thatched hut and quietly departed! (This piece is excerpted from *Silicon Valley Brahma Sound*, Gold Sage Monastery, December 2011.)

**[Addendum 7]** Elder Hsu's final verse at the end of his life:

> Of old, pitying the lives of ants, one did not cast [remains] into the water;  
> I, to comfort the water creatures, cast my body into the river.
>
> <small>蝦恤蟻命不投水  吾慰水族身擲江</small>

> I pray that all who receive my body, gladly offered,  
> Will together realize Bodhi and cross over living beings.
>
> <small>祈諸受我身願供   同證菩提度眾生</small>

> All you Dharma companions,  
> There is no need to worry.  
> Birth and death follow karma,  
> Like insects bound in a net.
>
> <small>諸各法侶  不必憂慮  生死循業  如蟲縛網</small>

> Greed and delusion never cease,  
> Imprisoned in sorrow and joy.  
> If you wish to be rid of this affliction,  
> Strive to cultivate and refine yourselves.
>
> <small>貪迷不休囚閒憂喜欲除此患努力修煉</small>

> Wondrously unite with the unproduced,  
> Clearly penetrate the ground of the nature,  
> Sever the emotions of love and hate,  
> And escape the perils of the turning wheel.
>
> <small>妙契無生明通性地斷愛憎情脫輪回險</small>

> Investigate the three studies and keep them pure,  
> Firmly uphold the four applications of mindfulness,  
> Vow to bring them to perfect completion:  
> This substance is illusion, dew, and lightning.
>
> <small>參淨三學堅持四念誓願圓成質幻露電</small>

> Awaken to and realize true emptiness:  
> The myriad dharmas are of one substance.  
> Meetings and partings, sorrows and joys,  
> Follow conditions, like bubbles on water.
>
> <small>證悟真空萬法一體離合悲歡隨緣泡水</small>

After my death, when my body has been cremated, I ask all of you to grind my ashes into a fine powder, mix them well with oil, sugar, and flour, make them into pellets, and please place them in the river as an offering to the water creatures. If you fulfill my wish, I will be endlessly grateful! Bowing in reverence, Hsu Yun, a debtor who has not repaid

**[Addendum 8]** Dharma Master Le Guan wrote in his article "Impressions of Master Hsu Yun":

As I see Elder Yun, he truly was a man of iron of the Chan school in this latter age, with the eye on the crown of his head, and in his final move his spiritual light shone forth all the more uniquely. After coming to Hong Kong to propagate the Dharma, he resolutely hurried back to the mainland in the spirit of one leaping into a pit of fire. When he went back, he cherished a compassionate vow to save the teaching and save the Sangha; yet, in order to carry through his great vow of compassion, he took no account of success or failure. What a state of mind that was!

With this spirit he struggled on the mainland and fought his way through. One could say that he gave up everything, even his life's blood; he endured every torment and went through every hardship and danger. His conduct in sacrificing himself to defend the teaching can stand alongside and shine together with that of several greatly virtuous ones who came before him: Tanshi in the time of Emperor Wu of Wei, Zhixuan in the time of Emperor Wu of Zhou, and Xuanchang in the time of Emperor Wu of Tang. And his final outcome closely resembles the circumstances of Great Master Jing'ai in the time of Emperor Wu of Zhou, who set forth to the emperor the retribution that destroying Buddhism must bring but could not change the emperor's wicked mind; he went to Mount Zhongnan and wept and wailed for seven days, then sat on a great rock, took a knife and cut away his own flesh, hung his intestines on a pine branch, and died holding his heart in his hands. That Buddhism has until now been able to preserve some seeds of the Buddhadharma and has not suffered complete destruction is due largely to the power of Elder Yun's protection and support. This is the price that Elder Yun's sacrifice obtained, and it is something that deserves to be written in large letters in the future history of Chinese Buddhism.

## 82. Respectfully Bringing Back the Sharira

On November 4 (the fourth day of the tenth lunar month), the Venerable Master sent two disciples, Xue Guo Su and Ma Guo Xian, to Mount Yunju to respectfully bring back Elder Master Hsu Yun's sharira. On the 18th they arrived back in Hong Kong, having respectfully obtained more than ten sharira of dazzling five colors, which they brought back to be enshrined for offerings...

The Venerable Master wrote a verse in praise of the sharira:

> Firm seeds of dazzling five colors,  
> Smelted from ten thousand virtues, round as the full moon;
>
> <small>五色缤紛堅固子   萬德煅煉滿月圓</small>

> Crystal-clear pearls of the precepts, their light blazing;  
> Tridacna jewels of samadhi, their colors bright and fresh;
>
> <small>晶瑩戒珠光灼灼   砗磲定寶色鮮鮮</small>

> Lapis lamps of wisdom illumine the Dharma Realm;  
> Fruits of prajna wisdom transform the three thousand worlds;
>
> <small>琉璃慧燈照法界    般若智果化三千</small>

> The size of lotus seeds, pure as jade,  
> Adorned all around with the merit and virtue of the precepts and the Vinaya.
>
> <small>蓮子大小潔如玉    戒律功德眾莊嚴</small>

—Respectfully inscribed by Hsuan Hua, his disciple and Dharma heir

"Sharira" are the bone relics that remain after a body is cremated; they are the crystallization of a cultivator's practice of the Way. The "Chapter on Giving Up the Body" of the *Golden Light Sutra* says, "Sharira come from being permeated by the cultivation of precepts, samadhi, and wisdom; they are extremely difficult to obtain." Therefore, to bow to and make offerings to sharira is to make offerings to the Triple Jewel.

Today Elder Hsu's sharira are enshrined at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas in the United States, and the *Pictorial Biography of Elder Master Hsu Yun* has also been published in Chinese in the United States.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

When I completed this Dharma assembly, I ran into a lot of jealousy and obstruction from people in Buddhist circles, who were angry that I had outshone them all. I sent people to bring Elder Hsu's sharira back to Hong Kong. At that time there was a certain person whose "crab ailment" I had cured; afterward she became jealous and slandered me terribly, even spreading the rumor that my sharira were fake and only hers were real. Why did she say this? She was afraid that ordinary people would believe in me, and if they believed in me, it would be bad for her.

**[Addendum 1]** In the winter of 1959, the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall published the *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun*:

Impressions on Reverently Beholding the Sharira Buddha-Jewels of Elder Master Hsu Yun

On the twenty-sixth day of the tenth month by the old calendar, I reverently went to the Buddhist Lecture Hall to pay homage to the sharira produced by the cremation of the Venerable Elder Master Yun, guided in my homage by Dharma Master To Lun, the Venerable Yun's direct Dharma heir. According to Master To, he had sent two disciples who braved ten thousand dangers to bring the sharira respectfully from Mount Yunju in Jiangxi. I observed that the sharira, large and small, numbered about ten in all. The one in the center, white in color, was the largest; crystal-clear and dazzling, it was like a pearl that shines in the night. Those arrayed around it were also of dazzling five colors, resplendent beyond compare. Now, such night-shining pearls are none other than mani pearls, the Buddha-jewels of seeing the nature and becoming a Buddha. Looking into the matter, the Venerable Yun had very great affinities with the people of Hong Kong. Besides coming here in person several times to propagate the Dharma and cross over living beings, he was, for example, invited to preside at the altar when the Tung Wah Group of Hospitals held an Assembly of Myriad Affinities some years ago. In recent years, when the assembly was held again, they again invited him to preside over the Dharma from within samadhi. Because of this, calamities and invasions quietly dissolved, and the whole territory benefited and relied on him. Now that the Master has entered perfect stillness, his sharira too were the first to be respectfully brought here, ahead of all other ports; this too is a matter of affinity. Master To now plans to build a stupa to enshrine them for offerings, and preparations are under way. That the people of Hong Kong will in future gain blessings and lasting protection is a certainty. Master To's resolve to repay his teacher's kindness is great indeed. Zhuangzi said, "It is as though something causes it." This is probably the guidance and transformation of the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas.

Respectfully recorded in Hong Kong by the lay Buddhist Shi Xing in the first month of winter of the jihai year

**[Addendum 1]** Instructional talk by Elder Dharma Master Shao Yun (excerpted from *Silicon Valley Brahma Sound*, December 2011):

The Elder Master entered perfect stillness on the twelfth day of the ninth month. On the nineteenth day of the ninth month his casket was sealed, and the cremation took place the next day; it was planned to open the furnace three days later to collect the sharira and the ashes. Unexpectedly, the next day a fire broke out on the hills outside Zhaozhou Pass. Nearly a hundred people lived on the mountain, and all the monks of the assembly went to fight the fire, leaving only some of the old, weak, sick, and disabled in the monastery. Among them, Master Kuan Huai, Master Kuan Ke, and some others ran to the outside of the opening of the cremation furnace and peeked in. They saw that the Elder Master's ashes after cremation were still sitting upright without collapsing, seated in full lotus there just like a living person. They thought this strange and casually picked up a small piece of tile and tossed it at the ashes, whereupon the ashes collapsed entirely. Master Kuan Huai immediately reached in and grabbed a handful of ashes; looking at them, he saw several crystal-clear, shining sharira, and at once he cried out about it.

After a while, the people who had gone to fight the fire came back one after another. Hearing that there were sharira in the Elder Master's ashes, they came running one after another, scrambling to reach in and grab a handful of ashes and then run off to some secluded spot in the mountains, because the political situation at the time did not allow anyone to say there were sharira. Dozens of people did this. Some found several sharira in a single handful of ashes; those with fewer still had one or two. They varied in size and differed in color, most of them being white and crystal-clear. So there was simply no way to count how many sharira there were in the Elder Master's ashes.

At the time, many people saw the Elder Master sitting upright, cross-legged, inside the sharira; in one of them even his long eyebrows could be seen clearly. That sharira is still kept in the sharira stupa today.

**[Addendum 2]** Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng He recounted:

Viewing Elder Master Hsu Yun's sharira in Hong Kong was the cause and condition for my coming to know the Master. In 1959 Elder Hsu entered perfect stillness, and the newspapers announced that the Buddhist Lecture Hall had Elder Hsu's sharira on view for people to pay their respects.

Driven by curiosity, I too went to see what it was all about. I looked and looked but didn't see anything special, so I said to the Master's lay disciples, "I've seen enough; I'm going home!" They told me to come and bow to the Buddha when I had time, saying that whatever you ask for, you get. I put them off, saying, "All right, I'll come again when I have time." Not long after, I dreamed one night that the Master asked me, "Why did you promise to come and bow to the Buddha and then not come?" I thought it strange: why did this monk want me to go and bow to the Buddha? So I decided to go to the Buddhist Lecture Hall again to see what was going on.

When I got to the Buddhist Lecture Hall and rang the doorbell, someone opened the door. The person who answered took one look at me and said, "Shifu has been looking for you. Shifu even asked where you live, but everyone said they didn't know." At that time the *Great Prajna Sutra* Dharma assembly was going on. Wen Guo Shu told me to take refuge with the Master. I thought taking refuge meant they wanted me to leave the home-life, so I said I was only 24, still young, and didn't want to leave home. Guo Shu explained that taking refuge was not leaving the home-life; it was believing in the Buddha and becoming a disciple of the Buddha. I said, "Believing in the Buddha is fine, but leaving home won't do. When you leave home you shave your head bald, and that's not pretty!" (Editor's note: Bhikshuni Heng He, lay name Zeng Xiujuan, took refuge with the Venerable Master in 1960 and left the home-life on August 3, 1977, with the Dharma name Guo Xi and the Dharma title Heng He. She received full ordination in 1979 and passed away on September 29, 2000. Her cremation yielded sharira and sharira flowers.)

## 83. Remembering the Venerable Yun (1)

In order to transmit Elder Hsu's immortal merit and virtue to the world and offer it to later students, the Venerable Master organized a "Call for Writings on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun" in countries throughout the world, and the memorial writings he received from well-known figures everywhere were collected, edited, and printed...

The *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun* was published and circulated in December 1959. The following is an essay written by the Venerable Master, excerpted from this special issue:

Remembering the Venerable Yun: Such Were the Causes and Conditions, Before and After

Shi To Lun

(The preceding part is omitted.) I reached Nanhua Monastery and paid my respects to the Venerable Yun, like an infant seeing its mother, like a wanderer returning home. My reverent longing of several years, my long-cherished wish, was at last fulfilled! When I first arrived, I served as the incense-and-lamp attendant of the Patriarchs' Hall. Dharma Master Zhi Can often came to visit me; we were in accord in our principles of the Way, and he recommended me to the Venerable Yun, saying I was a person of talent and a vessel of the Dharma. The Venerable Yun at once invited me to the abbot's quarters and told me to join the Vinaya Academy as proctor. I did not agree. He pressed me again and again. I said, "I, your student, have traveled ten thousand li to visit teachers in order to end birth and death and to draw near to a Good and Wise Advisor. If you, Elder, can guarantee that I will end birth and death, then even if I have to go through boiling water and fire and be ground to dust, I will not decline."

The Venerable Yun said, "You must end your own birth and death yourself, just as you fill yourself by eating your own food. If I said I could guarantee that you would end birth and death, I would be deceiving you, and I would never do that. Even so, in cultivation one should value inner skill and outer results, cultivating both blessings and wisdom; only then can one succeed. You must not be content to be one who looks after only himself and perfects only his own person. You should also benefit the whole world, practice the Bodhisattva Path, support and protect the monastery, and serve the assembly. Then you will be cultivating both blessings and wisdom, and birth and death will end of themselves." I still did not agree. The Venerable Yun said, "You came all the way from the Three Northeastern Provinces, thinking nothing of the ten thousand li, to draw near to me. If you won't follow my direction, how is that drawing near?" At this, I agreed to take up the post.

Ordinarily I would quietly observe the Venerable Yun's words, deeds, and movements. They were no different from anyone else's, except that he led by his own example and was austere with himself to a degree that ordinary people could not hope to reach. (The rest is omitted.)

Not long after I went to Yunmen, I developed a damp ailment that was hard to bear, so I asked the Elder for leave to go to Guangzhou for treatment. The Venerable Yun would not permit it, saying, "Don't go! If you go, it will be hard to come back!" I said, "Not so. My mind is made up; I must go." When the Elder heard this, tears of sorrow fell, and he clasped my hand and said, "If you go, we shall not meet again!" I said, "As soon as I recover I will come back. Please, Elder, set your mind at ease and don't worry!" The Venerable Yun said, "On this journey you should do Old Shakya proud and build Way-places for the Patriarchs of all generations. Your future is boundlessly bright. Work hard! Work hard! Do your best, and don't let down my hopes." With words of "Take good care," I bowed and bade him farewell.

I reached Guangzhou and went on to Hong Kong, where I stayed as a visiting monk at Tung Po Tor Monastery. In the seventh month I went back to Guangzhou again and stayed at Liurong Monastery, where Dharma Master Ming Guan asked me to serve as hall master and also as deputy treasurer. I accepted for the time being, intending to go back to Yunmen after the Mid-Autumn Festival. Who would have thought that in the first ten days of the eighth month Shaoguan would change hands and all transportation would be cut off? Having no choice, on the evening of the eighteenth day of the eighth month, with the boat fare provided by two lay Buddhists, Xie Kuan Hui and Chen Kuan Man, I returned to Hong Kong (omitted). Every time I recall the Dharma words the Elder gave me when I left, I regret that I did not heed my Good and Wise Advisor's admonitions then. Now I wish to draw near to the Venerable Yun again, but it can no longer be. How painful! How painful! What more is there to say? (The rest is omitted.)

In the seventh month of the jihai year, I heard that the Venerable Yun was gravely ill. I was uneasy day and night, knowing this was an inauspicious sign. Earlier, in the wuxu year, the Venerable Yun had had a Dharma photograph taken in which his two eyes looked straight ahead and his eyebrows, several inches long, could reach to his ears. When I saw it, I bowed to it, and I said with feeling, "In all his photographs the Venerable Yun has always had his eyes closed, but now he is gazing upon living beings with compassionate eyes. Such a departure from the usual means there must be a great change; within a year it will become clear." So I invited greatly virtuous masters and monks of the ten directions to bow the *Jeweled Repentance of the Medicine Master for Eradicating Disasters and Lengthening Life* and to hold a Universal Buddha Ceremony for prolonging life for several days. I also published the news in the newspapers to notify the Elder's disciples, in the hope that, pooling everyone's ideas and efforts and uniting our wills like a fortress, our sincerity would bring a response and no prayer would go unanswered. Then and there I instructed the assembly, saying, "This Universal Buddha Ceremony for prolonging the Elder's life, the bowing of the *Medicine Master Repentance*, and the other Buddhist services this time are probably the last; there will be no more chances." My voice was unusually heavy and sorrowful, and all who heard it were also choked with sobs. Soon afterward a letter came from Yunju saying that the Venerable Yun's illness had eased slightly, which was a great relief.

I then devoted myself wholeheartedly to having people design and paint the Elder's pictorial biography: over 200 exquisite Chinese-style paintings, with text running to tens of thousands of words, setting forth the Elder's lifelong superhuman virtue and his unique spirit of austerity, diligence, and toil in practice and vows, to stand as an everlasting model, to enlighten later worthies, and to provide a pattern for all ages to come. Then, unfortunately, the sad news came: on October 16 (that is, the fifteenth day of the ninth lunar month), a special telegram arrived, announcing: "The Elder passed away peacefully, entering perfect stillness, at 1:45 p.m. on the twelfth of this month at Zhenru Chan Monastery on Mount Yunju. He instructed those who come after him to diligently cultivate precepts, samadhi, and wisdom, extinguish greed, anger, and delusion, forget themselves for the sake of the Dharma, and respect and love one another," and so on.

To hear the news so suddenly was for me nothing less than as if the mountains had crumbled and the earth had quaked, as if the human world had been destroyed and the fire at the end of the kalpa were blazing through everything. I could not tell whether I was dreaming or awake, whether it was real or unreal; I was like a dull, senseless wooden puppet, like a clay image without consciousness. Only after a long while did my awareness return, and I was overwhelmed with grief! The next day, on the morning of the 17th, I called together the patrons of this Hall to discuss the memorial arrangements. It was decided then and there to hold a 21-day Buddha Recitation session, followed by a 120-day Great Prajna session, to show our remembrance and to repay the kindness of his milk of the Dharma. We immediately sent telegrams to our Dharma companions and fellow cultivators overseas: to the San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall, the Honolulu Buddhist Association, the Singapore Buddhist Association, Upasaka Lee Choon Seng, Upasika Bi Junhui, and others; to Upasaka Zhan Liwu in Canada; to the countries of Southeast Asia, Taiwan, Burma, Thailand, India, Ceylon, and other places; and to Upasaka Yu Zhendong, chairman of the World Fellowship of Buddhists, and others. More than a hundred telegrams were sent in all, and so everywhere people rose up in great numbers, like winds rising and clouds surging, in response, to remember the Elder. Then we notified the Buddhists of Hong Kong by telephone, and on the 18th (that is, the seventeenth day of the ninth lunar month) we released the news publicly in the Hong Kong newspapers to inform everyone. Who would have expected that this would invite jealousy? Petty people slandered, demons ran rampant, confusing what people saw and heard, and some who styled themselves Good and Wise Advisors were glad to tag along behind and chime in. It is truly lamentable! How true it is that good deeds attract many demons.

In short, if anyone understands me, will it not be because of the Venerable Yun? If anyone condemns me, will it not also be solely because of the Venerable Yun? When Confucius wrote the *Spring and Autumn Annals*, rebellious ministers and treacherous sons were afraid; when Sima compiled the *Records of the Grand Historian*, evildoers and lawbreakers perished. I am exerting myself for the Venerable Yun's sake, and even if ten thousand spears pierced my body, I would have no fear. Please use all your tricks to the full; I welcome and accept them most heartily. Great Master Yongjia said: "Contemplate evil words as merit and virtue; then they become my Good and Wise Advisors. If slander did not give rise to enmity or fondness, how could the power of patience and kindness born of non-production be shown?" He also said: "Let them slander, let them condemn; those who hold up a torch to burn the sky only tire themselves out. When I hear it, it is just like drinking sweet dew; it melts away, and I suddenly enter the inconceivable." Yet you should know that cause and effect are inconceivable, and retribution too is inconceivable. Be careful! Be careful! If you fall into the suffering of the tongue-pulling hell, it will be too late for regret!

On the fourth day of the tenth lunar month, I sent two laywomen, Xue Guo Feng and Ma Guo Xian, to Yunju to respectfully bring back the sharira and bone relics for offerings. On the seventh day they reached Zhenru Chan Monastery and obtained more than ten sharira, radiant with unusual brilliance, of dazzling five colors. On the sixteenth they set out from the monastery to return, and on the afternoon of the eighteenth they arrived at this Hall. I led the assembly of both ranks in welcoming them with incense and flowers; everyone bowed and prostrated, all greatly delighted, and I too felt as if a heavy burden had been lifted, relaxed and happy.

The next day, together with four lay Buddhists—Mao Wenda, Li Zhongyou, Xue, and Ma—I took the sharira to visit the elderly Upasaka Cen to discuss the special issue. He advocated publishing it at a later date, so as to wait for documents from overseas. That is why this publication is only now appearing (Editor's note: the *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun* produced by the Venerable Master Hua during his time in Hong Kong). From now on, I still hope that Buddhists of all countries of the world will be united in spirit and respect and love one another.

## 84. Remembering the Venerable Yun (2)

> Born in Fuzhou, near Mount Peng,  
> He received full ordination at Yongquan, with Miaolian conferring the precepts.
>
> <small>生於福州近彭山，受具湧泉戒妙蓮。</small>

> He paid homage at Putuo to Guanzizai,  
> Then bowed his way north to Wutai and Manjushri.
>
> <small>朝禮普陀觀自在，北拜五台文殊來。</small>

> Not dwelling on at Yunqi, he came to Nanhua Monastery;  
> Then Dajue at Yunmen rose up once again.
>
> <small>雲棲不棲南華寺，大覺雲門復又出。</small>

> At Yunju he peacefully manifested perfect stillness;  
> The Venerable Yun has ascended the summit of Nirvana.
>
> <small>雲居安詳示圓寂，雲公高登涅槃巅。</small>

—Respectfully inscribed by Hsuan Hua, his disciple and Dharma heir, 1959

Foreword to the *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun*

The Venerable Elder Yun, a greatly virtuous one of his generation and the leader of the Chan School, entered Nirvana on the twelfth day of the ninth month (lunar) of this year, and Buddhists throughout the world are overwhelmed with grief. As the saying goes, "The noble person is distressed lest his name not be honored after he has passed from the world." In creating this publication now, we are not only proclaiming the Elder's name and virtue but truly expressing the sorrow and regret of later students. Moreover, the Elder was a Bodhisattva who responded to the world and came again to this land to save living beings. Therefore we should all the more engrave his virtue in metal and stone and spread his holy name in our words; that is why we have specially created this publication.

Right after the Elder manifested stillness, the Permanent Memorial Committee for the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun was established, and a call for writings on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun was sent out to countries throughout the world. Well-known figures everywhere, such as Elder Tan Xu, Upasaka Yu Youren, Upasaka Yan Xishan, Upasaka Li Hanhun, Upasaka Zhan Liwu, Upasaka Yu Ta-wei, Upasaka Hang Li-wu, Upasaka Qu Yingguang, and others, as well as greatly virtuous ones of the various monasteries, kindly sent their fine writings and calligraphy, which have been compiled and printed in this special issue to commemorate the Elder's meritorious deeds and great accomplishments.

Elder Hsu Yun was renowned in China and abroad; his followers fill the world, and his disciples are beyond counting. As disciples, if we do not repay our teacher's kindness, how can we be at peace with ourselves? Now that the Elder has entered perfect stillness, if there were no writings to commemorate him and to transmit his immortal merit and virtue to the world for later generations to believe in, how could we repay the Elder's profound kindness?

He transmitted the Buddha's mind seal and continued the wisdom-lamp of the Patriarchs. The Elder's great spirit, in reviving the Buddhadharma and crossing over living beings, was enough to influence and transform both sentient and insentient beings; he was a model for students and an exemplary teacher for humans and gods. Now that he has entered Nirvana, later students should behold his radiance and take him as their pattern. Therefore this book has been compiled and printed, so that later generations will have something to choose from.

The Elder's great age of 120 years bears witness to his lifelong strict and exacting observance of the precepts and the Vinaya and to his extraordinary achievements; his name will be handed down for ten thousand generations. Therefore we have now specially created and printed this special issue as a memorial, so that ten thousand generations hence people will have a model to revere, to emulate, and to learn from.

**[Addendum]** After Elder Master Hsu Yun entered perfect stillness in 1959, the Venerable Master served as chairman of the editorial committee of the *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun* and sent out calls for writings everywhere. A great many letters came in; a few are selected below: (omitted)

Editor's Afterword to the *Special Commemorative Issue on the Nirvana of Elder Master Hsu Yun*

By the editor, a mendicant monk (Editor's note: that is, the Venerable Master Hua)

Tracing back the Venerable Yun's virtue: the more one looks up to it, the higher it is; the more one bores into it, the harder it becomes; one sees it ahead, and suddenly it is behind. How could ordinary people know even its outlines? Even a sage could not fathom its bounds. How much less would a mendicant of little study and scant learning presume to take on so great a responsibility as editing this special issue? But since I have the undeserved honor of being counted among his disciples and cherish the memory of my teacher's virtue, duty would not allow me to decline. Fortunately, greatly virtuous ones and venerable elders of all quarters, Dharma-protecting patrons, faithful devotees at home and abroad, eminent men of letters, scholars of broad and thorough learning, and great Confucian scholars and calligraphers enthusiastically took part in this undertaking, responding to the call and kindly contributing fine works, for which we are grateful beyond measure. However, since this publication was launched to remember the Venerable Yun, we regret that articles concerning politics, or writings leaning to either the left or the right, could not be included; pieces that seize upon the topic to air other views, or idle arguments, have likewise not been used; and old manuscripts about past events that have already been published elsewhere are also not printed. We hope for your understanding. Owing to various causes and conditions, this publication appears only now, to satisfy the eager hopes of the readers who have been awaiting it. The contents of this publication are gold and jade, pearls and gems, all smelted together in one furnace: essays, poems, eulogies, and verses, in which the myriad dharmas are fully displayed, offered in remembrance of the Venerable Yun and in reverent expression of our longing for him. The editor is deeply ashamed of his meager talent: making trouble where there was none, searching for a head when he already has one, wishing to help but doing harm instead, and incurring blame at every turn. My only wish is that from now on I may break off social ties and stop traveling about, close my door and decline visitors, rest my spirit in the bliss of Chan, and become a "living dead monk," so as to repay the kindness of the Venerable Yun's milk of the Dharma and to thank the Good and Wise Advisors of the world for their teaching. That is all.

> The Buddhadharma has not perished, but the Sangha destroys itself;  
> Virtue should be cultivated, yet people do not cultivate it.
>
> <small>佛法未滅僧自滅，道德應修人不修。</small>

> The honest and sincere draw public censure,  
> While the false and cunning are praised and favored.
>
> <small>老師真誠招物議，虛偽狡猾受褒憂。</small>

> In a world of the five turbidities, the clear are very few;  
> Living beings, drunk with the three poisons, have no season of waking.
>
> <small>舉世五濁清甚鮮，眾生三醉醒無秋，</small>

> Earnestly I send these words to the young monks:  
> The revival of our teaching rests with the Bhikshus.
>
> <small>殷勤寄語僧青輩，振興吾教在比丘。</small>

## 85. The Wealthy Merchant Goes Back on His Word (2)

A certain Zhang committed some crimes that defied Heaven, and before long he contracted an incurable disease. All the doctors declared that he would surely die within 100 days. To save his life, he put a public notice in the newspapers offering a reward of US$200,000 to whoever could save him, but despite the huge reward, no brave man came forward. Afterward he took refuge with the Venerable Master, and after 100 days, to everyone's surprise, he had not died...

Cause and effect are perfectly clear, but only those with clear eyes can see through them. Zhang killed people and made and sold fake medicines, deceiving the world, and that is why he contracted an incurable disease. But because he had revealed his faults and repented before the Buddha, taken refuge with the Triple Jewel, and vowed to give his wealth to make offerings to refugee monks and to build monasteries, he was granted a longer life. However, having made the vow, he did not fulfill it, deceiving the Buddha and cheating his teacher, making one mistake after another, and in the end he harmed others and harmed himself.

Zhang was certainly pitiable, but even more pitiable were the relatives and go-betweens who curried favor with him, and the blind followers who echoed them.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Back then, Zhang knelt before the Buddha in front of me and made a vow, saying that if he did not die within 100 days, he would give me US$200,000 to build a monastery. I waited eight years for him. That year, on the first day of the first lunar month, more than 100 people came, and I told everyone, "I've been waiting for Zhang to give US$200,000 to build a monastery; I've waited eight years for him. Starting today, I'm not waiting anymore. Whatever happens to Zhang from now on, I won't have anything to do with it!" Less than three months after I said this, Zhang's cancer flared up again. He went to other temples to bow repentances, but no Buddhist service of any kind worked. He had his family come to me to beg me, but I paid them no attention at all; even if they knelt at my door until they died, I would pay no attention! Within a few days he died.

Zhang's second younger brother died by jumping into the sea; that happened before Zhang took refuge with me. To tell you the truth, if Zhang had had faith when he took refuge with the Triple Jewel, he would not have died! If he hadn't gone down the wrong road, he would not have died! Because his faith was not firm, although he didn't die within 100 days, after eight years he died anyway, and he couldn't take with him any of the money he had swindled from people. A year after his death, the elder Dharma Master in his sixties who had curried favor with him also got cancer. People bowed repentances for him everywhere, but no matter how they bowed, he didn't get better, and after a year or so he died too! Just before I came to America, he came to see me; he was so thin it was awful to look at.

In March (1962) I went to Japan. Someone had the elder Dharma Master's disciple in Japan give me 10,000 yen. I said to this person, "Your teacher is about to die; he won't even last 100 days. Hurry back to Hong Kong and prepare for his funeral." This disciple of his thought I was cursing his teacher and flew into a rage, and even told others that it was very wrong of me to curse his teacher. You see, when you tell people the truth, nobody believes you, but when you deceive them, people believe you. I went to America in March, and in May he breathed his last. All his life he knew only money and didn't care about cultivation; he would do anything, by any means, for money, and in the end he lost even his own life. After the elder Dharma Master died, the layman who had introduced Zhang to that other Way-place got cancer of the tongue, because he had gone as a go-between and introduced Zhang there; he died too. So this whole affair is very strange! This man was very rich, and many monastics had deposited their money with him; he swindled the monastics out of more than a million. When he died, those monastics' money was all gone too.

## 86. Circulating the Dharma Treasure

After the Venerable Master came to Hong Kong, he was busy all day long propagating the Buddhadharma. Besides lecturing on the sutras and speaking the Dharma and founding *Mind Dharma* magazine, he also printed large numbers of sutras for free distribution. In fact, as early as when the Venerable Master was living beside his mother's grave, he never let a book out of his hands, reading widely in the scriptures and reverently studying the great sutras...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

What does "circulate" mean? [Tr.: the Chinese word, liutong, is made up of liu, "flow," and tong, "get through."] Liu, "flow," means to flow from this place to that place; tong, "get through," means that it didn't get through before, and now we make it get through. It's the same principle as water, which just flows forward and flows into the great sea. A place that sells Buddhist sutras is called a "Buddhist sutra distribution center." That is, you didn't have it, you obtain a sutra here from me, and then you have it: that is circulating the sutras. Also, if this place doesn't have a certain sutra and you bring some sutras here from somewhere else, that is also circulating. And if there are no sutras at all, and we all resolve to print sutras, that too is circulating. We should do our utmost to explain, write out, print, and circulate them. The merit and virtue of printing sutras is the greatest, and it also opens your inherent wisdom. So we must not overlook the importance of circulating the sutras. If you can print sutras widely and circulate them, you will open your wisdom, and your wisdom will increase.

Why hasn't your wisdom opened? It's because you have no merit and virtue. With little merit and virtue you have little wisdom; with great merit and virtue you'll have great wisdom, and then you won't be foolish or deluded; you'll be awakened. Why are you so deluded? Because your merit and virtue are still not enough. "I make offerings to the Triple Jewel, I print sutras, and I do the meritorious work of liberating life too; how can I still be so deluded?" You feel you've done a lot, but in fact the merit and virtue you've done is very little. A few days ago I wrote about the "three ordinary affairs": what you eat, what you wear, and where you live. Tell me, how much money do you spend on these three ordinary affairs? Compare them. Do you spend more money on merit and virtue, or more money on food? Do you spend more money on merit and virtue, or more money on buying clothes? Compare them yourself!

Compare again: do you spend more money on merit and virtue, or more money on buying cars? Do you spend more money on merit and virtue, or more money on going around having fun and traveling? If you're someone who likes to gamble, compare again: is the money you lose gambling more, or the money for merit and virtue? You spend so much money on clothing, food, and housing; tell me, is there any merit and virtue in that? When the money is spent on yourself, you don't feel it's much; but when you print a few sutras or liberate a few lives, you feel you've spent a great deal of money and your merit and virtue is already very great.

**[Addendum 1]** The Venerable Master's reflections after the facsimile reprinting of the *Great Means Expansive Buddha Flower Adornment Sutra*:

While I lived beside the grave, I read widely in the scriptures. When I first read the *Dharma Flower Sutra*, I leapt for joy as if mad. I knelt and recited it for seven days and seven nights, forgetting sleep and food, until my eyes bled and my eyesight weakened. Next I read the *Shurangama Sutra*, investigating its great samadhi to the end and cultivating it in stillness, with the three stoppings and three contemplations, neither moving nor still, until I first attained a single mind, deep and still, and began to penetrate the principle of the nature. When I came to read the *Avatamsaka Sutra*, I felt it was vast and boundless, broad beyond naming, lofty and unsurpassed, clear and formless. As Qingliang [Tr.: National Master Qingliang (Chengguan), the great Tang-dynasty commentator on the *Avatamsaka Sutra*] said, "It splits open the profound and subtle, illuminates and opens up the mind and its states, exhausts principle and fully realizes the nature, penetrates the fruition and encompasses the cause. Vast as the ocean, harmoniously blended, it is great and complete in every respect." How true that is! So I could not put the book down; I reverently read the great sutra, as one cannot be without clothing for even a moment, or go a single day without food. I then privately made a vow to circulate it widely. Later, when Vinaya Master Cizhou manifested stillness, his students borrowed this Hall to hold an Avatamsaka session, and so it was decided to make a facsimile reprint of this sutra. But the Dharma-Ending Age is full of obstacles: two or three laymen actually monopolized the project from within, taking everything into their own hands and manipulating it, regarding it as a rare commodity to be hoarded; they wasted the funds and dragged things out, so it has been delayed until now. This is truly lamentable! So I personally supervised the printing, and only then was I able to fulfill this vow. I hope that the Good and Wise Advisors at home and abroad who have subscribed in advance for this sutra will understand Lun's difficult position and forgive his foolishness and clumsiness. For this I pray most earnestly.

Respectfully written by this mountain monk, Shi To Lun, at the Avatamsaka Sutra Facsimile Reprint Office of the Buddhist Lecture Hall, Hong Kong Island

The nineteenth day of the sixth month of the gengzi year, Mahayana Buddhist Era 2987

(Editor's note: that is, July 12, 1960 CE)

**[Addendum 2]** The Venerable Master's account of the origins of the facsimile reprint of the *Wonderful Dharma Lotus Flower Sutra* printed by Huiyun Cloister in Xiuzhou:

Now, in the Dharma-Ending Age, the ways of the world are in decline, deviant doctrines run rampant, and demonic paths arise in droves. Foolish and deluded living beings have been entangled for kalpa upon kalpa with no prospect of escape. Whenever I think of this, how can I not grieve?

In the past, the Tathagata, out of pity for living beings, manifested in the world, proclaimed the wondrous doctrine, widely transmitted the holy teaching, and devised all kinds of expedient means, serving as a bridge to rescue living beings and a ship on the sea of suffering, so that living beings might thereby be liberated from the turning wheel and rise to proper enlightenment. Yet without the sutras the Way would have nowhere to dwell, and without the sutras the Dharma could not be transmitted. Relying on the sutras we seek the Dharma; relying on the Dharma we understand the mind. This is the purpose for which the Tathagata spoke the sutras.

The *Dharma Flower Sutra* is the king of sutras, encompassing the true meaning of all the Buddha's sutras. Pure and subtly wonderful, it is worthy of praise as rare indeed. If one can receive and uphold it, one's body and mind will surely be healthy and at peace, the light of one's nature will certainly grow gradually brighter, and one will even realize that the five skandhas are all empty and attain the purity of the six faculties; and in the future one will surely ascend the jeweled lion's throne. Thus we know that this sutra is the unsurpassed king of sutras, unequaled and beyond compare, true and not false. How could we living beings fail to receive and uphold it sincerely and cherish it without forgetting, and instead let it slip by when it is right before our eyes?

Here we have a Song-dynasty edition of the *Wonderful Dharma Lotus Flower Sutra*, placed in a stupa at Huiyun Cloister in Xiuzhou by the Bhikshu Deqiu during the Qiandao reign period of the Song. Later, on the thirteenth day of the seventh month of the jiawu year, the tenth year of Qiandao, lightning destroyed the right-hand stupa; then, on the night of the ninth day of the seventh month of the yiwei year, the sutra was stolen. Deqiu was greatly distressed and could find no peace, but in the end it was returned. This is enough to prove the supremacy of this sutra, which can move people in invisible ways. This sutra is traditionally said to have been written out by Su Dongpo of the Song dynasty, but unfortunately the calligrapher did not sign his name, so there is no way to confirm it. Still, given the excellence of the calligraphy, there can be no doubt that it was written by a famous master. The calligraphy of this sutra has an air of grace within its strength and vigor, and a measure of variety within its neatness and precision. Moreover, the woodblocks were carved with great precision, and not a single character is missing or damaged; it is a fine edition. With this peerless calligraphy writing out this unsurpassed king of sutras, it can be said that each brings out the full beauty of the other, and each enhances the other. Therefore, when this mountain monk, together with the Buddhist Publishing House of the Hong Kong Buddhist Sutra Distribution Center and many lay Buddhists of Hong Kong and Kowloon, saw it, we praised it as a treasure and resolved to make a facsimile reprint of it for the benefit of humans and gods. But this is like a cup of water against a cartload of burning firewood; I truly feel that my meager strength is not up to it. Moreover, if this sutra is to be reproduced in facsimile, it must be done in large quantity and of fine quality, so that it can be widely circulated. Therefore I hope that all you great lay Buddhists will bring forth the mind of great vows and subscribe in advance as much as you can, to help with the facsimile reprinting of this sutra; then you will obtain limitless blessings!

Printing sutras brings ten kinds of merit and virtue, which are inconceivable; and the merit and virtue of making a facsimile reprint of this king of sutras is all the more limitless, needless to say. First: all the evil karma one created in the past will be wiped out invisibly, like an iceberg meeting the sun of wisdom. Second: one will be spared all calamities such as plagues, floods and fires, wars, and imprisonment. Third: one will be able to resolve all enmities with foes and kin. Fourth: one will not be harmed by yakshas and evil ghosts, nor by any venomous snakes or ferocious beasts. Fifth: one will obtain great peace and happiness; by day there will be no dangerous incidents, by night one's dreams will be peaceful, and all things will be auspicious. Sixth: one will have ample clothing and plentiful food, a harmonious family, and long life. Seventh: one will receive the respect of humans and gods. Eighth: one will gain great wisdom and good health in body and mind. Ninth: one will leave the evil paths forever and be born in the good paths, with upright features and enduring blessings and wealth. Tenth: one will be able to plant good roots for all living beings. Ah! The merit and virtue of printing sutras truly has such wonderful benefits. If one does it, above one accords with the Buddha's mind and below one benefits living beings; the Tathagata's seed-wisdom will flourish in the field of one's mind, and one will surely go on until one sees the Buddha and becomes a Buddha. How could we living beings let this wonderfully effective merit and virtue right before us flow away like water going east? Surely we will be glad to do it.

Composed by Shi To Lun, a Bhikshu in the mountains, in the bingshen year

**[Addendum 3]** The Venerable Master's preface to the facsimile reprint of Master Boshan's *True Exposition on Transcendence*:

The eighty-four thousand Dharma-doors counter the afflictions of living beings; the limitless hundreds of thousands of samadhis all enter into the dhyana of the Patriarchs. I, An Tse, was born between the White Mountains and the Black Waters, and developed a nature that loves stillness and dislikes activity. In childhood I visited Good and Wise Advisors and drew near to the Chan lineage and its greatly virtuous ones. When I grew up, I heard of the Venerable Elder Master Hsu Yun, a towering figure of the Chan lineage and a field of blessings for living beings, who was propagating the Dharma in Fujian and Guangdong and residing at Nanhua. So I vowed to pay homage to the true body of the Sixth Patriarch and to seek Elder Hsu's guidance. I made plans for a long journey, gave up my encumbering robes and bedding, and traveled alone for ten thousand li, sleeping in the open amid dew and frost, pressing on day and night, crossing seas and climbing mountains. When I passed through Nanjing, Master Liaocheng, abbot of Doushuai Monastery on Mount Kongqing, was holding a Chan session attended by several dozen people, and I too was counted among them. The senior members among them were Dharma Master Ming Guan, Great Master Jing Kong, and Masters Miao Zhen, Ji Zhong, Fo Fa, Zhi Yuan, Lü Zhen, Ji Guang, and others, all of them Bodhisattvas with resolve for the Way. Rousing their spirits, they advanced with courageous vigor, sitting constantly without lying down, without self and without others, until the session concluded. I continued south, from Shanghai to Huizhou, without half a cent to my name; fortunately, aided by the Buddha's blessing, I made it all the way to the court of the Patriarch, where I reverently bowed before the true image, paid homage to the eminent monk, received his teachings in person, and heard his kind words imparted face to face. Several years passed in a flash as I set myself aside to till others' fields, following Elder Hsu. Then I came to Yunmen. A single false thought stirred, and I wanted to travel to visit teachers; in the gengyin year I bowed farewell to Elder Hsu. Wandering like the clouds and water to Hong Kong, I lived in seclusion in the wonderful Guanyin Cave and met with a response from the people here. I lectured on the *Earth Store Sutra* and the *Vajra Sutra* at Tung Sin Tan and practiced impartial giving in Hong Kong. I built Western Bliss Gardens at Ma Shan, devoted to propagating the Pure Land, and built Cixing Monastery at Ling Wui, to receive and guide those who investigate Chan. In the middle of the seventh month of the yiwei year, at Cixing Chan Monastery on Ling Wui Shan on Lantau Island, I was discussing the profound mysteries of the Chan Dharma with Great Master Fo Fa. When we spoke of the decline of the Chan lineage, we could not stop shaking our heads and sighing. Then I recalled Master Boshan's book *True Exposition on Transcendence*, an essential classic of the Chan lineage and a bright lamp for rescuing Chan practitioners. Printing this book would be an unsurpassed bridge, and so I resolved to have it printed, to remedy the present decline of the Mind School. The book is excellent in both its illustrations and its text and clear at a glance; even the most foolish can gain real benefit from reading it. Its content is substantial and its theory perfectly integrated; it accords with the Tathagata's original intent in establishing the teaching and, further, captures the secret purport of the Patriarchs' transmission of the mind. It is not only the prajna of words but truly a fine remedy for the ignorance and afflictions of living beings. Therefore this mountain monk is willing to do his utmost, slight as his strength is, to have this book printed; then Shakyamuni, Boshan, and all the Patriarchs and Elder Masters will surely rejoice endlessly in the Eternal Stillness and Light.

The fifteenth day of the first month of the bingshen year

Prefaced by To Lun, a Bhikshu in the mountains, at the right of his seat in his humble dwelling at Western Bliss Gardens

## 87. The San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall

The Tan sisters, who had taken refuge with the Venerable Master when they were young, went to study in the United States one after the other. The elder sister, Tan Guo Zheng, went first to study in San Francisco. She found there was no Buddhist hall there, so she wrote to tell the Venerable Master. In January 1958, when the younger sister, Tan Guo Shi...

was leaving to study in San Francisco, the Venerable Master said, "Together with your sister, bring the Buddhadharma to America!" Following the Venerable Master's instructions, the two sisters found a basement in Ross Alley, near San Francisco's Chinatown, to serve as the premises of a Buddhist hall. After asking the Venerable Master for his approval, they founded the San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall in February 1958. A few months after the Lecture Hall was founded, the number of people gathering every Saturday evening had grown from a dozen or so to several dozen. Later it moved to Pacific Street in San Francisco, and on March 11, 1959, it was officially registered under the name "Buddhist Lecture Hall," in English and in Chinese.

In early August 1961, because its lease had expired, the Buddhist Lecture Hall was forced to move, and it moved temporarily to Clay Street, to a basement owned by the Tan family. On August 6, after the move to the new site, a Universal Buddha Ceremony was held in celebration, with Dharma Master Zu Yin as the presiding Dharma Master, and many faithful devotees came to join in and rejoice.

Because the Tan sisters had all along been leading their fellow Buddhists in Dharma assemblies and group cultivation, many people who had never seen the Venerable Master came to the Buddhist Lecture Hall seeking to take refuge, and they hoped the Venerable Master could come to America to propagate the Dharma. Before the Venerable Master came to America, Tan Guo Shi once said to the Venerable Master, "Shifu, coming to America will be very hard! You should think it over clearly: do you really want to come to America?" The Venerable Master said, "I'm not afraid of hardship! You know how hard things were for me when I was at Fu Yung Shan!" Later, when the formalities had been completed, the Venerable Master did not go to America right away but flew to Australia to propagate the Dharma.

From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

After I graduated from high school, I could go to either England or America. If I went to England, I had already completed one of the two years of preparatory courses. But my father wanted me to come to America for university; because he had property in America, he wanted me to come to America. One day I was on Lantau Island (I think it was during a Chan session) when my father sent over a letter from the consulate: they had approved my application to go to America. I cried instead of being happy. The Master told me to win glory for Buddhism. He said, "You can go to America and try to spread Buddhism!"

I arrived in San Francisco in 1955. My cousin Tan Wenrang, who was in business in America (he later took refuge and received the Dharma name Guo Quan), took me to a private home to attend a Taoist organization, where a dozen or so overseas Chinese businessmen gathered. A Taoist master from Hong Kong, Wu Peilin, was doing planchette writing there; Wu Peilin practiced Buddhism and Taoism together. There I met a group of lay Buddhists, as well as the painter You Yunshan (who later left the home-life in Taiwan, with the Dharma title Dharma Master Xiao Yun). After that I went to visit the "Buddhist Chan Society Ritual Hall" in Chinatown, which was centered on family clans. I also went with Japanese classmates to the Buddhist Churches of America, which was first established in San Francisco's Japantown, to take part in their Dharma services, which followed a ritual like that of a Catholic church. I thought that what was needed here was a Buddhist Way-place of the Proper Dharma, so when Guo Shi came to America in 1958, she brought Dharma instruments and items for a Buddha shrine.

We rented an inexpensive basement in Chinatown and began to set up a Buddhist association, which we named the "San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall." This was the earliest Buddhist Way-place in Chinatown. Later, more and more people came to the Buddhist hall to bow to the Buddha, so Liang Minhui (Dharma name Guo Min), who had also taken refuge with the Venerable Master at the Lecture Hall, found a very good storefront for the Lecture Hall and also signed a two-year lease for it. In this place we could hold lectures and communal meals. Since Guo Shi and I both had to attend school, and most of the devotees had to work on weekdays, the Dharma assemblies were all held on weekend evenings and on Sundays. By this time the Buddhist Lecture Hall had been registered as a nonprofit organization, and everyone donated money to hire a lawyer to apply for the Venerable Master to come to America. Just when the lease was about to expire, Liang Minhui told us that the landlord would not renew the lease and we had to move; he would keep looking for a place. At that time I took him at his word. When the lease expired, we had no choice but to move the Buddhist hall temporarily into the basement of my father's property and then look for a suitable place. Then the lawyer also wrote to say, "The American consulate in Hong Kong has sent a notice asking the Venerable Master to come for his visa, but there has been no word." Because the Venerable Master had no fixed address in Australia, there was no way to notify him. Just after the Buddhist hall moved out, news came that Liang Minhui had rented another floor and set up the "Buddhist Association of America," and some of the devotees, not knowing the inside story, drifted along with the current and went over to it! The Buddhist Association of America was set up purely so that laypeople managed the finances, and Dharma Masters would be appointed as leaders of the Dharma assemblies. Whenever a Chinese Dharma Master arrived in America, Liang Minhui would go and invite him to stay at the temple for a while. Dharma Masters newly arrived in America were glad to have a place to stay, and that is how the association won the praise of great Dharma Masters and senior lay Buddhists who looked only at the surface.

Not long after I went back to Hong Kong in 1961, the Venerable Master returned from Australia, and only then did he go to the American consulate to collect his visa. Although I had reported to the Venerable Master on the split in the Buddhist Lecture Hall, the Venerable Master took it calmly and did not rush to make a special trip to America; he even decided to visit Buddhist organizations in Japan and Honolulu along the way. So the hardships the Venerable Master faced after arriving in America go without saying.

**[Addendum 1]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted on March 4, 2015:

Liang Minhui set up the "Buddhist Association of America" in 1961 and took some of the members of the Buddhist Lecture Hall with him. At the end of 1961, when we moved to Clay Street, some of the members came back again.

However, one whose contribution must not be overlooked is Yu Guo Xing. He not only contributed money and effort but also often urged the disciples who had taken refuge with the Master not to run off to other places, but to guard and protect their own Master's place. During the time when Guo Zheng had gone back to Hong Kong on business, it was Yu Guo Xing who helped me keep the Lecture Hall going all along. There was also an Upasaka Yang, who helped us get news items published in the newspapers. Huang Guo Ren, Li Guo Qian, and others all came to the Lecture Hall at that time, and they brought their classmates along too.

**[Addendum 1]** *Gold Mountain Times*, August 7, 1961:

Notice of Thanks on Moving to New Premises

Yesterday, August 6 (the twenty-fifth day of the sixth lunar month), our association moved to its new site and held a Universal Buddha Ceremony in celebration. We were honored that the Venerable Dharma Master Zu Yin graciously came to preside at the altar, and that patrons, faithful devotees, and Buddhist friends came to join in and rejoice and kindly bestowed generous gifts and fresh flowers. For their noble friendship and deep kindness we are sincerely grateful, and we respectfully set down these few words to express our thanks.

Furthermore, the pure funds that the faithful devotees offered that day with the merit-and-virtue dedication forms they signed have all been donated on their behalf to the Chinese Hospital (Tung Wah Hospital), as a field of blessings to increase the good fortune of all the faithful devotees; for this, too, we express our thanks.

With respectful thanks from all the members of the San Francisco Buddhist Lecture Hall

**[Addendum 2]** *Gold Mountain Times*, September 8, 1961:

Buddhist Lecture Hall to Celebrate the Birthday of Earth Store Bodhisattva

The Buddhist Lecture Hall at 824 Clay Street will celebrate the holy birthday of Earth Store Bodhisattva at 8:00 p.m. on Saturday, the 9th of this month (the thirtieth day of the seventh lunar month). People from all walks of life are welcome to attend.

Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

Originally I wasn't going to come to America. I had applied to study in Australia, and the Australian side had approved it, but my father hoped I would come to America. I said to the Master, "Shifu, where do you think it would be better for me to go?" The Master said very simply, "America is mei, 'beautiful'; Australia is ao, 'regretful'!" [Tr.: a pun: America (Meiguo) begins with mei, "beautiful," and Australia (Aozhou) begins with ao, as in aohui, "regret."] So I applied again to come to America to study, and, probably because the conditions were right, it was approved very quickly. At the same time, Guo Mi went to Australia. Before I came to America, my sister wrote to tell the Master that there was no Buddhist hall here, only Taoist temples, so in January 1958, before I flew to America, the Master told me, "You and your sister must do a good job of bringing the Buddhadharma to America!"

In 1958 we sisters founded the "Buddhist Lecture Hall" in America, and later, when it was formally registered, its Chinese and English names followed the model of the Hong Kong Buddhist Lecture Hall entirely. Every Saturday and Sunday we held various Buddhist services. I led the Dharma assemblies, lectured on the sutras, and gave instructional talks, and we also invited Buddhists, professors, elderly overseas Chinese, and others to give talks. The number of people attending the Dharma assemblies grew from a dozen or so to over a hundred, most of them elderly overseas Chinese and merchants from the Siyi region of Guangdong. They saw that we sisters were very skilled at performing Buddhist services, and when they heard the Buddhadharma we spoke, they knew we had been trained in Hong Kong, so they treated us with great respect.

More and more devotees came to the Buddhist Lecture Hall. The older generation of overseas Chinese all knew my father, and when they heard that our family had taken refuge with the Master, some of them also wanted to take refuge with the Master. We asked the Master for instructions; the Master sent their Dharma names by letter and specified when the refuge ceremony should be held, and we held the refuge ceremony here in America. At that time I presided over the ceremony, conferring the refuge on the Master's behalf.

At the beginning of 1960, because most of the disciples who had taken refuge with the Master had never seen the Master in person (they could only see the Master's virtuous likeness on the altar), the proposal was made to "invite the Master to come to America to propagate the Buddhadharma." With everyone's agreement and support, my sister began to engage a lawyer to handle the application for the Master to come to America and to raise funds. Because the procedures of the U.S. immigration authorities were complicated, the matter of the Master's coming to America progressed slowly. Later, the Master's application to come to America was finally approved, but just then the Master had agreed to go to Australia to propagate the Dharma (1961), so it was not until March 27, 1962, that the Master's American refuge disciples finally saw the greatly virtuous and kind teacher whom they had long admired.

## 88. The Journey to Australia (1)

In 1961 the Venerable Master stayed in Australia for a year, traveling back and forth between Melbourne and Sydney, but very few people recognized who he was! Only two or three scholars followed him. Although the conditions had not yet ripened, 36 years later Gold Coast Dharma Realm Sagely Monastery stood tall in Australia!

On May 11, the Venerable Master was invited to speak at the Sydney Buddhist Society on the day of the Buddha's birthday celebration, with Upasaka Liu Jining, a scholar at the University of Sydney, interpreting for him. The Venerable Master began with Shakyamuni Buddha's birth in India, his awakening to the Way upon seeing the bright star at night, his speaking of the *Avatamsaka Sutra*, and his expounding of the *Agamas*, and from there he introduced the basic teachings of the Four Noble Truths and the Twelve Causes and Conditions, encouraging everyone to cultivate and turn toward goodness.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

People said of me, "He can't even read; how could he lecture on the sutras? Don't go listen to his lectures; he just talks nonsense. It's all right for him to lecture to Americans and Australians, because they don't understand the Buddhadharma, so they can accept something shallow." Even though others say I can't lecture on the sutras, I'm still going to lecture. I call this "tossing out a brick to draw forth jade." When those who can lecture on the sutras see this, they'll feel embarrassed—too embarrassed to stay lazy and idle anymore: "We also have a share in teaching and transforming living beings; we shouldn't fail to fulfill our responsibility!" To make them all bring forth the Bodhi resolve to cross over living beings—that is my intention.

"The Thirty-seven Wings of Enlightenment" are a Dharma cultivated in the Small Vehicle, but they also extend to the Mahayana; Mahayana practitioners can cultivate them too, because this Dharma is common to both the Great and the Small Vehicles. The Mahayana is born from the Small Vehicle, and the Small Vehicle is contained within the Mahayana; without the Small Vehicle, one cannot reach the fruition of the Mahayana directly. And without the Mahayana, the position of the Small Vehicle could not be revealed either. So when I spoke the Dharma for them in Australia, I explained this principle in great detail; therefore, those who study the Mahayana should also know the Thirty-seven Wings of Enlightenment.

The Buddha's Birthday, May 11, 1961

Text of the Venerable Master's talk at the Sydney Buddhist Society:

Today is May 11, the day on which the Buddhists of Sydney, Australia, celebrate the Buddha's birthday—a most solemn and grand ceremony. I, To Lun, am fortunate to be present at this splendid occasion, and I have also received the sincere invitation of the chairman and all the Dharma-protecting laypeople to attend and give a talk for the general public, so as to establish Dharma affinities. Facing so many guests and participants from all walks of life here, this mountain monk feels deeply honored. Moreover, Mr. Liu Jining, a scholar of the University of Sydney, has kindly supported this assembly; graciously acceding to everyone's wishes, he has generously taken time from his busy schedule to serve as English interpreter, for which this mountain monk is especially and most deeply grateful.

Tracing back, our Buddhism originated in India and has spread throughout the world. Its founder, Shakyamuni Buddha, was born into a royal family as the crown prince of King Shuddhodana, and was named Siddhartha. Wandering out through the four city gates, he saw birth, old age, sickness, and death, felt that birth and death are impermanent, and was filled with grief, sorrow, suffering, and distress, his spirit uneasy. He then renounced his kingdom and city, his wife and child, together with glory, honor, wealth, nobility, pleasure, happiness, and all of life's enjoyments, and went to the Snowy Mountains to cultivate. He practiced asceticism for six years, eating only one sesame seed and one grain of wheat a day to sustain his life. Later he sat beneath the Bodhi tree, and upon seeing the bright star at night, he awakened to the Way. Three times he exclaimed, "How wondrous!" and said, "All living beings possess the wisdom and virtuous characteristics of the Thus Come One, but because of false thinking and attachments, they cannot realize them." Thereupon he first spoke the *Great Means Expansive Buddha Flower Adornment Sutra*, teaching the Bodhisattva Dharma; the great beings of the Dharma body heard the Dharma and awakened, and through faith, understanding, practice, and certification, each obtained benefit. Only the people of the Two Vehicles were as if deaf and mute—as it is said, "They have eyes, yet do not see Vairochana Buddha; they have ears, yet do not hear the perfect and sudden teaching." The World Honored One then, for the sake of the real, set forth the provisional, and accommodating himself to the capacities of those of the Two Vehicles, spoke the teaching of the *Agama Sutras*.

He expounded the "Four Noble Truths"—suffering, accumulation, cessation, and the Way—and spoke the Twelve Causes and Conditions: ignorance conditions activity, activity conditions consciousness, consciousness conditions name and form, name and form condition the six entrances, the six entrances condition contact, contact conditions feeling, feeling conditions craving, craving conditions grasping, grasping conditions becoming, becoming conditions birth, and birth conditions old age and death. He spoke the "Six Perfections (paramitas)":

1. Giving: the giving of wealth, the giving of Dharma, and the giving of fearlessness.

2. Upholding precepts: those of the shramanera, the Bhikshu, and the Bodhisattva.

3. Patience: patience with living beings, patience with dharmas, and patience with the non-production of dharmas.

4. Vigor: there is vigor of body and vigor of mind.

5. Dhyana samadhi: there are the four dhyanas and eight samadhis, the nine successive samadhis, and so forth.

6. Prajna: the prajna of language, the prajna of contemplative illumination, and the prajna of the real mark.

Proceeding step by step, he spoke the Vaipulya teaching; to turn people from the small toward the great, he spoke the Prajna teaching. When he came to the teaching of the Dharma Flower and the Nirvana, he opened up the provisional to reveal the real; this teaching is pure, perfect, and uniquely wonderful. The Thus Come One's original intent in coming into the world was here at last fully expressed: he held nothing back and presented everything in its entirety. He proclaimed the Dharma for teaching Bodhisattvas, of which the Buddhas are protective and mindful, and which is called the true, ultimate, complete meaning, the foremost wonderful Dharma. Finally came the special single transmission: pointing directly to the human mind, seeing one's nature and becoming a Buddha. The World Honored One held up a flower, and Kashyapa smiled, comprehending the wonderful purport. The World Honored One then gave him the seal of certification, saying, "I have the treasury of the proper Dharma eye, the wonderful mind of Nirvana, the real mark which is without marks, a special transmission outside the teachings; I have entrusted it to Mahakashyapa!" From then on, it was transmitted singly from patriarch to patriarch, with mind sealing mind; this was the beginning of the Chan School. It was passed down to the twenty-eighth patriarch in India, the Venerable Bodhidharma, who then came east to China, crossed the river on a reed, and brought the flow of the Dharma into China; he was the First Patriarch. He transmitted the robe and bowl to the Second Patriarch, Shenguang, to whom he gave the name Huike. It was then passed down to the Sixth Patriarch, Chan Master Huineng (Dajian), and "one flower opened into five petals." This is the source of the Chan lineage's transmission in China.

Surveying the Buddhism of the world today, it falls into no more than two great schools. 1) The Mahayana, which teaches the Bodhisattva Dharma. "Bodhisattva" is translated as "a living being with a great resolve for the Way," and is also called "an enlightener of sentient beings." It takes as its basis the sutras and teachings translated by Dharma Masters such as Kumarajiva and Xuanzang of the Tang dynasty, and it has spread widely throughout China, Japan, Korea, Singapore, Malaya, Penang, Manila, India, Vietnam, Cambodia, Indonesia, Thailand, Burma, the United States, Brazil, and elsewhere. The seeds of the Mahayana have been sown everywhere; they will open into countless Bodhisattva flowers and bear countless fruits of Buddhahood. 2) The teaching of the Two Vehicles, the Arhat Dharma. "Arhat" has three meanings: 1. worthy of offerings, 2. slayer of thieves, 3. without birth. It takes the *Agamas* and other sutras as its basis and now flourishes in Ceylon, Burma, Thailand, India, Nepal, Pakistan, and elsewhere.

Yet these two were originally one body. Later followers have recklessly created entanglements, arguing about the great and the small and rejecting one another. This is truly putting a head on top of one's head and seeking marks within marks—most lamentable! However, it has always been that the great can contain the small, while the small can hardly encompass the great; this is a truth acknowledged by the world. When I gave a talk in Burma several years ago, I said, "Buddhists should look out upon the entire world, and their resolve should embrace the nature of the Dharma Realm. We should promote Buddhism in its original, complete form, unifying the teachings and the vehicles so that the great and the small return to the source.
