May everyone together reach the other shore, and may each one see the nature and become a Buddha." So it is today as well! I hope that all of you will be happy now and become Buddhas in the future, universally transforming your fellow beings so that all accomplish proper and equal Bodhi!

## 89. The Journey to Australia (2)

Now that China has grown strong, learning Chinese has become a trend. Back in 1961, the Venerable Master had already given this advice in Australia: "If you don't learn Chinese now, in a few decades people who don't know Chinese will have a hard time finding work; they'll be unemployed and have nothing to eat. I'm not saying it will be like that for everyone, but most will meet with this fate."

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In the spring of 1960, several universities in Australia learned that I was a Dharma Master of the Chan School and invited me to come and lecture, but I didn't accept right away, and it was not until November 25 that I set out. When I first arrived in Australia, no one looked after me. Later, at the invitation of the Chinese consul in Australia, I would sometimes go and sit at the consulate. Mr. Chen, who worked at the Chinese consulate, and his wife were very devoted; I stayed at their home for several days. Mrs. Chen bought me a dictionary and also sewed clothes for me. It's a pity she has a very bitter fate; she will be in a pitiable state in the future!

I encountered many strange things in Australia. For instance, once when I was waiting for a bus and had no money, someone actually drove up beside me and offered to give me a ride for free. Canberra lies between Melbourne and Sydney. One day a Westerner was driving to Melbourne but took the wrong road and went north to Sydney. Just as he arrived in Sydney, he saw me walking along George Street. He hurriedly stopped, but by the time he had parked his car and gone to look for me, I had vanished without a trace. So he went to ask in the shops in Chinatown, and someone told him, "He comes here every day! Wait here; he's sure to come." He waited three hours and still didn't see me. Then he went everywhere asking for my address, but because I had moved several times, at every place he tried he was told I had already moved. He searched from Saturday to Monday without finding me, so he left his phone number and address with the Chinese consulate in Australia. Two days later, when I went to the consulate, I learned about this. This Westerner was a university professor who believed in Mahayana Buddhism, and he engaged me to lecture on Mahayana Buddhist principles at the university.

When I was in Australia, I taught them Chinese as a volunteer, without charging any money. I told them, "There are now a great many Chinese people, and there are many Chinese all over the world. In the future, relations between people in this world will certainly become close; languages must be exchanged, cultures must be exchanged, and wisdom must be exchanged. If you don't learn Chinese now, in a few decades people who don't know Chinese will have a hard time finding work; they'll be unemployed and have nothing to eat. I'm not saying it will be like that for everyone, but most will meet with this fate." Now, if you learn Chinese well, it will be very useful in the future. All of you should pay attention to this; it's very important.

When Americans say "good day," in English it's "Good day." How do Australians say it? "G'day, G'day" (it sounds like "Good die"). An Australian came to America and, on meeting someone, said nothing else but "Good die, Good die." The American lost his temper the moment he heard it: "How come the minute we meet you're wishing me a good death?" In Australia, I saved some of my food every day to feed the little birds. The ostriches in Australia can run 40 miles an hour, as fast as a car. I also once saw a little bird that could call out, "Mummy! Mummy!" and "Honey! Honey!" It could call out all sorts of things, and it could even say, "Good morning! Good afternoon! Good evening! Good night!" If you didn't see that little bird, you'd think there was a person over there talking.

I once met a follower of a non-Buddhist path who claimed to cultivate wisdom of the mind. He asked me, "What is cause and effect?" I said, "First tell me your opinion!" The non-Buddhist said, "Cause and effect is nothing but 'death.'" I said, "If it's death, why are you still alive? Can it be that you no longer fall within cause and effect?" In southern China and throughout Southeast Asia, there is a kind of gu poison [Tr.: a form of sorcery using venomous creatures to harm or control people]. This gu sorcery makes you obey the sorcerer's commands forever; if you don't obey, you lose your life—you die. In Australia, too, there is a kind of non-Buddhist spell craft: someone recites spells up on a mountain and can, by reciting, make a person's head become like an egg; he uses these as exhibits. You see, this world is so big that there's nothing strange it doesn't have—all kinds of strange and bizarre things!

Don't say, "I haven't seen it, so I don't believe it!" If you don't believe, it's because you're too foolish. Why? There are a great many things you can't see. If you wait until you've seen everything before you believe, you'll never finish seeing all the things in this world in this lifetime. I often say that before anyone discovered this New World, no one knew there was an America. If you had told people, "There's a place called America, and there's Honolulu there, and this and that," they would have said, "You're talking in your sleep! Where in this world is there an America? There's no such place." He doesn't believe there is an America; he says there isn't. Well then, does this land of America exist or not? Would this land cease to exist just because he says it doesn't? No. If he says it exists, it exists; if he says it doesn't, it still exists. So whether you believe or not, it exists.

[Supplementary Note 1] From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

There was an Australian, aged about 50-odd, a very devout professor, who had taught the Master English in Australia. So after the Master returned to Hong Kong, he began studying English, and I studied English along with the Master. Not long after the Master returned to Hong Kong, this professor wrote him a letter—

Dharma Master To Lun, greetings. We are grateful that while you were in Australia you explained Buddhism to us; the doctrines you taught were extraordinary. I truly wish you knew a bit more English, Dharma Master—we would benefit even more. I have finished editing the English translation of *Records of Dharma Master To Lun* and will mail it to you, so that it can be reprinted and circulated. Now I would like to tell you about a strange event connected with you, Dharma Master. On my way home, I usually pass along a quiet little lane lined with trees on both sides, and I habitually carry a flashlight to light the way. One night, I suddenly heard a voice say, "Good morning!" I immediately switched on my flashlight and looked all around, but saw no one. I thought, "It's night now; why would someone say 'Good morning'?" I found it baffling. Not until three weeks later, when I met you, Dharma Master, and the very first thing you said to me was "Good morning!", did I finally understand that what happened that night was a prophecy!

[Supplementary Note 2] In 1997, in the suburbs of the Gold Coast in Queensland, Australia, the Dharma Realm Buddhist Association established its first branch Way-place in Australia, named "Gold Coast Dharma Realm." Since 2006, DRBA has held a series of Dharma assemblies at Gold Coast Dharma Realm every year.

## 90. Returning to Hong Kong from Australia

The Venerable Master was invited to lecture at the Sydney Buddhist Society and at universities, and at the same time he realized that for Buddhism to go out into the world, "languages must be exchanged, cultures must be exchanged, and wisdom must be exchanged"...

The Venerable Master was alone in Australia, in a completely unfamiliar environment. Although there was something of a language barrier, through his wisdom, broad-mindedness, and conduct he widely established good affinities, giving the Buddhadharma the opportunity to sow its seeds in the land of Australia.

On September 11, 1961, the Venerable Master took a Qantas flight back to Hong Kong. On the 13th, about 20-odd people went to meet him at the airport: Dharma Master Ci Rui, Dharma Master Xuan Yang, Dharma Master Heng Yue, and other Dharma Masters. Among the laypeople were Huang Guo Jun, Gan Guo Yan, Tan Guo Zheng, Ye Guo Qing, Xue Guo Feng, and others.

After the Venerable Master got off the plane, a Dharma Master asked, "Dharma Master To Lun, you seem to have gotten thinner." The Venerable Master replied with a laugh, "I'm not yet able to be neither produced nor destroyed, neither defiled nor pure, but now I'm already neither increasing nor decreasing!" By the time everyone had escorted the Venerable Master and the Dharma Masters to the Buddhist Lecture Hall, it was already past twelve o'clock. The Venerable Master did not eat his meal; instead, he gave everyone the strawberry powder he had brought back from Australia.

Not long afterwards, a Dharma Master came to visit and shook hands with the Venerable Master as soon as he came in the door. Surprised that he would imitate Western manners, the Venerable Master gripped his hand back hard. The Dharma Master bore the pain, unable to speak. The Venerable Master said with a laugh, "What's the matter?"

"My hand really hurts!" the Dharma Master managed to reply.

"So you're attached to this body too?" said the Venerable Master.

[Supplementary Note] A Hong Kong newspaper, September 1961:

Chan Master To Lun Propagates the Dharma in the Australian Capital; Soon to Visit the Philippines and Return Home Shortly

Chan Master To Lun, who flew by invitation from Hong Kong to Sydney, Australia, on November 26 of last year, has already achieved tremendous results in propagating the Dharma and transforming beings. In the past, among the Buddhist leaders from countries around the world who have come to Australia to promote Buddhism, apart from one person from India as long as 20 years ago, our Chan Master To Lun is in fact the second from the Far East, and the very first from China. Master To Lun's pioneering spirit is truly admirable. After eight months of spreading the teachings by speech and in writing, with tireless efforts that left him no time even to warm his seat, it can be said that the future of Buddhism there is bright: a smooth road is in sight, and it can stride forward without obstruction.

The Chan Master's Buddhist learning is profound, and his virtue and magnanimity are lofty. In particular, he has carefully studied Chinese classical learning as well as the historical facts and the deeds of famous people of every dynasty, commenting on them in detail and knowing them like the back of his hand. He is a widely learned and erudite eminent monk of our country in modern times. On this trip, not only did the overseas Chinese living in Australia eagerly look forward to his coming, but the Australian people, too, gained much benefit from the Dharma.

It is reported that Chan Master To Lun plans to go to the Philippines on July 15, and on July 25 to proceed from the Philippines to Taiwan, where he will stay for ten days before returning to Hong Kong. The Chan Master has recently written two books, *Impressions of Buddhism in Australia* and *Water Mirror Reflecting Heaven*, totaling several hundred thousand words, which will soon be available to fellow Buddhists in the homeland.

## 91. Eradicating Karma and Accumulating Blessings

In the course of a lifetime, people experience all kinds of things every day, over and over in endless cycles, the six faculties and six sense objects turning along in the flow of karma. Busily rushing east and west, without realizing it they create one new string of karma after another, spreading all sorts of karmic nets for the next round of rebirth...

Dharma Master Guo Ren (Bhikshuni Heng Qi) had formerly managed a catering business in Borneo, Indonesia, and came to Hong Kong and took refuge with the Venerable Master. The Venerable Master urged her not to kill anymore, but she thought that since she assigned others to do it, she herself was not killing, so she did not stop her restaurant work. Not long afterwards, she dreamed that the Venerable Master, wearing a yellow robe, came down a flight of steps, frowning, and said to her sternly, "I told you not to kill, and you kill again! I told you not to kill, and you kill again! Why don't you listen to me?" The Venerable Master paced back and forth in front of her

and kept on rebuking her. The dream was so vivid that she could never forget it. Afterwards she quit her job at the restaurant and went to help in the kitchen of the Buddhist Lecture Hall, where she worked for a full 18 years. Because she read the *Sutra of the Past Vows of Earth Store Bodhisattva* and understood the grave retribution for killing, she decided to leave the home-life. At that time the Venerable Master had already left Hong Kong for America, so every day she bowed in repentance before the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas. One day, while she was bowing, she saw Elder Master Hsu Yun standing before her, instructing her to offer a lamp. Not long after she had done this, she received a phone call from the Venerable Master: he was going to return to Hong Kong to propagate the Dharma. Her prayer had met with the best possible response. After leaving the home-life, Dharma Master Guo Ren served as chief cook at the Buddhist Lecture Hall all along. In her old age, when she went to the hospital for a checkup, she passed away peacefully in the hospital.

Zhang Guo Hong, at 42, took refuge with the Venerable Master in 1957. Together with Dharma Master Heng Yi, she supported the Way-place by cooking for the assembly. Three times she asked the Venerable Master to let her leave the home-life, but the Venerable Master said that her karmic obstacles were very heavy and that she should do more work at the Way-place to eradicate her karmic obstacles and accumulate blessings. She followed the Venerable Master's guidance: "If you cultivate well, Earth Store Bodhisattva will come to receive you in the future!" She lived at the Way-place for more than 30 years, doing all kinds of ascetic practices. In 1988, when the Venerable Master returned to Hong Kong to propagate the Dharma, she finally left the home-life at Cixing Monastery, with the Dharma name Guo Hong; she was 73 that year. In September 1995 she received full ordination at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas in America, with the Dharma name Heng Wei. In 2000, Dharma Master Heng Wei, then 85, moved into the Chi Lin Home for the Aged. She passed away in 2009 at the age of 94.

[Supplementary Note] Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Wei: excerpted from *Vajra Bodhi Sea*, the monthly magazine of the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas

> Ever upholding the virtue of tending the kitchen;  
> with great vows protecting the Way-place [Tr.: the two lines begin with the two characters of her name, Heng and Wei.]
>
> <small>恆守監齋德  偉願護道場</small>

Bhikshuni Dharma Master Heng Wei, whose lay name was Zhang Ai, was born in 1915. In 1957 she took refuge with the Venerable Master and received the Dharma name Guo Hong. After taking refuge, she was often at Western Bliss Gardens in Hong Kong, the Buddhist Lecture Hall, and Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island, listening to the sutras and hearing the Dharma, reciting sutras and bowing in repentance, and protecting and supporting the Way-places. She had a good temper and did everything wholeheartedly and with all her strength; no one ever saw her vent resentment in the kitchen. She loved reciting the Buddha's name: her recitation beads never left her hand, and her mouth never stopped reciting the Buddha's name. Her resolve to cultivate and to support the Way-places remained the same from beginning to end. She left the home-life in 1988, at the age of 73. In 1995 she received the Triple Platform Ordination. Not long after receiving full ordination, she returned to live permanently at Cixing Monastery in Hong Kong.

As she grew older, her body gradually deteriorated. She developed gallstones and needed surgery. She was anxious and prayed to the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas and to the Venerable Master for their aid. The day before the operation, she came from Cixing Monastery on Lantau Island to stay overnight at the Buddhist Lecture Hall, so that she could go to the hospital for surgery the next day. That night she had a strange dream: she dreamed that the Master came up to her, reached into her body, and with one pull of his hand the gallstones were gone, and the Master was gone too. When she woke up the next day, her body didn't hurt as much. When she went to the hospital for X-rays and was waiting for the operation, the doctor finished looking at her X-ray films: the gallstones had miraculously disappeared, and the films were different from the earlier ones. The doctor told her, "You don't need an operation; you can be discharged." Her body also gradually recovered. In another case, she asked the Venerable Master to help her find her son, from whom she had been separated for several decades, and this, too, truly came about. All these responses are facts that I, Guo Quan, heard and saw in the 28 years I was close to this elderly lady.

In 2000 she moved into the Chi Lin Home for the Aged, where she also helped tidy and look after the large and small halls of the home. She passed away in 2009 at the age of 94. That she was able to contribute so much to the monastic community in her lifetime truly deserves our respect. We feel deep sorrow at her relinquishing of this life.

> Unwilling to go, yet going—  
> coming and going,  
> bearing a heart full of compassion
>
> <small>不願去卻去  來來去去  帶著滿懷慈悲</small>

> Riding on vows, coming back again and again—  
> ever in haste,  
> to save and transform the human world
>
> <small>乘願來又來  匆匆忙忙 為了度化人間</small>

## 92. Living Beings Are Hard to Save

Although under the Venerable Master's guidance many devotees gradually came to know the Buddhadharma and took refuge with the Triple Jewel—some receiving and upholding the Three Refuges and the Five Precepts, others shaving their heads and leaving the home-life—after all, they were all ordinary worldly people...

Toward his disciples—whose causes and conditions differed, who were a mixture of good and bad, and who did not follow his instructions—the Venerable Master's compassion was earnest and urgent. Like a kind father, a strict teacher, and a good physician, he guided them patiently step by step and instructed them tirelessly; at times he scolded them sternly, and at times he taught them by manifesting examples. Everything he said and did had a purpose!

In the Venerable Master's own words:

In Hong Kong there were often Dharma assemblies for bowing to the Buddhas or for sutra lectures. Some laypeople would come, and then what? They'd fight with others over bowing benches to bow on. Seeing that someone else had a bowing bench to bow to the Buddha on while he had none, one would go over, take that person's bench away, carry it somewhere else, and bow on it himself. And what happens then? The person whose bench was snatched gets so angry that he never comes back. Why? "So I don't have to come here to bow to the Buddha and be bullied!" In Hong Kong, when they fought over bowing benches, I didn't interfere. If you want to grab, if you want to be a bandit, go ahead—I don't interfere. But this person who grabs a bench to bow to the Buddha will definitely not get any merit and virtue from bowing. We who study the Buddhadharma should not be like this.

So the laypeople who often come here and the people who live here, whether they come to listen to the sutras or for anything else, should all look after newcomers and tell them what to do. Nobody knows a person who comes to the Way-place for the first time; he may be a Bodhisattva, or perhaps even an Arhat. If you don't receive him well, you'll miss him right in front of your face and have no merit and virtue. Sometimes when there are a lot of people and not enough bowing benches, you might as well give up your own bench for him to bow on and go bow off to the side yourself. Otherwise, the newcomer will take one look around here and think, "You people are so selfish—you only look out for yourselves and don't care about others!" And he'll never come again.

In Hong Kong, quite a few disciples were asking me day and night to explain the Shurangama Mantra, but because the conditions were not yet ripe, I never agreed, so I didn't explain it. When I lectured on the sutras and spoke the Dharma in Hong Kong, I seldom taught people to sit in meditation, and I didn't teach anyone the Dharma of the "Great Compassion Thousand Hands and Thousand Eyes" either, because the "Thousand Hands and Thousand Eyes" Dharma is extremely important and I was unwilling to transmit it outside. Now I am transmitting this Dharma that I know to every disciple, every follower. If someone comes from Hong Kong and you tell him what Dharma you learned here with me, he'll say, "Impossible! None of us ever learned that." It's the same as the principle with the Small Vehicle and the Mahayana: the Small Vehicle says the Buddha didn't speak the Mahayana Dharma, that the Mahayana sutras weren't spoken by the Buddha! It's just like now: I'm transmitting this Great Compassion Dharma to you, and if you tell my Hong Kong disciples, they'll say, "That's not right! Shifu himself doesn't know the Great Compassion Dharma—how could he transmit it?" They think I don't know it, that I don't understand any of it. In fact, I simply didn't want to transmit it to them!

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master once told me, "Even when I haven't seen something with my own eyes, I can know everything just by closing my eyes." The Master knew about the drawing of fortune sticks at Cixing Monastery. At mealtime he told them to bring the Guanyin fortune-stick canister and the book for interpreting the sticks, and he shouted, "Bring the stick canister! I'll draw a stick to find out when I'm going to die!" When the Master scolded people, no one dared make a sound; only one person said, "Shifu, we can't find it right now." The Master was extremely angry and utterly disheartened, feeling that it was hard to transform people.

Once, when the Master was on Lantau Island, because his disciples did not follow his instructions, the Master set out from the mountain gate and bowed at every step all the way to the Buddha Hall. Everyone was moved, and only then did they correct their bad worldly habits.

One day the Master said, "I don't want to see this bunch of people anymore; they've made me too disheartened!" I said, "Shifu, doctors treat the seriously ill; why would people who aren't sick need to see a doctor?" The Master replied, "What you say makes sense, but this bunch is already beyond any cure!" The Master said, "Haven't you seen the four characters I wrote, 'I'll just die'? When you can't sleep, you can think about what this means. Everything I say and do has a purpose." Alas! I'm so dull—how could I figure it out?

**[Addendum 2]** Lay Buddhist Lu Guo Yan's oral account, given on January 12, 2010:

I took refuge with the Master at the Buddhist Lecture Hall in 1956. The Master lectured on the sutras in the evenings; I was in my thirties then. The Master was very kind and gentle with us; he often chatted with us, taught us how to bow to the Buddha, and gave us many instructions. We Dharma brothers and sisters got along very well. On the first and fifteenth days of the lunar month or on the Buddha's birthday, we would gather to share a vegetarian meal—as many as eight to ten tables of people—and at the New Year there would be more than 20 tables of people coming for the meal.

In the past, I didn't understand the Buddhadharma. Once, while reading the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra* lying in bed, I dreamed that the Master and two of his disciples were walking toward me from the opposite direction. I thought it was just a chance dream. The next night I was again reading the *Sixth Patriarch Sutra* lying in bed, and again I dreamed of the Master. The Master said, "You have no manners! How can you read a sutra lying in bed?" Only then did I know that the Master had really appeared.

## 93. Those Who Believe Will Be Saved

Countless people came to ask the Venerable Master to cure their illnesses: strange illnesses, illnesses caused by demons, depression, people unable to walk, people critically ill and beyond saving. However, the Venerable Master had a secret for curing illness...

Looking into the causes of each person's illness, the Venerable Master would have them bow to the Buddhas and hold mantras, or take refuge with the Triple Jewel. All those who were sincere and firm in their faith received a response without exception and gradually recovered.

An Upasika named Jing Kuan came to see the Venerable Master and pleaded with him: "My nephew has fallen ill in a very strange way. One day he felt dizzy, and just like that he fell ill and hasn't been able to get up since. The doctors can't diagnose what the illness is."

The Venerable Master closed his eyes for a moment and asked, "You took refuge with a certain eminent monk; why don't you go and ask him?" Jing Kuan said nothing. The Master had her write down her nephew's name and address, had someone give her a copy of the Venerable Master's biography, and told her, "Those who believe will be saved. You can recite 'Amitabha Buddha' facing this Dharma image."

Because the Venerable Master's name was printed beside the image, the woman, only half understanding, asked, "So I recite the name that's written in the book, right?" The Venerable Master said, "That's all right too."

Afterwards, the Venerable Master said to Tan Guo Zheng, who had been interpreting beside him, "That's because I can't say that myself." The Venerable Master then told her about Wang Fengjiu. When the Venerable Master was in Manchuria, Wang Fengjiu had deep and steadfast faith in him. Once, his son fell gravely ill and would not wake up. He prayed to the Venerable Master, but for several days there was no improvement. Yet his faith remained firm, and he kept bowing at home, beseeching the Venerable Master. One night he dreamed that the Venerable Master was holding a bowl of medicine and giving it to his son to drink. The next day his son suddenly woke up, cured without any medicine.

The Venerable Master said:

Ever since I was born, I've had only one secret: being true in everything—a true mind, true words, true conduct—without the slightest admixture of falseness, and without wearing a mask. You must add truth upon truth and genuineness upon genuineness! In this way, every sentence you speak is a "true word"—a mantra—and every word is an efficacious spell. Why is it that some people hold mantras, and keep on holding them, yet get no response? It's simply because their minds aren't true enough, so they can't connect with the mantra.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

Upasika He Guo Zhang had been ill for five years and was as thin and frail as a vine. Her younger sister, Guo Yin, wanted to ask the Master to accept her elder sister as a disciple, but the Master did not agree. The Master said, "Her karmic obstacles are very heavy; it won't be easy to deal with." Later, out of compassion for her, the Master accepted her for refuge, and only then did her illness gradually get better day by day. I have an elder female cousin who adopted a child. This child had had meningitis three times, and the doctor said, "If the illness recurs again, it will be incurable!" After he took refuge with the Master, the illness never recurred. His elder sister had suffered from depression, and she, too, recovered without treatment after taking refuge. When sick people take refuge with the Master—truly, as long as you have faith, with the aid of the Buddha's power, everyone will naturally recover.

**[Addendum 2]** Lay Buddhist Tan Guo Yang recounted on June 7, 2014:

My mother's name is Ruan Guo Ni. My father was in the hardware business. Although my mother had taken refuge with the Venerable Master very early on through someone's introduction and often went to bow to the Buddha, she was not a regular vegetarian. At that time, my father was in poor health; he had heart disease. My mother bowed to the Buddha very devoutly, hoping my father's health would improve a little.

One day in 1956, the Venerable Master saw my mother and said to her, "Tell your husband to go up to Cixing Monastery and stay a few days, on such-and-such days of such-and-such month." Although my mother was the Venerable Master's disciple, my father had his business to look after, so of course he wouldn't listen, and he didn't go to stay at Cixing Monastery. At that time, the living conditions at Cixing Monastery were quite poor: there was no electricity, no telephone, and no way to contact the outside world; if you got sick, you had to walk down the mountain to see a doctor. My father thought, "Why should I stay up on the mountain?"

Who would have known that during exactly the period the Venerable Master had mentioned, my father's business ran into a cash-flow problem? Dealing with it put him under too much pressure, and he had a heart attack and passed away. Afterwards, when my mother thought back on it, she realized that the Venerable Master had told my father to stay at Cixing Monastery for a few days precisely so that he would avoid the impact of this matter; if things had been allowed to ease off a bit, my father would not have had the heart attack. Whenever my mother thinks of this, she deeply regrets not having listened to the Venerable Master back then.

In 1977, after my mother closed down the business, she cultivated with great vigor every day. She never missed morning and evening recitation; she bowed to the Buddhas, recited sutras, held mantras, and sat in meditation in full lotus, and she often went to the Buddhist Lecture Hall to attend Dharma assemblies and to volunteer. Although our family was fairly well off, at her ordinary meals my mother ate only plain rice with preserved mustard greens or stir-fried green vegetables. She didn't buy new clothes and lived very frugally. Later, as she grew older, she chose to cultivate on her own at home and no longer went around. Now my mother is over 90, and she is still cultivating at home as best she can.

## 94. "Our Master, Master Cheng, Said"

The Venerable Master said, "Many people don't understand the meaning of Zhuzi's (Zhu Xi's) phrase 'Zi Chengzi yue.' The first 'zi' is what Zhu Xi used to refer to his teacher, namely Chengzi (Mr. Cheng Yi). The meaning of 'Zi Chengzi yue' is 'Chengzi, the teacher of me, Zhu Xi, said.'"

In the Venerable Master's own words:

There was once a Professor Cheng Xi, who wrote a book called *New Chan Sayings*. He said that in arithmetic, if you write a large number and draw a circle after this large number, the number becomes a bit larger. For example, take this "1" and keep drawing zeros after it until you've filled heaven and earth with circles—how much would that be, after all? However much you draw, there's never any end to it. This number, this large number: every circle you draw adds another place, every circle you draw makes it bigger. You draw on the wall, you fill up the ground below, you fill up the sky above too—how big a number is it, after all? So this number is endless and inexhaustible.

**[Addendum 1]** Professor Cheng Xi (courtesy name Zhongyan), from Iowa State University

He was engaged in education at the University of Iowa for more than 20 years and wrote many works, of which *Cheng's New Chan Sayings* is the most widely known. In 1993 Professor Cheng visited the Venerable Master at Long Beach Monastery. Professor Cheng said, "The elder Dharma Master never talked to me about spiritual powers; he was very ordinary. But he put what he knew into practice right away, and that's not easy!"

Professor Cheng Xi had met the Venerable Master once, at a discussion meeting in Hong Kong on reprinting the *Great Prajna Sutra*. Professor Cheng said, "Several decades have passed since that meeting. More than 30 years ago, I published a little book, *Cheng's New Chan Sayings*, and printed 1,000 copies to give away to establish affinities. Later the elder Dharma Master saw it and printed 5,000 copies using the same paper and format. That autumn I went to Cambridge, England, and didn't see the elder Dharma Master again. While the elder Dharma Master was in America, he selected a number of passages from *Cheng's New Chan Sayings* to reprint in *Vajra Bodhi Sea*, the monthly magazine of the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas. During that period I also published *Sayings in the Form of Questions*, and after the elder Dharma Master saw it, he assigned disciples to translate it into English for publication. The elder Dharma Master's kind regard for these little books has always stayed deep in my heart."

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

When the Master was talking to me about Cheng Xi's book *Cheng's New Chan Sayings*, he brought up the phrase "Zi Chengzi yue" from the Four Books and explained it. I said to the Master, "I'd like to put up half the money to help print this book." The Master said to me, "If you're going to print it, put up all the money; otherwise, don't print it."

**[Addendum 3]** In 1995, in an article commemorating the Venerable Master, Professor Cheng Xi wrote:

A few years ago I went to California to see the elder Dharma Master, and it happened that I fell ill at the time. The elder Dharma Master promptly had someone take me to the clinic of a doctor of Chinese medicine, and he phoned the doctor again and again, asking him to take special care in treating me. When the herbs were brought to Long Beach Monastery, his disciples carefully decocted the medicine, put it in a thermos so it would stay warm and not cool, and brought it in, and I drank it all down in one go. From this incident one can see the compassionate care, warmth, attentiveness, and thoughtfulness of the people in the Way-place, which outsiders might not be able to imagine.

The last time I saw the elder Dharma Master was in the summer of 1994. When I flew into Los Angeles, some of the elder Dharma Master's followers came to meet me at the airport and then took me to Long Beach Monastery. I talked with the elder Dharma Master for several hours. The elder Dharma Master had long known my true character, so I didn't need any conversational art and held nothing back in what I said. So once we started talking, we spoke freely and to our hearts' content, frankly and without reservation, and the time stretched out accordingly. Just before I left, some visitors came to call, among them a doctor of Chinese medicine skilled in treating muscles and bones. Busy as he was, the elder Dharma Master had this guest examine the sprain in my left arm. After some tuina massage, it felt as if a knot in the sinews of my arm had been untied; for several months I had never felt so relaxed. From then on, my left arm regained its flexibility, up and down.

**[Addendum 4]** In an article commemorating the Venerable Master, Professor Yang Fusen wrote:

Back when the Master was propagating the Dharma in Hong Kong, he became friends in the Way with Cheng Xi, and they often visited each other. Cheng Xi and I were both graduates of Yenching University. When I was teaching at the University of Pittsburgh, I once invited Professor Cheng to teach summer school at Pitt (Professor Cheng taught at the University of Iowa for many years, until he retired), so we were also quite well acquainted.

## 96. The Opening-the-Light Ceremony for the Jade Buddha

On February 19, 1962 (the fifteenth day of the first lunar month), the Opening-the-Light ceremony for the jade Buddha was held at the Buddhist Lecture Hall. This jade Buddha was a white jade stone Buddha image, majestically carved, which the Venerable Master had chosen during a visit to Burma after looking at all the carved jade Buddha images in Burma...

Those who came to the Buddhist Lecture Hall for the Opening-the-Light ceremony of the jade Buddha included fellow cultivators from monasteries far and wide and people from all walks of life; that day more than 40 tables were laid for the meal. Some Dharma Masters exclaimed that they had been to all the major monasteries and temples but had never seen such a majestic Buddha image. A craftsman who made Buddha images, and who had been making them for more than ten years, said he had never seen such a majestic jade Buddha.

The completion and Opening-the-Light of this jade Buddha image fulfilled a wish the Venerable Master had held for five years. The Venerable Master hoped that his disciples and devotees would all be able to see the Buddha and understand their own nature, opening up the light of the wisdom of their own nature and the light of the compassion of their own nature.

From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

January 8: The white jade Buddha image, which had been shipped back from Burma the year before and temporarily stored in a warehouse, was moved to the Buddha hall. The image weighs over a ton. The workers all said there was no way to get it up to the twelfth floor. Later the Master thought of a way: install a crane at the corner of the back stairway of our building, and then remove the back window on the twelfth floor. Only in this way could the wooden crate be brought into the Buddha hall. The image is incomparably majestic, its smile warm and radiant; everyone who sees it is filled with joy and is suddenly moved to reverence. Beaming with delight, the Master said, "My wish of five years is now finally fulfilled." The Master said that he had looked all over Burma, and there was only this one.

January 16: I went to the Lecture Hall just as the Buddha shrine was being built. The shrine was also designed by the Master; it is majestic and dignified, noble and elegant, and the workers couldn't stop praising it.

On the evening of the Opening-the-Light ceremony, a man none of us knew came to see the Master; as soon as he came in, he bowed. This man had once written to the Master asking for money. He had originally been disabled in one leg, and his name was Huang Juefei. On the second day of the first lunar month he had come to the Lecture Hall on crutches asking to see the Master, but the Master didn't want to see him. Later he wrote words on the window, and only then did the Master come out to see him. When he saw the Master, he begged the Master to take pity on him. The Master scolded him, "You're not a woman—why are you begging for pity? You have no backbone at all!" The Master also said, "I have the first line of a couplet: 'White and water make a spring; within, one and great make heaven'. If you can match it with a second line, I'll give you whatever you want."

Although he thought for a long time without coming up with a matching line, the Master later still gave him 200 dollars and some canned food. After Huang Juefei went home, on the seventh and eighth days of the month, in a state between dreaming and waking, he saw the Master massaging his bad leg. Afterwards, he was actually able to walk without his crutches, so he came to the Lecture Hall especially to bow and thank the Master. The Master said, "At first he didn't harbor any expectations in his heart; that's why there was such a response." Huang Juefei said that he had gone begging for alms at Buddhist halls everywhere and had met many great Dharma Masters at those halls, but not one monastic had treated him as well as the Master.

## 96. Making a Point of Saving Bad People

There are often people who lack self-cultivation and are of poor character, and who have committed serious wrongs of defiance and rebellion. Once they meet a Good and Wise Advisor who enlightens them, they suddenly wake up and are willing to wash their minds clean and turn over a new leaf, abandoning evil and turning to good. Yet training such "prodigal sons," whose bad habits have become second nature, still takes a great deal of effort...

Even so, the Venerable Master, with a heart of compassion, guided them patiently step by step, leading them in accord with their circumstances, even to the point of accepting them to shave their heads and leave the home-life, so that they could turn from delusion and return to awakening, in the hope that they would become great heroes standing tall between heaven and earth. The *Avatamsaka Sutra* says:

"For those who have lost their way, he points out the right road;

for those in the dark of night, he becomes a bright light." On March 5, 1962, 41 people took refuge, and the Venerable Master also shaved the head of one of them, Wang Ziyi, a man of habitually bad conduct, so that he could leave the home-life. The Venerable Master said, "I make a point of saving these bad people. Guo Shun was like this too before he left the home-life, but two years later he obtained the power of knowing others' thoughts."

The Venerable Master had Wang Ziyi tell everyone his faults, and he admitted and repented of each one. The Venerable Master urged him to quit drugs, admonishing him, "As long as you have resolve, you can quit drugs without taking medicine. You must remember that your Dharma name is Guo Wu, 'Fruit of Awakening,' not Guo Wu, 'Fruit of Error'!"

"I'm determined to draw a breath of pride!" Wang Ziyi replied.

"Whatever you do, don't let that turn into 'determined to draw a puff of smoke'! I'm willing to place a bet and accept you as a disciple. Although others have objections, I'm not afraid of trouble; I hope you can change your evil ways and obtain the treasure of redemption," said the Venerable Master.

The Venerable Master sent him to live on Lantau Island. After he had gone, the Venerable Master told everyone, "Even though he's bad, at most he'll kill me. Even if he kills me, I'll still..." The Venerable Master's vast magnanimity and Bodhisattva heart were truly beyond the reach of ordinary people.

**[Addendum]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

A Bhikshu who had returned to lay life came to ask the Master for help; someone had introduced him. The Master asked him out of the blue, "Why did you cause the death of the man surnamed Zhang?" The man immediately became frightened and said nervously, "It wasn't me! He hanged himself!" It turned out that when this former Bhikshu had been living at a certain monastery, he had embezzled tens of thousands of dollars from the temple, and the monastic in charge of finances (whose lay surname was Zhang), having been falsely accused by him, hanged himself to show that he had been wronged. With just a casual question, the Master exposed his secret.

## 97. Heng Ding's Demonic Test

The Venerable Master admonished his disciples to apply more effort and establish more virtue. No matter what Dharma door one cultivates, one must first emphasize virtuous conduct; if one's virtue is insufficient, whatever Dharma one cultivates will meet with demonic obstacles...

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Why did I give him the name "Heng Ding," "Constantly Settled"? Precisely because he was "constantly unsettled," so I took away the "un-," and then Heng Ding's cultivation could have a bit of constant samadhi. I had a wish to memorize the *Dharma Flower Sutra* and the *Shurangama Sutra*, but I never had the time to fulfill it. Hearing me praise these two sutras so highly, he spent a full five years and memorized both the *Dharma Flower Sutra* and the *Shurangama Sutra*. He could recite the *Shurangama Sutra* from beginning to end without a single mistake. Why did I have him study it? It's the sutra I've loved most all my life. The prose of the *Shurangama Sutra* is the best and most wonderful—better than China's *Guwen Guanzhi* and the Four Books and Five Classics!

Ordinarily I don't concern myself with Heng Ding. All these years since I left Hong Kong, Heng Ding hasn't schemed for anything either; he shuts his door, doesn't see people, and doesn't chatter with anyone. He has certain Dharma protectors who, when the time comes, bring him rice, food, and things he needs. Someone says, "Then won't he starve to death?" He doesn't even talk anymore—why worry about whether he starves? Wouldn't starving to death be the best thing? Someone says, "I couldn't do that!" If you could do it, you would attain the fundamental substance of True Suchness.

Later he cultivated in Hong Kong for more than ten years. After about 12 years, he himself knew that in one more month he would become enlightened and have some accomplishment. But just one month short of that, he became possessed by demons. Three great demon kings went to trouble him. He knew it himself and could see them too. The three demons had Heng Ding in their grip and bewildered him until he was all upside down; they made it so that he couldn't sleep, his mind couldn't settle down, and he couldn't bow to the Buddhas.

As the saying goes, "When the Way rises a foot, the demons rise ten feet." If you have no merit and virtue, the demons pay no attention to you; but if you have merit and virtue, they want to come and take a share of it, so as to increase their own power. Not everyone who cultivates the Way will necessarily encounter this; if you haven't reached that level, the demons won't bother with you either. It's like how a knight-errant sometimes runs into great bandits of the rivers and seas; if you're not a knight-errant, you won't even run into them, and if you did, you wouldn't know it.

Heng Ding had no way out, so (in 1972) he phoned from Hong Kong and wrote a letter asking me to save him.

Now I'll tell you a piece of news that no one would believe—you won't believe it either! After Heng Ding phoned, the gods, dragons, and the rest of the eightfold division here, along with the monastics and laypeople, all dedicated merit to him. He originally wanted me to go back, but I thought, why spend several hundred dollars on a plane ticket? Right here, using something the demon kings like to eat, I caught them and brought them over. Our demon-king prison here now has three more Chinese demon kings. Now Heng Ding's demonic illness is gone. Isn't that wonderful? Do you believe this news or not? If you believe it, that's fine; if you don't believe it, that matters even less—it doesn't matter! I'm just telling it; you just listen.

Recently three Bhikshus went to Hong Kong, and I specially told them to go see him and check whether this illness of his was cured. They said he was well; there was no more demonic light in his eyes.

How does one become possessed by demons? No matter what Dharma door you cultivate, you must first emphasize virtuous conduct; if your virtue is insufficient, whatever Dharma you cultivate will meet with demonic obstacles. So in daily life you should do more meritorious work and establish more virtue. In cultivation, you must do a lot of meritorious and virtuous deeds. Supporting the Way-place while you are in the Way-place, working hard and bearing complaints without resentment—this is establishing merit. Why are we willing to do things others aren't willing to do? It's precisely to cultivate our own outer blessings, our outer merit. Establishing virtue means not obstructing others and not causing others to give rise to afflictions.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshu Heng Ding's oral account, given on January 14, 2001, at the Boat Temple in Hong Kong:

In the thirty-eighth year of the Republic (1949), I had just finished receiving the precepts. At first I was at Nanhua Monastery and Yunmen; then the *** came, and I followed the Master to Hong Kong. In Hong Kong I followed the Master for eight years; at that time the Master had not yet built a Way-place. Then I went to Tung Po Tor Monastery and lived there for nine years, and after that I moved to live at the Guanyin Boat Temple.

I had memorized the *Vajra Sutra* before I left the home-life. At Nanhua Monastery I memorized a bit of the *Shurangama Sutra*, and at Tung Po Tor I finished memorizing the *Shurangama Sutra*; it took me five years. Elder Master Hsu Yun had all the disciples he taught memorize the *Shurangama Sutra*; there are many benefits in it. When I was at Cixing Monastery, the Master printed the *Shurangama Sutra* and the *Vajra Sutra*, and I was in charge of proofreading, because I had long since memorized them. At that time the Master was building the Way-place. I went to live at Cixing Monastery for a year and a half and memorized the *Dharma Flower Sutra*, and I experienced many states: in samadhi I saw Shakyamuni Buddha and many great Bhikshus, one of whom was wearing large earrings.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

I think the Master was fond of Dharma Master Heng Ding. The Master often said that he was a university student, and that he spent an entire year memorizing the *Shurangama Sutra*. In the 1970s, Dharma Master Heng Ding wrote to the Master saying that he was entangled by demonic states, and begged the Master to save him.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Tan Guo Shi recounted:

After the Master came to America, he asked Dharma Master Heng Ding many times to come to America, but he refused. He stayed at Guanyin Cave and didn't leave until the mid-1970s, when he moved to live in another cave, which was beside the "Guanyin Boat Temple." From then on he lived in the cave and looked after that Guanyin boat. Because he didn't go out to seek alms, he often had nothing to eat. Although the Master was far away in America, he never forgot to ask his Hong Kong disciples to make offerings to him. Dharma Master Heng Ding would come to the Buddhist Lecture Hall on the first day of the first lunar month and on the fifteenth day of the seventh lunar month; the rest of the time he sat quietly in the cave investigating Chan. The dampness of the cave also caused him to become hunchbacked later in life.

When I first met the Master, Dharma Master Heng Ding was still cultivating at Guanyin Cave on Fu Yung Shan in Tsuen Wan, in the New Territories. He didn't like the bustling city life of Hong Kong. He was an ascetic practitioner. When he and the Master came out to Hong Kong, they often had nothing to eat, or ate food that had gone moldy. I seldom saw him at Western Bliss Gardens Monastery; I saw him only on the first day of the lunar new year. As I remember, if he wasn't investigating Chan at Guanyin Cave, he was in closed retreat in a little thatched hut beside the mountain path at Cixing Chan Monastery on Lantau Island.

Because of his cultivation, the residents nearby gradually came to know of him and went to make offerings to him. Nearby temples would also look after him with a meal. Many people came from Taiwan and overseas wanting to take refuge with him, but he refused them all. Why was he unwilling to take disciples? He said he was not yet qualified to be anyone's teacher; he wanted to put an end to the cycle of birth and death in this very life. Dharma Master Heng Ding said, "If I can't even control my own birth and death, how can I be anyone's teacher?"

Dharma Master Heng Ding knew in advance that his time had come and that he was going to depart. Three days before he passed away, he told the layperson who regularly brought him meals that there was no need to bring food anymore. The layperson found this strange and asked Dharma Master Heng Ding the reason. Dharma Master Heng Ding said he was going to leave, but the layperson did not grasp the real meaning of this and still thought that Dharma Master Heng Ding ought to keep eating until the day he left. So he had no choice but to tell the layperson to bring only liquid food, and the layperson did as told. On the third day, the meal was delivered as usual, but Dharma Master Heng Ding was found seated in full lotus before the Buddha, having passed away! (Editor's note: Dharma Master Heng Ding passed away on the morning of October 11, 2003. He had been a monastic for 60 years and was 80 years old.)

## 98. On the Eve of Leaving Hong Kong

On the eve of leaving Hong Kong for America, the Venerable Master still used all kinds of expedient means to skillfully transform living beings—even at the dining table when he hosted Urban Council member and barrister Brook Bernacchi...

In 1962 (the renyin year), the date set for the Venerable Master's departure from Hong Kong was the second day of the second lunar month (March 7), which was precisely his mother's birthday. When the Venerable Master was young, he served his mother with the utmost filial piety, settling her in at night and greeting her in the morning, keeping her warm in winter and cool in summer, caring for her in every detail; he even lived in a hut beside his mother's grave to observe mourning, and people called him "Filial Son Bai." The Venerable Master's choice of this day to set out was in effect commemorating his mother with his resolve to propagate the teaching in the West on this journey.

On March 6, the Venerable Master invited Urban Council member and barrister Brook Bernacchi and his friends to lunch at the Buddhist Lecture Hall, with Upasika Tan Guo Zheng and Upasaka Wen Guo Mi keeping them company. During the meal, the conversation turned to the Venerable Master's eating one meal a day, at midday; the Venerable Master had vowed to save the food he didn't eat and dedicate it to hungry living beings. Bernacchi said, "Look, the government dumps lots of rice into the sea. Your not eating doesn't do any good." The Venerable Master replied, "Each person has his own resolve." When the Venerable Master was eating, if he happened to drop a grain of rice on the floor, he would immediately pick it up and eat it.

Since he would soon be setting out on a long journey, the Venerable Master personally recorded the Great Compassion Mantra and the Shurangama Mantra on a tape recorder for everyone, and he exhorted them, "Whoever listens to these countless tens of thousands of times can be cured of any illness whatsoever."

In the Venerable Master's own words:

Ever since I was young, I've been willing to take on suffering on behalf of living beings. All the blessings I ought to enjoy I give to everyone, and everyone's suffering I take on myself. At that time, it was just when Japan was invading Manchuria in China's Northeast. I was observing mourning at my mother's grave, and someone told me, "The Japanese are seizing Chinese people to do forced labor. They don't get enough to eat and aren't given clothes to wear. Many freeze to death or starve to death, and then the Japanese have dogs eat the corpses!" When I thought of so many people with no clothes to wear and no food to eat, with so many starving and freezing to death, I made a vow: I would eat one meal a day, and leave my morning meal and my evening meal for people who have no food.

Most of my disciples eat one meal a day—not only the monastics; many laypeople also eat one meal a day. They're willing to learn this stupid method of mine. Using this stupid method to cultivate in this age of science, you could say it's not knowing how to keep accounts, not knowing how to calculate—or you could say it's calculating very clearly. The food we don't eat, we give to others to eat, forming affinities of food and drink with them; I believe that to the end of the future we will never starve to death. Because we're afraid of starving to death, we save some of what we ought to eat, to eat slowly later.

**[Addendum 1]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

The Master has booked his plane ticket. The date of departure from Hong Kong is the second day of the second lunar month, which is precisely the Master's mother's birthday. The Master chose this day to set out in order to commemorate his mother with a mind set on propagating Buddhism. The seventh day of the tenth lunar month is the Master's father's birthday, and on that day the year before last, the Master flew to Australia.

**[Addendum 2]** Upasaka Wen Guo Mi recounted:

Bernacchi was British and had been sent to Hong Kong by the Queen. In 1960 he became a Queen's Counsel, and he was also a council member in Hong Kong for many years. Bernacchi greatly respected the Master. He had a tea garden at Ngong Ping on Lantau Island—he was its owner—so he often visited the Master at Cixing Monastery and at the Buddhist Lecture Hall. Once he invited the Master to lunch at his tea garden, and the Master took me along. Bernacchi's Ngong Ping tea garden was the first tea garden in Hong Kong.

## 99. Tears at the Airport

The Venerable Master had returned to Hong Kong from Australia, and his visa for America had already been approved for some time. So he prepared to fly to America on March 7, stopping for one week in Tokyo, Japan, and then for two weeks in Honolulu, Hawaii, before flying on to San Francisco. During his 14 years in Hong Kong, the Venerable Master had spent every day rushing about to propagate the Dharma. Now that his Dharma affinities lay in America and he was about to leave Hong Kong, his disciples could hardly bear to part with him...

Early in the morning of March 7, Wen Guo Mi drove to the Lecture Hall to take the Venerable Master to the airport. People from all walks of life, fellow Buddhists, and many disciples also went to the airport to see him off. When the plane took off at 9 a.m., Guo Mi wept with grief, unable to bear the parting, and the crowd seeing him off could not hold back their tears either.

In the Venerable Master's own words:

For the sake of building Cixing Monastery, I worked there until my hair turned white, and most people thought I would surely be unable to let it go. "When I see others die, my heart burns hot as fire; not that I burn for the others—look, look, my turn is coming!" When I was leaving Hong Kong, I wrote these few lines, and some people who saw them didn't even dare to read them aloud.

While I was in Hong Kong, I went to Australia, Burma, Thailand, and Singapore, but wherever I went, I wasn't welcomed. In this world, many things go awry through mix-ups, and there are people who deliberately turn right and wrong upside down. People who tell the truth are envied, and people like those who tell lies. Now Hong Kong stinks, because there are too many people; this stench has changed Hong Kong, so teaching and transforming living beings in Hong Kong isn't easy—it's very difficult. But after difficulty, there is no more difficulty. If I hadn't undergone this kind of difficulty, I wouldn't know what kind of world this is; I'd still think this world was very happy. Only after going through many difficulties do you know that this world is full of suffering. Although it is suffering, we must still, in the spirit of the Six Perfections and the myriad practices, go everywhere to propagate the Buddhadharma and work hard to transform it into a world of ultimate bliss.

When I was in Hong Kong, some of my refuge disciples were also careless and sloppy, not knowing how good their teacher was to them. After I left, little by little they came to realize how hard it is to be without their teacher, but even if they wanted to see me, they couldn't. So this one writes a letter hoping I'll go back, that one writes a letter hoping I'll go back, and I don't pay attention to any of them. That's how people are: when they can see you every day, they don't think it's important; once they can't see you and there's nothing they can do about it, then they feel it's important.

**[Addendum 1]** Bhikshu Elder Zhi Ding recounted in his article "In Memory of Elder To Lun":

In 1962, when Elder To Lun was coming to America to propagate the Dharma, he passed through Hawaii and stayed for two weeks. I did my modest part as host, looked after him properly, and held a welcome reception, and on many occasions I invited Elder To Lun to give instructional talks to the fourfold assembly of disciples here. After staying in Hawaii for two weeks, Elder To Lun flew directly to San Francisco.

**[Addendum 2]** From the diary of Upasika Tan Guo Zheng:

On the afternoon of March 8, I went to the Buddhist Lecture Hall to mail packages of sutras to America. Suddenly everything everywhere seemed gloomy, and I didn't know when we would see the light of day again.

**[Addendum 3]** Upasika Mei Miaoling recounted:

The Master left Hong Kong for America in 1962. My mother asked the Master why he was leaving. The Master said his Hong Kong disciples were not well-behaved and seldom came to the temple, so he was leaving.

**The Venerable Master said:**

I have vowed to reorganize Buddhism in the West and make it flourish anew, so that people will understand what Buddhism is really about.

Why did I come to America to propagate the Buddhadharma? Because this country doesn't have a long history, and the people's habits aren't so cunning; they're all honest, straightforward people who can very easily cultivate according to the Dharma and very easily accept the principles of Buddhism. Because of this, I came to this country to propagate the Buddhadharma, hoping to make everyone understand the true principles of Buddhism.

## 100. Seeing Hong Kong Again

At the end of 1974, the Venerable Master returned to Hong Kong from America for the first time. Later, in 1978, 1981, and 1988, at the invitation of the Buddhist Association of Taiwan, Buddhist circles in Southeast Asia, and the Third World Buddhist Sangha Council, the Venerable Master returned to his old haunts...

From late November 1974 to January 1975, the Venerable Master, who had been away from Hong Kong for a full 12 years, led two American disciples who had earlier made the "three steps, one bow" pilgrimage, Dharma Masters Heng Ju and Heng Yo, on a tour to propagate the Dharma and make visits in Taiwan, Hong Kong, Thailand, India, Ceylon, Singapore, Vietnam, and Japan. During the tour, besides speaking the Dharma at monasteries, Way-places, and universities in each place, he also presided over a Buddha Recitation session in Hong Kong. This was the Venerable Master's first return to Hong Kong from America.

From July to September 1978, at the invitation of Buddhist circles throughout Southeast Asia, the Venerable Master led a delegation to propagate the Dharma in Malaysia, Singapore, Bangkok in Thailand, Hong Kong, and other places. He arrived in Hong Kong at 8 p.m. on September 16; this was the Venerable Master's second return to Hong Kong. From September 22 to September 26, from 7 to 9 p.m., the Venerable Master gave instructional talks at the Buddhist Studies Lecture Hall of Tung Lin Kok Yuen.

At the end of November 1981, having been invited to attend the Third World Buddhist Sangha Council hosted by the Buddhist Association of Taiwan, the Venerable Master once again led a delegation to propagate the Dharma in Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore, and Malaysia. This was the Venerable Master's third return to Hong Kong.

In December 1988, at the invitation of Buddhist circles in Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore, and Malaysia, the Venerable Master once again led a delegation to propagate the Dharma in these places. This was the Venerable Master's fourth return to Hong Kong, and also his last.

Over more than 40 years, there were also many disciples in Hong Kong who followed the Venerable Master with the utmost sincerity. These devout refuge disciples—such as Huang Jiefang; the couple Li Jiajing and Huang Jiemei; Liang Huixian; Ruan Guo Ni; the couple Zeng Jinyan and Zeng Wanhua; Zeng Guo Sheng; Lin Guo Sen; and others—for decades, the same from one day to the next, have held firm faith in the Buddhadharma, supported the Way-places, and revered and followed the Venerable Master consistently from beginning to end.

How difficult it was for the Venerable Master to break new ground on rocky soil, sowing the Buddha-seeds of the Mahayana Buddhadharma in Hong Kong and lighting the lamp of the mind for living beings!

**Summary of the Venerable Master's instructional talk to his Hong Kong disciples, November 26, 1974:**

You say you hope I'll come back twice a year, three times a year. If I came back every day, you'd say, "Shifu isn't good in this way, and Shifu isn't right in that way either!" "Shifu plays favorites! He's good to this disciple and not good to that disciple." There would be plenty of gossip! This time at Chi Lin Nunnery I said it very clearly: if you can cultivate the Way, that is truly making offerings to me. If you don't cultivate the Way but give me good things to eat, I don't like that. Whoever among you can truly apply effort in cultivating the Way and earnestly put the Buddhadharma into practice—that is what it means to give me good things to eat.

Everyone should bring forth a true mind and use a true mind to cultivate the Way. That is "true vigor, and is called a true Dharma offering..." To tell you the truth, for decades I haven't dwelt on forms, sounds, smells, tastes, tangible objects, or dharmas. If you just keep giving me good things to eat, the better I eat, the unhappier I am. It uses up all my blessings, and my wisdom too, and I'd become someone even more stupid than you. Then how could I be your teacher?

On this trip back to Hong Kong, someone wanted to hand his Way-place over to me to manage and invited me to be its abbot. I gave him three conditions: "All your monastic and lay disciples must keep the rule of not eating after noon [Tr.: the monastic precept of taking no food after midday]; they must not eat after midday. That's the first condition. The second condition: no rushing about performing sutra recitations and repentances for payment; we are not afraid of starving to death. The third condition: no one, whether monastic or lay, may smoke cigarettes, drink alcohol, or eat meat." He couldn't accept any of my three conditions. Even if he had accepted just one condition, I wouldn't have wanted it. However big the Way-place, if it's so sloppy and its reputation so bad, I wouldn't take it no matter what!

November 1972

The Venerable Master's instructional talk to three American disciples who were about to go to Hong Kong:

When you get there, if anyone gives you something to eat after twelve noon—say he brings milk—pour the milk on the ground; if he brings candy, throw it into the toilet. And say this: "These things you've given me—I'm now telling them not to pass through a human stomach but to go straight to the toilet. You people are too bad! Why do you want to destroy our precepts? It's precisely because you didn't listen to his teachings that Shifu left and stopped teaching and transforming you. And to this day you still don't know to repent—you're still acting recklessly!"

**[Addendum 1]** *Kung Sheung Daily News*, Monday, September 17, 1978: (content omitted)

**[Addendum 2]** *Kung Sheung Daily News*, Wednesday, September 19, 1978: (content omitted)

**[Addendum 3]** *Kung Sheung Daily News*, Sunday, September 23, 1978: (content omitted)

**[Addendum 4]** In 1978, when the Venerable Master went to Asia to propagate the Dharma, while speaking the Dharma at Tung Lin Kok Yuen in Hong Kong, he revealed the hardships he had faced in propagating the Dharma in Hong Kong:

I have let down my compatriots and fellow cultivators in Hong Kong! I'm not saying this casually; these words come from the bottom of my heart. I lived in Hong Kong for more than ten years, from 1949 to 1962, and there wasn't a single day I didn't want to make the Buddhadharma flourish. But this place is very peculiar: if you tell people the truth, they don't believe it; if you tell them lies, especially rumors and gossip born of deviant views, they gladly accept them. I have always followed the principle that "the straight mind is the Way-place" and have never told lies, yet nobody likes to hear what I say. But no matter what, I'm going to speak the truth!

**[Addendum 5]** At the end of November 1981, the Venerable Master was invited to Asia to propagate the Dharma. From December 11 to 14, at City Hall in North Point, Hong Kong, he gave a series of Dharma lectures on four consecutive evenings, from 7 to 9 p.m. The audience listened to the Venerable Master's incisive instructional talks with great interest.

On the evening of the first day (December 11), the Venerable Master began right away by saying, "People who study Buddhism must first themselves have proper knowledge and views; only with proper knowledge and views can they know how to cultivate. In cultivation, one must first understand the Dharma door of the Seven Shares of Bodhi." He also spoke of "dhyana," saying, "When you gain a little benefit in samadhi, you give rise to arrogance and pride, or you think you've become enlightened. Remember this! You must not claim to have attained what you have not attained, babbling that you're enlightened—what kind of enlightenment have you got! This is telling a great lie, and in the future you'll fall into the Hell of Tongue Pulling. Therefore, all who study Buddhism must not create karma that is a mixture of good and evil, must not put tall hats on themselves, 'sell' their cultivation, or advertise their own virtue. That's the behavior of a con man!"

On the evening of the second day (December 12), the Venerable Master said, "I lived in Hong Kong for more than ten years, and many young people followed me in studying the Buddhadharma, because I'm also very fond of young people. Later, when the conditions ripened, I went to America." He also said, "American Buddhism is a brand-new Buddhism, but American Buddhism originally doesn't belong to America; rather, it belongs to the whole world. Buddhism should not have distinctions of nationality, race, or self and others. Buddhism is the religion of the whole world and all humanity. True Buddhism is not limited by distinctions of nationality, region, or self and others.

"In Asia, people generally acknowledge that this is the Dharma-Ending Age. But the Dharma itself does not have a Proper Dharma and a Dharma-Ending; rather, it is because people don't cultivate that there is a Dharma-Ending Age. As the saying goes, 'People can propagate the Dharma; it is not the Dharma that propagates people.' If people cultivate earnestly and without interruption, then the Proper Dharma will long abide in the world. When people don't cultivate, that is the Dharma-Ending Age."

On the evening of the third day—since for several days running the Hong Kong newspapers had all been carrying news of the Venerable Master's return to Hong Kong to propagate the Dharma, and one of the "hot" topics was the question "Why do monastics wear the kashaya?"—the Venerable Master, in response to these circumstances, patiently explained that evening the importance of Bhikshus wearing the kashaya. Then the Venerable Master recounted an incident from San Francisco. While he told it, the audience listened with rapt attention; not one person left in the middle. What incident was it? In 1978 the Golden Dragon Massacre took place in San Francisco, shocking the community and throwing the overseas Chinese of Chinatown into panic. Afterwards, the Wah Ching gang wanted to launch a second or even a third Golden Dragon massacre, all because the Wah Ching and the Joe Boys were fighting over turf. But later they all took refuge with the Triple Jewel, took refuge at the City of Ten Thousand Buddhas, and threw their weapons into the sea. After they reformed, those who went to school went to school and those who worked went to work, and they never again did evil. The Venerable Master said, "I would rather teach people through the power of moral transformation than oppress people with force."

On the evening of the fourth day (December 14), the Venerable Master first explained "the Venerable Ananda's four questions to the Buddha," and then expounded the "Four Applications of Mindfulness" among the "Thirty-seven Wings of Enlightenment," namely, "Contemplate the body as impure, contemplate feelings as suffering, contemplate the mind as impermanent, and contemplate dharmas as without self." The Venerable Master said, "The sea of suffering is boundless; turn your head around, and there is the shore. Don't keep bobbing up and down in the sea of suffering anymore; otherwise you will never have a day of liberation." Because the audience was delighted to hear the essentials of the Dharma and did not want it to end, the Venerable Master continued: "In the past I lived in Hong Kong for 14 years, and in 1962 I left Hong Kong for America. Although I lived in Hong Kong for so long, I made no contribution at all to Hong Kong. Coming back from America this time, I wish to establish Dharma affinities with all you elders, brothers, and compatriots of Hong Kong, and truly deliver the Buddhadharma into your hearts. If I can do this, I will feel comforted."

**[Addendum 6]** The Venerable Master said:

Because Hong Kong's environment is very peculiar—neither like Chinese culture nor like Western culture, neither Chinese nor Western—it's very complicated. In this kind of environment, doing business comes first, and whoever makes the most money is considered the most capable. As for the Buddhadharma, you could say people have only a superficial understanding, and not many study it seriously. Among the virtuous ones who have dabbled a little in Buddhist studies, very few truly cultivate, and it's very hard to find people who truly want to enter deeply into the sutra treasury and have wisdom like the sea. All the people here know about is profit, and all they understand is obtaining profit. There is very little study of Buddhism, so no matter where a sutra lecture or Dharma assembly is held, very few people come. This is the result of Hong Kong's peculiar environment.

Reproduced from the Taiwan Xuefo website (http://www.xuefo.tw)

| 打妄想 | have idle thoughts; 妄想 false thinking |
| 業風 | karmic winds |
